--- teach
The last course ended by finding and fixing a slow query. That was the last new tool. This course puts all three SQL courses to work on whole projects.

First come three databases, built step by step, each lesson carrying on from the last. Then come three [[capstones|capstone]], where you design everything yourself from a written **specification**: a description of what the database must do, with no code in it.

### Project one: an online store

**Project one is an online store's database**, from an empty file to the reports the owners read every Monday. It takes five lessons: the schema, cleaning the export, loading the data, a revenue report and a customer report.

This lesson is the first: the **schema**, meaning the set of tables, their columns and their rules. You write only `CREATE TABLE`s. No data goes in yet.

### The table you start with

The shop has been running on spreadsheets. Its export is already here, as a table called `import_rows`. Each row is one **order line**: one product in one order. Here are four of its 12 rows, with some columns left out:

| order_ref | email | customer_name | sku | product_name | price | qty | status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1001 | ana@mail.com | Ana Ruiz | MUG | Mug | 9.00 | 2 | paid |
| 1001 | ana@mail.com | Ana Ruiz | PEN | Fountain pen | 32.50 | 1 | paid |
| 1002 | BO@mail.com | Bo Chen | LAMP | Desk lamp | 45.00 | 1 | SHIPPED |
| 1006 | bo@mail.com | Bo Chen | HEAD | Headphones | 79.99 | 1 | paid |

The customer and the product are copied onto every line. That is the "notebook" from the lesson "Design: normalizing a flat table". The other columns are `placed` (when the order was made), `country` and `category`. You will clean this data in the next lesson and load it in the one after.

### Four tables, each about one thing

As in "Design: normalizing a flat table", each fact goes in the table it describes:

- `customers`: who buys.
- `products`: what is sold.
- `orders`: one purchase. Who made it, when, and what state it is in.
- `order_items`: one product within one order. How many, and at what price.

The next six steps are decisions about these tables. Each one is worth making on purpose.

### Decision 1: money in whole cents

In the lesson "Aggregates" you met floating-point numbers, and saw that `SELECT 0.1 + 0.2;` gives `0.30000000000000004`, not `0.3`. Decimals are stored as floating-point numbers, which cannot hold most decimals exactly. Add up thousands of prices, and the total drifts by fractions of a cent.

Whole numbers do not drift. So store money as a whole number of cents, in an `INTEGER` column:

```sql
price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
```

A 9.00 mug is stored as `900`. You divide by `100.0` only at the very end, when you show the number to a person: `900 / 100.0` gives `9.0`. This is how [[real payment systems|minor-units]] do it. The `CHECK` refuses a negative price.

### Decision 2: the price charged lives on the order line

`products` keeps today's **list price**, the price the shop is asking now, in `price_cents`. But each line in `order_items` keeps its own `unit_cents`: the price **charged** on that line, for one unit.

Why both? Prices change, and history must not. In the export, the headphones sold for 89.99 in February and 79.99 in a March sale. If the old orders only pointed at today's price, a price change would rewrite what customers paid. This is the `unit_price` idea from "Design: normalizing a flat table", now in cents.

### Decision 3: natural keys stay unique

Each customer gets an `id` that the database fills in. That `id` is what `orders` points at. But an email may still belong to only one customer, and a SKU to only one product. Say so with `UNIQUE`:

```sql
email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
```

Without it, the same person could [[end up as two customers|two-customers]], each with half the orders. As you saw in "Design: normalizing a flat table", an email or a SKU is a natural key, and the `id` is an invented one.

### Decision 4: states are a closed list

An order's `status` can only be `'paid'`, `'shipped'` or `'refunded'`. That is a **closed list**: a fixed set of allowed values, with nothing else accepted. A `CHECK` with `IN`, from the lesson "Constraints: rules the database enforces", enforces it:

```sql
status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))
```

Now `'Shipped '` (capital S, space at the end) and `'shiped'` (a typo) can never be stored. Try it, and the insert fails with `CHECK constraint failed`. A report that counts `'shipped'` orders can then [[trust the list|status-list]].

### Decision 5: one line per product per order

Buying two mugs is one line with `qty = 2`, not two lines. Make the database hold you to that with a composite primary key, written after the columns:

```sql
PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)
```

The pair must be unique. Order 1001 can have a mug line and a pen line, but not two mug lines. A second mug line fails with `UNIQUE constraint failed`. A `CHECK (qty > 0)` makes sure every line has at least 1 of something.

### Decision 6: children go with their parent

Each line of `order_items` points at its order and at its product. In the lesson "Foreign keys" you chose what happens when the row being pointed at is deleted:

- Deleting an order should delete its lines. A line means nothing without its order. That is `ON DELETE CASCADE` on `order_id`.
- Deleting a product that has been sold should be refused. Those sales really happened. That is the default, `NO ACTION`, so `product_id` needs only `REFERENCES products(id)`.

Here is [[what each rule does|cascade-picture]] when you delete. And remember the SQLite catch from that lesson: none of this is enforced until you run `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;`.

**Watch out:** without `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;` every `REFERENCES` is only a comment. An order line can point at an order that does not exist, deleting an order leaves its lines behind, and nothing warns you. Put the pragma first, before any table is used.

::: context capstone What a capstone is
A **capstone** is the flat stone laid on top of a wall to finish it. Schools borrowed the word for the final project of a course, the one that uses everything learned before it. In this course's capstones, you get only a written list of what the database must do, the way a real job starts. Engineering teams work from specifications all the time: a spacecraft's flight software has a written list of requirements, and every one of them must be met and tested.
:::

::: context minor-units How payment systems store money
Payment services such as Stripe take every amount as a whole number in the currency's smallest unit: 900 means 9.00 dollars or 9.00 euros. The smallest unit is called the **minor unit**. Some currencies have none: the Japanese yen has no cents, so an amount of 900 there means 900 yen. Whole numbers add up exactly, so a total of a million payments is right to the cent. Only the screen or the receipt divides by 100 and adds the decimal point.
:::

::: context two-customers Why an id alone is not enough
An `id` is always unique, because the database hands out a new one for each row. So the `id` cannot stop the same person being added twice. Import the export carelessly, and Ana could become customer 1 and customer 6, with half her orders on each. Every report would then undercount her spending. `UNIQUE` on the email refuses the second copy at the door. That turns a silent mistake into a loud error that you fix once.
:::

::: context status-list Why a closed list pays off
Spreadsheets let anyone type anything, which is how this export ended up with `'paid'`, `'Paid'`, `'SHIPPED'` and `'shipped'`. A report that counts `WHERE status = 'shipped'` quietly misses the `'SHIPPED'` one. With a closed list, every stored value is one of three exact words. Spacecraft software uses the same idea for its **modes**: a vehicle is in exactly one of a fixed set, such as launch, cruise or safe mode, never in a mode someone misspelled.
:::

::: context cascade-picture Deleting an order, deleting a product
On the left, deleting order 1001 also deletes its two lines, because `order_id` has `ON DELETE CASCADE`. On the right, deleting the mug is refused with `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed`, because order lines still point at it and `product_id` uses the default, `NO ACTION`.

--- task
Write the store's schema. The starter already turns foreign keys on and creates `customers`. Add the other three tables:

1. Keep `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;` as the first line, and keep `customers` as it is: `id` integer primary key; `email` text, required, unique; `name` text, required; `country` text, required.
2. `products`: `id` integer primary key; `sku` text, required, unique; `name` text, required; `category` text, required; `price_cents` integer, required, not negative (`>= 0`).
3. `orders`: `id` integer primary key; `customer_id` integer, required, referencing `customers(id)`; `placed` text, required; `status` text, required, one of `'paid'`, `'shipped'`, `'refunded'`.
4. `order_items`: `order_id` integer, required, referencing `orders(id)`, and deleted with its order (`ON DELETE CASCADE`); `product_id` integer, required, referencing `products(id)`; `qty` integer, required, at least 1 (`> 0`); `unit_cents` integer, required, not negative; primary key `(order_id, product_id)`.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE customers (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  country TEXT NOT NULL
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE customers (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  country TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE products (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  category TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))
);
CREATE TABLE order_items (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES products(id),
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  unit_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (unit_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)
);
--- hint
Model each table on `customers`: one column per line, the type, then its rules. Go through the lesson's six decisions and find the rule each one needs.
--- hint
Rules stack after the type, like `price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)` and `status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))`. A link is `customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id)`.
--- hint
In `order_items`, `order_id` is `INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE`. After the last column, add a comma and `PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)`.
--- check query | Foreign keys are on
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | A customer, a product and an order with a line can be stored
INSERT INTO customers (id, email, name, country) VALUES (1, 'ana@mail.com', 'Ana', 'ES');
INSERT INTO products (id, sku, name, category, price_cents) VALUES (1, 'MUG', 'Mug', 'home', 900);
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status) VALUES (1, 1, '2024-01-03 10:00:00', 'paid');
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, unit_cents) VALUES (1, 1, 2, 900) RETURNING order_id, qty
=> [[1, 2]]
--- check query | A second customer with the same email is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO customers (id, email, name, country) VALUES (2, 'ana@mail.com', 'Ana B', 'ES') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | A duplicate SKU or a negative price is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO products (id, sku, name, category, price_cents) VALUES (2, 'MUG', 'Mug 2', 'home', 900), (3, 'CUP', 'Cup', 'home', -1) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | An unknown status is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status) VALUES (2, 1, '2024-01-04 10:00:00', 'Shipped ') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | A zero quantity, or the same product twice in one order, is refused
INSERT INTO products (id, sku, name, category, price_cents) VALUES (4, 'PEN', 'Pen', 'stationery', 3250);
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, unit_cents) VALUES (1, 1, 1, 900), (1, 4, 0, 3250) RETURNING qty
=> []
--- check query | order_items points at orders (cascading) and products
SELECT "table", "from", on_delete FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('order_items') ORDER BY "from"
=> [["orders", "order_id", "CASCADE"], ["products", "product_id", "NO ACTION"]]
--- check query | Deleting an order deletes its lines
DELETE FROM orders WHERE id = 1;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM order_items
=> [[0]]

+++ practice | A parts catalog with rules
--- task
A workshop keeps its parts catalog in a table called `parts`. Create it with these columns and rules:

1. `id`: integer primary key.
2. `part_no`: text, required, and no two parts may share one.
3. `name`: text, required.
4. `price_cents`: the price in whole cents, an integer, required, and not negative. A free part, at 0 cents, is allowed.
5. `status`: text, required, and only `'active'` or `'retired'`.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE parts (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  part_no TEXT,
  name TEXT,
  price_cents INTEGER,
  status TEXT
);
--- solution
CREATE TABLE parts (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  part_no TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('active', 'retired'))
);
--- hint
Each rule in the list is one rule on a column: required is `NOT NULL`, "no two may share" is `UNIQUE`, and a range or a closed list is a `CHECK`.
--- hint
"Not negative" includes 0, so the price test is `>= 0`, not `> 0`. The closed list is `CHECK (status IN ('active', 'retired'))`.
--- check query | A part can be stored, and a free part is allowed
INSERT INTO parts (id, part_no, name, price_cents, status) VALUES (1, 'V-100', 'Valve', 0, 'active') RETURNING id, price_cents
=> [[1, 0]]
--- check query | A second part with the same part number is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO parts (id, part_no, name, price_cents, status) VALUES (2, 'V-100', 'Valve B', 500, 'active') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | A negative price, or a status outside the list, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO parts (id, part_no, name, price_cents, status) VALUES (3, 'V-200', 'Pump', -1, 'active'), (4, 'V-300', 'Seal', 150, 'Active') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | A part with no name or no status is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO parts (id, part_no, name, price_cents, status) VALUES (5, 'V-400', NULL, 100, 'retired'), (6, 'V-450', 'Clamp', 100, NULL) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | A retired part is accepted
INSERT INTO parts (id, part_no, name, price_cents, status) VALUES (7, 'V-500', 'Hose', 1250, 'retired') RETURNING status
=> [["retired"]]

+++ practice | Tracks on a playlist
--- schema
CREATE TABLE playlists (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE tracks (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  title TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO playlists (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Launch day'), (2, 'Night shift');
INSERT INTO tracks (id, title) VALUES (1, 'Ignition'), (2, 'Max Q'), (3, 'Orbit');
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `playlists` (`id`, `name`) and `tracks` (`id`, `title`). Each track on a playlist sits in a numbered slot. Write the schema for that:

1. Turn foreign keys on with `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;`.
2. Create `playlist_items` with `playlist_id` (integer, required, referencing `playlists(id)`, and deleted with its playlist), `track_id` (integer, required, referencing `tracks(id)`; deleting a track that is on a playlist must be refused, which is the default), and `position` (integer, required, 1 or more).
3. A track may appear on a playlist only once: make `(playlist_id, track_id)` the primary key.
4. Two tracks may not share a slot on the same playlist: `(playlist_id, position)` must be unique too.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE playlist_items (
  playlist_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES playlists(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  track_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES tracks(id),
  position INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (position >= 1),
  PRIMARY KEY (playlist_id, track_id),
  UNIQUE (playlist_id, position)
);
--- hint
Two of the rules look at a pair of columns. A pair rule goes after the last column, as its own line: one `PRIMARY KEY (…, …)` and one `UNIQUE (…, …)`.
--- hint
Only `playlist_id` gets `ON DELETE CASCADE`. For `track_id`, a plain `REFERENCES tracks(id)` already refuses the delete.
--- check query | Foreign keys are on
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Tracks can be placed in slots
INSERT INTO playlist_items (playlist_id, track_id, position) VALUES (1, 1, 1), (1, 2, 2), (2, 1, 1) RETURNING position
=> [[1], [2], [1]]
--- check query | The same track twice on one playlist is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO playlist_items (playlist_id, track_id, position) VALUES (1, 1, 3) RETURNING track_id
=> []
--- check query | Two tracks in one slot, or a slot numbered 0, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO playlist_items (playlist_id, track_id, position) VALUES (1, 3, 2), (2, 2, 0) RETURNING track_id
=> []
--- check query | playlist_id cascades; track_id uses the default
SELECT "table", "from", on_delete FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('playlist_items') ORDER BY "from"
=> [["playlists", "playlist_id", "CASCADE"], ["tracks", "track_id", "NO ACTION"]]
--- check query | Deleting a playlist deletes its items
DELETE FROM playlists WHERE id = 1;
SELECT playlist_id, track_id FROM playlist_items
=> [[2, 1]]

+++ practice | Invoices, their lines and their totals
--- schema
CREATE TABLE clients (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO clients (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Acme'), (2, 'Bolt');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `clients` (`id`, `name`). Build a small invoicing schema on top of it:

1. Turn foreign keys on.
2. `invoices`: `id` (integer primary key), `client_id` (integer, required, referencing `clients(id)`), `issued` (text, required), `status` (text, required, one of `'draft'`, `'sent'`, `'paid'`).
3. `invoice_lines`: `invoice_id` (integer, required, referencing `invoices(id)`, deleted with its invoice), `line_no` (integer, required, 1 or more), `description` (text, required), `cents` (integer, required, not negative). The primary key is `(invoice_id, line_no)`.
4. A view `invoice_totals` with one row for **every** invoice, and the columns `invoice_id`, `client` (the client's name), `lines` (how many lines it has) and `total_cents` (the sum of its lines, and 0 when it has none).
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE invoices (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  client_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES clients(id),
  issued TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE invoices (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  client_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES clients(id),
  issued TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('draft', 'sent', 'paid'))
);
CREATE TABLE invoice_lines (
  invoice_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES invoices(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  line_no INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (line_no >= 1),
  description TEXT NOT NULL,
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (invoice_id, line_no)
);
CREATE VIEW invoice_totals AS
SELECT i.id AS invoice_id, c.name AS client,
       COUNT(l.line_no) AS lines,
       COALESCE(SUM(l.cents), 0) AS total_cents
FROM invoices i
JOIN clients c ON c.id = i.client_id
LEFT JOIN invoice_lines l ON l.invoice_id = i.id
GROUP BY i.id, c.name;
--- hint
The tables follow the store's `orders` and `order_items`. The view is the new part: an invoice with no lines must still get a row, so the lines are joined with `LEFT JOIN`.
--- hint
Count a column of the lines, such as `COUNT(l.line_no)`, not `COUNT(*)`, and wrap the sum in `COALESCE(…, 0)`: an invoice with no lines has one joined row full of NULLs.
--- hint
The view is `SELECT i.id AS invoice_id, c.name AS client, COUNT(l.line_no) AS lines, COALESCE(SUM(l.cents), 0) AS total_cents FROM invoices i JOIN clients c … LEFT JOIN invoice_lines l … GROUP BY i.id, c.name`.
--- check query | Invoices and their lines can be stored
INSERT INTO invoices (id, client_id, issued, status) VALUES (1, 1, '2024-05-01', 'sent'), (2, 2, '2024-05-02', 'draft'), (3, 1, '2024-05-03', 'paid');
INSERT INTO invoice_lines (invoice_id, line_no, description, cents) VALUES (1, 1, 'Design', 50000), (1, 2, 'Hosting', 1999), (3, 1, 'Audit', 120000) RETURNING line_no
=> [[1], [2], [1]]
--- check query | An unknown status is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO invoices (id, client_id, issued, status) VALUES (4, 1, '2024-05-04', 'void') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | A repeated line number, line 0, or a negative amount is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO invoice_lines (invoice_id, line_no, description, cents) VALUES (1, 2, 'Again', 100), (2, 0, 'Zero', 100), (2, 1, 'Credit', -500) RETURNING line_no
=> []
--- check query | Every invoice has a total, the empty draft included
SELECT invoice_id, client, lines, total_cents FROM invoice_totals ORDER BY invoice_id
=> [[1, "Acme", 2, 51999], [2, "Bolt", 0, 0], [3, "Acme", 1, 120000]]
--- check query | Deleting an invoice deletes its lines
DELETE FROM invoices WHERE id = 1;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM invoice_lines
=> [[1]]

+++ practice | Coupons and their limits
--- task
A shop wants a `coupons` table whose rules hold right up to the edges. Create it:

1. `id`: integer primary key.
2. `code`: text, required, unique.
3. `pct_off`: the discount as a whole percentage, required, from 1 to 100 **inclusive**. 1 and 100 are both allowed; 0 and 101 are not.
4. `max_uses`: an integer. NULL means the coupon can be used any number of times. Otherwise it must be at least 1.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE coupons (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  code TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  pct_off INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (pct_off > 0 AND pct_off < 100),
  max_uses INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (max_uses > 0)
);
--- solution
CREATE TABLE coupons (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  code TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  pct_off INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (pct_off BETWEEN 1 AND 100),
  max_uses INTEGER CHECK (max_uses >= 1)
);
--- hint
Test the edges one by one: what does the starter do with a coupon of exactly 100 percent, and with one that has no use limit?
--- hint
`BETWEEN 1 AND 100` includes both ends. A `CHECK` only refuses a row when its test is false, and a comparison with NULL is not false, so `max_uses` stays optional as long as it is not `NOT NULL`.
--- check query | 1 percent and 100 percent are allowed, and so is no use limit
INSERT INTO coupons (id, code, pct_off, max_uses) VALUES (1, 'TINY', 1, NULL), (2, 'FREE', 100, 1) RETURNING pct_off, max_uses
=> [[1, null], [100, 1]]
--- check query | 0 percent and 101 percent are refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO coupons (id, code, pct_off, max_uses) VALUES (3, 'NONE', 0, 5), (4, 'MORE', 101, 5) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | A use limit of 0 or less is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO coupons (id, code, pct_off, max_uses) VALUES (5, 'ZERO', 10, 0), (6, 'NEG', 10, -5) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | A missing code or percentage, or a repeated code, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO coupons (id, code, pct_off, max_uses) VALUES (7, NULL, 10, 1), (8, 'HALF', NULL, 1), (9, 'TINY', 20, 1) RETURNING id
=> []

+++ practice | The cascade on the wrong column
--- task
A shop's cart schema has a bug. Deleting a **cart** should delete its items, and deleting a **product** that sits in a cart should be refused. Instead, deleting a cart fails with `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed`, and deleting a product would silently empty it out of every cart.

Fix the schema so that `cart_items.cart_id` is deleted with its cart and `cart_items.product_id` keeps the default rule. Keep everything else as it is.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE products (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE carts (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  owner TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE cart_items (
  cart_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES carts(id),
  product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES products(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (cart_id, product_id)
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE products (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE carts (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  owner TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE cart_items (
  cart_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES carts(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES products(id),
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (cart_id, product_id)
);
--- hint
Read the two `REFERENCES` lines of `cart_items` and ask of each one: when the row it points at is deleted, what should happen to the item?
--- hint
`ON DELETE CASCADE` belongs on `cart_id`. Take it off `product_id`, so that line has a plain `REFERENCES products(id)`.
--- check query | Foreign keys are on
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | cart_id cascades; product_id uses the default
SELECT "table", "from", on_delete FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('cart_items') ORDER BY "from"
=> [["carts", "cart_id", "CASCADE"], ["products", "product_id", "NO ACTION"]]
--- check query | Deleting a cart deletes its items and leaves the others
INSERT INTO products (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Mug'), (2, 'Pen');
INSERT INTO carts (id, owner) VALUES (1, 'ana'), (2, 'bo');
INSERT INTO cart_items (cart_id, product_id, qty) VALUES (1, 1, 2), (1, 2, 1), (2, 1, 1);
DELETE FROM carts WHERE id = 1;
SELECT cart_id, product_id FROM cart_items
=> [[2, 1]]
--- check query | Both products are still there
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM products
=> [[2]]

+++ practice | Sensors and their readings
--- task
Design a schema for a test stand's sensors:

1. Turn foreign keys on.
2. `sensors`: `id` (integer primary key), `serial` (text, required, unique), `kind` (text, required, one of `'temp'`, `'pressure'`, `'voltage'`), `installed` (text, required).
3. `readings`: `sensor_id` (integer, required, referencing `sensors(id)`, deleted with its sensor), `taken_at` (text, required), `value_milli` (integer, required: the reading in thousandths of its unit, so 21.375 degrees is stored as 21375; it may be negative). A sensor has at most one reading per moment: the primary key is `(sensor_id, taken_at)`.
4. A view `latest_readings` with one row per sensor that has readings, and the columns `serial`, `kind`, `taken_at` (the time of its most recent reading) and `value` (that reading in whole units, `value_milli` divided by 1000.0).
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE sensors (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  serial TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  kind TEXT NOT NULL,
  installed TEXT NOT NULL
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE sensors (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  serial TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  kind TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (kind IN ('temp', 'pressure', 'voltage')),
  installed TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE readings (
  sensor_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES sensors(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  taken_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  value_milli INTEGER NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (sensor_id, taken_at)
);
CREATE VIEW latest_readings AS
WITH ranked AS (
  SELECT sensor_id, taken_at, value_milli,
         ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY sensor_id ORDER BY taken_at DESC) AS rn
  FROM readings
)
SELECT s.serial, s.kind, r.taken_at, r.value_milli / 1000.0 AS value
FROM ranked r
JOIN sensors s ON s.id = r.sensor_id
WHERE r.rn = 1;
--- hint
Whole thousandths do for readings what whole cents do for money: they add up exactly. So `value_milli` is an `INTEGER`, with no `CHECK`, since readings can be below zero.
--- hint
The latest reading of each sensor is a top-1-per-group question, as in "Top N per group": number each sensor's readings with `ROW_NUMBER()`, newest first, and keep number 1.
--- hint
Inside the view: `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY sensor_id ORDER BY taken_at DESC) AS rn` in a CTE, then join `sensors` and keep `rn = 1`. Divide by `1000.0`, not `1000`.
--- check query | Sensors and readings can be stored, negative readings included
INSERT INTO sensors (id, serial, kind, installed) VALUES (1, 'T-01', 'temp', '2024-01-01'), (2, 'P-01', 'pressure', '2024-01-01'), (3, 'V-01', 'voltage', '2024-02-01');
INSERT INTO readings (sensor_id, taken_at, value_milli) VALUES (1, '2024-06-01 10:00', 21375), (1, '2024-06-01 10:01', -1250), (2, '2024-06-01 10:00', 101325) RETURNING value_milli
=> [[21375], [-1250], [101325]]
--- check query | A second reading at the same moment, or an unknown kind, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO readings (sensor_id, taken_at, value_milli) VALUES (1, '2024-06-01 10:00', 22000) RETURNING sensor_id;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO sensors (id, serial, kind, installed) VALUES (4, 'H-01', 'humidity', '2024-03-01') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | The latest reading per sensor; a sensor with none is left out
SELECT serial, kind, taken_at, value FROM latest_readings ORDER BY serial
=> [["P-01", "pressure", "2024-06-01 10:00", 101.325], ["T-01", "temp", "2024-06-01 10:01", -1.25]]
--- check query | Deleting a sensor deletes its readings
DELETE FROM sensors WHERE id = 1;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM readings
=> [[1]]

=== sqlp-01b | Store 1b: cleaning the export
--- schema
CREATE TABLE import_rows (
  order_ref INTEGER NOT NULL,
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  customer_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  country TEXT NOT NULL,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL,
  product_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  category TEXT NOT NULL,
  price TEXT NOT NULL,
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO import_rows VALUES
  (1001, '2024-01-03 10:00:00', 'ana@mail.com',  'Ana Ruiz',  'ES', 'MUG',   'Mug',          'home',        '9.00',  2, 'paid'),
  (1001, '2024-01-03 10:00:00', 'ana@mail.com',  'Ana Ruiz',  'ES', 'PEN',   'Fountain pen', 'stationery',  '32.50', 1, 'paid'),
  (1002, '2024-01-15 12:30:00', 'BO@mail.com ',  'Bo Chen',   'UK', 'LAMP',  'Desk lamp',    'home',        '45.00', 1, 'SHIPPED'),
  (1003, '2024-02-01 09:00:00', 'cy@mail.com',   'Cy Diaz',   'US', 'MUG',   'Mug',          'home',        '9.00',  4, 'shipped'),
  (1003, '2024-02-01 09:00:00', 'cy@mail.com',   'Cy Diaz',   'US', 'NOTE',  'Notebook',     'stationery',  '4.35',  3, 'shipped'),
  (1003, '2024-02-01 09:00:00', 'cy@mail.com',   'Cy Diaz',   'US', 'NOTE',  'Notebook',     'stationery',  '4.35',  3, 'shipped'),
  (1004, '2024-02-14 18:00:00', ' ana@mail.com', 'Ana Ruiz',  'ES', 'HEAD',  'Headphones',   'electronics', '89.99', 1, 'Paid'),
  (1005, '2024-02-20 11:11:00', 'dee@mail.com',  'Dee Evans', 'UK', 'PEN',   'Fountain pen', 'stationery',  '32.50', 2, 'refunded'),
  (1006, '2024-03-05 16:45:00', 'bo@mail.com',   'Bo Chen',   'UK', 'HEAD',  'Headphones',   'electronics', '79.99', 1, 'paid'),
  (1006, '2024-03-05 16:45:00', 'bo@mail.com',   'Bo Chen',   'UK', 'CABLE', 'USB cable',    'electronics', '6.50',  3, 'paid'),
  (1007, '2024-03-18 08:20:00', 'cy@mail.com',   'Cy Diaz',   'US', 'LAMP',  'Desk lamp',    'home',        '45.00', 1, 'shipped'),
  (1008, '2024-03-29 21:00:00', 'eli@mail.com',  'Eli Fox',   'US', 'MUG',   'Mug',          'home',        '9.00',  1, 'paid');
--- teach
Last lesson you designed the store's four tables, with strict rules on what they accept. Next lesson you will load the spreadsheet export into them. This lesson comes in between: you look hard at the export, find every problem in it, and write the expressions that fix each one. That way nothing reaches the tables until it is clean.

### The table

`import_rows` is the export from last lesson: 12 rows, one per order line. Its columns are `order_ref` (the order number), `placed`, `email`, `customer_name`, `country`, `sku`, `product_name`, `category`, `price`, `qty` and `status`.

### Look before you load

The first step of any load is to [[read the data with your own eyes|look-first]]:

```sql
SELECT order_ref, email, sku, price, qty, status FROM import_rows;
```

| order_ref | email | sku | price | qty | status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1001 | ana@mail.com | MUG | 9.00 | 2 | paid |
| 1001 | ana@mail.com | PEN | 32.50 | 1 | paid |
| 1002 | BO@mail.com␣ | LAMP | 45.00 | 1 | SHIPPED |
| 1003 | cy@mail.com | MUG | 9.00 | 4 | shipped |
| 1003 | cy@mail.com | NOTE | 4.35 | 3 | shipped |
| 1003 | cy@mail.com | NOTE | 4.35 | 3 | shipped |
| 1004 | ␣ana@mail.com | HEAD | 89.99 | 1 | Paid |
| 1005 | dee@mail.com | PEN | 32.50 | 2 | refunded |
| 1006 | bo@mail.com | HEAD | 79.99 | 1 | paid |
| 1006 | bo@mail.com | CABLE | 6.50 | 3 | paid |
| 1007 | cy@mail.com | LAMP | 45.00 | 1 | shipped |
| 1008 | eli@mail.com | MUG | 9.00 | 1 | paid |

The `␣` marks a space. On screen, spaces [[do not show|invisible-spaces]], so they are drawn here. There are five problems in these 12 rows. The next five steps take them one at a time.

### Problem 1: emails with capitals and spaces

`'BO@mail.com '` has capitals and a space at the end. `' ana@mail.com'` has a space at the start. To the database, `'BO@mail.com '` and `'bo@mail.com'` are two different emails, so Bo would become two customers.

The fix is the one from the lesson "Working with text": **normalize**, meaning make every value follow the same form. `trim` removes the spaces at both ends, and `lower` turns capitals into small letters:

```sql
SELECT email, lower(trim(email)) AS clean FROM import_rows WHERE order_ref IN (1002, 1004);
```

| email | clean |
| --- | --- |
| BO@mail.com␣ | bo@mail.com |
| ␣ana@mail.com | ana@mail.com |

### Problem 2: statuses in mixed case

The status column holds `'SHIPPED'` and `'Paid'` next to `'shipped'` and `'paid'`. Your `orders` table has `CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))`, so it would (rightly) refuse `'SHIPPED'`. The same fix works: `lower(trim(status))` gives `'shipped'` and `'paid'`.

### Problem 3: prices are text, in dollars

The `price` column is `TEXT`: `'79.99'` is a string of characters, not a number. Your tables want whole cents, as numbers.

The first tool is **`CAST`**, which converts a value to another type. You met it in "Problem solving: cohort retention", turning a month's text into a number. You write the value, the word `AS`, and the type you want:

```sql
SELECT CAST('79.99' AS REAL), CAST('9.00' AS INTEGER);
```

| CAST('79.99' AS REAL) | CAST('9.00' AS INTEGER) |
| --- | --- |
| 79.99 | 9 |

`REAL` is the type for numbers with decimals, and `INTEGER` for whole numbers. So `CAST(price AS REAL)` turns the text into a number of dollars. Times 100, that is cents. Or so you would think.

### Problem 4: the floating-point trap

Look at what multiplying by 100 really gives:

```sql
SELECT sku, price, CAST(price AS REAL) * 100 AS times_100
FROM import_rows WHERE sku IN ('HEAD', 'NOTE');
```

| sku | price | times_100 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| NOTE | 4.35 | 434.99999999999994 |
| NOTE | 4.35 | 434.99999999999994 |
| HEAD | 89.99 | 8999.0 |
| HEAD | 79.99 | 7998.999999999999 |

The floating-point numbers from Decision 1 last lesson are back. 4.35 and 79.99 have no exact binary form, so times 100 they land a hair *below* the whole number.

Now `CAST(… AS INTEGER)` makes it worse. Turning a decimal into an integer does not round. It [[truncates|truncate-picture]]: it chops off everything after the decimal point. So 7998.999999999999 becomes 7998, and the headphones lose a cent. The notebook becomes 434.

The fix is to **round first**. `ROUND(x)` with no second argument rounds to the nearest whole number: `ROUND(7998.999999999999)` gives `7999.0`. That is still a `REAL`, so cast it to an integer at the end. Read this from the inside out:

```sql
CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER)
```

1. `CAST(price AS REAL)`: text to a number of dollars, 79.99.
2. `* 100`: dollars to cents, 7998.999999999999.
3. `ROUND(...)`: to the nearest whole number, 7999.0.
4. `CAST(... AS INTEGER)`: to a whole-number type, 7999.

Every price in the export comes out right: 900, 3250, 4500, 435, 8999, 7999 and 650.

### Problem 5: a line that appears twice

Order 1003 has two notebook lines, both `qty` 3 at 4.35. A customer who bought 6 notebooks would have one line with `qty` 6. So this is the export [[counting one line twice|export-duplicates]].

`SELECT DISTINCT`, from the lesson "DISTINCT, UNION and UNION ALL", keeps one copy of each repeated row. The two notebook lines match in every column, so `DISTINCT` drops one.

One more thing to notice, which is not a problem: the headphones sold at 89.99 in order 1004 and 79.99 in order 1006, a sale. Both are real. Each line keeps the price it was charged, as you decided last lesson.

**Watch out:** `CAST(… AS INTEGER)` on money without `ROUND` gives no error and looks fine on most rows. 9.00 and 32.50 come out right, so a quick glance at a few rows says "works". Only 4.35 and 79.99 are a cent short. Always round before you cast.

::: context look-first Most of the work is looking
People who move data between systems say that most of the job is understanding and cleaning it, not writing the final `INSERT`. A quick way to find problems in a column is to group by it: `SELECT status, COUNT(*) FROM import_rows GROUP BY status;` gives five rows, `'Paid'`, `'SHIPPED'`, `'paid'`, `'refunded'` and `'shipped'`, where there should be three. The same habit helps with telemetry: before trusting a new sensor feed, engineers look at its raw values and hunt for gaps, repeats and impossible readings.
:::

::: context invisible-spaces Seeing what you cannot see
A space at the end of a value is invisible in a result table. Two tricks make it show. `length(email)` counts the characters: `'BO@mail.com '` has 12, one more than the 11 you can see. Or glue brackets around the value with `||`, from the lesson "Building text: joining, replacing and LIKE": `'[' || email || ']'` shows `[BO@mail.com ]`, with the space inside the brackets.
:::

::: context truncate-picture Chopping versus rounding
On the number line, 7998.999999999999 sits a tiny step to the left of 7999. `CAST(… AS INTEGER)` chops off the decimals, which moves it back to 7998. `ROUND` moves it to the nearest whole number, which is 7999. For negative numbers the chop goes towards zero: `CAST(-2.7 AS INTEGER)` gives -2.

```svg

  <defs>
    <marker id="tr" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#b4232c"/>
    </marker>
    <marker id="rd" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <line x1="20" y1="70" x2="340" y2="70" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="50" y1="62" x2="50" y2="78" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="310" y1="62" x2="310" y2="78" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="50" y="96" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">7998</text>
  <text x="310" y="96" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">7999</text>
  <circle cx="298" cy="70" r="5" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="250" y="116" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">7998.999999999999</text>
  <path d="M292 62 Q175 10 56 60" fill="none" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#tr)"/>
  <text x="165" y="26" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">CAST chops: 7998</text>
  <path d="M300 63 Q306 30 310 62" fill="none" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#rd)"/>
  <text x="312" y="24" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">ROUND: 7999</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context export-duplicates Where duplicate lines come from
Exports repeat rows more often than you would think. A script that fails halfway and is run again may write the first half twice. A join inside the export can fan out, the way you saw in "Debugging: the join that double-counts". Whatever the cause, a doubled line would count 3 notebooks as 6 and add 13.05 dollars of revenue that never happened. That is why a load checks for repeats, and why `order_items` has a primary key that would refuse the second copy anyway.
:::
--- task
Write one `SELECT` that returns the export's lines, cleaned and ready to load, with these six columns in this order:

1. `order_ref`, as it is.
2. `email`: the email trimmed and in lower case.
3. `sku`, as it is.
4. `qty`, as it is.
5. `cents`: the price in whole cents, rounded before it is cast to an integer.
6. `status`: the status trimmed and in lower case.

Use `SELECT DISTINCT` so the doubled notebook line appears once, and sort by `order_ref`, then `sku`. You should get 11 rows.
--- starter
SELECT order_ref, email, sku, qty, CAST(price AS REAL) * 100 AS cents, status
FROM import_rows
ORDER BY order_ref, sku;
--- solution
SELECT DISTINCT order_ref,
       lower(trim(email)) AS email,
       sku,
       qty,
       CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER) AS cents,
       lower(trim(status)) AS status
FROM import_rows
ORDER BY order_ref, sku;
--- hint
Each problem has its own fix from the lesson: `lower(trim(...))` for the email and the status, `CAST` and `ROUND` for the price, and `DISTINCT` for the doubled line.
--- hint
Run the starter first and look at the `cents` column: the notebook shows 434.99999999999994. Wrap it: `CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER) AS cents`.
--- hint
Start with `SELECT DISTINCT order_ref, lower(trim(email)) AS email, sku, qty,`, then the cents expression, then `lower(trim(status)) AS status`. Keep `FROM import_rows ORDER BY order_ref, sku;`.
--- check result | Eleven clean lines: normal emails and statuses, exact cents, no duplicate
ordered

+++ practice | Refunds in whole cents
--- schema
CREATE TABLE refunds_raw (
  ref INTEGER NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  amount TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO refunds_raw (ref, email, amount) VALUES
  (501, ' Kim@Mail.com', '0.29'),
  (502, 'raj@mail.com',  '19.99'),
  (503, 'LEE@MAIL.COM ', '1.15'),
  (504, 'kim@mail.com',  '100.00'),
  (505, 'uma@mail.com',  '8.10');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `refunds_raw`, exported from a payments tool: `ref` (a number), `email` (text, typed by hand) and `amount` (text, in dollars, like `'19.99'`).

Write one `SELECT` that returns three columns, sorted by `ref`:

1. `ref`, as it is.
2. `email`: trimmed and in lower case.
3. `cents`: the amount as a whole number of cents, rounded before it is cast to an integer. `'19.99'` must give 1999.
--- starter
SELECT ref, email, CAST(CAST(amount AS REAL) * 100 AS INTEGER) AS cents
FROM refunds_raw
ORDER BY ref;
--- solution
SELECT ref,
       lower(trim(email)) AS email,
       CAST(ROUND(CAST(amount AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER) AS cents
FROM refunds_raw
ORDER BY ref;
--- hint
Run the starter and compare `cents` with the amounts: three of the five are one cent short. The cast chops, it does not round.
--- hint
Wrap the multiplication in `ROUND(...)` and cast that: `CAST(ROUND(CAST(amount AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER)`. The email needs `lower(trim(email))`.
--- check result | Exact cents and clean emails, in ref order
ordered
[[501, "kim@mail.com", 29], [502, "raj@mail.com", 1999], [503, "lee@mail.com", 115], [504, "kim@mail.com", 10000], [505, "uma@mail.com", 810]]
--- check source | Rounds before casting
[Rr][Oo][Uu][Nn][Dd]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type the cents

+++ practice | Which prices lose a cent?
--- schema
CREATE TABLE price_list (
  sku TEXT NOT NULL,
  price TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO price_list (sku, price) VALUES
  ('BOLT',   '0.07'),
  ('NUT',    '0.57'),
  ('WASHER', '1.10'),
  ('GASKET', '2.01'),
  ('HOSE',   '9.95'),
  ('PUMP',   '12.00'),
  ('VALVE',  '16.08'),
  ('CLIP',   '1.13');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `price_list`, with the columns `sku` and `price` (text, in dollars).

Before fixing a loader, a careful engineer measures the damage. Return the prices that the careless conversion, `CAST(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100 AS INTEGER)`, gets wrong. Four columns:

1. `sku`.
2. `price`, as it is.
3. `naive`: the careless conversion's cents.
4. `correct`: the cents rounded before the cast.

Keep only the rows where `naive` and `correct` differ, and sort by `sku`.
--- starter
SELECT sku, price, CAST(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100 AS INTEGER) AS naive
FROM price_list
ORDER BY sku;
--- solution
WITH converted AS (
  SELECT sku, price,
         CAST(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100 AS INTEGER) AS naive,
         CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER) AS correct
  FROM price_list
)
SELECT sku, price, naive, correct
FROM converted
WHERE naive <> correct
ORDER BY sku;
--- hint
Work out both conversions side by side first, then keep the rows where they disagree. A CTE lets you name the two columns once and filter on them.
--- hint
In the CTE, `naive` is the starter's expression and `correct` adds `ROUND(...)` around the multiplication. The outer query keeps `WHERE naive <> correct`.
--- check result | Five prices lose a cent; the rest convert cleanly
ordered
[["CLIP", "1.13", 112, 113], ["GASKET", "2.01", 200, 201], ["HOSE", "9.95", 994, 995], ["NUT", "0.57", 56, 57], ["VALVE", "16.08", 1607, 1608]]
--- check source | Compares the two conversions
<>|!=
--- check source absent | Does not pick the rows by name
'(CLIP|GASKET|HOSE|NUT|VALVE)'

+++ practice | Statuses, spellings and money
--- task
Before loading, the store wants a summary of the export by status. Treat two rows of `import_rows` that match in every column as one line, then return one row per cleaned status, `lower(trim(status))`, with four columns:

1. `status`: the cleaned status.
2. `lines`: how many lines have it.
3. `spellings`: how many different raw spellings of it appear, such as `'paid'` and `'Paid'`.
4. `cents`: the money in those lines, `qty` times the price in whole cents (rounded before the cast).

Sort by `status`. The three `cents` together must come to 45303.
--- starter
SELECT lower(trim(status)) AS status, COUNT(*) AS lines
FROM import_rows
GROUP BY lower(trim(status))
ORDER BY status;
--- solution
WITH lines AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT * FROM import_rows
)
SELECT lower(trim(status)) AS status,
       COUNT(*) AS lines,
       COUNT(DISTINCT status) AS spellings,
       SUM(qty * CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER)) AS cents
FROM lines
GROUP BY lower(trim(status))
ORDER BY status;
--- hint
First remove the doubled line: `SELECT DISTINCT * FROM import_rows` in a CTE keeps one copy of each repeated row. Then group that CTE by the cleaned status.
--- hint
The raw spellings are the different values of the uncleaned `status` inside each group: `COUNT(DISTINCT status)`. The money is `SUM(qty * …cents expression…)`.
--- check result | paid, refunded and shipped, with their spellings and exact money
ordered
[["paid", 6, 2, 24898], ["refunded", 1, 1, 6500], ["shipped", 4, 2, 13905]]
--- check source | Removes the doubled line with DISTINCT
[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]\s+\*
--- check source | Rounds before casting
[Rr][Oo][Uu][Nn][Dd]\s*\(

+++ practice | Blank amounts and refunds
--- schema
CREATE TABLE payments_raw (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  amount TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO payments_raw (id, amount) VALUES
  (1, '19.99'),
  (2, '-4.35'),
  (3, ' 7 '),
  (4, '0.29'),
  (5, ''),
  (6, '   '),
  (7, '1000');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `payments_raw`, with the columns `id` and `amount` (text, in dollars). The export is messy at the edges:

- a refund is a negative amount, like `'-4.35'`, and must stay negative;
- some amounts have spaces around them, or no decimals at all;
- a blank amount (empty, or only spaces) means the amount is unknown.

Return `id` and `cents`, sorted by `id`. `cents` is the amount in whole cents, rounded before the cast, and NULL when the amount is blank. A blank must never turn into 0.
--- starter
SELECT id, CAST(ROUND(CAST(amount AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER) AS cents
FROM payments_raw
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id,
       CAST(ROUND(CAST(NULLIF(trim(amount), '') AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER) AS cents
FROM payments_raw
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
Run the starter: rows 5 and 6 come out as 0. `CAST('' AS REAL)` is 0.0, so a blank looks like a free payment. Turn the blank into NULL before the cast.
--- hint
`NULLIF(a, b)`, from "Debugging: the rows NULL made disappear", gives NULL when `a` equals `b`. Trim first, so a run of spaces is also `''`: `NULLIF(trim(amount), '')`. NULL then passes through `CAST` and `ROUND` as NULL.
--- check result | Refunds stay negative, spaces are ignored, blanks are NULL
ordered
[[1, 1999], [2, -435], [3, 700], [4, 29], [5, null], [6, null], [7, 100000]]
--- check source | Rounds before casting
[Rr][Oo][Uu][Nn][Dd]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not pick rows by id
\bid\s*(=|[Ii][Nn])\s*\(?\s*\d

+++ practice | Seven customers who are really five
--- task
This query is meant to list the store's customers from `import_rows`, one row per customer, with the number of different orders each placed:

```sql
SELECT lower(email) AS email, COUNT(DISTINCT order_ref) AS orders
FROM import_rows
GROUP BY lower(email)
ORDER BY email;
```

It returns seven rows, but the shop has five customers. Fix it so each customer appears once, with the email cleaned the way the lesson cleaned it. Keep the two columns, `email` and `orders`, and the sort by `email`.
--- starter

FROM import_rows
GROUP BY lower(email)
ORDER BY email;
--- solution
SELECT lower(trim(email)) AS email, COUNT(DISTINCT order_ref) AS orders
FROM import_rows
GROUP BY lower(trim(email))
ORDER BY email;
--- hint
Look at the seven emails with brackets around them: `'[' || lower(email) || ']'`. Two of them carry a space.

Clean the email in both places, the `SELECT` and the `GROUP BY`: `lower(trim(email))`.
