+++ practice | Temperatures from two kinds of thermometer
--- schema
CREATE TABLE readings_raw (
  station TEXT NOT NULL,
  taken TEXT NOT NULL,
  temp TEXT NOT NULL,
  unit TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO readings_raw (station, taken, temp, unit) VALUES
  ('north ', '2024-03-01 06:00', '21.35', 'C'),
  ('North',  '2024-03-01 06:00', '21.35', 'C'),
  ('NORTH',  '2024-03-01 07:00', '70.2',  'f'),
  ('south',  '2024-03-01 06:00', '-3.05', ' C'),
  ('south',  '2024-03-01 07:00', '',      'C'),
  ('South ', '2024-03-01 08:00', '26.6',  'F'),
  ('east',   '2024-03-01 06:00', '4.35',  'c');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `readings_raw`, a weather export with the columns `station`, `taken` (a time), `temp` (text) and `unit` (`'C'` for Celsius or `'F'` for Fahrenheit, typed in either case, sometimes with spaces).

Return the clean readings, one row per station and time, with three columns:

1. `station`: trimmed and in capitals, with `upper(...)`.
2. `taken`, as it is.
3. `centi`: the temperature in **hundredths of a degree Celsius**, as a whole number, rounded before the cast. 21.35 °C is 2135. A Fahrenheit reading is converted first: Celsius is (Fahrenheit − 32) × 5 ÷ 9.

Leave out readings whose `temp` is blank. Two rows that are the same after cleaning count once. Sort by `station`, then `taken`.
--- starter
SELECT station, taken, CAST(CAST(temp AS REAL) * 100 AS INTEGER) AS centi
FROM readings_raw
ORDER BY station, taken;
--- solution
SELECT DISTINCT upper(trim(station)) AS station,
       taken,
       CAST(ROUND(
         CASE WHEN upper(trim(unit)) = 'F' THEN (CAST(temp AS REAL) - 32) * 5 / 9
              ELSE CAST(temp AS REAL)
         END * 100) AS INTEGER) AS centi
FROM readings_raw
WHERE trim(temp) <> ''
ORDER BY station, taken;
--- hint
Four fixes: clean the station, drop blank temperatures, convert Fahrenheit, and round before the cast. `DISTINCT` then removes the repeated north reading, because after cleaning its two rows match.
--- hint
The unit needs cleaning too before you test it: `upper(trim(unit)) = 'F'`. A `CASE` picks the formula: `(CAST(temp AS REAL) - 32) * 5 / 9` for Fahrenheit, `CAST(temp AS REAL)` otherwise.
--- hint
Multiply the `CASE` by 100, then `CAST(ROUND(…) AS INTEGER)`. Keep blank temperatures out with `WHERE trim(temp) <> ''`.
--- check result | Five clean readings in hundredths of a degree Celsius
ordered
[["EAST", "2024-03-01 06:00", 435], ["NORTH", "2024-03-01 06:00", 2135], ["NORTH", "2024-03-01 07:00", 2122], ["SOUTH", "2024-03-01 06:00", -305], ["SOUTH", "2024-03-01 08:00", -300]]
--- check source | Rounds before casting
[Rr][Oo][Uu][Nn][Dd]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type the answers
\b(2135|2122|435|305|300)\b

=== sqlp-02 | Store 2: loading the export
--- schema
CREATE TABLE customers (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  country TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE products (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  category TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))
);
CREATE TABLE order_items (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES products(id),
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  unit_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (unit_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)
);
CREATE TABLE import_rows (
  order_ref INTEGER NOT NULL,
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  customer_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  country TEXT NOT NULL,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL,
  product_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  category TEXT NOT NULL,
  price TEXT NOT NULL,
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO import_rows VALUES
  (1001, '2024-01-03 10:00:00', 'ana@mail.com',  'Ana Ruiz',  'ES', 'MUG',   'Mug',          'home',        '9.00',  2, 'paid'),
  (1001, '2024-01-03 10:00:00', 'ana@mail.com',  'Ana Ruiz',  'ES', 'PEN',   'Fountain pen', 'stationery',  '32.50', 1, 'paid'),
  (1002, '2024-01-15 12:30:00', 'BO@mail.com ',  'Bo Chen',   'UK', 'LAMP',  'Desk lamp',    'home',        '45.00', 1, 'SHIPPED'),
  (1003, '2024-02-01 09:00:00', 'cy@mail.com',   'Cy Diaz',   'US', 'MUG',   'Mug',          'home',        '9.00',  4, 'shipped'),
  (1003, '2024-02-01 09:00:00', 'cy@mail.com',   'Cy Diaz',   'US', 'NOTE',  'Notebook',     'stationery',  '4.35',  3, 'shipped'),
  (1003, '2024-02-01 09:00:00', 'cy@mail.com',   'Cy Diaz',   'US', 'NOTE',  'Notebook',     'stationery',  '4.35',  3, 'shipped'),
  (1004, '2024-02-14 18:00:00', ' ana@mail.com', 'Ana Ruiz',  'ES', 'HEAD',  'Headphones',   'electronics', '89.99', 1, 'Paid'),
  (1005, '2024-02-20 11:11:00', 'dee@mail.com',  'Dee Evans', 'UK', 'PEN',   'Fountain pen', 'stationery',  '32.50', 2, 'refunded'),
  (1006, '2024-03-05 16:45:00', 'bo@mail.com',   'Bo Chen',   'UK', 'HEAD',  'Headphones',   'electronics', '79.99', 1, 'paid'),
  (1006, '2024-03-05 16:45:00', 'bo@mail.com',   'Bo Chen',   'UK', 'CABLE', 'USB cable',    'electronics', '6.50',  3, 'paid'),
  (1007, '2024-03-18 08:20:00', 'cy@mail.com',   'Cy Diaz',   'US', 'LAMP',  'Desk lamp',    'home',        '45.00', 1, 'shipped'),
  (1008, '2024-03-29 21:00:00', 'eli@mail.com',  'Eli Fox',   'US', 'MUG',   'Mug',          'home',        '9.00',  1, 'paid');
--- teach
Last lesson you found five problems in the export and wrote an expression to fix each one. Now you use those fixes to [[load the export|etl]] into the four tables you designed, so every fact lands once, in the right place.

### The tables

The four tables from "Store 1: the schema" are here, empty: `customers`, `products`, `orders` and `order_items`, with all their rules. Next to them is `import_rows`, the same 12-row export as last lesson.

### Recap: the fixes

From last lesson:

- Emails: `lower(trim(email))`. Statuses: `lower(trim(status))`.
- Prices in cents: `CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER)`. Without `ROUND`, 79.99 becomes 7998.
- The doubled notebook line in order 1003: `SELECT DISTINCT` drops it. The primary key on `order_items` would refuse it anyway.
- The headphones sold at two prices, 89.99 and 79.99 on sale. Each line keeps what it was charged.

### Parents before children

Each table is filled with `INSERT … SELECT`, from the lesson "Design: normalizing a flat table": the rows a `SELECT` returns are inserted. One table at a time, and **parents before children**. A **parent** is a table that others point at; a **child** points at it. An order points at a customer, so the customer must exist first, or the foreign key refuses the order. That gives [[this order|load-order]]: `customers` and `products`, then `orders`, then `order_items`.

### Step 1: one customer per email

Group by the cleaned email, and each customer becomes one group:

```sql
SELECT lower(trim(email)), MIN(customer_name), MIN(country)
FROM import_rows
GROUP BY lower(trim(email))
ORDER BY MIN(order_ref);
```

| lower(trim(email)) | MIN(customer_name) | MIN(country) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ana@mail.com | Ana Ruiz | ES |
| bo@mail.com | Bo Chen | UK |
| cy@mail.com | Cy Diaz | US |
| dee@mail.com | Dee Evans | UK |
| eli@mail.com | Eli Fox | US |

Why `MIN(customer_name)`? The name is neither grouped nor inside an aggregate. As you saw in "GROUP BY: expressions, NULLs and extra columns", that is a bare column: SQLite would pick it from some row, and most other databases refuse the query. Every row of one customer carries the same name, so `MIN` of it is that name. It is [[an honest way to pick it|min-pick]], and because it is an aggregate, the query works in any database. `MIN(country)` is the same trick.

`ORDER BY MIN(order_ref)` hands out the ids in the order customers first bought: Ana gets `id` 1, Bo 2, and so on. Put `INSERT INTO customers (email, name, country)` on the line above, and five customers go in.

### Step 2: one product per SKU

The same shape, grouped by `sku`. The product's `price_cents` is its list price. Here the rule is: the **highest price it has sold at**, so `MAX` of the cleaned cents.

```sql
SELECT sku, MIN(product_name), MIN(category),
       MAX(CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER))
FROM import_rows
GROUP BY sku
ORDER BY MIN(order_ref), sku;
```

That gives 6 products. The headphones get 8999, the higher of 8999 and 7999. That rule is [[a choice the shop makes|list-price]], and the task states it.

### Step 3: orders, linked to their customer

`orders` needs a `customer_id`. The export does not have one. It has an email. So join back to the customers you inserted in Step 1, on the cleaned email, and take their `id`:

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT r.order_ref, c.id, r.placed, lower(trim(r.status))
FROM import_rows r
JOIN customers c ON c.email = lower(trim(r.email));
```

| order_ref | id | placed | lower(trim(r.status)) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1001 | 1 | 2024-01-03 10:00:00 | paid |
| 1002 | 2 | 2024-01-15 12:30:00 | shipped |
| 1003 | 3 | 2024-02-01 09:00:00 | shipped |
| 1004 | 1 | 2024-02-14 18:00:00 | paid |
| 1005 | 4 | 2024-02-20 11:11:00 | refunded |
| 1006 | 2 | 2024-03-05 16:45:00 | paid |
| 1007 | 3 | 2024-03-18 08:20:00 | shipped |
| 1008 | 5 | 2024-03-29 21:00:00 | paid |

- The order's own `id` can be the export's `order_ref`. It is already a unique number per order.
- `DISTINCT` is needed because an order with two lines, like 1001, appears on two rows. Without it the second copy of 1001 clashes with the primary key, and the whole insert fails.
- Orders 1002 and 1004, with the messy emails, found Bo and Ana, because the join cleans the email on the export's side too.

`INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status)` goes on top.

### Step 4: the order lines

`order_items` finds its `product_id` the same way, through the SKU: `JOIN products p ON p.sku = r.sku`. Each line takes `r.order_ref` as its `order_id`, its `qty`, and its own price in cents as `unit_cents`. `SELECT DISTINCT` loads the doubled notebook line once. That makes 11 lines. The headphones line in order 1006 keeps 7999, the sale price, even though the product's list price is 8999.

### Step 5: reconcile

**Reconciling** means checking that two totals that should agree really do. Here: the money in the lines you loaded must equal the money in the export, minus the duplicate.

- The whole export, duplicate included, is 46608 cents: `SELECT SUM(qty * CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER)) FROM import_rows;`
- The doubled line is 3 notebooks at 435 cents: 1305 cents.
- So the loaded lines must come to 46608 − 1305 = 45303 cents.

```sql
SELECT SUM(qty * unit_cents) FROM order_items;
```

| SUM(qty * unit_cents) |
| --- |
| 45303 |

It matches. Numbers that do not reconcile are [[a bug in the load|reconcile]], not a rounding curiosity.

**Watch out:** join on the *cleaned* email. With `JOIN customers c ON c.email = r.email`, the rows `'BO@mail.com '` and `' ana@mail.com'` match no customer. An inner join drops them without a word, and orders 1002 and 1004 are never loaded: 6 orders instead of 8. This is exactly the kind of mistake the reconcile step catches.

::: context etl Extract, transform, load
Moving data from one system into another has a name: **ETL**, for extract, transform, load. *Extract* is getting the data out, here the spreadsheet export. *Transform* is cleaning and reshaping it, last lesson's fixes. *Load* is writing it into the new tables, this lesson. Data teams run ETL jobs every night, and space missions run the same shape of job on telemetry: raw packets come down from the spacecraft, get decoded and checked, and land in tables that engineers query.
:::

::: context load-order Which table first
Each arrow is a foreign key, pointing from the child to its parent. A table can only be filled once every table it points at is full. `customers` and `products` point at nothing, so they go first, in either order. `orders` needs `customers`. `order_items` needs both `orders` and `products`, so it goes last.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="lo" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#1f2a44"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <rect x="15" y="20" width="110" height="30" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="70" y="40" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1. customers</text>
  <rect x="235" y="20" width="110" height="30" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="290" y="40" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1. products</text>
  <rect x="15" y="110" width="110" height="30" rx="4" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="70" y="130" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2. orders</text>
  <rect x="235" y="110" width="110" height="30" rx="4" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="290" y="130" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">3. order_items</text>
  <line x1="70" y1="108" x2="70" y2="53" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#lo)"/>
  <line x1="290" y1="108" x2="290" y2="53" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#lo)"/>
  <line x1="233" y1="125" x2="128" y2="125" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#lo)"/>
  <text x="180" y="160" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">fill in number order: parents first</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context min-pick Why MIN, and not the bare column
`MIN` on text picks the value that comes first in alphabetical order. When every row in the group holds the same value, the first is that value, so nothing is lost. If the rows ever disagreed, say Ana changed her name between orders, `MIN` would still pick one of them without an error. That is why a careful load checks first: `SELECT lower(trim(email)), COUNT(DISTINCT customer_name) FROM import_rows GROUP BY lower(trim(email));` should show 1 on every row. Here it does.
:::

::: context list-price A rule written down
Nothing in the export says what a product's list price is. The owners chose "the highest price it has sold at", so that a past sale price does not become the price on the website. Another shop might choose the most recent price. Neither is wrong. What matters is that the choice is made on purpose and written down, in the task and in the load, so the next person who reads `price_cents` knows where it came from.
:::

::: context reconcile Totals that must agree
Accountants reconcile every month: the money the books say came in must match the money the bank says came in. A difference of one cent means something is wrong, and they hunt it down. Data loads use the same test: count the rows and add up the money on both sides. Spacecraft ground systems do it too: they count the telemetry packets sent and the packets stored, and a gap means data was lost on the way.
:::
--- task
Load `import_rows` into the four tables with four `INSERT … SELECT` statements, parents first:

1. `customers`: one row per cleaned email, `lower(trim(email))`, with `MIN(customer_name)` as the name and `MIN(country)` as the country. Leave `id` out, so SQLite numbers them.
2. `products`: one row per `sku`, with `MIN(product_name)`, `MIN(category)`, and `price_cents` the highest price it sold at, in whole cents.
3. `orders`: one row per `order_ref`, using it as the order `id`, with the `customer_id` found by joining `customers` on the cleaned email, `placed`, and the status in lower case and trimmed.
4. `order_items`: one row per order and product, with `order_id` from `order_ref`, the `product_id` found by joining `products` on `sku`, `qty`, and `unit_cents` the price on that line in whole cents. The doubled line goes in once.

Then check your load: `SELECT SUM(qty * unit_cents) FROM order_items;` should give 45303.
--- starter
INSERT INTO customers (email, name, country)
SELECT email, customer_name, country FROM import_rows;
--- solution
INSERT INTO customers (email, name, country)
SELECT lower(trim(email)), MIN(customer_name), MIN(country)
FROM import_rows
GROUP BY lower(trim(email))
ORDER BY MIN(order_ref);

INSERT INTO products (sku, name, category, price_cents)
SELECT sku, MIN(product_name), MIN(category), MAX(CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER))
FROM import_rows
GROUP BY sku
ORDER BY MIN(order_ref), sku;

INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status)
SELECT DISTINCT r.order_ref, c.id, r.placed, lower(trim(r.status))
FROM import_rows r
JOIN customers c ON c.email = lower(trim(r.email));

INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, unit_cents)
SELECT DISTINCT r.order_ref, p.id, r.qty, CAST(ROUND(CAST(r.price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER)
FROM import_rows r
JOIN products p ON p.sku = r.sku;
--- hint
The lesson's Steps 1 to 4 are your four statements, in that order. Each one is `INSERT INTO table (columns)` on top of a `SELECT` from `import_rows`.
--- hint
Customers: `INSERT INTO customers (email, name, country) SELECT lower(trim(email)), MIN(customer_name), MIN(country) FROM import_rows GROUP BY lower(trim(email));`. Products the same way, grouped by `sku`, with `MAX(CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER))` for the price.
--- hint
Orders: `SELECT DISTINCT r.order_ref, c.id, r.placed, lower(trim(r.status)) FROM import_rows r JOIN customers c ON c.email = lower(trim(r.email))`. Lines: `SELECT DISTINCT r.order_ref, p.id, r.qty,` the cents expression on `r.price`, `FROM import_rows r JOIN products p ON p.sku = r.sku`.
--- check query | 5 customers, 6 products, 8 orders, 11 lines
SELECT (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM customers), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM products), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM order_items)
=> [[5, 6, 8, 11]]
--- check query | Emails are normalised, one customer each
SELECT email, name, country FROM customers ORDER BY email
=> [["ana@mail.com", "Ana Ruiz", "ES"], ["bo@mail.com", "Bo Chen", "UK"], ["cy@mail.com", "Cy Diaz", "US"], ["dee@mail.com", "Dee Evans", "UK"], ["eli@mail.com", "Eli Fox", "US"]]
--- check query | Prices are exact cents, list price the highest sold
SELECT sku, price_cents FROM products ORDER BY sku
=> [["CABLE", 650], ["HEAD", 8999], ["LAMP", 4500], ["MUG", 900], ["NOTE", 435], ["PEN", 3250]]
--- check query | Orders belong to the right customers, with clean statuses
SELECT o.id, c.email, o.status FROM orders o JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id ORDER BY o.id
=> [[1001, "ana@mail.com", "paid"], [1002, "bo@mail.com", "shipped"], [1003, "cy@mail.com", "shipped"], [1004, "ana@mail.com", "paid"], [1005, "dee@mail.com", "refunded"], [1006, "bo@mail.com", "paid"], [1007, "cy@mail.com", "shipped"], [1008, "eli@mail.com", "paid"]]
--- check query | Each line keeps the price it was charged; the total reconciles
SELECT (SELECT unit_cents FROM order_items oi JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id WHERE oi.order_id = 1006 AND p.sku = 'HEAD'), SUM(qty * unit_cents) FROM order_items
=> [[7999, 45303]]
--- check query | Products keep their names and categories
SELECT sku, name, category FROM products ORDER BY sku
=> [["CABLE", "USB cable", "electronics"], ["HEAD", "Headphones", "electronics"], ["LAMP", "Desk lamp", "home"], ["MUG", "Mug", "home"], ["NOTE", "Notebook", "stationery"], ["PEN", "Fountain pen", "stationery"]]

+++ practice | One author per name
--- schema
CREATE TABLE authors (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  country TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE book_rows (
  row_no INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL,
  country TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO book_rows (row_no, title, author, country) VALUES
  (1, 'Dune',             'Frank Herbert',     'US'),
  (2, 'Emma',             ' Jane Austen',      'UK'),
  (3, 'Children of Dune', 'Frank Herbert ',    'US'),
  (4, 'Kindred',          'Octavia E. Butler', 'US'),
  (5, 'Persuasion',       'Jane Austen',       'UK'),
  (6, 'Solaris',          'Stanislaw Lem',     'PL'),
  (7, 'Dawn',             'Octavia E. Butler', 'US');
--- task
This problem has its own tables. `book_rows` is a flat list of books (`row_no`, `title`, `author`, `country`), with each author typed again on every book, sometimes with stray spaces. `authors` (`id`, `name`, `country`) is empty, and `name` is unique.

Fill `authors` with one `INSERT … SELECT`: one row per author, with `name` the author trimmed of spaces (keep the capitals as typed) and `country` from `MIN(country)`. Leave `id` out so SQLite numbers the authors, and insert them in the order they first appear in `book_rows` (by `row_no`).
--- starter
INSERT INTO authors (name, country)
SELECT author, country FROM book_rows;
--- solution
INSERT INTO authors (name, country)
SELECT trim(author), MIN(country)
FROM book_rows
GROUP BY trim(author)
ORDER BY MIN(row_no);
--- hint
One row per author means grouping, and the author must be cleaned before it is grouped: `GROUP BY trim(author)`.
--- hint
The country is not grouped, so give it an aggregate: `MIN(country)`. `ORDER BY MIN(row_no)` hands out the ids in order of first appearance.
--- check query | Four authors, numbered in order of first appearance
SELECT id, name, country FROM authors ORDER BY id
=> [[1, "Frank Herbert", "US"], [2, "Jane Austen", "UK"], [3, "Octavia E. Butler", "US"], [4, "Stanislaw Lem", "PL"]]
--- check query | No stored name has spaces around it
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM authors WHERE name <> trim(name)
=> [[0]]
--- check query | Every book finds its author
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM book_rows r JOIN authors a ON a.name = trim(r.author)
=> [[7]]

+++ practice | Books that find their author
--- schema
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;
CREATE TABLE authors (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  author_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES authors(id),
  title TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  year INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE book_rows (
  row_no INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL,
  year TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO authors (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Frank Herbert'), (2, 'Jane Austen'), (3, 'Octavia E. Butler');
INSERT INTO book_rows (row_no, title, author, year) VALUES
  (1, 'Dune',       'frank herbert',      '1965'),
  (2, 'Emma',       'JANE AUSTEN ',       '1815'),
  (3, 'Kindred',    ' Octavia E. Butler', '1979'),
  (4, 'Dune',       'Frank Herbert',      '1965'),
  (5, 'Persuasion', 'Jane Austen',        '1817'),
  (6, 'Dawn',       'octavia e. butler',  '1987');
--- task
This problem has its own tables. `authors` (`id`, `name`) is already loaded. `books` (`id`, `author_id`, `title`, `year`) is empty; `title` is unique and `year` is an integer. `book_rows` (`row_no`, `title`, `author`, `year`) is the export, where the author is typed in any capitals with stray spaces, `year` is text, and one book is listed twice.

Fill `books` with one `INSERT … SELECT`: one row per title, its `author_id` found by matching the export's author to `authors.name` ignoring capitals and spaces, and `year` stored as an integer. Leave `id` out. Every book in the export must be loaded.
--- starter
INSERT INTO books (author_id, title, year)
SELECT DISTINCT a.id, r.title, r.year
FROM book_rows r
JOIN authors a ON a.name = r.author;
--- solution
INSERT INTO books (author_id, title, year)
SELECT DISTINCT a.id, r.title, CAST(r.year AS INTEGER)
FROM book_rows r
JOIN authors a ON lower(a.name) = lower(trim(r.author));
--- hint
Run the starter's `SELECT` on its own: it finds only two books. An inner join quietly drops every row whose author does not match exactly.
--- hint
Clean both sides of the join so they meet in the same form: `lower(a.name) = lower(trim(r.author))`. Then `CAST(r.year AS INTEGER)` for the year.
--- check query | Five books, each with the right author
SELECT b.title, a.name, b.year FROM books b JOIN authors a ON a.id = b.author_id ORDER BY b.title
=> [["Dawn", "Octavia E. Butler", 1987], ["Dune", "Frank Herbert", 1965], ["Emma", "Jane Austen", 1815], ["Kindred", "Octavia E. Butler", 1979], ["Persuasion", "Jane Austen", 1817]]
--- check query | Each author has all of their books
SELECT a.name, COUNT(b.id) FROM authors a LEFT JOIN books b ON b.author_id = a.id GROUP BY a.id ORDER BY a.id
=> [["Frank Herbert", 1], ["Jane Austen", 2], ["Octavia E. Butler", 2]]
--- check query | Every title in the export was loaded once
SELECT (SELECT COUNT(DISTINCT title) FROM book_rows), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM books)
=> [[5, 5]]

+++ practice | Statuses under other names
--- schema
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;
CREATE TABLE customers (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))
);
CREATE TABLE order_rows (
  order_ref INTEGER NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO customers (id, email) VALUES (1, 'ana@mail.com'), (2, 'bo@mail.com'), (3, 'cy@mail.com');
INSERT INTO order_rows (order_ref, email, placed, status, sku) VALUES
  (2001, 'ana@mail.com',  '2024-04-01 10:00:00', 'PAID',       'MUG'),
  (2001, 'ana@mail.com',  '2024-04-01 10:00:00', 'PAID',       'PEN'),
  (2002, 'Bo@mail.com',   '2024-04-02 11:00:00', 'dispatched', 'LAMP'),
  (2003, 'cy@mail.com',   '2024-04-03 12:00:00', 'Shipped ',   'MUG'),
  (2004, 'ana@mail.com ', '2024-04-04 13:00:00', 'refund',     'HEAD'),
  (2005, 'bo@mail.com',   '2024-04-05 14:00:00', 'REFUNDED',   'CABLE'),
  (2006, 'cy@mail.com',   '2024-04-06 15:00:00', 'on hold',    'NOTE'),
  (2007, 'cy@mail.com',   '2024-04-07 16:00:00', 'Paid',       'LAMP');
--- task
This problem has its own tables. `customers` (`id`, `email`) is loaded. `orders` (`id`, `customer_id`, `placed`, `status`) is empty, and its `status` must be one of `'paid'`, `'shipped'` or `'refunded'`. `order_rows` is a new export, one row per order line, from a warehouse that uses its own words for statuses.

Load `orders` with one `INSERT … SELECT`: one row per `order_ref` (used as the order's `id`), with the `customer_id` found through the cleaned email, `placed`, and the status translated after trimming and lowering it:

- `'paid'` stays `'paid'`;
- `'shipped'` and `'dispatched'` become `'shipped'`;
- `'refunded'` and `'refund'` become `'refunded'`.

An order whose status is none of these is left out of the load.
--- starter
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status)
SELECT DISTINCT r.order_ref, c.id, r.placed, lower(trim(r.status))
FROM order_rows r
JOIN customers c ON c.email = lower(trim(r.email));
--- solution
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status)
SELECT DISTINCT m.order_ref, c.id, m.placed, m.clean_status
FROM (
  SELECT order_ref, email, placed,
         CASE lower(trim(status))
           WHEN 'paid' THEN 'paid'
           WHEN 'shipped' THEN 'shipped'
           WHEN 'dispatched' THEN 'shipped'
           WHEN 'refunded' THEN 'refunded'
           WHEN 'refund' THEN 'refunded'
         END AS clean_status
  FROM order_rows
) m
JOIN customers c ON c.email = lower(trim(m.email))
WHERE m.clean_status IS NOT NULL;
--- hint
A `CASE`, from "CASE: decisions inside a query", translates each cleaned status. A `CASE` with no `ELSE` gives NULL for anything it does not list, which marks the rows to leave out.
--- hint
Do the translation in a subquery in the `FROM` and give it a name, such as `clean_status`. The outer query can then join the customers and keep `WHERE m.clean_status IS NOT NULL`.
--- hint
`CASE lower(trim(status)) WHEN 'paid' THEN 'paid' WHEN 'dispatched' THEN 'shipped' … END AS clean_status`, one `WHEN` per word in the list. The outer `SELECT DISTINCT` keeps one row per order.
--- check query | Six orders, each with the translated status
SELECT o.id, c.email, o.status FROM orders o JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id ORDER BY o.id
=> [[2001, "ana@mail.com", "paid"], [2002, "bo@mail.com", "shipped"], [2003, "cy@mail.com", "shipped"], [2004, "ana@mail.com", "refunded"], [2005, "bo@mail.com", "refunded"], [2007, "cy@mail.com", "paid"]]
--- check query | The order on hold was left out
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders WHERE id = 2006
=> [[0]]
--- check query | Two orders of each status
SELECT status, COUNT(*) FROM orders GROUP BY status ORDER BY status
=> [["paid", 2], ["refunded", 2], ["shipped", 2]]

+++ practice | Lowest prices and missing prices
--- schema
CREATE TABLE products (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
);
CREATE TABLE catalog_rows (
  row_no INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  price TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO catalog_rows (row_no, sku, name, price) VALUES
  (1, 'mug',  'Mug',          '9.00'),
  (2, 'MUG ', 'Mug',          '9.50'),
  (3, 'pen',  'Fountain pen', '32.50'),
  (4, 'PEN',  'Fountain pen', ''),
  (5, 'clip', 'Paper clip',   '0.29'),
  (6, 'lamp', 'Desk lamp',    ''),
  (7, 'LAMP', 'Desk lamp',    '  ');
--- task
This problem has its own tables. `catalog_rows` (`row_no`, `sku`, `name`, `price`) lists every price each product has been sold at; SKUs are typed in any capitals with stray spaces, and a blank `price` (empty or only spaces) means the price was not recorded. `products` (`id`, `sku`, `name`, `price_cents`) is empty.

Load `products` with one `INSERT … SELECT`, one row per SKU:

1. `sku`: trimmed and in capitals, with `upper(...)`.
2. `name`: `MIN(name)`.
3. `price_cents`: the **lowest** recorded price in whole cents (rounded before the cast). Blank prices are ignored, not read as 0. A product with no recorded price at all gets 0.

Leave `id` out.
--- starter
INSERT INTO products (sku, name, price_cents)
SELECT upper(trim(sku)), MIN(name), MIN(CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER))
FROM catalog_rows
GROUP BY upper(trim(sku));
--- solution
INSERT INTO products (sku, name, price_cents)
SELECT upper(trim(sku)),
       MIN(name),
       COALESCE(MIN(CAST(ROUND(CAST(NULLIF(trim(price), '') AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER)), 0)
FROM catalog_rows
GROUP BY upper(trim(sku));
--- hint
Run the starter and look at the pen: its lowest price comes out as 0, because `CAST('' AS REAL)` is 0.0. `MIN` skips NULLs, so turn each blank into NULL first.
--- hint
`NULLIF(trim(price), '')` makes a blank NULL. A product whose prices are all blank then has `MIN` of nothing, which is NULL: `COALESCE(…, 0)` turns it into the 0 the task asks for.
--- check query | Four products with their lowest recorded price
SELECT sku, name, price_cents FROM products ORDER BY sku
=> [["CLIP", "Paper clip", 29], ["LAMP", "Desk lamp", 0], ["MUG", "Mug", 900], ["PEN", "Fountain pen", 3250]]
--- check query | A blank price does not pull the pen down to 0
SELECT price_cents FROM products WHERE sku = 'PEN'
=> [[3250]]
--- check query | One row per SKU, however it was typed
SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(DISTINCT sku) FROM products
=> [[4, 4]]

+++ practice | Two customers with one name
--- schema
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;
CREATE TABLE customers (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  placed TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE order_rows (
  order_ref INTEGER NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  customer_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  placed TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO customers (id, email, name) VALUES (1, 'sam@a.io', 'Sam Lee'), (2, 'sam.lee@b.io', 'Sam Lee'), (3, 'kay@c.io', 'Kay Obi');
INSERT INTO order_rows (order_ref, email, customer_name, placed) VALUES
  (3001, 'sam@a.io',     'Sam Lee', '2024-06-01'),
  (3002, 'kay@c.io',     'Kay Obi', '2024-06-02'),
  (3003, 'Sam.Lee@b.io', 'Sam Lee', '2024-06-03'),
  (3004, 'kay@c.io ',    'Kay Obi', '2024-06-04');
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `customers` (`id`, `email`, `name`), already loaded, an empty `orders` (`id`, `customer_id`, `placed`), and the export `order_rows` (`order_ref`, `email`, `customer_name`, `placed`).

The starter loads the orders, but it stops with `UNIQUE constraint failed: orders.id` and loads nothing. Find why, and fix the load so each order goes to the customer whose email it carries (emails in the export may have capitals and stray spaces). Keep `order_ref` as the order's `id`.
--- starter
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed)
SELECT DISTINCT r.order_ref, c.id, r.placed
FROM order_rows r
JOIN customers c ON c.name = r.customer_name;
--- solution
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed)
SELECT DISTINCT r.order_ref, c.id, r.placed
FROM order_rows r
JOIN customers c ON c.email = lower(trim(r.email));
--- hint
Run the starter's `SELECT` alone and look at order 3001. How many customers are called Sam Lee?
--- hint
A name is not a key: two people can share one. Join on the natural key, the email, cleaned on the export's side: `c.email = lower(trim(r.email))`.
--- check query | Four orders, each with the customer who placed it
SELECT o.id, c.email FROM orders o JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id ORDER BY o.id
=> [[3001, "sam@a.io"], [3002, "kay@c.io"], [3003, "sam.lee@b.io"], [3004, "kay@c.io"]]
--- check query | Each Sam Lee has exactly one order
SELECT customer_id, COUNT(*) FROM orders WHERE customer_id IN (1, 2) GROUP BY customer_id ORDER BY customer_id
=> [[1, 1], [2, 1]]
--- check query | Nothing was lost
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders
=> [[4]]

+++ practice | A mission log, loaded and reconciled
--- schema
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;
CREATE TABLE crew (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE missions (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  code TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE assignments (
  mission_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES missions(id),
  crew_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES crew(id),
  role TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (role IN ('commander', 'pilot', 'specialist')),
  hours_tenths INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (hours_tenths >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (mission_id, crew_id)
);
CREATE TABLE log_rows (
  row_no INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  mission TEXT NOT NULL,
  crew_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  crew_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  role TEXT NOT NULL,
  hours TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO log_rows (row_no, mission, crew_email, crew_name, role, hours) VALUES
  (1, 'ARTEMIS-2',  'ada@lunar.org',  'Ada Okafor', 'Commander',   '230.5'),
  (2, 'artemis-2',  'ben@lunar.org',  'Ben Salo',   'pilot',       '230.5'),
  (3, 'ARTEMIS-2 ', 'cleo@lunar.org', 'Cleo Park',  'specialist',  '230.5'),
  (4, 'ARTEMIS-2',  'Cleo@Lunar.org', 'Cleo Park',  'Specialist ', '230.5'),
  (5, 'GATEWAY-1',  'cleo@lunar.org', 'Cleo Park',  'commander',   '96.25'),
  (6, 'gateway-1',  'dev@lunar.org ', 'Dev Rao',    'pilot',       '96.25'),
  (7, 'GATEWAY-1',  'ben@lunar.org',  'Ben Salo',   'Specialist',  '0.75');
--- task
This problem has its own tables. `log_rows` is a mission log kept in a spreadsheet: one row per crew member per mission, with `mission` (a code), `crew_email`, `crew_name`, `role` and `hours` (text). Codes, emails and roles are typed in any capitals with stray spaces, and one row is entered twice. Three empty tables wait for it:

- `crew` (`id`, `email`, `name`), with `email` unique;
- `missions` (`id`, `code`), with `code` unique;
- `assignments` (`mission_id`, `crew_id`, `role`, `hours_tenths`), with `role` one of `'commander'`, `'pilot'`, `'specialist'` and the primary key `(mission_id, crew_id)`.

Load all three, parents first, with one `INSERT … SELECT` each:

1. `crew`: one row per `lower(trim(crew_email))`, with `MIN(crew_name)`, numbered in order of first appearance.
2. `missions`: one row per `upper(trim(mission))`, numbered in order of first appearance.
3. `assignments`: the ids found through the cleaned code and email, the role trimmed and in lower case, and `hours_tenths` the hours in tenths of an hour, rounded before the cast (230.5 hours is 2305).

The loaded `hours_tenths` must add up to 8849.
--- starter
INSERT INTO crew (email, name)
SELECT lower(trim(crew_email)), MIN(crew_name)
FROM log_rows
GROUP BY lower(trim(crew_email))
ORDER BY MIN(row_no);
--- solution
INSERT INTO crew (email, name)
SELECT lower(trim(crew_email)), MIN(crew_name)
FROM log_rows
GROUP BY lower(trim(crew_email))
ORDER BY MIN(row_no);

INSERT INTO missions (code)
SELECT upper(trim(mission))
FROM log_rows
GROUP BY upper(trim(mission))
ORDER BY MIN(row_no);

INSERT INTO assignments (mission_id, crew_id, role, hours_tenths)
SELECT DISTINCT m.id, c.id, lower(trim(r.role)), CAST(ROUND(CAST(r.hours AS REAL) * 10) AS INTEGER)
FROM log_rows r
JOIN missions m ON m.code = upper(trim(r.mission))
JOIN crew c ON c.email = lower(trim(r.crew_email));
--- hint
The starter already loads `crew`. `missions` has the same shape with a different key. `assignments` goes last, because it points at both.
--- hint
`assignments` joins `log_rows` to both parents on cleaned values: `m.code = upper(trim(r.mission))` and `c.email = lower(trim(r.crew_email))`. `SELECT DISTINCT` loads the doubled row once, since after cleaning it matches its twin.
--- hint
Tenths of an hour are the hours times 10: `CAST(ROUND(CAST(r.hours AS REAL) * 10) AS INTEGER)`. Without `ROUND`, 96.25 hours becomes 962 tenths instead of 963.
--- check query | 4 crew, 2 missions, 6 assignments
SELECT (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM crew), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM missions), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM assignments)
=> [[4, 2, 6]]
--- check query | Crew and missions are numbered in order of first appearance
SELECT 'crew', id, email FROM crew UNION ALL SELECT 'mission', id, code FROM missions ORDER BY 1, 2
=> [["crew", 1, "ada@lunar.org"], ["crew", 2, "ben@lunar.org"], ["crew", 3, "cleo@lunar.org"], ["crew", 4, "dev@lunar.org"], ["mission", 1, "ARTEMIS-2"], ["mission", 2, "GATEWAY-1"]]
--- check query | Each assignment has its clean role and rounded hours
SELECT m.code, c.email, a.role, a.hours_tenths FROM assignments a JOIN missions m ON m.id = a.mission_id JOIN crew c ON c.id = a.crew_id ORDER BY m.code, c.email
=> [["ARTEMIS-2", "ada@lunar.org", "commander", 2305], ["ARTEMIS-2", "ben@lunar.org", "pilot", 2305], ["ARTEMIS-2", "cleo@lunar.org", "specialist", 2305], ["GATEWAY-1", "ben@lunar.org", "specialist", 8], ["GATEWAY-1", "cleo@lunar.org", "commander", 963], ["GATEWAY-1", "dev@lunar.org", "pilot", 963]]
--- check query | The hours reconcile
SELECT SUM(hours_tenths) FROM assignments
=> [[8849]]

=== sqlp-03 | Store 3: the revenue report
--- schema
CREATE TABLE customers (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  country TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE products (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  category TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))
);
CREATE TABLE order_items (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES products(id),
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  unit_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (unit_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)
);
INSERT INTO customers (id, email, name, country) VALUES
  (1, 'ana@mail.com', 'Ana Ruiz', 'ES'),
  (2, 'bo@mail.com', 'Bo Chen', 'UK'),
  (3, 'cy@mail.com', 'Cy Diaz', 'US'),
  (4, 'dee@mail.com', 'Dee Evans', 'UK'),
  (5, 'eli@mail.com', 'Eli Fox', 'US');
INSERT INTO products (id, sku, name, category, price_cents) VALUES
  (1, 'MUG', 'Mug', 'home', 900),
  (2, 'PEN', 'Fountain pen', 'stationery', 3250),
  (3, 'LAMP', 'Desk lamp', 'home', 4500),
  (4, 'NOTE', 'Notebook', 'stationery', 435),
  (5, 'HEAD', 'Headphones', 'electronics', 8999),
  (6, 'CABLE', 'USB cable', 'electronics', 650);
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status) VALUES
  (1001, 1, '2024-01-03 10:00:00', 'paid'),
  (1002, 2, '2024-01-15 12:30:00', 'shipped'),
  (1003, 3, '2024-02-01 09:00:00', 'shipped'),
  (1004, 1, '2024-02-14 18:00:00', 'paid'),
  (1005, 4, '2024-02-20 11:11:00', 'refunded'),
  (1006, 2, '2024-03-05 16:45:00', 'paid'),
  (1007, 3, '2024-03-18 08:20:00', 'shipped'),
  (1008, 5, '2024-03-29 21:00:00', 'paid');
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, unit_cents) VALUES
  (1001, 1, 2, 900),
  (1001, 2, 1, 3250),
  (1002, 3, 1, 4500),
  (1003, 1, 4, 900),
  (1003, 4, 3, 435),
  (1004, 5, 1, 8999),
  (1005, 2, 2, 3250),
  (1006, 5, 1, 7999),
  (1006, 6, 3, 650),
  (1007, 3, 1, 4500),
  (1008, 1, 1, 900);
--- teach
Last lesson you loaded the export and proved the load with a reconcile. The store's data is now clean and in its tables. Now the owners ask their first Monday question: **where does the money come from, month by month?**

### The tables

The four tables are here, filled with exactly the data from last lesson: 5 customers, 6 products, 8 orders and 11 order lines. In short:

- `orders`: `id`, `customer_id`, `placed` (like `'2024-02-14 18:00:00'`) and `status` (`'paid'`, `'shipped'` or `'refunded'`). Order 1005 is the only refunded one.
- `order_items`: `order_id`, `product_id`, `qty` and `unit_cents`.
- `products`: `id`, `sku`, `name`, `category` (`'home'`, `'stationery'` or `'electronics'`) and `price_cents`.

The money is on the order lines. The month is on the order. The category is on the product. So the report needs all three tables.

### A report is a pipeline

Think of a car factory. The body is welded at one station, painted at the next, and the seats go in at a third. Each station does one job and hands its work on. You can stop the line after any station and inspect what it made.

A report like this one is built the same way: a **pipeline** of stages, each taking the last one's rows. In SQL, each stage is a named step in a `WITH`, a CTE, as in the lesson "CTEs: a query in named steps". This report has three stages: line revenue, totals, and shares.

### Stage 1: revenue per line

Join each line to its order and its product. Drop refunded orders. Work out what each line brought in, `qty * unit_cents`, and tag it with its month and category:

```sql
WITH lines AS (
  SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month, p.category, oi.qty * oi.unit_cents AS cents
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
  JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
)
SELECT * FROM lines;
```

`strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed)` turns the order's time into its month, like `'2024-02'`, as in the lesson "Dates and times". `<>` means "not equal". Here are the first five of the 10 rows it returns (11 lines, minus the refunded one):

| month | category | cents |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2024-01 | home | 1800 |
| 2024-01 | stationery | 3250 |
| 2024-01 | home | 4500 |
| 2024-02 | home | 3600 |
| 2024-02 | stationery | 1305 |

The first row is order 1001's two mugs: 2 × 900 = 1800 cents.

### Stage 2: totals per month and category

The second stage groups the first one's rows. Put a comma after Stage 1's closing bracket, add the new step, and change the last line to `SELECT * FROM totals;`:

```sql
totals AS (
  SELECT month, category, SUM(cents) AS cents
  FROM lines
  GROUP BY month, category
)
```

| month | category | cents |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2024-01 | home | 6300 |
| 2024-01 | stationery | 3250 |
| 2024-02 | electronics | 8999 |
| 2024-02 | home | 3600 |
| 2024-02 | stationery | 1305 |
| 2024-03 | electronics | 9949 |
| 2024-03 | home | 5400 |

January's home line is 1800 + 4500 = 6300 cents: the mugs, and Bo's desk lamp.

### Stage 3: each category's share of its month

"Share" means this category's percentage of the whole month. For that, each row needs its month's total next to it, without squashing the rows together. That is a window function, as in the lesson "Running totals with SUM() OVER":

```sql
SUM(cents) OVER (PARTITION BY month) AS month_cents
```

`PARTITION BY month` splits the rows into one group per month, and `SUM(cents) OVER (...)` puts that group's total [[on every row of the group|window-picture]]:

| month | category | cents | month_cents |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2024-01 | home | 6300 | 9550 |
| 2024-01 | stationery | 3250 | 9550 |
| 2024-02 | electronics | 8999 | 13904 |
| 2024-02 | home | 3600 | 13904 |
| 2024-02 | stationery | 1305 | 13904 |
| 2024-03 | electronics | 9949 | 15349 |
| 2024-03 | home | 5400 | 15349 |

The share is then `100.0 * cents / month_cents`. For January's home row that is 100.0 × 6300 ÷ 9550 = 65.968…, which `ROUND(…, 1)` makes 66.0. Put `100.0` at the front, as in "CTEs: a query in named steps": with only whole numbers, SQLite does integer division and `6300 / 9550` is 0.

### Keep the cents until the very end

Every stage so far has worked in whole cents. Convert to dollars once, in the last `SELECT`:

```sql
ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS revenue
```

`9949 / 100.0` is 99.49. Again `100.0`, not `100`: `9949 / 100` is 99, and the cents are lost.

Why only at the end? Converting each line to dollars first and then adding brings back the [[floating-point drift|drift]] you designed the schema to avoid.

### Refunds: decide out loud

A refunded order is money the shop gave back. This report leaves refunds out, which is the simplest honest choice for a revenue report. A finance report would show a refund as a negative line instead.

Either way, decide it on purpose, and say so in the column name or the report's title. Including refunds silently is how [[two dashboards end up disagreeing|one-definition]].

**Watch out:** `ROUND(cents / 100, 2)` gives no error, but `cents / 100` is integer division: 9949 becomes 99, and every revenue loses its cents. The same goes for the share. Put a real number, `100.0`, in every division.

::: context window-picture The month's total on every row
`GROUP BY month` would squash February's three rows into one. `SUM(cents) OVER (PARTITION BY month)` keeps all three and writes February's total, 13904, beside each. Each row can then divide its own cents by that total.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 150" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="30" width="200" height="90" fill="#fff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <text x="120" y="22" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">partition: 2024-02</text>
  <text x="30" y="55" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">electronics</text>
  <text x="190" y="55" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="end">8999</text>
  <text x="30" y="82" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">home</text>
  <text x="190" y="82" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="end">3600</text>
  <text x="30" y="109" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">stationery</text>
  <text x="190" y="109" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="end">1305</text>
  <rect x="250" y="40" width="80" height="22" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="290" y="55" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">13904</text>
  <rect x="250" y="67" width="80" height="22" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="290" y="82" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">13904</text>
  <rect x="250" y="94" width="80" height="22" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="290" y="109" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">13904</text>
  <text x="290" y="30" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">month_cents</text>
  <text x="180" y="142" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">8999 + 3600 + 1305 = 13904, on all three rows</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context drift Why the order of adding matters
With floating-point numbers, even the order you add in can change the answer. In SQLite, `SELECT 0.1 + 0.2 + 0.3;` gives 0.6000000000000001, but `SELECT 0.3 + 0.2 + 0.1;` gives 0.6. A report that turns every line into dollars and then adds thousands of them can end up a fraction of a cent off, and a different fraction depending on the order the rows arrive in. Whole cents add up exactly, in any order. So the report divides by 100.0 once, on the final total.
:::

::: context one-definition One name, one meaning
Suppose the sales team's dashboard counts refunded orders as revenue and the finance team's does not. Both say "February revenue", and they differ by 65 dollars. Now two meetings are spent working out why. Data teams avoid this by writing one agreed definition for each number, such as **gross revenue** (before refunds) and **net revenue** (after them), and naming columns to match. Engineering telemetry has the same rule: a column called `altitude` must say whether it is measured above sea level or above the ground.
:::
--- task
Write one query that returns revenue by month and category, leaving out refunded orders. Build it as a pipeline of CTEs, as the lesson did. Return four columns, in this order:

1. `month`: the order's month, as `'YYYY-MM'`.
2. `category`: the product's category.
3. `revenue`: the total of `qty * unit_cents` for that month and category, turned into dollars and rounded to 2 decimals.
4. `share`: that category's percentage of the month's revenue, rounded to 1 decimal.

Sort by `month`, then by `revenue` from highest to lowest. You should get 7 rows.
--- starter
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS revenue
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY month;
--- solution
WITH lines AS (
  SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month, p.category, oi.qty * oi.unit_cents AS cents
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
  JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
), totals AS (
  SELECT month, category, SUM(cents) AS cents
  FROM lines
  GROUP BY month, category
)
SELECT month, category,
       ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS revenue,
       ROUND(100.0 * cents / SUM(cents) OVER (PARTITION BY month), 1) AS share
FROM totals
ORDER BY month, revenue DESC;
--- hint
Put Stage 1 (`lines`) and Stage 2 (`totals`) from the lesson into one `WITH`, separated by a comma. The final `SELECT` reads from `totals`.
--- hint
In the final `SELECT`: `month, category, ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS revenue`, and a share built from `SUM(cents) OVER (PARTITION BY month)`.
--- hint
`share` is `ROUND(100.0 * cents / SUM(cents) OVER (PARTITION BY month), 1)`. End with `FROM totals ORDER BY month, revenue DESC;`.
--- check result | Seven month-and-category rows, refund excluded
ordered
[["2024-01", "home", 63.0, 66.0], ["2024-01", "stationery", 32.5, 34.0], ["2024-02", "electronics", 89.99, 64.7], ["2024-02", "home", 36.0, 25.9], ["2024-02", "stationery", 13.05, 9.4], ["2024-03", "electronics", 99.49, 64.8], ["2024-03", "home", 54.0, 35.2]]

+++ practice | Revenue by country
--- task
The owners want to know which countries the money comes from. Write one query over the store's tables that leaves out refunded orders and returns one row per customer country, with three columns:

1. `country`: the customer's country.
2. `revenue`: the total of `qty * unit_cents` for that country, in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals.
3. `share`: that country's percentage of all the revenue, rounded to 1 decimal.

Keep the money in whole cents until the last step. Sort by `revenue`, highest first.
--- starter
SELECT c.country, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS revenue
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.country;
--- solution
WITH lines AS (
  SELECT c.country, oi.qty * oi.unit_cents AS cents
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o    ON o.id = oi.order_id
  JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
), totals AS (
  SELECT country, SUM(cents) AS cents
  FROM lines
  GROUP BY country
)
SELECT country,
       ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS revenue,
       ROUND(100.0 * cents / SUM(cents) OVER (), 1) AS share
FROM totals
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
--- hint
The country lives on `customers`, so the lines reach it through `orders`. Build it in two stages, as in the lesson: first every line's cents with its country, then the totals per country.
--- hint
A share of **all** the revenue needs the grand total on every row. A window with nothing inside the brackets covers every row: `SUM(cents) OVER ()`.
--- check result | UK, Spain, then the US, with shares that add up to 100
ordered
[["UK", 144.49, 37.2], ["ES", 140.49, 36.2], ["US", 103.05, 26.6]]
--- check source | Uses a window for the grand total
[Oo][Vv][Ee][Rr]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Never divides by a whole 100
/\s*100(?![.\d])

+++ practice | A running total by month
--- task
Write one query that shows how the year's revenue builds up, leaving out refunded orders. One row per month, with three columns:

1. `month`: the order's month, as `'YYYY-MM'`.
2. `revenue`: that month's total of `qty * unit_cents`, in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals.
3. `running`: the total of every month up to and including this one, in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals.

Sort by `month`.
--- starter
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) / 100.0 AS revenue
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- solution
WITH totals AS (
  SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS cents
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
  GROUP BY month
)
SELECT month,
       ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS revenue,
       ROUND(SUM(cents) OVER (ORDER BY month) / 100.0, 2) AS running
FROM totals
ORDER BY month;
--- hint
The starter counts the refund in February. Filter it out, and keep each month's total in cents in a CTE.
--- hint
A running total is a window with an order: `SUM(cents) OVER (ORDER BY month)`, from "Running totals with SUM() OVER". Divide it by `100.0` only after the sum.
--- check result | Three months, each running total including the months before
ordered
[["2024-01", 95.5, 95.5], ["2024-02", 139.04, 234.54], ["2024-03", 153.49, 388.03]]
--- check source | Uses a window with an ORDER BY
[Oo][Vv][Ee][Rr]\s*\(\s*[Oo][Rr][Dd][Ee][Rr]\s+[Bb][Yy]
--- check source absent | Never divides by a whole 100
/\s*100(?![.\d])

+++ practice | Gross, refunds and net
--- task
The finance team wants both definitions of revenue side by side, so nobody argues about which one a chart shows. Write one query with one row per month and four columns:

1. `month`: the order's month, as `'YYYY-MM'`.
2. `gross`: the total of `qty * unit_cents` over **all** orders that month, refunded ones included.
3. `refunds`: the part of that total that belongs to refunded orders (0 when there are none).
4. `net`: `gross` minus `refunds`.

Show the three money columns in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals, and sort by `month`.
--- starter
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month,
       SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS gross
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- solution
WITH totals AS (
  SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month,
         SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS gross,
         SUM(CASE WHEN o.status = 'refunded' THEN oi.qty * oi.unit_cents ELSE 0 END) AS refunds
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id
  GROUP BY month
)
SELECT month,
       ROUND(gross / 100.0, 2) AS gross,
       ROUND(refunds / 100.0, 2) AS refunds,
       ROUND((gross - refunds) / 100.0, 2) AS net
FROM totals
ORDER BY month;
--- hint
This time the refunded order stays in: nothing is filtered in a `WHERE`. The refunds column adds up only some of each month's lines, which is the conditional aggregation of "Pivoting with conditional aggregation".
--- hint
`SUM(CASE WHEN o.status = 'refunded' THEN oi.qty * oi.unit_cents ELSE 0 END)` gives each month's refunds, and 0 for a month without any. Work out `net` in cents, then divide every column by `100.0`.
--- check result | February's refund shows in gross and refunds, not in net
ordered
[["2024-01", 95.5, 0.0, 95.5], ["2024-02", 204.04, 65.0, 139.04], ["2024-03", 153.49, 0.0, 153.49]]
--- check source | Adds up with a CASE inside the SUM
[Ss][Uu][Mm]\s*\(\s*[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]
--- check source absent | Never divides by a whole 100
/\s*100(?![.\d])

+++ practice | Months with no sales still count
--- task
Create a view named `monthly_revenue` for a chart that must show every month from `2024-01` to `2024-05`, including months with no sales. It has one row per month and three columns:

1. `month`: `'YYYY-MM'`.
2. `orders`: how many different non-refunded orders were placed that month.
3. `revenue`: those orders' total of `qty * unit_cents`, in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals.

A month with no non-refunded orders shows `0` and `0.0`. Remember that one order can have several lines.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW monthly_revenue AS
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month,
       COUNT(*) AS orders,
       ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) / 100.0, 2) AS revenue
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
GROUP BY month;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW monthly_revenue AS
WITH RECURSIVE months(m) AS (
  SELECT '2024-01-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(m, '+1 month') FROM months WHERE m < '2024-05-01'
), totals AS (
  SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month,
         COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders,
         SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS cents
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
  GROUP BY month
)
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', mo.m) AS month,
       COALESCE(t.orders, 0) AS orders,
       ROUND(COALESCE(t.cents, 0) / 100.0, 2) AS revenue
FROM months mo
LEFT JOIN totals t ON t.month = strftime('%Y-%m', mo.m);
--- hint
Two things go wrong in the starter. Months with no sales vanish, and `COUNT(*)` counts order lines, not orders. The first needs a calendar; the second needs a count of distinct order ids.
--- hint
Make the calendar a recursive CTE of first days of the month, `'2024-01-01'` stepping by `date(m, '+1 month')` while `m < '2024-05-01'`. Then `LEFT JOIN` the monthly totals onto it, matching on `strftime('%Y-%m', …)`.
--- hint
Inside the totals CTE, count `COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)`. In the final `SELECT`, wrap both numbers in `COALESCE(…, 0)` before rounding.
--- check query | Five months; April and May are empty
SELECT month, orders, revenue FROM monthly_revenue ORDER BY month
=> [["2024-01", 2, 95.5], ["2024-02", 2, 139.04], ["2024-03", 3, 153.49], ["2024-04", 0, 0.0], ["2024-05", 0, 0.0]]
--- check query | A refunded order in May still leaves May empty
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status) VALUES (1009, 5, '2024-05-10 09:00:00', 'refunded');
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, unit_cents) VALUES (1009, 1, 3, 900);
SELECT month, orders, revenue FROM monthly_revenue WHERE month = '2024-05'
=> [["2024-05", 0, 0.0]]
--- check query | A two-line order in April counts once
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status) VALUES (1010, 6, '2024-04-02 10:00:00', 'paid');
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, unit_cents) VALUES (1010, 3, 1, 4500), (1010, 6, 2, 650);
SELECT month, orders, revenue FROM monthly_revenue WHERE month = '2024-04'
=> [["2024-04", 1, 58.0]]

+++ practice | Shares that come out whole
--- task
This report is meant to show revenue by category, leaving out refunded orders, with each category's share of the total:

```sql
WITH totals AS (
  SELECT p.category, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS cents
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
  JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
  GROUP BY p.category
)
SELECT category, ROUND(cents / 100, 2) AS revenue, ROUND(100 * cents / SUM(cents) OVER (), 1) AS share
FROM totals
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
```

It runs without an error, but stationery shows 45 dollars instead of 45.55, and every share is a whole number. Fix the two divisions. Keep the columns `category`, `revenue` (dollars, 2 decimals) and `share` (percent, 1 decimal), and the sort.
--- starter
WITH totals AS (
  SELECT p.category, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS cents
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
  JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
  GROUP BY p.category
)
SELECT category, ROUND(cents / 100, 2) AS revenue, ROUND(100 * cents / SUM(cents) OVER (), 1) AS share
FROM totals
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
--- solution
WITH totals AS (
  SELECT p.category, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS cents
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
  JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
  GROUP BY p.category
)
SELECT category, ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS revenue, ROUND(100.0 * cents / SUM(cents) OVER (), 1) AS share
FROM totals
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
--- hint
Both divisions have whole numbers on both sides. What does SQLite do with the remainder when it divides one whole number by another?
--- hint
Make one side of each division a real number: `cents / 100.0`, and `100.0 * cents` at the front of the share.
--- check result | Electronics, home and stationery, with cents and one-decimal shares
ordered
[["electronics", 189.48, 48.8], ["home", 153.0, 39.4], ["stationery", 45.55, 11.7]]
--- check source absent | Never divides by a whole 100
/\s*100(?![.\d])
--- check source absent | Never multiplies by a whole 100
\b100\s*\*

+++ practice | The best seller of each month
--- task
For each month, the owners want the one product that brought in the most revenue, leaving out refunded orders. Return one row per month with four columns:

1. `month`: `'YYYY-MM'`.
2. `sku`: the best-selling product's SKU that month. If two products tie, take the one whose SKU comes first alphabetically.
3. `revenue`: that product's total of `qty * unit_cents` that month, in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals.
4. `share`: its percentage of the month's whole revenue (all products), rounded to 1 decimal.

Sort by `month`.
--- starter
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month, p.sku, MAX(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS revenue
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
GROUP BY month;
--- solution
WITH lines AS (
  SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month, p.sku, oi.qty * oi.unit_cents AS cents
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
  JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
), per_product AS (
  SELECT month, sku, SUM(cents) AS cents
  FROM lines
  GROUP BY month, sku
), ranked AS (
  SELECT month, sku, cents,
         SUM(cents) OVER (PARTITION BY month) AS month_cents,
         ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY month ORDER BY cents DESC, sku) AS rn
  FROM per_product
)
SELECT month, sku,
       ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS revenue,
       ROUND(100.0 * cents / month_cents, 1) AS share
FROM ranked
WHERE rn = 1
ORDER BY month;
--- hint
Three stages: every line's cents with its month and SKU; the total per month and SKU; then pick the top SKU of each month. The pick is "Top N per group" with N = 1.
--- hint
In the last stage, compute two windows over the same rows before you filter: the month's total, `SUM(cents) OVER (PARTITION BY month)`, and a rank, `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY month ORDER BY cents DESC, sku)`.
--- hint
Filter on `rn = 1` in an outer query, not in the same `SELECT` as the windows: the share must be measured against every product of the month, not only the winner.
--- check result | The desk lamp in January, the headphones in February and March
ordered
[["2024-01", "LAMP", 45.0, 47.1], ["2024-02", "HEAD", 89.99, 64.7], ["2024-03", "HEAD", 79.99, 52.1]]
--- check source | Ranks within each month
[Pp][Aa][Rr][Tt][Ii][Tt][Ii][Oo][Nn]\s+[Bb][Yy]
--- check source absent | Never divides by a whole 100
/\s*100(?![.\d])

=== sqlp-04 | Store 4: the customer report
--- schema
CREATE TABLE customers (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  country TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE products (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  category TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))
);
CREATE TABLE order_items (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES products(id),
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  unit_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (unit_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)
);
