--- teach
Last lesson's report answered "where does the money come from?". This lesson answers the owners' second Monday question: **who are our customers, and how much is each one worth?** It is the last lesson of the store project, and it brings back three traps from earlier courses at once.

### The tables

The tables are the same as last lesson, with one new row in `customers`: Fay (`id` 6, `fay@mail.com`), who signed up yesterday and has not ordered yet. So there are six customers:

- Ana, Bo and Cy have two orders each, and Eli has one.
- Dee's only order, 1005, was refunded.
- Fay has no orders at all.

### Every customer, so start from customers

A customer report has to include **every** customer, including the ones who have not bought. "Signed up but never bought" is exactly who [[marketing wants to reach|win-back]].

That decides the shape of the query. Start from `customers`, and `LEFT JOIN` everything else, as in the lesson "LEFT JOIN and NULL". A customer with no match still gets a row, with NULL in the joined columns.

### Trap 1: where the refund filter goes

Refunded orders must not count. But Dee, whose only order was refunded, must still appear. Put the filter in the `WHERE`, and watch what happens:

```sql
SELECT c.email, COUNT(o.id) AS orders
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
GROUP BY c.id;
```

| email | orders |
| --- | --- |
| ana@mail.com | 2 |
| bo@mail.com | 2 |
| cy@mail.com | 2 |
| eli@mail.com | 1 |

Dee and Fay are gone. Fay's row has `o.status` = NULL, and `NULL <> 'refunded'` is not true, so the `WHERE` throws her away. Dee's only order is refunded, so she goes too. The `LEFT JOIN` kept them; the `WHERE` undid it.

As in the lesson "LEFT JOIN: where the filter goes", the filter belongs in the `ON`:

```sql
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status <> 'refunded'
```

Now the refunded order finds no match, and Dee and Fay each appear with 0 orders.

### Trap 2: fan-out

The report also needs the money, and the money is on the order lines. Join them in, and each order becomes one row per *line*. That is **fan-out**, from the lesson "Debugging: the join that double-counts". Now `COUNT(o.id)` counts lines, not orders:

```sql
SELECT c.email, COUNT(o.id) AS lines_counted, COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status <> 'refunded'
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id;
```

| email | lines_counted | orders |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ana@mail.com | 3 | 2 |
| bo@mail.com | 3 | 2 |
| cy@mail.com | 3 | 2 |
| dee@mail.com | 0 | 0 |
| eli@mail.com | 1 | 1 |
| fay@mail.com | 0 | 0 |

Ana has 2 orders, but order 1001 has [[two lines|fanout-ana]], so it is counted twice. `COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)` counts each order once.

The other fix, and the one this report uses, is to **pre-aggregate**: shrink each order to one row, with its total, before joining. A CTE does it:

```sql
WITH order_totals AS (
  SELECT o.id, o.customer_id, o.placed, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS cents
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
  GROUP BY o.id
)
SELECT * FROM order_totals;
```

| id | customer_id | placed | cents |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1001 | 1 | 2024-01-03 10:00:00 | 5050 |
| 1002 | 2 | 2024-01-15 12:30:00 | 4500 |
| 1003 | 3 | 2024-02-01 09:00:00 | 4905 |
| 1004 | 1 | 2024-02-14 18:00:00 | 8999 |
| 1006 | 2 | 2024-03-05 16:45:00 | 9949 |
| 1007 | 3 | 2024-03-18 08:20:00 | 4500 |
| 1008 | 5 | 2024-03-29 21:00:00 | 900 |

One row per order that counts. Order 1005, the refund, is not there. Here the `WHERE` is safe: it runs *inside* the CTE, before any `LEFT JOIN`. It only removes an order. When `customers` is later `LEFT JOIN`ed to `order_totals`, Dee finds no match and still gets her row.

### Trap 3: NULL is not zero

Join `customers` to `order_totals`, and add up each customer's orders:

```sql
SELECT c.email, COUNT(t.id) AS orders, SUM(t.cents) AS cents
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN order_totals t ON t.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id;
```

| email | orders | cents |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ana@mail.com | 2 | 14049 |
| bo@mail.com | 2 | 14449 |
| cy@mail.com | 2 | 9405 |
| dee@mail.com | 0 | NULL |
| eli@mail.com | 1 | 900 |
| fay@mail.com | 0 | NULL |

`COUNT(t.id)` is 0 for Dee and Fay, because `COUNT` skips NULLs. But `SUM` of nothing is NULL, not 0, as you saw in "Testing for NULL". A report should [[say what it means|null-zero]]: `COALESCE(SUM(t.cents), 0)` gives 0 when the sum is NULL.

The last order's date works the same way. `MAX(t.placed)` is the latest order's time, and `date(...)`, from "Dates and times", keeps only the `'YYYY-MM-DD'` part: `date('2024-03-05 16:45:00')` is `'2024-03-05'`. For Dee and Fay it stays NULL, which is right: they have no last order.

### A segment: turning numbers into action

A **segment** is a group of customers who get the same treatment. Numbers alone do not tell the shop what to do. A label does. A `CASE`, from "CASE: decisions inside a query", sorts each customer by lifetime spend, in cents:

```sql
CASE WHEN cents >= 10000 THEN 'vip'
     WHEN cents > 0 THEN 'active'
     ELSE 'inactive' END AS segment
```

- `'vip'`: 10000 cents (100 dollars) or more.
- `'active'`: anything above 0.
- `'inactive'`: everyone else.

`CASE` takes the first `WHEN` that is true, so the order matters. The boundaries are written into the task, and it is worth [[testing the edges|edge-tests]]: 10000 cents gives `'vip'`, 9999 gives `'active'`, and 0 gives `'inactive'`. These labels are a small version of a well-known way to [[score customers|rfm]].

**Watch out:** write the `WHEN`s from the strictest down. With `WHEN cents > 0 THEN 'active'` first, Ana's 14049 cents is above 0, so she is labeled `'active'` and the `'vip'` line is never reached. No error, and no VIPs.

::: context win-back Customers who have not bought yet
Shops send special emails to people who signed up but never ordered, or who have not ordered for a while: a reminder, a discount, a "we miss you". Marketing teams call these **win-back** or re-engagement campaigns. They can only send them if the report lists those people. Drop the customers with no orders, and the very list marketing wanted is empty. That is why this report starts from `customers`, not from `orders`.
:::

::: context fanout-ana Ana's rows after the join
Ana has two orders that count. Order 1001 has two lines, a mug and a pen, and order 1004 has one, the headphones. Joined to their lines, the two orders become three rows. `COUNT(o.id)` counts the id on every row: 1001, 1001, 1004 makes 3. `COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)` counts each different id once: 2.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 140" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="70" y="18" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">orders</text>
  <text x="250" y="18" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">rows after the join</text>
  <rect x="20" y="30" width="100" height="26" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="70" y="47" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1001</text>
  <rect x="20" y="84" width="100" height="26" rx="3" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="70" y="101" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1004</text>
  <rect x="190" y="26" width="120" height="24" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="250" y="42" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1001, mug</text>
  <rect x="190" y="56" width="120" height="24" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="250" y="72" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1001, pen</text>
  <rect x="190" y="86" width="120" height="24" rx="3" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="250" y="102" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1004, headphones</text>
  <line x1="120" y1="43" x2="188" y2="38" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="120" y1="43" x2="188" y2="68" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="120" y1="97" x2="188" y2="98" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="132" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">2 orders, 3 rows: COUNT(o.id) says 3</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context null-zero Unknown versus nothing
NULL means "no value", and 0 is a real number. For spending, the truth is "this customer spent nothing", which is 0. Leave the NULL in, and the report shows an empty cell that people read as "missing data". Worse, the NULL spreads: an average of spend skips NULL rows, so it would be taken over buyers only, not over all customers. `COALESCE` turns the NULL into the 0 you mean, so the report says what it means.
:::

::: context edge-tests Testing the boundaries
Bugs gather at boundaries. Is "100 dollars or more" written `>=` or `>`? Only a customer with exactly 10000 cents can tell the two apart. So testers pick values right on each edge and one step either side: 9999, 10000 and 10001, then 0 and 1. The same habit runs through flight software: if an engine must shut down when a temperature *reaches* a limit, the test checks the reading exactly at the limit, not only far above it.
:::

::: context rfm Recency, frequency, money
This report holds three classic measures of a customer. **Recency**: how long since the last order (`last_order`). **Frequency**: how many orders (`orders`). **Monetary value**: how much they spent (`spent`). Marketers call this **RFM**, and it has been used since long before online shops, by mail-order catalogs. Real RFM gives each measure a score, often 1 to 5, and combines them. This lesson's segment uses spend alone, which is a fine place to start.
:::
--- task
Write one query that returns one row per customer, all six of them, with these five columns in this order:

1. `email`.
2. `orders`: how many non-refunded orders they have.
3. `spent`: the total of those orders in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals, and 0 if they have none.
4. `last_order`: the date of their latest non-refunded order, as `'YYYY-MM-DD'`, or NULL if they have none.
5. `segment`: `'vip'` for spend of 100 dollars (10000 cents) or more, `'active'` for any spend above 0, and `'inactive'` otherwise.

Sort by `spent`, highest first, then by `email`. Dee and Fay should appear, both `'inactive'`.
--- starter
SELECT c.email, COUNT(o.id) AS orders
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id;
--- solution
WITH order_totals AS (
  SELECT o.id, o.customer_id, o.placed, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS cents
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
  GROUP BY o.id
), per_customer AS (
  SELECT c.email,
         COUNT(t.id) AS orders,
         COALESCE(SUM(t.cents), 0) AS cents,
         date(MAX(t.placed)) AS last_order
  FROM customers c
  LEFT JOIN order_totals t ON t.customer_id = c.id
  GROUP BY c.id
)
SELECT email, orders,
       ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS spent,
       last_order,
       CASE WHEN cents >= 10000 THEN 'vip'
            WHEN cents > 0 THEN 'active'
            ELSE 'inactive' END AS segment
FROM per_customer
ORDER BY spent DESC, email;
--- hint
Start from the lesson's `order_totals` CTE: one row per non-refunded order, with its total in cents. That removes the fan-out.
--- hint
Add a second CTE that starts `FROM customers c LEFT JOIN order_totals t ON t.customer_id = c.id` and groups by `c.id`. Its columns: `c.email`, `COUNT(t.id) AS orders`, `COALESCE(SUM(t.cents), 0) AS cents` and `date(MAX(t.placed)) AS last_order`.
--- hint
The final `SELECT` reads that second CTE: `email, orders, ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS spent, last_order`, and the segment `CASE` on `cents`, with `>= 10000` for `'vip'` first, then `> 0` for `'active'`. End with `ORDER BY spent DESC, email`.
--- check result | Six customers; Dee (refund only) and Fay (no orders) are inactive
ordered
[["bo@mail.com", 2, 144.49, "2024-03-05", "vip"], ["ana@mail.com", 2, 140.49, "2024-02-14", "vip"], ["cy@mail.com", 2, 94.05, "2024-03-18", "active"], ["eli@mail.com", 1, 9.0, "2024-03-29", "active"], ["dee@mail.com", 0, 0.0, null, "inactive"], ["fay@mail.com", 0, 0.0, null, "inactive"]]

+++ practice | Every member of the gym
--- schema
CREATE TABLE members (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE workouts (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  day TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('done', 'skipped')),
  minutes INTEGER NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO members (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ada'), (2, 'Ben'), (3, 'Cal'), (4, 'Dot');
INSERT INTO workouts (id, member_id, day, status, minutes) VALUES
  (1, 1, '2024-05-01', 'done',    30),
  (2, 1, '2024-05-03', 'done',    45),
  (3, 1, '2024-05-06', 'done',    20),
  (4, 2, '2024-05-02', 'done',    60),
  (5, 2, '2024-05-04', 'skipped',  0),
  (6, 2, '2024-05-05', 'done',    15),
  (7, 3, '2024-05-02', 'skipped',  0),
  (8, 3, '2024-05-07', 'skipped',  0);
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `members` (`id`, `name`) and `workouts` (`id`, `member_id`, `day`, `status`, `minutes`), where `status` is `'done'` or `'skipped'`.

Write one query with one row for **every** member, including members who have never finished a workout, and four columns:

1. `name`.
2. `done`: how many workouts they finished (status `'done'`).
3. `minutes`: the total minutes of those workouts, and 0 if there are none.
4. `last_done`: the day of their latest finished workout, or NULL if there is none.

Sort by `minutes`, highest first, then by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT m.name, COUNT(w.id) AS done, SUM(w.minutes) AS minutes, MAX(w.day) AS last_done
FROM members m
LEFT JOIN workouts w ON w.member_id = m.id
WHERE w.status = 'done'
GROUP BY m.id
ORDER BY minutes DESC, m.name;
--- solution
SELECT m.name,
       COUNT(w.id) AS done,
       COALESCE(SUM(w.minutes), 0) AS minutes,
       MAX(w.day) AS last_done
FROM members m
LEFT JOIN workouts w ON w.member_id = m.id AND w.status = 'done'
GROUP BY m.id
ORDER BY minutes DESC, m.name;
--- hint
Run the starter: Cal and Dot are missing. The `WHERE` throws away the rows the `LEFT JOIN` kept for them.
--- hint
Move `w.status = 'done'` into the join's `ON`, joined with `AND`. Then wrap the sum in `COALESCE(…, 0)`, because a sum over no rows is NULL.
--- check result | Ada and Ben with their totals; Cal and Dot with 0 and no date
ordered
[["Ada", 3, 95, "2024-05-06"], ["Ben", 2, 75, "2024-05-05"], ["Cal", 0, 0, null], ["Dot", 0, 0, null]]
--- check source | Keeps every member with a LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source absent | Does not pick members by name
'(Ada|Ben|Cal|Dot)'

+++ practice | Customers who have gone quiet
--- task
Marketing wants to reach customers who have not bought anything since 1 March 2024. Return every customer who has **no** non-refunded order placed on or after `'2024-03-01'`, including customers who have never ordered, with two columns:

1. `email`.
2. `last_order`: the date (`'YYYY-MM-DD'`) of their latest non-refunded order, or NULL if they have none.

Sort by `email`.
--- starter
SELECT c.email, date(MAX(o.placed)) AS last_order
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id
ORDER BY c.email;
--- solution
SELECT c.email, date(MAX(o.placed)) AS last_order
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status <> 'refunded'
GROUP BY c.id
HAVING MAX(o.placed) < '2024-03-01' OR MAX(o.placed) IS NULL
ORDER BY c.email;
--- hint
Keep every customer with a `LEFT JOIN`, and put the refund filter in its `ON`. Then filter the groups on each customer's latest order with `HAVING`.
--- hint
`HAVING MAX(o.placed) < '2024-03-01'` alone drops Dee and Fay: their latest order is NULL, and `NULL < '2024-03-01'` is not true. Add `OR MAX(o.placed) IS NULL`.
--- check result | Ana has been quiet since February; Dee and Fay never bought
ordered
[["ana@mail.com", "2024-02-14"], ["dee@mail.com", null], ["fay@mail.com", null]]
--- check source | Keeps every customer with a LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source absent | Does not type any email
@mail\.com

+++ practice | Average order and rank
--- task
Write one query with one row for every customer, all six, and five columns:

1. `email`.
2. `orders`: how many non-refunded orders they have.
3. `spent`: the total of those orders in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals, and 0 if none.
4. `avg_cents`: their average order in whole cents: the total in cents divided by the number of orders, as a real number, then rounded with `ROUND` and cast to an integer. NULL for a customer with no orders, because an average of nothing is unknown.
5. `spend_rank`: their rank by total spend, highest first, using `RANK()`, so customers with the same spend share a rank.

Sort by `spend_rank`, then by `email`.
--- starter
SELECT c.email, COUNT(o.id) AS orders, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) / COUNT(o.id) AS avg_cents
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status <> 'refunded'
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id;
--- solution
WITH order_totals AS (
  SELECT o.id, o.customer_id, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS cents
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
  GROUP BY o.id
), per_customer AS (
  SELECT c.email, COUNT(t.id) AS orders, COALESCE(SUM(t.cents), 0) AS cents
  FROM customers c
  LEFT JOIN order_totals t ON t.customer_id = c.id
  GROUP BY c.id
)
SELECT email, orders,
       ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS spent,
       CAST(ROUND(1.0 * cents / NULLIF(orders, 0)) AS INTEGER) AS avg_cents,
       RANK() OVER (ORDER BY cents DESC) AS spend_rank
FROM per_customer
ORDER BY spend_rank, email;
--- hint
Start from the lesson's pre-aggregated orders, one row per non-refunded order with its total, so the order count is not inflated by the lines.
--- hint
Dividing by 0 must give NULL, not an error or 0: `NULLIF(orders, 0)` turns a zero count into NULL, and anything divided by NULL is NULL. Put `1.0 *` at the front so the division keeps its decimals before `ROUND`.
--- hint
`RANK() OVER (ORDER BY cents DESC)` in the final `SELECT` ranks the customers. Dee and Fay both spent 0, so they share rank 5.
--- check result | Bo first; Dee and Fay share the last rank with no average
ordered
[["bo@mail.com", 2, 144.49, 7225, 1], ["ana@mail.com", 2, 140.49, 7025, 2], ["cy@mail.com", 2, 94.05, 4703, 3], ["eli@mail.com", 1, 9.0, 900, 4], ["dee@mail.com", 0, 0.0, null, 5], ["fay@mail.com", 0, 0.0, null, 5]]
--- check source | Ranks with RANK()
[Rr][Aa][Nn][Kk]\s*\(\s*\)
--- check source | Guards the division with NULLIF
[Nn][Uu][Ll][Ll][Ii][Ff]\s*\(

+++ practice | Tiers that hold at the edges
--- task
Create a view named `customer_tiers` with one row for **every** customer and three columns:

1. `email`.
2. `spent_cents`: the total of `qty * unit_cents` over their non-refunded orders, and 0 if none.
3. `tier`: `'gold'` for 15000 cents or more, `'silver'` for 5000 or more, `'bronze'` for anything above 0, and `'none'` otherwise.

The checks add new orders and read the view again, so the boundaries must be exact: 15000 is gold, 14999 is silver, 5000 is silver, 4999 is bronze.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW customer_tiers AS
SELECT c.email,
       SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS spent_cents,
       CASE WHEN SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) > 0 THEN 'bronze'
            WHEN SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) > 5000 THEN 'silver'
            WHEN SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) > 15000 THEN 'gold'
            ELSE 'none' END AS tier
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
GROUP BY c.id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW customer_tiers AS
WITH spend AS (
  SELECT c.email, COALESCE(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents), 0) AS spent_cents
  FROM customers c
  LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status <> 'refunded'
  LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  GROUP BY c.id
)
SELECT email, spent_cents,
       CASE WHEN spent_cents >= 15000 THEN 'gold'
            WHEN spent_cents >= 5000 THEN 'silver'
            WHEN spent_cents > 0 THEN 'bronze'
            ELSE 'none' END AS tier
FROM spend;
--- hint
Three separate problems in the starter: customers with no orders vanish, the `WHEN`s are in the wrong order, and `>` is used where the task says "or more".
--- hint
Work out `spent_cents` first, in a CTE that starts `FROM customers c` and `LEFT JOIN`s the orders (refund filter in the `ON`) and their lines. Here a plain sum is safe: nothing is counted.
--- hint
Then the `CASE`, strictest first: `WHEN spent_cents >= 15000 THEN 'gold' WHEN spent_cents >= 5000 THEN 'silver' WHEN spent_cents > 0 THEN 'bronze' ELSE 'none' END`.
--- check query | Every customer with their tier
SELECT email, spent_cents, tier FROM customer_tiers ORDER BY email
=> [["ana@mail.com", 14049, "silver"], ["bo@mail.com", 14449, "silver"], ["cy@mail.com", 9405, "silver"], ["dee@mail.com", 0, "none"], ["eli@mail.com", 900, "bronze"], ["fay@mail.com", 0, "none"]]
--- check query | Exactly 5000 cents is silver; 4999 is bronze
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status) VALUES (1101, 6, '2024-04-01 10:00:00', 'paid'), (1102, 4, '2024-04-01 11:00:00', 'paid');
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, unit_cents) VALUES (1101, 1, 5, 1000), (1102, 4, 1, 4999);
SELECT email, spent_cents, tier FROM customer_tiers WHERE email IN ('dee@mail.com', 'fay@mail.com') ORDER BY email
=> [["dee@mail.com", 4999, "bronze"], ["fay@mail.com", 5000, "silver"]]
--- check query | Exactly 15000 cents is gold; a refund changes nothing
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status) VALUES (1103, 3, '2024-04-02 10:00:00', 'shipped'), (1104, 5, '2024-04-03 10:00:00', 'refunded');
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, unit_cents) VALUES (1103, 2, 1, 5595), (1104, 5, 2, 8999);
SELECT email, spent_cents, tier FROM customer_tiers WHERE email IN ('cy@mail.com', 'eli@mail.com') ORDER BY email
=> [["cy@mail.com", 15000, "gold"], ["eli@mail.com", 900, "bronze"]]

+++ practice | Shipped orders per customer
--- task
This query is meant to list every customer with the number of their orders that have status `'shipped'`:

```sql
SELECT c.email, COUNT(o.id) AS shipped
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status = 'shipped'
GROUP BY c.id
ORDER BY c.email;
```

It returns only Bo and Cy. Every one of the six customers should appear, with 0 when they have no shipped order. Fix it, keeping the columns `email` and `shipped` and the sort.
--- starter
SELECT c.email, COUNT(o.id) AS shipped
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status = 'shipped'
GROUP BY c.id
ORDER BY c.email;
--- solution
SELECT c.email, COUNT(o.id) AS shipped
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status = 'shipped'
GROUP BY c.id
ORDER BY c.email;
--- hint
Ana has orders, but none shipped. After the join, which of her rows survive `WHERE o.status = 'shipped'`?
--- hint
A condition on the right-hand table of a `LEFT JOIN` belongs in its `ON`: `ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status = 'shipped'`.
--- check result | Six customers; only Bo and Cy have shipped orders
ordered
[["ana@mail.com", 0], ["bo@mail.com", 1], ["cy@mail.com", 2], ["dee@mail.com", 0], ["eli@mail.com", 0], ["fay@mail.com", 0]]
--- check source absent | The status test is not in a WHERE
[Ww][Hh][Ee][Rr][Ee]\s+o\.status
--- check source absent | Does not type any email
@mail\.com

+++ practice | How long until the second order?
--- task
The owners want to know how quickly customers come back. Write one query with one row for **every** customer and four columns:

1. `email`.
2. `first_order`: the date (`'YYYY-MM-DD'`) of their first non-refunded order, or NULL.
3. `second_order`: the date of their second non-refunded order, or NULL if they have fewer than two.
4. `days_to_repeat`: the whole days from the first to the second, as an integer, or NULL.

Sort by `email`.
--- starter
SELECT c.email, date(MIN(o.placed)) AS first_order, date(MAX(o.placed)) AS second_order
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id
ORDER BY c.email;
--- solution
WITH numbered AS (
  SELECT customer_id, date(placed) AS day,
         ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY placed) AS n
  FROM orders
  WHERE status <> 'refunded'
), firsts AS (
  SELECT customer_id,
         MAX(CASE WHEN n = 1 THEN day END) AS first_order,
         MAX(CASE WHEN n = 2 THEN day END) AS second_order
  FROM numbered
  GROUP BY customer_id
)
SELECT c.email, f.first_order, f.second_order,
       CAST(julianday(f.second_order) - julianday(f.first_order) AS INTEGER) AS days_to_repeat
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN firsts f ON f.customer_id = c.id
ORDER BY c.email;
--- hint
`MAX` is not "the second order": for Eli, with one order, it is the first one again. Number each customer's orders in time order with `ROW_NUMBER()`, then pick numbers 1 and 2.
--- hint
Turn the numbered rows into one row per customer with conditional aggregation: `MAX(CASE WHEN n = 1 THEN day END)` and the same for `n = 2`. Then `LEFT JOIN` that onto `customers`.
--- hint
The days are `CAST(julianday(second_order) - julianday(first_order) AS INTEGER)`. When either date is NULL the result is NULL, which is what the task wants.
--- check result | Three repeat customers; Eli has one order; Dee and Fay none
ordered
[["ana@mail.com", "2024-01-03", "2024-02-14", 42], ["bo@mail.com", "2024-01-15", "2024-03-05", 50], ["cy@mail.com", "2024-02-01", "2024-03-18", 46], ["dee@mail.com", null, null, null], ["eli@mail.com", "2024-03-29", null, null], ["fay@mail.com", null, null, null]]
--- check source | Numbers the orders with ROW_NUMBER
[Rr][Oo][Ww]_[Nn][Uu][Mm][Bb][Ee][Rr]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type any email
@mail\.com

=== sqlp-05 | Analytics 1: ingesting raw events
--- schema
CREATE TABLE raw_events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  received_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  body TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO raw_events (id, received_at, body) VALUES
  (1,  '2024-07-01 09:00:01', '{"event_id": "e1", "user_id": 1, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:00:00"}'),
  (2,  '2024-07-01 09:03:01', '{"event_id": "e2", "user_id": 1, "name": "signup", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:03:00"}'),
  (3,  '2024-07-01 09:03:09', '{"event_id": "e2", "user_id": 1, "name": "signup", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:03:00"}'),
  (4,  '2024-07-01 10:00:01', '{"event_id": "e3", "user_id": 2, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 10:00:00"}'),
  (5,  '2024-07-01 10:20:00', 'not json at all'),
  (6,  '2024-07-01 11:00:01', '{"event_id": "e4", "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 11:00:00"}'),
  (7,  '2024-07-02 08:00:01', '{"event_id": "e5", "user_id": "3", "name": "Visit", "ts": "2024-07-02 08:00:00"}'),
  (8,  '2024-07-02 10:30:02', '{"event_id": "e6", "user_id": 2, "name": "signup", "ts": "2024-07-02T10:30:00Z"}'),
  (9,  '2024-07-02 12:00:01', '{"event_id": "e7", "user_id": 1, "name": "activate", "ts": "2024-07-02 12:00:00", "props": {"plan": "pro"}}'),
  (10, '2024-07-03 09:00:01', '{"user_id": 4, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-03 09:00:00"}');
--- teach
Last lesson finished the online store with a report on every customer. Now the second project begins: **product analytics** for an app. The app sends a small message every time a user does something, such as `visit` (opened the app), `signup` or `activate` (started really using it). Each message is an **event**: one thing one user did, at one moment. The team wants three numbers from them: daily active users, a signup funnel, and retention. That makes four lessons, and this first one gets the data in.

### The table

Events arrive in `raw_events` exactly as the phones and browsers sent them. It has three columns:

- `id`: the row's number, in the order the rows arrived;
- `received_at`: when the server got the message;
- `body`: the message itself, as [[JSON|json-text]] text.

Here are a few of the ten bodies:

```
id  body
1   {"event_id": "e1", "user_id": 1, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:00:00"}
5   not json at all
7   {"event_id": "e5", "user_id": "3", "name": "Visit", "ts": "2024-07-02 08:00:00"}
10  {"user_id": 4, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-03 09:00:00"}
```

Each good body has four keys: `event_id` (the event's own id, made by the app), `user_id`, `name` and `ts` (the time it happened). Row 9 also carries an extra `props` key, which this project does not need.

### The picture

Think of a post room. Letters pour in all day. A few are torn and unreadable. Some have no name on them. One was sent twice. Before anyone delivers anything, a sorter checks each letter, fixes what can be fixed, and puts only good ones in the pigeonholes. The pigeonholes are built so that a bad letter does not fit.

Loading raw data into a clean table is called **ingest**. It is the first step of every [[data pipeline|pipeline]]. The rule of an ingest step has two halves:

1. The **output table** has constraints strict enough that bad data **cannot** land in it.
2. The **query** does the cleaning that makes good data fit.

The clean table is `events`, with `event_id` as its primary key, and `user_id`, `name` and `ts` all `NOT NULL`. Now read the raw rows. They have five kinds of problem.

### Problem 1: a body that is not JSON

Row 5's body is `not json at all`. The functions that read JSON stop with an error when they meet it. This query tries to read every row's name:

```sql
SELECT body ->> '$.name' FROM raw_events;
```

```
Error: malformed JSON
```

One bad row, and the whole query fails. `->>`, from the lesson "JSON columns", reads one key out of the JSON as a plain value.

The fix is a new function. `json_valid(body)` gives 1 when `body` is well-formed JSON and 0 when it is not. It never errors, so you can test it first:

```sql
SELECT id, json_valid(body) AS ok FROM raw_events WHERE id IN (4, 5, 6);
```

| id | ok |
| --- | --- |
| 4 | 1 |
| 5 | 0 |
| 6 | 1 |

Put `json_valid(body)` first in the `WHERE`, and the bad row is dropped before anything else reads it.

### Problem 2: missing required fields

A key that is not in the JSON reads as NULL. Here are the ids of every valid row:

```sql
SELECT id, body ->> '$.event_id' AS event_id, body ->> '$.user_id' AS user_id
FROM raw_events
WHERE json_valid(body);
```

| id | event_id | user_id |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | e1 | 1 |
| 2 | e2 | 1 |
| 3 | e2 | 1 |
| 4 | e3 | 2 |
| 6 | e4 | NULL |
| 7 | e5 | 3 |
| 8 | e6 | 2 |
| 9 | e7 | 1 |
| 10 | NULL | 4 |

Row 6 has no `user_id`, and row 10 has no `event_id`. An event you cannot tie to a user, or cannot tell apart from a copy, is not usable. Skip both with `IS NOT NULL` tests in the `WHERE`. Test the event id yourself, even though it will be the primary key: in SQLite, [[a text primary key lets NULL in|null-key]]. A real pipeline would also [[count what it skipped|rejects]].

### Problem 3: the wrong type

Row 7 says `"user_id": "3"`, with quotes. That is the text `'3'`, not the number 3. `typeof(x)`, from "Constraints: rules the database enforces", shows the kind of value:

```sql
SELECT id,
       typeof(body ->> '$.user_id') AS raw_type,
       typeof(CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER)) AS cast_type
FROM raw_events
WHERE id IN (2, 7);
```

| id | raw_type | cast_type |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2 | integer | integer |
| 7 | text | integer |

`CAST(… AS INTEGER)`, from "Problem solving: cohort retention", turns it into a whole number. Run it on every row: a value that is already a number stays as it is.

### Problem 4: the same thing written two ways

Row 7's name is `"Visit"` with a capital V, and every other row says `"visit"`. `lower()`, from "Working with text", makes every name small letters.

Row 8's time is `"2024-07-02T10:30:00Z"`, with a `T` between the date and the time and a `Z` at the end. Every other row uses the form `'2024-07-02 10:30:00'`. `datetime(x)` reads a time in [[either form|t-and-z]] and always writes it back in the plain one:

```sql
SELECT lower('Visit') AS name, datetime('2024-07-02T10:30:00Z') AS ts;
```

| name | ts |
| --- | --- |
| visit | 2024-07-02 10:30:00 |

Now every name and every time is written one way, so grouping and comparing work later.

### Problem 5: the same event twice

Rows 2 and 3 are both event `e2`, eight seconds apart. The phone lost signal, never heard back, and [[sent it again|retries]]. It is one signup, not two.

The event carries its own id, so the **id decides** what is a duplicate, not the row. Two parts do the work:

- `event_id` is the primary key of `events`, so the table cannot hold `e2` twice.
- `INSERT OR IGNORE`, from "Constraints: rules the database enforces", skips any row that breaks a rule, instead of stopping with an error. The first copy goes in. The later copy is skipped.

"First" must mean the copy that arrived first. So put `ORDER BY id` at the end of the `SELECT`, and rows go in in the order they came.

### Putting it together

In "Store 2: loading the export" you filled a table with `INSERT … SELECT`: an `INSERT` whose rows come from a query instead of a `VALUES` list. The ingest has the same shape:

```sql
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts)
SELECT …each field, cleaned…
FROM raw_events
WHERE …only rows you can use…
ORDER BY id;
```

- The `SELECT` list reads each key with `->>` and cleans it: `CAST` for the user id, `lower` for the name, `datetime` for the time.
- The `WHERE` keeps valid JSON with both ids present.
- The primary key and `OR IGNORE` drop the retry.

Ten raw rows go in and six clean events come out.

**Watch out:** with a plain `INSERT`, the retried `e2` breaks the primary key, and SQLite answers `UNIQUE constraint failed: events.event_id`. A failed statement changes nothing, so not one row is loaded, not even the good ones. Leave out `json_valid(body)` and the same happens with `malformed JSON`. One bad row must never block the good ones: filter what you can see, and let `OR IGNORE` catch the rest.

::: context json-text JSON, the language apps send
**JSON** (JavaScript Object Notation) is a way to write data as text: keys in quotes, a colon, then a value, all inside curly brackets. Nearly every app and website sends its data this way, because any programming language can read and write it. You queried JSON columns in the lesson "JSON columns". Spacecraft ground systems use it too: many mission-control tools pass commands and telemetry between programs as JSON messages, and they check every message the same way this lesson does before trusting it.
:::

::: context pipeline A pipeline, one step at a time
A **data pipeline** is a chain of steps that carries data from where it is made to where people read it. Each step reads one table and writes the next. Ingest is the front door: it takes whatever arrived and writes a table the later steps can trust. The next three lessons read only from `events`, never from `raw_events`, so they never have to worry about broken JSON again.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 150" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="ar" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <rect x="10" y="30" width="95" height="44" rx="4" fill="#fff" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="57" y="49" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">raw_events</text>
  <text x="57" y="65" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">10 rows</text>
  <rect x="133" y="30" width="95" height="44" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="49" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">ingest</text>
  <text x="180" y="65" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">check and clean</text>
  <rect x="256" y="30" width="95" height="44" rx="4" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="303" y="49" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">events</text>
  <text x="303" y="65" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">6 rows</text>
  <line x1="105" y1="52" x2="131" y2="52" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#ar)"/>
  <line x1="228" y1="52" x2="254" y2="52" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#ar)"/>
  <line x1="180" y1="74" x2="180" y2="98" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="114" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">skipped: 1 bad JSON, 2 missing ids, 1 retry</text>
  <text x="180" y="138" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">later lessons read only the clean table</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context null-key A primary key that lets NULL in
You might expect `PRIMARY KEY` to refuse NULL, as it does in most databases. In an ordinary SQLite table, only an `INTEGER PRIMARY KEY` is guaranteed never to be NULL. A `TEXT PRIMARY KEY` accepts NULL, and even accepts it twice, because NULL is never equal to anything, not even another NULL. It is an old bug that SQLite keeps so that older programs still work. So without the `IS NOT NULL` test, row 10 would land in `events` with no event id. Writing `event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY NOT NULL` would close that door in the table too.
:::

::: context rejects Counting what you skipped
Skipping a bad row quietly is fine for a lesson, but a real pipeline also keeps a tally: 10 received, 6 loaded, 4 skipped, and why. Some keep the skipped rows in a separate table so someone can look at them later. If the tally suddenly jumps, say half of today's events have no `user_id`, it usually means a new version of the app has a bug. Ground stations do the same with telemetry: every frame that fails its check is counted, and a rising count is an early warning that something is wrong with the radio link.
:::

::: context t-and-z What the T and the Z mean
`2024-07-02T10:30:00Z` is the international standard way to write a time, called **ISO 8601**. The `T` only separates the date from the time. The `Z` means the time is in **UTC**, the world's reference clock (Z is said "Zulu" by pilots and mission controllers). The other rows here are in UTC too, so dropping the `Z` loses nothing. If a time carries an offset instead, like `+02:00`, `datetime()` converts it to UTC: `datetime('2024-07-02T12:30:00+02:00')` also gives `'2024-07-02 10:30:00'`.
:::

::: context retries Why phones send things twice
A phone sends an event and waits for the server to say "got it". If the signal drops, the reply never arrives, so the phone cannot tell whether the event was lost or only the reply. The safe choice is to send it again. That means the server must expect copies. An operation that gives the same result however many times it runs is called **idempotent**. Giving each event its own id and ignoring an id already seen makes this load idempotent: run it twice and `events` still holds six rows.
:::
--- task
Load the clean `events` table from `raw_events`. The starter already creates `events`: `event_id` (text, primary key), `user_id` (integer, required), `name` (text, required) and `ts` (text, required). Keep it, and below it write one `INSERT OR IGNORE … SELECT` that:

1. keeps only rows whose `body` is valid JSON (`json_valid`);
2. keeps only rows that have both an `event_id` and a `user_id`;
3. stores `user_id` as an integer (`CAST`);
4. stores `name` in small letters (`lower`);
5. stores `ts` in the plain form (`datetime`);
6. stores each `event_id` once, keeping the first copy received (order by `id`).
--- starter
CREATE TABLE events (
  event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  ts TEXT NOT NULL
);
--- solution
CREATE TABLE events (
  event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  ts TEXT NOT NULL
);

INSERT OR IGNORE INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts)
SELECT body ->> '$.event_id',
       CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER),
       lower(body ->> '$.name'),
       datetime(body ->> '$.ts')
FROM raw_events
WHERE json_valid(body)
  AND body ->> '$.event_id' IS NOT NULL
  AND body ->> '$.user_id' IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
Start with the `WHERE`: three tests joined with `AND`. The body is valid JSON, the `event_id` is not NULL, and the `user_id` is not NULL. Put `json_valid(body)` first.
--- hint
The `SELECT` list reads each key with `body ->> '$.key'` and cleans it: `CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER)`, `lower(body ->> '$.name')`, `datetime(body ->> '$.ts')`. The event id needs no cleaning.
--- hint
The shape is `INSERT OR IGNORE INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts) SELECT … FROM raw_events WHERE json_valid(body) AND body ->> '$.event_id' IS NOT NULL AND body ->> '$.user_id' IS NOT NULL ORDER BY id;`. The primary key drops the second `e2`.
--- check query | Six clean events: bad JSON, missing ids and the retry are gone
SELECT event_id, user_id, name, ts FROM events ORDER BY event_id
=> [["e1", 1, "visit", "2024-07-01 09:00:00"], ["e2", 1, "signup", "2024-07-01 09:03:00"], ["e3", 2, "visit", "2024-07-01 10:00:00"], ["e5", 3, "visit", "2024-07-02 08:00:00"], ["e6", 2, "signup", "2024-07-02 10:30:00"], ["e7", 1, "activate", "2024-07-02 12:00:00"]]
--- check query | Every user_id is stored as an integer
SELECT DISTINCT typeof(user_id) FROM events
=> [["integer"]]
--- check query | A replayed event is still ignored
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts) VALUES ('e1', 1, 'visit', '2024-07-01 09:00:00') RETURNING event_id
=> []
--- check source | Reads the rows in the order they arrived (ORDER BY id)
[Oo][Rr][Dd][Ee][Rr]\s+[Bb][Yy]\s+(\w+\.)?id\b

+++ practice | Sensor messages worth keeping
--- schema
CREATE TABLE raw_readings (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  body TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO raw_readings (id, body) VALUES
  (1, '{"sensor": "T1", "temp": 21.5, "at": "2024-07-01 10:00:00"}'),
  (2, '{"sensor": "T2", "temp": 19.0, "at": "2024-07-01 10:00:00"}'),
  (3, '{"sensor": "T1", "temp": 21.7'),
  (4, '{"sensor": "T3", "at": "2024-07-01 10:00:00"}'),
  (5, '{"sensor": "T2", "temp": -4.25, "at": "2024-07-01 10:01:00"}'),
  (6, '{"temp": 18.0, "at": "2024-07-01 10:01:00"}'),
  (7, 'ERROR 503');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `raw_readings`, with the columns `id` and `body`. Each `body` should be a JSON message with the keys `sensor`, `temp` and `at`, but some messages were cut off, some are not JSON at all, and some are missing a key.

Write one `SELECT` that returns the usable readings, with three columns: `id`, `sensor` and `temp` (both read from the JSON). A reading is usable when its body is valid JSON and it has both a `sensor` and a `temp`. Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT id, body ->> '$.sensor' AS sensor, body ->> '$.temp' AS temp
FROM raw_readings
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id, body ->> '$.sensor' AS sensor, body ->> '$.temp' AS temp
FROM raw_readings
WHERE json_valid(body)
  AND body ->> '$.sensor' IS NOT NULL
  AND body ->> '$.temp' IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
Run the starter: it stops with `malformed JSON`, because one bad body breaks the whole query. Test each body with `json_valid(body)` before anything reads from it.
--- hint
Put `json_valid(body)` first in the `WHERE`, then `AND body ->> '$.sensor' IS NOT NULL AND body ->> '$.temp' IS NOT NULL`.
--- check result | Three usable readings, the negative one included

--- check source absent | Does not pick rows by id

+++ practice | A tally of what the ingest skipped
--- task
A real pipeline counts what it throws away. For the lesson's `raw_events`, give every raw row exactly one reason and count the rows per reason:

- `'bad json'`: the body is not valid JSON;
- `'missing field'`: valid JSON, but no `event_id` or no `user_id`;
- `'duplicate'`: a complete event whose `event_id` already arrived in an earlier row (smaller `id`);
- `'loaded'`: everything else.

Return two columns, `reason` and `events` (the count), one row per reason that occurs, sorted by `reason`. The counts must add up to the 10 raw rows.
--- starter
SELECT CASE WHEN json_valid(body) THEN 'loaded' ELSE 'bad json' END AS reason,
       COUNT(*) AS events
FROM raw_events
GROUP BY reason
ORDER BY reason;
--- solution
WITH valid AS (
  SELECT id, body ->> '$.event_id' AS event_id, body ->> '$.user_id' AS user_id
  FROM raw_events
  WHERE json_valid(body)
), tagged AS (
  SELECT CASE WHEN event_id IS NULL OR user_id IS NULL THEN 'missing field'
              WHEN ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY event_id ORDER BY id) > 1 THEN 'duplicate'
              ELSE 'loaded' END AS reason
  FROM valid
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'bad json' FROM raw_events WHERE NOT json_valid(body)
)
SELECT reason, COUNT(*) AS events
FROM tagged
GROUP BY reason
ORDER BY reason;
--- hint
Read the JSON only for valid rows: first a CTE of the valid rows with their `event_id` and `user_id`, then tag each of them. The invalid rows get their tag separately, and `UNION ALL` stacks the two lists.
--- hint
"Already arrived earlier" is a numbering question: `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY event_id ORDER BY id)` is 1 for the first copy of each event and 2 for a retry.
--- hint
A `CASE` checks the reasons in order: `WHEN event_id IS NULL OR user_id IS NULL THEN 'missing field' WHEN ROW_NUMBER() OVER (…) > 1 THEN 'duplicate' ELSE 'loaded' END`. Then group all the tags and count.
--- check result | 1 bad, 1 duplicate, 6 loaded, 2 missing a field
ordered
[["bad json", 1], ["duplicate", 1], ["loaded", 6], ["missing field", 2]]
--- check source | Tests the JSON with json_valid
json_valid\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type the counts
\b(1|2|6)\s+AS\s+events

+++ practice | Profiles, latest message wins
--- schema
CREATE TABLE raw_profiles (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  body TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO raw_profiles (id, body) VALUES
  (1, '{"user_id": 1, "email": "Ana@X.io", "plan": "free"}'),
  (2, '{"user_id": 2, "email": "bo@x.io", "plan": "free"}'),
  (3, '{"user_id": 1, "email": "ana@x.io ", "plan": "pro"}'),
  (4, '{"user_id": 3, "plan": "free"}'),
  (5, 'oops'),
  (6, '{"user_id": "2", "email": "BO@x.io", "plan": "team"}'),
  (7, '{"user_id": 4, "email": "cy@x.io", "plan": "free"}');
CREATE TABLE profiles (
  user_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  plan TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (plan IN ('free', 'pro', 'team')),
  updated_by INTEGER NOT NULL
);
--- task
This problem has its own tables. `raw_profiles` (`id`, `body`) holds profile messages in the order they arrived; a later message about the same user replaces the earlier one. `profiles` (`user_id`, `email`, `plan`, `updated_by`) is empty; `updated_by` records the raw `id` of the message the row came from.

Load `profiles` with one `INSERT … SELECT` that is also an upsert (`ON CONFLICT (user_id) DO UPDATE`):

1. Skip bodies that are not valid JSON, and messages with no `user_id` or no `email`.
2. Store `user_id` as an integer, `email` trimmed and in lower case, `plan` as it is, and `updated_by` as the raw row's `id`.
3. Read the messages in the order they arrived, so for each user the **latest** message wins.
--- starter
INSERT INTO profiles (user_id, email, plan, updated_by)
SELECT body ->> '$.user_id', body ->> '$.email', body ->> '$.plan', id
FROM raw_profiles
WHERE json_valid(body);
--- solution
INSERT INTO profiles (user_id, email, plan, updated_by)
SELECT CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER),
       lower(trim(body ->> '$.email')),
       body ->> '$.plan',
       id
FROM raw_profiles
WHERE json_valid(body)
  AND body ->> '$.user_id' IS NOT NULL
  AND body ->> '$.email' IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY id
ON CONFLICT (user_id) DO UPDATE
SET email = excluded.email, plan = excluded.plan, updated_by = excluded.updated_by;
--- hint
This is the lesson's ingest turned around: instead of keeping the first copy with `OR IGNORE`, each later message overwrites the row, which is an upsert from "UPSERT: insert or update".
--- hint
Clean the fields in the `SELECT` list, filter in the `WHERE`, and put `ORDER BY id` last before the upsert, so rows are inserted in the order they arrived and the latest one is applied last.
--- hint
End with `ON CONFLICT (user_id) DO UPDATE SET email = excluded.email, plan = excluded.plan, updated_by = excluded.updated_by;`. `excluded` is the row that tried to go in.
--- check query | Three profiles, each from its latest message
SELECT user_id, email, plan, updated_by FROM profiles ORDER BY user_id
=> [[1, "ana@x.io", "pro", 3], [2, "bo@x.io", "team", 6], [4, "cy@x.io", "free", 7]]
--- check query | The message with no email was skipped
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM profiles WHERE user_id = 3
=> [[0]]
--- check query | Every stored email is trimmed and in lower case
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM profiles WHERE email <> lower(trim(email))
=> [[0]]
--- check source | Uses an upsert
[Oo][Nn]\s+[Cc][Oo][Nn][Ff][Ll][Ii][Cc][Tt]

+++ practice | Every way a field can be wrong
--- schema
CREATE TABLE raw_events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  body TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO raw_events (id, body) VALUES
  (1,  '{"event_id": "a1", "user_id": 7, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:00:00"}'),
  (2,  '{"event_id": "a2", "user_id": "7", "name": " Visit ", "ts": "2024-07-01T09:05:00Z"}'),
  (3,  '{"event_id": "a3", "user_id": 7.0, "name": "signup", "ts": "2024-07-01T11:10:00+02:00"}'),
  (4,  '{"event_id": "a4", "user_id": "abc", "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 10:00:00"}'),
  (5,  '{"event_id": "a5", "user_id": 8, "name": "visit", "ts": "yesterday"}'),
  (6,  '{"event_id": "a6", "user_id": 8, "name": "VISIT", "ts": "2024-07-02"}'),
  (7,  '{"event_id": "a7", "user_id": 0, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-02 08:00:00"}'),
  (8,  '{"event_id": " ", "user_id": 9, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-02 09:00:00"}'),
  (9,  '{"event_id": "a2", "user_id": 7, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01T09:05:00Z"}'),
  (10, '{"event_id": "a8", "user_id": 9, "name": "", "ts": "2024-07-02 10:00:00"}');
--- task
This problem has its own `raw_events` table (`id`, `body`), where every body is valid JSON but the fields inside are not to be trusted. The starter creates the clean `events` table and loads it the way the lesson did. Keep the table, and change the load so that an event is stored only when all of these hold:

1. `event_id` is present and not blank (not empty and not only spaces); store it trimmed.
2. `user_id`, cast to an integer, is above 0. A text like `"abc"` casts to 0, so it is skipped.
3. `name` is present and not blank; store it trimmed and in small letters.
4. `ts` can be read by `datetime()`: when `datetime(x)` gives NULL, the time is unreadable and the event is skipped.

As before, each `event_id` is stored once, keeping the first copy received.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE events (
  event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  ts TEXT NOT NULL
);

INSERT OR IGNORE INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts)
SELECT body ->> '$.event_id',
       CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER),
       lower(body ->> '$.name'),
       datetime(body ->> '$.ts')
FROM raw_events
WHERE json_valid(body)
  AND body ->> '$.event_id' IS NOT NULL
  AND body ->> '$.user_id' IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
CREATE TABLE events (
  event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  ts TEXT NOT NULL
);

INSERT OR IGNORE INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts)
SELECT trim(body ->> '$.event_id'),
       CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER),
       lower(trim(body ->> '$.name')),
       datetime(body ->> '$.ts')
FROM raw_events
WHERE json_valid(body)
  AND trim(body ->> '$.event_id') <> ''
  AND CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER) > 0
  AND trim(body ->> '$.name') <> ''
  AND datetime(body ->> '$.ts') IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
Run the starter and read `events`: user 0 appears twice, an event id made of a space got in, and so did an event with an empty name. Each of the four rules becomes one test in the `WHERE`.
--- hint
`trim(x) <> ''` is false for a blank and NULL for a missing key, and a `WHERE` keeps only true, so one test handles both. The user test is `CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER) > 0`.
--- hint
Test the time with `datetime(body ->> '$.ts') IS NOT NULL`, and trim in the `SELECT` list too: `trim(body ->> '$.event_id')` and `lower(trim(body ->> '$.name'))`.
--- check query | Four good events; every bad field kept its event out
SELECT event_id, user_id, name, ts FROM events ORDER BY event_id
=> [["a1", 7, "visit", "2024-07-01 09:00:00"], ["a2", 7, "visit", "2024-07-01 09:05:00"], ["a3", 7, "signup", "2024-07-01 09:10:00"], ["a6", 8, "visit", "2024-07-02 00:00:00"]]
--- check query | No user 0 and no blank id or name
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM events WHERE user_id <= 0 OR trim(event_id) = '' OR trim(name) = ''
=> [[0]]
--- check query | Every time is stored in the plain form
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM events WHERE ts <> datetime(ts)
=> [[0]]

+++ practice | The retry that rewrote the time
--- schema
CREATE TABLE raw_events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  body TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO raw_events (id, body) VALUES
  (1, '{"event_id": "b1", "user_id": 1, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:00:00"}'),
  (2, '{"event_id": "b2", "user_id": 1, "name": "signup", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:03:00"}'),
  (3, '{"event_id": "b2", "user_id": 1, "name": "signup", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:04:30"}'),
  (4, '{"event_id": "b3", "user_id": 2, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 10:00:00"}'),
  (5, '{"event_id": "b3", "user_id": 2, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 10:02:00"}');
--- task
This problem has its own `raw_events` table (`id`, `body`). This app stamps each retry with the moment it was **re-sent**, so a retried copy carries a later `ts` than the event really had. The rule is the lesson's: the first copy received wins.

The starter creates `events` and loads it without an error, but events `b2` and `b3` end up with their retry's time. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE events (
  event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  ts TEXT NOT NULL
);

INSERT OR REPLACE INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts)
SELECT body ->> '$.event_id',
       CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER),
       lower(body ->> '$.name'),
       datetime(body ->> '$.ts')
FROM raw_events
WHERE json_valid(body)
  AND body ->> '$.event_id' IS NOT NULL
  AND body ->> '$.user_id' IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
CREATE TABLE events (
  event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  ts TEXT NOT NULL
);

INSERT OR IGNORE INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts)
SELECT body ->> '$.event_id',
       CAST(body ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER),
       lower(body ->> '$.name'),
       datetime(body ->> '$.ts')
FROM raw_events
WHERE json_valid(body)
  AND body ->> '$.event_id' IS NOT NULL
  AND body ->> '$.user_id' IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
The rows go in in the order they arrived. When the second copy of `b2` meets the primary key, what does `OR REPLACE` do with the row already there?
--- hint
`OR REPLACE` deletes the old row and stores the new one, so the last copy wins. `OR IGNORE` skips the new one, so the first copy wins.
--- check query | Each event keeps the time of its first copy
SELECT event_id, ts FROM events ORDER BY event_id
=> [["b1", "2024-07-01 09:00:00"], ["b2", "2024-07-01 09:03:00"], ["b3", "2024-07-01 10:00:00"]]
--- check query | Three events, one per event id

+++ practice | Events that arrive in batches
--- schema
CREATE TABLE raw_batches (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  body TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO raw_batches (id, body) VALUES
  (1, '{"batch": "x1", "events": [{"event_id": "c1", "user_id": 1, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:00:00"}, {"event_id": "c2", "user_id": 1, "name": "Signup", "ts": "2024-07-01T09:02:00Z"}]}'),
  (2, '{"batch": "x2", "events": [{"event_id": "c3", "user_id": 2, "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 10:00:00"}, {"event_id": "c4", "name": "visit", "ts": "2024-07-01 10:05:00"}]}'),
  (3, '{"batch": "x3", "events": [{"event_id": "c2", "user_id": 1, "name": "signup", "ts": "2024-07-01 09:02:00"}, {"event_id": "c5", "user_id": "1", "name": "activate", "ts": "2024-07-02 08:00:00"}]}'),
  (4, '{"batch": "x4", "events": [{"event_id": "c6", "user_i'),
  (5, '{"batch": "x5", "events": []}');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `raw_batches` (`id`, `body`). To save battery, the app now sends events in **batches**: each body is one JSON object with a `batch` name and an `events` array, and each element of the array is an event with the usual four keys. One batch was cut off in transit, one is empty, and a retry of `c2` came in a later batch.

1. Create `events` with `event_id` (text, primary key), `user_id` (integer, required), `name` (text, required), `ts` (text, required) and `batch` (text, required): the name of the batch the stored copy came from.
2. Load it with one `INSERT OR IGNORE … SELECT`. Skip batches that are not valid JSON and events with no `event_id` or no `user_id`. Clean `user_id`, `name` and `ts` as in the lesson. When an event arrives twice, keep the copy from the earliest batch.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE events (
  event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  ts TEXT NOT NULL,
  batch TEXT NOT NULL
);
--- solution
CREATE TABLE events (
  event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  ts TEXT NOT NULL,
  batch TEXT NOT NULL
);

INSERT OR IGNORE INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts, batch)
SELECT e.value ->> '$.event_id',
       CAST(e.value ->> '$.user_id' AS INTEGER),
       lower(e.value ->> '$.name'),
       datetime(e.value ->> '$.ts'),
       b.body ->> '$.batch'
FROM (SELECT id, body FROM raw_batches WHERE json_valid(body)) b,
     json_each(b.body, '$.events') AS e
WHERE e.value ->> '$.event_id' IS NOT NULL
  AND e.value ->> '$.user_id' IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY b.id, e.key;
--- hint
`json_each(b.body, '$.events')`, from "JSON columns", turns each batch's array into one row per event, and `e.value` is that event's object. Only valid batches may reach it, so filter them first in a subquery in the `FROM`.
--- hint
Read the fields from `e.value`: `e.value ->> '$.event_id'` and so on, and the batch name from `b.body ->> '$.batch'`. The empty batch simply gives no rows.
--- hint
"Earliest batch first" is the order the rows go in: `ORDER BY b.id, e.key`, where `e.key` is the event's position in its array. `OR IGNORE` and the primary key then drop the later copy of `c2`.
--- check query | Four events, each from the batch it first came in
SELECT event_id, user_id, name, ts, batch FROM events ORDER BY event_id
=> [["c1", 1, "visit", "2024-07-01 09:00:00", "x1"], ["c2", 1, "signup", "2024-07-01 09:02:00", "x1"], ["c3", 2, "visit", "2024-07-01 10:00:00", "x2"], ["c5", 1, "activate", "2024-07-02 08:00:00", "x3"]]
--- check query | The event with no user was skipped
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM events WHERE event_id = 'c4'
=> [[0]]
--- check query | The retry of c2 from batch x3 was ignored
SELECT batch FROM events WHERE event_id = 'c2'
=> [["x1"]]

=== sqlp-06 | Analytics 2: daily active users
--- teach
Last lesson you loaded clean events and threw away the broken ones. From here on, every lesson in this project reads the clean `events` table. The first number every product team watches is how many people used the app each day. This lesson counts it honestly, with no missing days and no person counted twice.

### The table

`events` has the columns from last lesson: `event_id`, `user_id`, `name` (`visit`, `signup` or `activate`) and `ts`, the time. It now holds 25 events from six users over ten days, 1 to 10 July 2024. Here are the users seen each day, one entry per event:

| day | users, one per event |
| --- | --- |
| 2024-07-01 | 1, 1, 2, 2 |
| 2024-07-02 | 1, 3, 3, 2 |
| 2024-07-03 | 3, 1, 4, 4 |
| 2024-07-04 | (no events) |
| 2024-07-05 | 2, 5, 5, 1 |
| 2024-07-06 | 4, 3 |
| 2024-07-07 | 6, 6, 2 |
| 2024-07-08 | 1 |
| 2024-07-09 | 5, 3 |
| 2024-07-10 | 4 |

### The picture

A shop door has a counter that clicks every time the door opens. At closing time it says 40. But one customer went in and out four times, so 40 clicks is not 40 customers. And on a day the shop was shut, the counter writes nothing at all, so a list of daily counts has no line for that day. This lesson fixes both.

### Two numbers per day

- **[[Daily active users|dau-word]]**, or **DAU**: how many *different* users did anything that day.
- **New users**: how many of them were seen that day for the very first time.

### Trap 1: activity is not users

Counting rows per day counts events, like the door counter:

```sql
SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(*) AS dau
FROM events
GROUP BY day;
```

On 1 July this says 4. But the table above shows only users 1 and 2 that day. `COUNT(DISTINCT user_id)` counts each different user once:

```sql
SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
FROM events
GROUP BY date(ts);
```

| day | dau |
| --- | --- |
| 2024-07-01 | 2 |
| 2024-07-02 | 3 |
| 2024-07-03 | 3 |
| 2024-07-05 | 3 |
| 2024-07-06 | 2 |
| 2024-07-07 | 2 |
| 2024-07-08 | 1 |
| 2024-07-09 | 2 |
| 2024-07-10 | 1 |

`date(ts)` keeps only [[the day part|whose-day]] of the time, as in "Date arithmetic and date ranges".

### Trap 2: days with no events vanish

Look at the list again: it jumps from 3 July to 5 July. `GROUP BY` makes a row only for a day that has events, so [[a dead day disappears|missing-day]] instead of showing 0.

You met this in "Recursive CTEs: generating a series". The fix is a calendar: a recursive CTE with one row for every day, and the numbers joined onto it with `LEFT JOIN`. For the first week:

```sql
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day < '2024-07-07'
)
SELECT day FROM days;
```

That gives seven rows, 1 to 7 July, with 4 July included.

### New users: each user's first day

"New on a day" needs each user's **first-ever** day. That is a per-user `MIN`:

```sql
SELECT user_id, MIN(date(ts)) AS first_day
FROM events
GROUP BY user_id;
```

| user_id | first_day |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | 2024-07-01 |
| 2 | 2024-07-01 |
| 3 | 2024-07-02 |
| 4 | 2024-07-03 |
| 5 | 2024-07-05 |
| 6 | 2024-07-07 |

A day's new users are the users whose `first_day` is that day. So wrap this query and count its rows per `first_day`:

```sql
SELECT first_day AS day, COUNT(*) AS new_users
FROM (SELECT user_id, MIN(date(ts)) AS first_day FROM events GROUP BY user_id)
GROUP BY first_day;
```

| day | new_users |
| --- | --- |
| 2024-07-01 | 2 |
| 2024-07-02 | 1 |
| 2024-07-03 | 1 |
| 2024-07-05 | 1 |
| 2024-07-07 | 1 |

The `MIN` looks at **all** events, not only the week in the report. That matters: a user first seen before the report starts is [[never counted as new inside it|all-history]].

### One calendar, two separate counts

Now there are three pieces: the calendar, the DAU per day and the new users per day. Make each count its own CTE, grouped down to **one row per day**. Then join both onto the calendar:

```sql
FROM days d
LEFT JOIN active a ON a.day = d.day
LEFT JOIN firsts f ON f.day = d.day
```

Each count has at most one row per day, so every join matches [[one row to one row|one-to-one]], and nothing is repeated. The first week comes out like this:

| day | dau | new_users |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2024-07-01 | 2 | 2 |
| 2024-07-02 | 3 | 1 |
| 2024-07-03 | 3 | 1 |
| 2024-07-04 | NULL | NULL |
| 2024-07-05 | 3 | 1 |
| 2024-07-06 | 2 | NULL |
| 2024-07-07 | 2 | 1 |

The NULLs are days where a count had no row. `COALESCE(x, 0)`, from "Testing for NULL", turns each one into 0. A quiet day should read 0, not "unknown".

### Check it by hand

On 6 July, users 4 and 3 were active, so DAU is 2. Both were first seen earlier, so there are 0 new users. On 7 July, users 6 and 2 were active, and user 6 was brand new: 2 and 1. The table agrees.

**Watch out:** do not work out both numbers in one query over `events`. This looks tidy:

```sql
SELECT date(e.ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT e.user_id) AS dau, COUNT(f.user_id) AS new_users
FROM events e
LEFT JOIN (SELECT user_id, MIN(date(ts)) AS first_day FROM events GROUP BY user_id) f
  ON f.user_id = e.user_id AND f.first_day = date(e.ts)
GROUP BY date(e.ts);
```

But it says 4 new users on 1 July, not 2. Each new user matches once **per event** they had that day, and users 1 and 2 had two events each. It is the fan-out from "Debugging: the join that double-counts". Counting in separate CTEs, one row per day each, avoids it.

::: context dau-word DAU, WAU and MAU
**DAU** is the number of different people who used a product on one day. Teams also count **WAU** (weekly) and **MAU** (monthly) the same way, over a longer window. A user active on all seven days of a week counts seven times in the week's daily numbers but only once in its WAU. Dividing DAU by MAU gives a rough "stickiness": the share of the month's users who show up on a typical day. Games and chat apps watch it closely, because it says whether people come back by habit.
:::

::: context whose-day Whose day is it?
`date(ts)` cuts the day at midnight in whatever clock `ts` is written in, here UTC. For a user in Tokyo, 9 hours ahead, an evening visit at 20:00 local time is 11:00 UTC the same day, but a morning visit at 07:00 local time is 22:00 UTC the day before. Big apps pick one clock for their daily numbers and write it on the chart, so nobody compares one team's Monday with another team's.
:::

::: context missing-day A missing day tells a lie
A chart draws what it is given. Give it the grouped rows and it places 3 July and 5 July side by side, and the day with no events is invisible. With the calendar, 4 July stays on the axis at 0, and anyone looking asks "what happened that day?". That question is often the most useful thing on the dashboard: an outage, a failed app release, a holiday.

```svg

  <text x="180" y="16" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">DAU, 1 to 7 July, joined onto a calendar</text>

  <text x="89" y="146" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2</text>
  <text x="133" y="146" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">3</text>
```

:::

::: context all-history Why "new" looks at all of history
Suppose the report covered only 5 to 7 July, and the `MIN` looked only at those days. User 1 was active on 5 July, and inside that window it is their earliest day, so they would be counted as new, although they have used the app since 1 July. Every report window would "discover" old users again. Taking each user's first day over all events, and only then picking the days to show, gives each user exactly one new day, ever.
:::

::: context one-to-one Why each count gets its own CTE
A join repeats a row once for every match. If both sides have at most one row per day, each day matches at most once, and the numbers stay as they were. That is why each count is grouped down to one row per day **before** it meets anything else. It is the same rule as pre-aggregating each order's total in "Store 4: the customer report": shrink each measure to the level of the report first, then join.
:::
--- task
Write one query that returns one row for every day from `2024-07-01` to `2024-07-07`, with these columns:

1. `day`: the date, as `'YYYY-MM-DD'`;
2. `dau`: how many different users had any event that day;
3. `new_users`: how many users had their first-ever event that day.

A day with no activity shows 0 in both number columns. Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(*) AS dau
FROM events
GROUP BY day;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day < '2024-07-07'
), active AS (
  SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
  FROM events
  GROUP BY date(ts)
), firsts AS (
  SELECT first_day AS day, COUNT(*) AS new_users
  FROM (SELECT user_id, MIN(date(ts)) AS first_day FROM events GROUP BY user_id)
  GROUP BY first_day
)
SELECT d.day, COALESCE(a.dau, 0) AS dau, COALESCE(f.new_users, 0) AS new_users
FROM days d
LEFT JOIN active a ON a.day = d.day
LEFT JOIN firsts f ON f.day = d.day
ORDER BY d.day;
--- hint
Three CTEs: a seven-day calendar (a recursive CTE, as in "Recursive CTEs: generating a series"), the DAU per day, and the new users per day. Then `LEFT JOIN` both counts onto the calendar.
--- hint
DAU per day is `COUNT(DISTINCT user_id)` grouped by `date(ts)`. New users: first each user's `MIN(date(ts))`, then count users per first day. Group each one down to one row per day.
--- hint
The last `SELECT` starts `FROM days d`, joins with `LEFT JOIN active a ON a.day = d.day` and `LEFT JOIN firsts f ON f.day = d.day`, wraps each count in `COALESCE(…, 0)`, and ends `ORDER BY d.day`.
--- check result | Seven days; 4 July is empty
ordered
[["2024-07-01", 2, 2], ["2024-07-02", 3, 1], ["2024-07-03", 3, 1], ["2024-07-04", 0, 0], ["2024-07-05", 3, 1], ["2024-07-06", 2, 0], ["2024-07-07", 2, 1]]

+++ practice | Players per day
--- schema
CREATE TABLE plays (
  player INTEGER NOT NULL,
  played_at TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO plays (player, played_at) VALUES
  (1, '2024-03-01 08:00'), (1, '2024-03-01 21:00'), (2, '2024-03-01 12:00'),
  (2, '2024-03-02 09:00'), (3, '2024-03-02 23:59'),
  (1, '2024-03-04 07:30'), (1, '2024-03-04 07:45'), (1, '2024-03-04 19:00'),
  (3, '2024-03-05 00:00'), (2, '2024-03-05 13:00'), (4, '2024-03-05 18:00');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `plays`, with one row each time a player started a game: `player` (a number) and `played_at` (a time).

Write one query with one row for every day from `2024-03-01` to `2024-03-05`, and three columns:

1. `day`: `'YYYY-MM-DD'`.
2. `players`: how many different players played that day.
3. `plays`: how many games were started that day.

A day with no games shows 0 in both. Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(played_at) AS day, COUNT(*) AS players, COUNT(*) AS plays
FROM plays
GROUP BY day
ORDER BY day;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-03-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day < '2024-03-05'
), daily AS (
  SELECT date(played_at) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT player) AS players, COUNT(*) AS plays
  FROM plays
  GROUP BY date(played_at)
)
SELECT d.day, COALESCE(p.players, 0) AS players, COALESCE(p.plays, 0) AS plays
FROM days d
LEFT JOIN daily p ON p.day = d.day
ORDER BY d.day;
--- hint
Two fixes: players are counted once each with `COUNT(DISTINCT player)`, and the quiet day comes back with a calendar joined to the counts.
--- hint
Build the five days with a recursive CTE, count per day in a second CTE, then `LEFT JOIN` the counts onto the calendar and wrap each in `COALESCE(…, 0)`.
--- check result | Five days; 3 March is empty
ordered
[["2024-03-01", 2, 3], ["2024-03-02", 2, 2], ["2024-03-03", 0, 0], ["2024-03-04", 1, 3], ["2024-03-05", 3, 3]]
--- check source | Counts players once each

--- check source absent | Does not type the middle days
2024-03-0[234]

+++ practice | Active in the last three days
--- task
The team wants a smoother number than DAU. For each day from `2024-07-03` to `2024-07-10`, count the different users who had any event in the **three days ending that day**: the day itself and the two days before it.

Return two columns, `day` (`'YYYY-MM-DD'`) and `active_3d`, sorted by `day`. A user active on several of those days counts once.
--- starter
SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS active_3d
FROM events
WHERE date(ts) >= '2024-07-03'
GROUP BY date(ts)
ORDER BY day;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-03'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day < '2024-07-10'
)
SELECT d.day, COUNT(DISTINCT e.user_id) AS active_3d
FROM days d
LEFT JOIN events e ON date(e.ts) BETWEEN date(d.day, '-2 days') AND d.day
GROUP BY d.day
ORDER BY d.day;
--- hint
Each calendar day now needs the events of a range of days, not one day. Join the events onto the calendar with a range condition instead of an equals sign.
--- hint
The range is `date(e.ts) BETWEEN date(d.day, '-2 days') AND d.day`. A user can match on up to three days, so count `DISTINCT e.user_id`.
--- check result | Eight days of three-day actives
ordered
[["2024-07-03", 4], ["2024-07-04", 4], ["2024-07-05", 5], ["2024-07-06", 5], ["2024-07-07", 6], ["2024-07-08", 5], ["2024-07-09", 5], ["2024-07-10", 4]]
--- check source | Counts users once each
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]
--- check source absent | Does not type the middle days
