+++ practice | Up or down from yesterday
--- task
For every day from `2024-07-01` to `2024-07-10`, return three columns:

1. `day`: `'YYYY-MM-DD'`.
2. `dau`: how many different users had any event that day (0 for a day with none).
3. `change`: `dau` minus the previous day's `dau`, or NULL on the first day.

The day before 5 July is 4 July, even though 4 July had no events. Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau,
       COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) - LAG(COUNT(DISTINCT user_id)) OVER (ORDER BY date(ts)) AS change
FROM events
GROUP BY date(ts)
ORDER BY day;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day < '2024-07-10'
), active AS (
  SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
  FROM events
  GROUP BY date(ts)
), daily AS (
  SELECT d.day, COALESCE(a.dau, 0) AS dau
  FROM days d
  LEFT JOIN active a ON a.day = d.day
)
SELECT day, dau, dau - LAG(dau) OVER (ORDER BY day) AS change
FROM daily
ORDER BY day;
--- hint
The starter compares 5 July with 3 July, because 4 July has no row. Fill the calendar first, with zeros, and only then look at the day before.
--- hint
Put the calendar-with-zeros in its own CTE. Then `dau - LAG(dau) OVER (ORDER BY day)`, from "LAG and LEAD: comparing with neighbors", gives the change, and NULL on the first row.
--- check result | Ten days; 4 July drops to 0 and 5 July climbs back
ordered
[["2024-07-01", 2, null], ["2024-07-02", 3, 1], ["2024-07-03", 3, 0], ["2024-07-04", 0, -3], ["2024-07-05", 3, 3], ["2024-07-06", 2, -1], ["2024-07-07", 2, 0], ["2024-07-08", 1, -1], ["2024-07-09", 2, 1], ["2024-07-10", 1, -1]]
--- check source | Compares with the day before using LAG
[Ll][Aa][Gg]\s*\(
--- check source | Turns the missing day into 0 with COALESCE
[Cc][Oo][Aa][Ll][Ee][Ss][Cc][Ee]\s*\(

+++ practice | New and returning, in a short window
--- task
Return one row for each day from `2024-07-05` to `2024-07-08`, with four columns:

1. `day`: `'YYYY-MM-DD'`.
2. `dau`: how many different users had any event that day.
3. `new_users`: how many of them had their first-ever event that day.
4. `returning_users`: `dau` minus `new_users`.

"First-ever" means over the whole `events` table, not only these four days: a user seen on 1 July is not new on 5 July. Every day in this window has events. Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
FROM events
WHERE date(ts) BETWEEN '2024-07-05' AND '2024-07-08'
GROUP BY date(ts)
ORDER BY day;
--- solution
WITH firsts AS (
  SELECT user_id, MIN(date(ts)) AS first_day
  FROM events
  GROUP BY user_id
), daily AS (
  SELECT date(e.ts) AS day,
         COUNT(DISTINCT e.user_id) AS dau,
         COUNT(DISTINCT CASE WHEN f.first_day = date(e.ts) THEN e.user_id END) AS new_users
  FROM events e
  JOIN firsts f ON f.user_id = e.user_id
  GROUP BY date(e.ts)
)
SELECT day, dau, new_users, dau - new_users AS returning_users
FROM daily
WHERE day BETWEEN '2024-07-05' AND '2024-07-08'
ORDER BY day;
--- hint
Work out each user's first day over **all** events first, before anything picks the window. Filter to 5–8 July only at the very end.
--- hint
Join each event to its user's first day. A user is new on a day when their `first_day` equals that day. Counting `DISTINCT` users inside a `CASE` counts each new user once, however many events they had.
--- hint
`COUNT(DISTINCT CASE WHEN f.first_day = date(e.ts) THEN e.user_id END)` gives `new_users`. Put the window's `WHERE` in the last `SELECT`, after the counting.
--- check result | Users 5 and 6 are the only new ones in the window
ordered
[["2024-07-05", 3, 1, 2], ["2024-07-06", 2, 0, 2], ["2024-07-07", 2, 1, 1], ["2024-07-08", 1, 0, 1]]
--- check source | Finds each user's first day with MIN
[Mm][Ii][Nn]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not pick users by id
user_id\s*(=|[Ii][Nn])\s*\(?\s*\d

+++ practice | One day too many
--- task
This query is meant to return DAU for every day from `2024-07-04` to `2024-07-10`, seven rows, with 0 for a day with no events:

```sql
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-04'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day <= '2024-07-10'
), active AS (
  SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
  FROM events
  GROUP BY date(ts)
)
SELECT d.day, COALESCE(a.dau, 0) AS dau
FROM days d
LEFT JOIN active a ON a.day = d.day
ORDER BY d.day;
```

It returns eight rows, ending with 11 July, a day the data does not even reach. Fix it so the calendar stops at 10 July. Keep the columns `day` and `dau`.
--- starter
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-04'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day <= '2024-07-10'
), active AS (
  SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
  FROM events
  GROUP BY date(ts)
)
SELECT d.day, COALESCE(a.dau, 0) AS dau
FROM days d
LEFT JOIN active a ON a.day = d.day
ORDER BY d.day;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-04'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day < '2024-07-10'
), active AS (
  SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
  FROM events
  GROUP BY date(ts)
)
SELECT d.day, COALESCE(a.dau, 0) AS dau
FROM days d
LEFT JOIN active a ON a.day = d.day
ORDER BY d.day;
--- hint
The `WHERE` in the recursive step decides whether to make **one more** row from the current one. When the current row is 10 July, should another row be made?
--- hint
With `<=`, the row for 10 July passes the test and makes 11 July. The step should run only while `day < '2024-07-10'`.
--- check result | Seven days, 4 to 10 July
ordered
[["2024-07-04", 0], ["2024-07-05", 3], ["2024-07-06", 2], ["2024-07-07", 2], ["2024-07-08", 1], ["2024-07-09", 2], ["2024-07-10", 1]]
--- check source absent | The calendar is not trimmed with a LIMIT
[Ll][Ii][Mm][Ii][Tt]
--- check source absent | The calendar does not stop at 11 July
2024-07-11

+++ practice | How sticky is the app?
--- task
A product team measures **stickiness**: on a typical day, what share of the week's users show up? For each day from `2024-07-07` to `2024-07-10`, return four columns:

1. `day`: `'YYYY-MM-DD'`.
2. `wau`: how many different users had any event in the **seven days ending that day** (the day and the six before it).
3. `avg_dau`: the average DAU over those same seven days, rounded to 2 decimals. A day with no events counts as a DAU of 0 and is part of the average.
4. `stickiness`: 100 × the average DAU ÷ `wau`, rounded to 1 decimal. Use the unrounded average.

Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
FROM events
GROUP BY date(ts)
ORDER BY day;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day < '2024-07-10'
), active AS (
  SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
  FROM events
  GROUP BY date(ts)
), daily AS (
  SELECT d.day, COALESCE(a.dau, 0) AS dau
  FROM days d
  LEFT JOIN active a ON a.day = d.day
), rolling AS (
  SELECT day, AVG(dau) OVER (ORDER BY day ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS avg_dau
  FROM daily
), weekly AS (
  SELECT d.day, COUNT(DISTINCT e.user_id) AS wau
  FROM days d
  JOIN events e ON date(e.ts) BETWEEN date(d.day, '-6 days') AND d.day
  GROUP BY d.day
)
SELECT r.day, w.wau,
       ROUND(r.avg_dau, 2) AS avg_dau,
       ROUND(100.0 * r.avg_dau / w.wau, 1) AS stickiness
FROM rolling r
JOIN weekly w ON w.day = r.day
WHERE r.day >= '2024-07-07'
ORDER BY r.day;
--- hint
Two separate measures over the same seven-day windows: WAU counts distinct users across the window, while the average DAU averages seven daily numbers. Build each in its own CTE, one row per day, and join them at the end.
--- hint
The average needs every day as a row, 4 July's 0 included, so start from a calendar with zeros. A seven-day moving average is a window frame, from "Window frames: moving averages": `AVG(dau) OVER (ORDER BY day ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)`.
--- hint
Start the calendar on 1 July so that 7 July's frame is full, and keep only days from 7 July in the last `SELECT`. WAU joins the events onto the calendar with `date(e.ts) BETWEEN date(d.day, '-6 days') AND d.day`.
--- check result | WAU stays at 6 while the average DAU falls
ordered
[["2024-07-07", 6, 2.14, 35.7], ["2024-07-08", 6, 2.0, 33.3], ["2024-07-09", 6, 1.86, 31.0], ["2024-07-10", 6, 1.57, 26.2]]
--- check source | Averages over a window frame
[Pp][Rr][Ee][Cc][Ee][Dd][Ii][Nn][Gg]
--- check source | Counts each user once in the week
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]

=== sqlp-07 | Analytics 3: a signup funnel with a deadline
--- teach
Last lesson you counted who used the app each day. Now the team asks where people give up. In "Problem solving: a conversion funnel" you built an ordered funnel for a shop. This one is the app's **signup funnel**, visit → signup → activate, with two new things: a **deadline** on the last step, and percentages measured against the **first** step.

### The table

The same `events` table as last lesson. Here are each user's first visit, signups and activation:

| user_id | first visit | signup | activate |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 07-01 09:00 | 07-01 09:05 | 07-02 08:00 |
| 2 | 07-01 10:00 | 07-02 11:00 | 07-05 11:00 |
| 3 | 07-02 09:00 | 07-02 09:02 | 07-03 15:00 |
| 4 | 07-03 18:00 | 07-03 18:10 | 07-10 10:00 |
| 5 | 07-05 11:00 | 07-05 11:30 | 07-09 09:00 |
| 6 | 07-07 22:00 | 07-07 21:00 | (none) |

All dates are July 2024. To **[[activate|activation]]** means to start really using the app after signing up.

### The picture

A shop hands you a coupon that says "valid for 72 hours". Use it on day two and you get the discount. Come back a week later and the coupon has run out, even though you did come back. The funnel's last step works like that coupon: activation only counts if it happens **within 72 hours of signing up**. A user who activates a week later is still a win, but not one the [[onboarding flow|onboarding]] can take credit for.

### Steps 1 and 2: as before

These work exactly as in "Problem solving: a conversion funnel". Each step is a CTE holding each user's time at that step:

- `s1`: each user's first `visit`, `MIN(ts)` grouped by user.
- `s2`: each user's first `signup` **after** their `s1` time. It joins `events` to `s1` and keeps `e.ts > s1.t`.

`s1` holds all six users. `s2` holds five. User 6 signed up at 21:00 and first visited at 22:00: they came in through an [[invite link|invite]], signed up, and only then looked around. In an ordered funnel their signup does not follow a visit, so they stop at step 1.

### Step 3 has two conditions

An activation counts only if it is:

1. **after** the signup, `e.ts > s2.t`, as before;
2. **no later than** 72 hours after it. That is new.

For the second condition you need the time between two moments. In "Sessionising events" you measured gaps with `unixepoch(ts)`, which turns a time into a whole number of seconds since [[1 January 1970|unix-time]]. Subtract two of them and you get the seconds in between, as an exact whole number.

72 hours is `72 * 3600` seconds, where `*` means "times" and 3600 is the number of seconds in one hour. That is 259,200 seconds. Here is every user's gap from signup to activation:

| user_id | seconds | hours | in time? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 82500 | about 22.9 | yes |
| 3 | 107880 | about 30.0 | yes |
| 2 | 259200 | exactly 72 | yes, on the line |
| 5 | 336600 | 93.5 | no |
| 4 | 575400 | about 159.8 | no |

So step 3 adds one line to the usual shape:

```sql
WHERE e.name = 'activate'
  AND e.ts > s2.t
  AND unixepoch(e.ts) - unixepoch(s2.t) <= 72 * 3600
```

`<=` means "less than or equal to". Users 1, 3 and 2 pass. Users 4 and 5 activated, but too late.

### Exactly on the line

User 2 signed up at 11:00 on 2 July and activated at 11:00 on 5 July: exactly 259,200 seconds. Is that "within 72 hours"? The team decided yes: "within" includes the boundary. So the test is `<=`, not `<`.

That is a decision, not a fact of nature. [[Write it down|boundary]] next to the report, so everyone reads the number the same way. Measuring in whole seconds is what lets the boundary be exact. A difference of `julianday` values is a fraction of a day, and as "Sessionising events" warned, turning it back into hours can land a hair off.

### Stacking the counts

As before, `UNION ALL` stacks one small row per step, each with its number, name and count:

| step | name | users |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | visit | 6 |
| 2 | signup | 5 |
| 3 | activate | 3 |

### Percent of the first step

In the shop funnel each step was measured against the step before, using `LAG`. This time the team wants each step against **step 1**: the share of all visitors who got this far. That is how an overall funnel chart reads.

`FIRST_VALUE(x)` is a window function that gives `x` from the **first** row of the window, on every row. With `OVER (ORDER BY step)`, the first row is step 1:

```sql
SELECT step, name, users,
       FIRST_VALUE(users) OVER (ORDER BY step) AS first_users,
       LAG(users) OVER (ORDER BY step) AS prev_users
FROM steps;
```

| step | name | users | first_users | prev_users |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | visit | 6 | 6 | NULL |
| 2 | signup | 5 | 6 | 6 |
| 3 | activate | 3 | 6 | 5 |

`first_users` is 6 on every row, so the percentage is `ROUND(100.0 * users / FIRST_VALUE(users) OVER (ORDER BY step), 1)`. Step 1 is 100.0, since it is measured against itself. Step 2 is 100.0 × 5 ÷ 6 = 83.3. Step 3 is 100.0 × 3 ÷ 6 = 50.0. Half the visitors [[activated in time|funnel-shape]].

**Watch out:** with `<` instead of `<=`, user 2 drops out and step 3 shows 2 users, 33.3%. Nothing errors, and the number looks believable. Boundaries are where funnel numbers quietly go wrong, so test the user who sits exactly on the line, as this lesson did with user 2.

::: context activation What "activation" means
Signing up only creates an account. **Activation** is the moment a new user first does the thing the app is for: sends a first message, uploads a first photo, finishes a first lesson. Product teams choose one event to stand for it, here `activate`. They care because users who activate early tend to stay, and users who sign up and do nothing tend to leave. That is why the signup funnel ends at activation, not at signup.
:::

::: context onboarding The onboarding flow
**Onboarding** is everything an app does to help a brand-new user get going: the welcome screens, the tips, the first-day emails. Its job is to turn a signup into an activation, fast. The 72-hour deadline measures exactly that. A user who comes back on their own after a week probably was not helped by the welcome screens, so counting them would make onboarding look better than it is.
:::

::: context invite An invite link
An **invite link** is a link one user sends another, often opening a signup page straight away. So the invited person signs up before they have "visited" in the funnel's sense. Nothing is broken, the same as the deep link in "Problem solving: a conversion funnel". An ordered funnel leaves them at step 1 because its question is how people move through the steps in order. A team that cares about invited users often builds a separate funnel for them.
:::

::: context unix-time Seconds since 1970
**Unix time** counts seconds from midnight UTC at the start of 1 January 1970, a date picked by the makers of the Unix operating system. `unixepoch('1970-01-01 00:00:00')` is 0, and `unixepoch('2024-07-02 11:00:00')` is 1719918000. Because it is one whole number, the difference of two moments is a plain subtraction, with no months or leap days to think about. Computers everywhere use it, and spacecraft often stamp telemetry with a similar count of seconds from their own chosen starting moment.
:::

::: context boundary Write the rule down
"Within 72 hours" can be read two ways, and both are reasonable. Engineers settle it once and write it down, in a comment beside the query or on the dashboard: "activation counts if it is at most 259,200 seconds after signup". Aerospace does the same with limits. A sensor reading "within limits" must say whether the limit itself counts, because a reading that sits exactly on it will happen sooner or later, and two teams reading the rule differently will disagree about whether to abort.
:::

::: context funnel-shape The funnel against its first step
Drawn against step 1, each bar shows the share of all visitors still in the funnel. The biggest drop here is from signup to activation: 5 users signed up, but only 3 activated in time. That is the step a team would look at first.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 140" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="80" y="20" width="192" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="80" y="56" width="160" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="80" y="92" width="96" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="272" y1="14" x2="272" y2="124" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1" stroke-dasharray="3 3"/>
  <text x="72" y="37" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="end">visit</text>
  <text x="72" y="73" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="end">signup</text>
  <text x="72" y="109" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="end">activate</text>
  <text x="280" y="37" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">6 · 100%</text>
  <text x="248" y="73" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">5 · 83.3%</text>
  <text x="184" y="109" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">3 · 50.0%</text>
  <text x="180" y="136" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">each bar measured against step 1 (dashed line)</text>
```

:::
--- task
Write one query that returns the funnel visit → signup → activate, one row per step, with these columns:

1. `step`: 1, 2 or 3;
2. `name`: `'visit'`, `'signup'` or `'activate'`;
3. `users`: how many users reached that step;
4. `pct`: `users` as a percentage of step 1's `users`, rounded to 1 decimal place.

A user reaches step 1 at their first `visit`, and step 2 at their first `signup` after that. They reach step 3 at their first `activate` that is after that signup **and** no more than 72 hours after it (exactly 72 hours counts). Sort by `step`.
--- starter
SELECT name, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS users
FROM events
GROUP BY name;
--- solution
WITH s1 AS (
  SELECT user_id, MIN(ts) AS t FROM events WHERE name = 'visit' GROUP BY user_id
), s2 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s1 ON s1.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'signup' AND e.ts > s1.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id
), s3 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s2 ON s2.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'activate' AND e.ts > s2.t
    AND unixepoch(e.ts) - unixepoch(s2.t) <= 72 * 3600
  GROUP BY e.user_id
), steps AS (
  SELECT 1 AS step, 'visit' AS name, COUNT(*) AS users FROM s1
  UNION ALL SELECT 2, 'signup', COUNT(*) FROM s2
  UNION ALL SELECT 3, 'activate', COUNT(*) FROM s3
)
SELECT step, name, users,
       ROUND(100.0 * users / FIRST_VALUE(users) OVER (ORDER BY step), 1) AS pct
FROM steps
ORDER BY step;
--- hint
One CTE per step, as in "Problem solving: a conversion funnel": `s1`, `s2` and `s3`. Each joins the one before on `user_id` and takes `MIN(e.ts)` of the qualifying events after the previous step's time.
--- hint
`s3` looks for `activate` events and has one extra condition next to `e.ts > s2.t`: `unixepoch(e.ts) - unixepoch(s2.t) <= 72 * 3600`.
--- hint
Stack the three counts in a CTE `steps` with `UNION ALL` (`SELECT 1 AS step, 'visit' AS name, COUNT(*) AS users FROM s1`, and so on). Then `ROUND(100.0 * users / FIRST_VALUE(users) OVER (ORDER BY step), 1) AS pct`. Check: 6, 5 and 3 users.
--- check result | 6 visitors, 5 signed up after visiting, 3 activated in time
ordered
[[1, "visit", 6, 100.0], [2, "signup", 5, 83.3], [3, "activate", 3, 50.0]]

+++ practice | Signed up within a day
--- task
Write a two-step funnel over `events`: visit → signup, with a deadline on the signup. Return one row per step with four columns:

1. `step`: 1 or 2.
2. `name`: `'visit'` or `'signup'`.
3. `users`: how many users reached that step.
4. `pct`: `users` as a percentage of step 1's `users`, rounded to 1 decimal.

A user reaches step 1 at their first `visit`. They reach step 2 at their first `signup` after that visit, but only if it is **no more than 24 hours** after the visit (exactly 24 hours counts). Measure the gap in whole seconds with `unixepoch`. Sort by `step`.
--- starter
SELECT 1 AS step, 'visit' AS name, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS users
FROM events
WHERE name = 'visit';
--- solution
WITH s1 AS (
  SELECT user_id, MIN(ts) AS t FROM events WHERE name = 'visit' GROUP BY user_id
), s2 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s1 ON s1.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'signup' AND e.ts > s1.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id, s1.t
  HAVING unixepoch(MIN(e.ts)) - unixepoch(s1.t) <= 24 * 3600
), steps AS (
  SELECT 1 AS step, 'visit' AS name, COUNT(*) AS users FROM s1
  UNION ALL SELECT 2, 'signup', COUNT(*) FROM s2
)
SELECT step, name, users,
       ROUND(100.0 * users / FIRST_VALUE(users) OVER (ORDER BY step), 1) AS pct
FROM steps
ORDER BY step;
--- hint
Two step CTEs, as in the lesson. The deadline is measured from the first visit to the **first** signup after it, so find that signup first and then test its gap.
--- hint
In `s2`, group by the user and their visit time, `GROUP BY e.user_id, s1.t`, to get `MIN(e.ts)`, the first signup after the visit. Then keep the group only if `unixepoch(MIN(e.ts)) - unixepoch(s1.t) <= 24 * 3600`. A test on a group's value goes in `HAVING`.
--- check result | 6 visitors; user 2 took 25 hours, so 4 signed up in time
ordered
[[1, "visit", 6, 100.0], [2, "signup", 4, 66.7]]
--- check source | Measures the gap in seconds
unixepoch\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type the counts
\b[46]\s+AS\s+users

+++ practice | How long each user took to activate
--- task
Instead of counts, the team wants to see each user. Return one row for every user who signed up after their first visit (step 2 of the lesson's funnel), with five columns:

1. `user_id`.
2. `signup_at`: the time of their first `signup` after their first `visit`.
3. `activate_at`: the time of their first `activate` after that signup, whenever it happened, or NULL if they never activated.
4. `hours`: the hours from `signup_at` to `activate_at`, rounded to 1 decimal (NULL if no activation). Work it out from `unixepoch` seconds divided by `3600.0`.
5. `in_time`: 1 if the activation came within 72 hours of the signup (exactly 72 counts), otherwise 0.

Sort by `user_id`.
--- starter
SELECT user_id, MIN(ts) AS signup_at
FROM events
WHERE name = 'signup'
GROUP BY user_id
ORDER BY user_id;
--- solution
WITH s1 AS (
  SELECT user_id, MIN(ts) AS t FROM events WHERE name = 'visit' GROUP BY user_id
), s2 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s1 ON s1.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'signup' AND e.ts > s1.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id
), s3 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s2 ON s2.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'activate' AND e.ts > s2.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id
)
SELECT s2.user_id,
       s2.t AS signup_at,
       s3.t AS activate_at,
       ROUND((unixepoch(s3.t) - unixepoch(s2.t)) / 3600.0, 1) AS hours,
       COALESCE(unixepoch(s3.t) - unixepoch(s2.t) <= 72 * 3600, 0) AS in_time
FROM s2
LEFT JOIN s3 ON s3.user_id = s2.user_id
ORDER BY s2.user_id;
--- hint
Build `s1`, `s2` and `s3` as in the lesson, but leave the 72-hour test out of `s3`: this report shows every activation and marks the late ones.
--- hint
Start the final `SELECT` from `s2` and `LEFT JOIN s3`, so a user who never activated still gets a row. A comparison such as `gap <= 72 * 3600` is itself a value: 1 when true, 0 when false.
--- hint
`hours` is `ROUND((unixepoch(s3.t) - unixepoch(s2.t)) / 3600.0, 1)`. For `in_time`, wrap the comparison in `COALESCE(…, 0)`, so a missing activation gives 0 instead of NULL.
--- check result | Five users; user 2 is exactly on the line, users 4 and 5 too late
ordered
[[1, "2024-07-01 09:05:00", "2024-07-02 08:00:00", 22.9, 1], [2, "2024-07-02 11:00:00", "2024-07-05 11:00:00", 72.0, 1], [3, "2024-07-02 09:02:00", "2024-07-03 15:00:00", 30.0, 1], [4, "2024-07-03 18:10:00", "2024-07-10 10:00:00", 159.8, 0], [5, "2024-07-05 11:30:00", "2024-07-09 09:00:00", 93.5, 0]]
--- check source | Measures the gap in seconds
unixepoch\s*\(
--- check source | Keeps users with no activation using a LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]

+++ practice | The funnel for each first-visit day
--- task
Break the lesson's funnel down by the day each user first visited. Return one row per day that had first visits, with four columns:

1. `day`: the date of the users' first `visit`, `'YYYY-MM-DD'`.
2. `visitors`: how many users first visited that day.
3. `signups`: how many of them reached step 2 (a `signup` after their first visit).
4. `activations`: how many of them reached step 3 (an `activate` after that signup, within 72 hours, exactly 72 counting).

Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS visitors
FROM events
WHERE name = 'visit'
GROUP BY date(ts)
ORDER BY day;
--- solution
WITH s1 AS (
  SELECT user_id, MIN(ts) AS t FROM events WHERE name = 'visit' GROUP BY user_id
), s2 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s1 ON s1.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'signup' AND e.ts > s1.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id
), s3 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s2 ON s2.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'activate' AND e.ts > s2.t
    AND unixepoch(e.ts) - unixepoch(s2.t) <= 72 * 3600
  GROUP BY e.user_id
)
SELECT date(s1.t) AS day,
       COUNT(*) AS visitors,
       COUNT(s2.user_id) AS signups,
       COUNT(s3.user_id) AS activations
FROM s1
LEFT JOIN s2 ON s2.user_id = s1.user_id
LEFT JOIN s3 ON s3.user_id = s1.user_id
GROUP BY date(s1.t)
ORDER BY day;
--- hint
Keep the three step CTEs from the lesson. Instead of stacking three counts with `UNION ALL`, put each user's steps side by side: start from `s1` and `LEFT JOIN` `s2` and `s3` on `user_id`.
--- hint
Each step CTE has one row per user, so the joins cannot fan out. Group by `date(s1.t)` and count a column from each step: `COUNT(s2.user_id)` counts only the users who reached step 2.
--- check result | Five first-visit days; only 1 July's visitors all activated in time

--- task
This problem has its own `events` table (`event_id`, `user_id`, `name`, `ts`), built so that each user sits on an edge a sloppy funnel gets wrong: an activation exactly on the deadline and one second past it, an activation before the signup, one in the same second as the signup, a user who signed up twice, and more.

Return the funnel visit → signup → activate with the columns `step`, `name`, `users` and `pct` (percentage of step 1, rounded to 1 decimal), sorted by `step`. The rules:

1. Step 1: the user's first `visit`.
2. Step 2: their first `signup` strictly after that visit.
3. Step 3: an `activate` strictly after that first signup, and no more than **48 hours** after it (exactly 48 hours counts). A later signup does not restart the clock.
--- starter
SELECT name, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS users
FROM events
GROUP BY name;
--- solution
WITH s1 AS (
  SELECT user_id, MIN(ts) AS t FROM events WHERE name = 'visit' GROUP BY user_id
), s2 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s1 ON s1.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'signup' AND e.ts > s1.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id
), s3 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s2 ON s2.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'activate' AND e.ts > s2.t
    AND unixepoch(e.ts) - unixepoch(s2.t) <= 48 * 3600
  GROUP BY e.user_id
), steps AS (
  SELECT 1 AS step, 'visit' AS name, COUNT(*) AS users FROM s1
  UNION ALL SELECT 2, 'signup', COUNT(*) FROM s2
  UNION ALL SELECT 3, 'activate', COUNT(*) FROM s3
)
SELECT step, name, users,
       ROUND(100.0 * users / FIRST_VALUE(users) OVER (ORDER BY step), 1) AS pct
FROM steps
ORDER BY step;
--- hint
Before writing anything, go through users 1 to 8 by hand and decide which steps each one reaches. Then check that your query agrees.
--- hint
"Strictly after" is `>`, "no more than" is `<=`, and the clock starts at `s2.t`, the first signup after the visit, which is a `MIN`. User 5's second signup must not be used.

+++ practice | A funnel with two deadlines
--- task
The team now puts a deadline on **both** later steps. Return the funnel visit → signup → activate with five columns, sorted by `step`:

1. `step`, 2. `name`, 3. `users`, as in the lesson;
4. `pct_first`: `users` as a percentage of step 1, rounded to 1 decimal;
5. `pct_prev`: `users` as a percentage of the step before, rounded to 1 decimal, and NULL for step 1.

The rules: step 1 is the user's first `visit`. Step 2 is their first `signup` after that visit, counted only if it is within **24 hours** of the visit. Step 3 is their first `activate` after that signup, counted only if it is within **72 hours** of the signup. Both limits include the boundary.
--- starter
SELECT 1 AS step, 'visit' AS name, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS users
FROM events
WHERE name = 'visit';
--- solution
WITH s1 AS (
  SELECT user_id, MIN(ts) AS t FROM events WHERE name = 'visit' GROUP BY user_id
), s2 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s1 ON s1.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'signup' AND e.ts > s1.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id, s1.t
  HAVING unixepoch(MIN(e.ts)) - unixepoch(s1.t) <= 24 * 3600
), s3 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s2 ON s2.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'activate' AND e.ts > s2.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id, s2.t
  HAVING unixepoch(MIN(e.ts)) - unixepoch(s2.t) <= 72 * 3600
), steps AS (
  SELECT 1 AS step, 'visit' AS name, COUNT(*) AS users FROM s1
  UNION ALL SELECT 2, 'signup', COUNT(*) FROM s2
  UNION ALL SELECT 3, 'activate', COUNT(*) FROM s3
)
SELECT step, name, users,
       ROUND(100.0 * users / FIRST_VALUE(users) OVER (ORDER BY step), 1) AS pct_first,
       ROUND(100.0 * users / LAG(users) OVER (ORDER BY step), 1) AS pct_prev
FROM steps
ORDER BY step;
--- hint
Each deadline is measured from the first qualifying event: the first signup after the visit, and the first activation after that signup. Find the first one with `MIN`, then test its gap.
--- hint
Group each step by user and test the gap in `HAVING`: `unixepoch(MIN(e.ts)) - unixepoch(s1.t) <= 24 * 3600` for step 2, and the same against `s2.t` with `72 * 3600` for step 3.
--- hint
The two percentages differ only in the window: `FIRST_VALUE(users) OVER (ORDER BY step)` for the first step, `LAG(users) OVER (ORDER BY step)` for the step before. `LAG` is NULL on step 1, so the division is NULL there too.
--- check result | 6, then 4 within a day, then 2 within 72 hours
ordered
[[1, "visit", 6, 100.0, null], [2, "signup", 4, 66.7, 66.7], [3, "activate", 2, 33.3, 50.0]]
--- check source | Compares with the step before using LAG
[Ll][Aa][Gg]\s*\(
--- check source | Measures the gaps in seconds
unixepoch\s*\(

=== sqlp-08 | Analytics 4: day-N retention
--- teach
Last lesson you followed users from their first visit to activation. The last analytics question is the one that decides whether an app survives: **do people come back?** In "Problem solving: cohort retention" you answered it by calendar month. This lesson asks it day by day, and meets a trap that the monthly report did not have to face.

### The table

The same `events` table. Each user's signup day, and every day they did anything:

| user_id | signup day | active on (July 2024) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 07-01 | 01, 02, 03, 05, 08 |
| 2 | 07-02 | 01, 02, 05, 07 |
| 3 | 07-02 | 02, 03, 06, 09 |
| 4 | 07-03 | 03, 06, 10 |
| 5 | 07-05 | 05, 09 |
| 6 | 07-07 | 07 |

The data ends on 10 July.

### The picture

A gym signs up a new member. The owner wonders: did they come back the next day? Two days later? A week later? Now ask that about every new member at once, and count what share came back on each of those days.

### Day N

A user's **day 0** is the calendar day they signed up. **Day N** is the Nth calendar day after it. User 1 signed up on 1 July, so their day 1 is 2 July and their day 7 is 8 July.

**Day-N retention** asks, for N = 1, 2, … 7: of the users who signed up, what share were active on their day N? The day counts by the [[calendar, not by 24-hour periods|calendar-day]].

### Building it from pieces

Every piece is something you have used before. Each becomes one CTE.

**Piece 1: each user's signup day.** The date of their `signup` event. Taking `MIN(ts)` makes sure a user who somehow signed up twice gets one day:

```sql
signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
)
```

It gives the six users and the signup days in the table above.

**Piece 2: each user's active days.** `SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts)` gives one row per user per day they did anything. User 1 had two events on 1 July, and `DISTINCT` keeps that day once.

**Piece 3: the numbers 1 to 7.** A recursive CTE that counts, as in "Recursive CTEs: a query that counts":

```sql
n(day) AS (
  SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT day + 1 FROM n WHERE day < 7
)
```

Each value in `n` is one N.

### Every user with every N

The report needs a yes-or-no for **every pair** of a user and an N. In "Design: choosing the joins for a report" you met `CROSS JOIN`, which pairs every row of one table with every row of the other. `n CROSS JOIN signups` gives 7 × 6 = 42 pairs.

### The date of day N

For each pair you need the date of that user's day N. `date(d0, '+3 days')` moves a date three days forward, as in "Date arithmetic and date ranges". But here the 3 is a number from `n`, not typed text. `||`, from "Building text: joining, replacing and LIKE", glues pieces of text together, so you can build the modifier:

```sql
SELECT '+' || 3 || ' days' AS modifier,
       date('2024-07-01', '+' || 3 || ' days') AS day_3;
```

| modifier | day_3 |
| --- | --- |
| +3 days | 2024-07-04 |

In the query, `date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days')` is user `s`'s day N.

### Was the user active that day?

`EXISTS (…)` is 1 when its subquery finds a row and 0 when it does not, as in "Problem solving: cohort retention". Call the flag `hit`:

```sql
EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
        WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id
          AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days')) AS hit
```

Then `SUM(hit)` counts the yeses.

### Users who have not had day N yet

This is the trap. User 6 signed up on 7 July, so their day 7 is 14 July. The data ends on 10 July. Day 7 **has not happened yet** for user 6, so nobody knows whether they will come back then.

Counting them as "not retained" on day 7 treats "not yet known" as "no". Every recent signup would drag the later days down, for no real reason. So only users whose day N is on or before the last day of data are **eligible** for day N. The eligible users are the denominator: the number you divide by. Statisticians call data that stops before the answer arrives [[censored|censored]].

Find the last day of data from the data itself, not by typing `'2024-07-10'` into the query. [[A typed date goes stale|last-day]] the moment new events arrive.

```sql
last AS (SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events)
```

`last` has exactly one row. `CROSS JOIN last` adds its `d` column to every pair, without making more rows, because one row times one row is still one row. Then the pairs can keep only eligible ones:

```sql
WHERE date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days') <= last.d
```

Here are the pairs for day 1 and day 7, after that filter:

| day | user_id | day N date | hit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1 | 2024-07-02 | 1 |
| 1 | 2 | 2024-07-03 | 0 |
| 1 | 3 | 2024-07-03 | 1 |
| 1 | 4 | 2024-07-04 | 0 |
| 1 | 5 | 2024-07-06 | 0 |
| 1 | 6 | 2024-07-08 | 0 |
| 7 | 1 | 2024-07-08 | 1 |
| 7 | 2 | 2024-07-09 | 0 |
| 7 | 3 | 2024-07-09 | 1 |
| 7 | 4 | 2024-07-10 | 1 |

Everyone has had day 1. Only users 1 to 4 have had day 7; users 5 and 6 are left out of it.

### One row per N

Group the pairs by `day`:

- `COUNT(*)` counts the eligible users;
- `SUM(hit)` counts the retained ones;
- `ROUND(100.0 * SUM(hit) / COUNT(*), 1)` is the percentage.

Day 7 is 3 retained of 4 eligible: 75.0. Put all seven days together and you have the app's [[retention curve|retention-curve]].

**Watch out:** leave out the eligibility filter and day 7 reads 3 of 6, 50.0, instead of 75.0. Users 5 and 6 are counted as "did not come back" on a day that is still in the future. Days 4, 5 and 7 all come out too low, and the newest signups make the app look worse than it is.

::: context calendar-day Calendar days, not 24 hours
User 6 signed up at 21:00 on 7 July. Their day 1 is all of 8 July, which starts only three hours later. A user who signed up at 06:00 gets a day 1 that starts eighteen hours later. Counting in 24-hour periods instead would treat them equally, but it is harder to read on a chart, and "came back the next day" is what people mean. Like "calendar months" in "Problem solving: cohort retention", it is a choice, and the report should say which one it made.
:::

::: context censored Answers that have not arrived
When the data stops before the answer is known, statisticians call the data **censored**: it was cut off, not answered "no". Doctors meet it in medical trials, where a patient who joined last month cannot yet say how they are doing after a year. Engineers meet it when testing how long parts last: a pump still running when the test ends has not failed, it has only not failed *yet*. The fix is always the same as here: count someone only for the times you could actually observe.
:::

::: context last-day Let the data say when it ends
A date typed into a query is right on the day it is written. Tomorrow more events arrive, the real last day moves to 11 July, and the typed `'2024-07-10'` quietly leaves out a whole day of eligible users. `MAX(date(ts))` moves with the data, so the same query stays right every day it runs. Scheduled reports, the kind that rebuild a dashboard every morning, depend on this.
:::

::: context retention-curve The retention curve
Drawn as bars, the seven percentages make this app's day-N retention curve. The small numbers underneath are the eligible users for each day. With only six users the curve jumps about: one person more or less changes a bar by about 17 to 25 percentage points. Real apps have thousands of users per day, and their curves are smooth. A curve that levels off instead of falling to zero means some people have found a reason to stay.

```svg

  <text x="180" y="16" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">retained, as a percentage of eligible users</text>

  <text x="180" y="184" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">fewer users are eligible as N grows</text>
```

:::
--- task
Write one query that returns one row for each N from 1 to 7, with these columns:

1. `day`: N;
2. `eligible`: how many signed-up users have a day N on or before the last day in `events`;
3. `retained`: how many of those eligible users had any event on exactly their day N;
4. `pct`: `retained` as a percentage of `eligible`, rounded to 1 decimal place.

A user's day 0 is the date of their `signup` event, and day N is the Nth calendar day after it. Find the last day from the data with `MAX(date(ts))`, not by typing it. Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT user_id, date(ts) AS signup_day
FROM events
WHERE name = 'signup';
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE n(day) AS (
  SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT day + 1 FROM n WHERE day < 7
), signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), active AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts) AS d FROM events
), last AS (
  SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events
), pairs AS (
  SELECT n.day, s.user_id,
         EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
                 WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days')) AS hit
  FROM n
  CROSS JOIN signups s
  CROSS JOIN last
  WHERE date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days') <= last.d
)
SELECT day, COUNT(*) AS eligible, SUM(hit) AS retained,
       ROUND(100.0 * SUM(hit) / COUNT(*), 1) AS pct
FROM pairs
GROUP BY day
ORDER BY day;
--- hint
Four CTEs first: `n`, the numbers 1 to 7; `signups`, each user's signup day `d0`; `active`, each user's distinct active days; and `last`, the one-row last day of data, `MAX(date(ts))`.
--- hint
A fifth CTE, `pairs`: `FROM n CROSS JOIN signups s CROSS JOIN last`, keeping only pairs where `date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days') <= last.d`. Flag each pair with `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = …that same date…) AS hit`.
--- hint
Finish with `SELECT day, COUNT(*) AS eligible, SUM(hit) AS retained, ROUND(100.0 * SUM(hit) / COUNT(*), 1) AS pct FROM pairs GROUP BY day ORDER BY day`. Check day 7: 4 eligible, 3 retained, 75.0.
--- check result | Seven days of retention, with shrinking eligibility
ordered
[[1, 6, 2, 33.3], [2, 6, 1, 16.7], [3, 6, 2, 33.3], [4, 5, 3, 60.0], [5, 5, 1, 20.0], [6, 4, 0, 0.0], [7, 4, 3, 75.0]]
--- check source absent | Finds the last day with MAX(date(ts)), not by typing a date
'\d{4}-\d\d-\d\d'

+++ practice | Back the next day, by signup day
--- task
Group the users by the day they signed up (the date of their `signup` event) and ask one question of each group: how many came back the very next calendar day?

Return one row per signup day, with three columns:

1. `signup_day`: `'YYYY-MM-DD'`.
2. `signups`: how many users signed up that day.
3. `back_next_day`: how many of them had any event on the day after their signup day.

Every signup day here is at least one day before the end of the data, so every user has had their next day. Sort by `signup_day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(ts) AS signup_day, COUNT(*) AS signups
FROM events
WHERE name = 'signup'
GROUP BY date(ts)
ORDER BY signup_day;
--- solution
WITH signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), active AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts) AS d FROM events
)
SELECT s.d0 AS signup_day,
       COUNT(*) AS signups,
       SUM(EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
                   WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+1 day'))) AS back_next_day
FROM signups s
GROUP BY s.d0
ORDER BY s.d0;
--- hint
Two pieces from the lesson: each user's signup day `d0`, and each user's distinct active days. Then group the signups by `d0`.
--- hint
For each signed-up user, `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+1 day'))` is 1 or 0. Adding those up with `SUM(…)` inside the grouped query counts the users who came back.
--- check result | Five signup days; users 1 and 3 came back the next day
ordered
[["2024-07-01", 1, 1], ["2024-07-02", 2, 1], ["2024-07-03", 1, 0], ["2024-07-05", 1, 0], ["2024-07-07", 1, 0]]
--- check source | Checks for a next-day event with EXISTS
[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type any signup day
2024-07-0\d

+++ practice | Back on day N or any day after
--- task
Day-N retention asks about one exact day. **Rolling retention** is kinder: did the user come back on day N **or any later day** in the data?

For each N from 1 to 5, return four columns, sorted by `day`:

1. `day`: N.
2. `eligible`: how many signed-up users have a day N on or before the last day in `events` (find it with `MAX(date(ts))`, as in the lesson).
3. `retained`: how many of those eligible users had any event on their day N or later.
4. `pct`: `retained` as a percentage of `eligible`, rounded to 1 decimal.

A user's day 0 is the date of their `signup` event.
--- starter
WITH RECURSIVE n(day) AS (
  SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT day + 1 FROM n WHERE day < 5
)
SELECT day FROM n;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE n(day) AS (
  SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT day + 1 FROM n WHERE day < 5
), signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), active AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts) AS d FROM events
), last AS (
  SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events
), pairs AS (
  SELECT n.day,
         EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
                 WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d >= date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days')) AS hit
  FROM n
  CROSS JOIN signups s
  CROSS JOIN last
  WHERE date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days') <= last.d
)
SELECT day, COUNT(*) AS eligible, SUM(hit) AS retained,
       ROUND(100.0 * SUM(hit) / COUNT(*), 1) AS pct
FROM pairs
GROUP BY day
ORDER BY day;
--- hint
Only one test changes from the lesson's query: "on exactly day N" becomes "on day N or later". Everything else, eligibility included, stays.
--- hint
In the `EXISTS`, compare with `>=`: `a.d >= date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days')`. Dates in `'YYYY-MM-DD'` form compare in time order.
--- check result | Rolling retention stays high; user 5 has not come back by day 5
ordered
[[1, 6, 5, 83.3], [2, 6, 5, 83.3], [3, 6, 5, 83.3], [4, 5, 5, 100.0], [5, 5, 4, 80.0]]
--- check source | Checks for later activity with EXISTS
[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Finds the last day from the data, not by typing it
'\d{4}-\d\d-\d\d'

+++ practice | Each user's day 1, day 3 and day 7
--- task
The support team wants to see individual users. Return one row per signed-up user with five columns:

1. `user_id`.
2. `d0`: their signup day, `'YYYY-MM-DD'`.
3. `day1`, 4. `day3`, 5. `day7`: for day 1, day 3 and day 7 after `d0`, a 1 if the user had any event on that exact day, a 0 if they did not, and **NULL** if that day is after the last day in `events`, because it has not happened yet.

Find the last day with `MAX(date(ts))`. Sort by `user_id`.
--- starter
SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0
FROM events
WHERE name = 'signup'
GROUP BY user_id
ORDER BY user_id;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE n(day) AS (
  SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT day + 1 FROM n WHERE day < 7
), signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), active AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts) AS d FROM events
), last AS (
  SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events
), pairs AS (
  SELECT s.user_id, s.d0, n.day,
         CASE WHEN date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days') > last.d THEN NULL
              ELSE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
                           WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days'))
         END AS hit
  FROM n
  CROSS JOIN signups s
  CROSS JOIN last
)
SELECT user_id, d0,
       MAX(CASE WHEN day = 1 THEN hit END) AS day1,
       MAX(CASE WHEN day = 3 THEN hit END) AS day3,
       MAX(CASE WHEN day = 7 THEN hit END) AS day7
FROM pairs
GROUP BY user_id, d0
ORDER BY user_id;
--- hint
Build every pair of a user and an N, as in the lesson, but instead of dropping the pairs that have not happened yet, give them a NULL. A `CASE` can do that: NULL when the day N date is after the last day, and the `EXISTS` otherwise.
--- hint
Then turn the pairs into one row per user with conditional aggregation, from "Pivoting with conditional aggregation": `MAX(CASE WHEN day = 1 THEN hit END) AS day1`, and the same for 3 and 7. `MAX` skips NULLs, and when every value is NULL it gives NULL.
--- check result | Users 5 and 6 have not reached day 7 yet
ordered
[[1, "2024-07-01", 1, 0, 1], [2, "2024-07-02", 0, 1, 0], [3, "2024-07-02", 1, 0, 1], [4, "2024-07-03", 0, 1, 1], [5, "2024-07-05", 0, 0, null], [6, "2024-07-07", 0, 0, null]]
--- check source | Checks each day with EXISTS
[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Finds the last day from the data, not by typing it
'\d{4}-\d\d-\d\d'

+++ practice | Midnight, second signups and silent users
--- schema
CREATE TABLE events (
  event_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  ts TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO events (event_id, user_id, name, ts) VALUES
  ('g01', 1, 'signup', '2024-08-01 23:59:00'),
  ('g02', 1, 'visit',  '2024-08-02 00:01:00'),
  ('g03', 1, 'visit',  '2024-08-04 12:00:00'),
  ('g04', 2, 'signup', '2024-08-01 10:00:00'),
  ('g05', 2, 'signup', '2024-08-03 09:00:00'),
  ('g06', 3, 'visit',  '2024-08-02 10:00:00'),
  ('g07', 4, 'signup', '2024-08-04 10:00:00'),
  ('g08', 5, 'signup', '2024-08-02 08:00:00'),
  ('g09', 5, 'visit',  '2024-08-02 20:00:00'),
  ('g10', 5, 'visit',  '2024-08-05 09:00:00');
--- task
This problem has its own `events` table (`event_id`, `user_id`, `name`, `ts`), full of edge cases: a signup two minutes before midnight, a user who signed up twice, a user who never signed up, a user who signed up the day before the data ends, and a user whose only other visit is on their signup day.

Return day-N retention for N = 1, 2 and 3, with the lesson's columns `day`, `eligible`, `retained` and `pct` (1 decimal), sorted by `day`. The rules are the lesson's:

- a user's day 0 is the date of their **first** `signup`; users with no signup are not counted;
- day N is the Nth calendar day after day 0, and any event that day counts, a second signup included;
- only users whose day N is on or before the last day in `events` are eligible. Find that day with `MAX(date(ts))`.
--- starter
SELECT user_id, date(MAX(ts)) AS d0
FROM events
WHERE name = 'signup'
GROUP BY user_id;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE n(day) AS (
  SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT day + 1 FROM n WHERE day < 3
), signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), active AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts) AS d FROM events
), last AS (
  SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events
), pairs AS (
  SELECT n.day,
         EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
                 WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days')) AS hit
  FROM n
  CROSS JOIN signups s
  CROSS JOIN last
  WHERE date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days') <= last.d
)
SELECT day, COUNT(*) AS eligible, SUM(hit) AS retained,
       ROUND(100.0 * SUM(hit) / COUNT(*), 1) AS pct
FROM pairs
GROUP BY day
ORDER BY day;
--- hint
Work the answer out by hand first. For each user, write down day 0, the dates of days 1 to 3, which of those dates are on or before the last day, and whether the user did anything on each.
--- hint
The starter takes the **last** signup as day 0, which moves user 2's clock. `MIN` gives the first. Every date comparison uses `date(...)`, so the visit at 00:01 lands on the next calendar day.
--- check result | Day 1: 1 of 4; day 2: 1 of 3; day 3: 2 of 3
ordered
[[1, 4, 1, 25.0], [2, 3, 1, 33.3], [3, 3, 2, 66.7]]
--- check source | Finds the last day with MAX
[Mm][Aa][Xx]\s*\(\s*date\s*\(
--- check source absent | Finds the last day from the data, not by typing it
'\d{4}-\d\d-\d\d'

+++ practice | The cohort that vanished
--- task
This query is meant to report day-3 retention for each signup day: how many users of that day are eligible (their day 3 is on or before the last day of data) and how many of them had an event on exactly day 3.

```sql
WITH signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), active AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts) AS d FROM events
), last AS (
  SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events
)
SELECT s.d0 AS signup_day, COUNT(*) AS eligible,
       SUM(EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
                   WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+3 days'))) AS retained
FROM signups s
CROSS JOIN last
WHERE date(s.d0, '+3 days') < last.d
GROUP BY s.d0
ORDER BY s.d0;
```

User 6 signed up on 7 July, so their day 3 is 10 July, the last day of the data. That day has happened, but the 7 July group is missing from the report. Fix the bug, keeping the three columns and the sort.
--- starter
WITH signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), active AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts) AS d FROM events
), last AS (
  SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events
)
SELECT s.d0 AS signup_day, COUNT(*) AS eligible,
       SUM(EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
                   WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+3 days'))) AS retained
FROM signups s
CROSS JOIN last
WHERE date(s.d0, '+3 days') < last.d
GROUP BY s.d0
ORDER BY s.d0;
--- solution
WITH signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), active AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts) AS d FROM events
), last AS (
  SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events
)
SELECT s.d0 AS signup_day, COUNT(*) AS eligible,
       SUM(EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
                   WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+3 days'))) AS retained
FROM signups s
CROSS JOIN last
WHERE date(s.d0, '+3 days') <= last.d
GROUP BY s.d0
ORDER BY s.d0;
--- hint
Read the eligibility rule in the task word by word, then read the `WHERE`. Is a day 3 that falls exactly on the last day allowed in?
--- hint
"On or before" is `<=`. With `<`, every user whose day 3 is the last day of data is dropped.
--- check result | Five signup days, 7 July included
ordered
[["2024-07-01", 1, 0], ["2024-07-02", 2, 1], ["2024-07-03", 1, 1], ["2024-07-05", 1, 0], ["2024-07-07", 1, 0]]
--- check source | Keeps the day that falls on the last day
<=\s*last\.d
--- check source absent | Finds the last day from the data, not by typing it
'\d{4}-\d\d-\d\d'

+++ practice | Active days in the first week
--- task
A single retention number hides how much people use the app. For every signed-up user whose **whole first week** is in the data, count the different days they were active in that week.

A user's first week is days 1 to 7 after their signup day (day 0, the date of their `signup` event). It is in the data only if day 7 is on or before the last day in `events` (find it with `MAX(date(ts))`); leave everyone else out, because their count is not finished yet.

Return `user_id`, `d0` and `active_days` (the number of different dates from day 1 to day 7 on which they had any event), sorted by `user_id`.
--- starter
SELECT e.user_id, COUNT(DISTINCT date(e.ts)) AS active_days
FROM events e
GROUP BY e.user_id
ORDER BY e.user_id;
--- solution
WITH signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), last AS (
  SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events
)
SELECT s.user_id, s.d0, COUNT(DISTINCT date(e.ts)) AS active_days
FROM signups s
CROSS JOIN last
LEFT JOIN events e
  ON e.user_id = s.user_id
 AND date(e.ts) BETWEEN date(s.d0, '+1 day') AND date(s.d0, '+7 days')
WHERE date(s.d0, '+7 days') <= last.d
GROUP BY s.user_id, s.d0
ORDER BY s.user_id;
--- hint
Two decisions: which users are counted at all (their day 7 must have happened), and which of their events count (only days 1 to 7). The first is a `WHERE` on the signup; the second belongs in the join to `events`.
--- hint
Join each eligible signup to its own events with a range: `date(e.ts) BETWEEN date(s.d0, '+1 day') AND date(s.d0, '+7 days')`. Use a `LEFT JOIN`, so a user with no active days still counts as 0.
--- hint
Count different dates, not events: `COUNT(DISTINCT date(e.ts))`. Users 5 and 6 are left out because their day 7 falls after 10 July.
--- check result | Users 1 to 4, each with their active days in week one
ordered
[[1, "2024-07-01", 4], [2, "2024-07-02", 2], [3, "2024-07-02", 3], [4, "2024-07-03", 2]]
--- check source | Counts different days
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]
--- check source absent | Finds the last day from the data, not by typing it
'\d{4}-\d\d-\d\d'

=== sqlp-09 | Library 1: the schema
--- schema
CREATE TABLE members (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
INSERT INTO members (id, name, email) VALUES (1, 'Ana', 'ana@mail.com'), (2, 'Bo', 'bo@mail.com'), (3, 'Cy', 'cy@mail.com');
--- teach
Last lesson you finished the analytics project by measuring who comes back. **Project three is a library.** It has four lessons: the schema, what can be borrowed right now, overdue fines, and returns that charge fines by themselves. Like "Store 1: the schema", this first lesson designs the tables and writes the library's rules into them.

### The table you start with

The town's membership list already exists as `members`:

| id | name | email |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Ana | ana@mail.com |
| 2 | Bo | bo@mail.com |
| 3 | Cy | cy@mail.com |

You design everything else.

### A book is not a copy

Walk into a library and ask for *Dune*. The catalog has one entry for it: the title, the author, the [[ISBN|isbn]]. On the shelf there may be three physical *Dune*s, each with its own barcode sticker. One is out with a member, one is on the shelf, one has coffee spilled on page 40.

So there are two different things here:

- A **book** is the title as a whole: one row for *Dune*, however many copies there are.
- A **copy** is one physical item you can hold, with its own barcode.

You lend copies, not books. This is the first [[modeling decision|type-and-instance]] of the project, and a classic one. Get it wrong, with a single `books` table and a `quantity` column, and you cannot say *which* copy is overdue or damaged. "Two of three are out" does not tell you who has which.

### Three new tables

- `books` — the title: ISBN (unique), title, author
- `copies` — one physical item: which book it is, and its barcode (unique)
- `loans` — one lending: which copy, which member, when it was lent, when it is due, when it came back

A copy points at its book. A loan points at a copy and at a member. Those pointers are foreign keys, from the lesson "Foreign keys", so remember that SQLite only checks them after `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;`.

A loan that is still out has no return date yet. So `returned_on` is **NULL while the copy is out**, and gets a date when it comes back. That one NULL is what the rest of this project leans on.

### Dates stored as text

The three dates are text like `'2024-07-15'`: year, month, day. As you saw in "Date arithmetic and date ranges", text in this shape sorts in time order. So `>` ("greater than") between two of these dates means "later than", and a rule can compare them.

### Rule 1: due after it starts

A loan must be due *after* the day it was lent:

```sql
CHECK (due_on > loaned_on)
```

This rule looks at two columns of the same row, so it goes at the end of the table, after the last column, as in "Constraints: rules the database enforces". A loan lent on 10 July and due on 1 July is refused with `CHECK constraint failed: due_on > loaned_on`.

### Rule 2: not returned before it was lent

```sql
CHECK (returned_on IS NULL OR returned_on >= loaned_on)
```

Read it in words: "either it has not come back yet, or it came back on or after the day it was lent". `OR` means at least one side must be true, and `>=` means "on or after". A loan lent on 10 July and returned on 9 July is refused. An open loan, with `returned_on` NULL, passes by the first half. [[The IS NULL half|is-null-or]] says that out loud.

### Rule 3: one open loan per copy

This is the interesting one. A copy cannot be in two people's bags at once, so it can be out on only one loan at a time.

A first try is `UNIQUE (copy_id)` at the end of `loans`. But that allows one loan per copy *ever*. The second time anybody borrows copy D-1, even long after it came back, the insert is refused. The library needs every old loan kept as [[history|loan-history]].

What you want is "unique among the loans that are still out".

### A partial index

In "Deduplicating: keep the latest row" you met a **unique index**: `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX` builds an index and refuses two rows with the same value in it. Add a `WHERE` to it and you get a **partial index**: an index that holds only the rows matching its condition.

```sql
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_copy ON loans(copy_id) WHERE returned_on IS NULL;
```

- `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_copy` makes a unique index with that name.
- `ON loans(copy_id)` says it indexes the `copy_id` column of `loans`.
- `WHERE returned_on IS NULL` says only open loans go into it.

Only [[the rows inside the index are compared|partial-picture]], so the uniqueness applies to open loans alone. Closed loans pile up freely as history.

### The rules at work

Say the library has *Dune* with copies 1 (D-1) and 2 (D-2), and copy 1 goes out to Ana: loan 1, lent on 1 July, due on 15 July. Now try these, one after another:

| you try | what happens |
| --- | --- |
| lend copy 1 to Bo on 2 July | refused: `UNIQUE constraint failed: loans.copy_id` |
| set loan 1's `returned_on` to 5 July | accepted, and loan 1 leaves the index |
| lend copy 1 to Bo on 6 July | accepted: it is now copy 1's only open loan |
| lend copy 9, which does not exist | refused: `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed` |
| a second book with Dune's ISBN | refused: `UNIQUE constraint failed: books.isbn` |

**Watch out:** write `WHERE returned_on IS NULL`, never `WHERE returned_on = NULL`. Anything compared with `= NULL` is unknown, not true, so that index holds no rows at all. SQLite creates it without an error, and then it refuses nothing: two open loans for the same copy go straight in.

::: context isbn The number on the back of a book
An **ISBN**, International Standard Book Number, names one edition of a book. Since 2007 it has 13 digits, and it is printed as the barcode on the back cover. Every copy of the same paperback *Dune* carries the same ISBN, 9780441013593, so it belongs on `books`. The library's own barcode sticker is different: the library prints one per copy, so it belongs on `copies`. Store the ISBN as text, not as a number. You never do arithmetic with it, and the older 10-digit form can start with a 0 or end in the letter X.
:::

::: context type-and-instance Part numbers and serial numbers
Engineers call this **type** versus **instance**: the design of a thing versus one real thing built to it. Aerospace lives by it. A *part number* names a design, such as a valve, and every identical valve shares it. A *serial number* names one physical valve, with its own test records and hours of use. If one valve fails an inspection, you ground that serial number, not every valve ever made. Books and copies are the same split.

```svg
    <marker id="ar" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" orient="auto">

    </marker>

</svg>
```
:::

::: context is-null-or Saying the NULL case out loud
Strictly, `CHECK (returned_on >= loaned_on)` alone would behave the same. In "Constraints: rules the database enforces" you saw that a `CHECK` only refuses a row when its condition is false. With `returned_on` NULL the comparison gives NULL, not false, so the open loan gets in anyway. Writing `returned_on IS NULL OR` spells out that an open loan is fine on purpose. The next person who reads the table does not have to remember how NULL behaves inside a `CHECK` to trust the rule.
:::

::: context loan-history Why old loans are kept
When a copy comes back, the loan is not deleted: it gets a `returned_on` date and stays. Those old rows answer real questions. Who had this copy before the page was torn? Which books are borrowed most, so the library buys more copies? How many loans ran late last year? Deleting them would throw that away. Some libraries do strip the member's name from old loans after a while, to protect readers' privacy, but they keep the loan itself for the counts. Mission logs work the same way: you add new rows and never overwrite the old ones.
:::

::: context partial-picture Only open loans go in
Here copy 1 has two loans: loan 1, already back, and loan 3, still out. Only loan 3 and loan 2 are inside the index, so copy 1 appears there once. A new open loan for copy 1 would be a second copy 1 inside the index, and the index refuses it. A partial index is also smaller than a full one, and a query whose `WHERE` repeats the condition, such as `WHERE returned_on IS NULL`, can use it to find the open loans fast.

```svg
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
    </marker>

  <text x="105" y="26" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">loans (every row)</text>

```
