--- task
Build the library's schema. The starter already does steps 1 and 2.

1. Turn foreign key enforcement on with `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;`.
2. Create `books`: `id` integer primary key; `isbn` text, required, unique; `title` and `author` text, required.
3. Create `copies`: `id` integer primary key; `book_id` required, referencing `books(id)`; `barcode` text, required, unique.
4. Create `loans`: `id` integer primary key; `copy_id` required, referencing `copies(id)`; `member_id` required, referencing `members(id)`; `loaned_on` and `due_on` text, required; `returned_on` text, left NULL while the copy is out. Add two rules at the end: the due date is after the loan date, and `returned_on` is either NULL or on or after the loan date.
5. Create a unique partial index named `one_open_loan_per_copy` on `loans(copy_id)`, holding only the loans that are still out, so a copy can have only one open loan at a time.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE copies (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  book_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES books(id),
  barcode TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  copy_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES copies(id),
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  loaned_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  due_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  returned_on TEXT,
  CHECK (due_on > loaned_on),
  CHECK (returned_on IS NULL OR returned_on >= loaned_on)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_copy ON loans(copy_id) WHERE returned_on IS NULL;
--- hint
`copies` is built like `books`: an `id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY`, then `book_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES books(id)` and `barcode TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE`. `loans` follows the same pattern, with two foreign keys.
--- hint
Two `CHECK`s go at the end of `loans`, after `returned_on TEXT`: `CHECK (due_on > loaned_on)` and `CHECK (returned_on IS NULL OR returned_on >= loaned_on)`.
--- hint
The one-open-loan rule is its own statement after the tables: `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_copy ON loans(copy_id) WHERE returned_on IS NULL;`
--- check query | Books, copies and a loan can be stored
INSERT INTO books (id, isbn, title, author) VALUES (1, '9780441013593', 'Dune', 'Frank Herbert');
INSERT INTO copies (id, book_id, barcode) VALUES (1, 1, 'D-1'), (2, 1, 'D-2');
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on) VALUES (1, 1, 1, '2024-07-01', '2024-07-15') RETURNING id, returned_on
=> [[1, null]]
--- check query | A duplicate ISBN or barcode is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO books (id, isbn, title, author) VALUES (2, '9780441013593', 'Dune again', 'F. H.') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | The same copy cannot go out twice at once
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on) VALUES (2, 1, 2, '2024-07-02', '2024-07-16') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Once returned, the copy can go out again
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-05' WHERE id = 1;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on) VALUES (3, 1, 2, '2024-07-06', '2024-07-20') RETURNING id
=> [[3]]
--- check query | A due date before the loan, or a return before it, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on, returned_on) VALUES (4, 2, 1, '2024-07-10', '2024-07-01', NULL), (5, 2, 1, '2024-07-10', '2024-07-24', '2024-07-09') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | loans references copies and members
SELECT "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('loans') ORDER BY "from"
=> [["copies", "copy_id"], ["members", "member_id"]]
--- check query | copies references books
SELECT "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('copies')
=> [["books", "book_id"]]
--- check query | The required columns are NOT NULL, and returned_on is not
SELECT 'copies', name FROM pragma_table_info('copies') WHERE "notnull" = 1
UNION ALL
SELECT 'loans', name FROM pragma_table_info('loans') WHERE "notnull" = 1
=> [["copies", "book_id"], ["copies", "barcode"], ["loans", "copy_id"], ["loans", "member_id"], ["loans", "loaned_on"], ["loans", "due_on"]]

+++ practice | One ride per bike
--- schema
CREATE TABLE bikes (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  code TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE riders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO bikes (id, code) VALUES (1, 'B-01'), (2, 'B-02'), (3, 'B-03');
INSERT INTO riders (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo');
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `bikes` (`id`, `code`) and `riders` (`id`, `name`). A bike-share needs a table of rides.

1. Turn foreign keys on.
2. Create `rides`: `id` (integer primary key), `bike_id` (integer, required, referencing `bikes(id)`), `rider_id` (integer, required, referencing `riders(id)`), `started` (text, required) and `ended` (text, NULL while the ride is going on). Add a rule at the end: `ended` is either NULL or strictly after `started`.
3. Create a unique partial index named `one_open_ride_per_bike` on `rides(bike_id)`, holding only the rides still going on, so a bike can be on only one ride at a time. Finished rides stay in the table as history.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE rides (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  bike_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES bikes(id),
  rider_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES riders(id),
  started TEXT NOT NULL,
  ended TEXT
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE rides (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  bike_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES bikes(id),
  rider_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES riders(id),
  started TEXT NOT NULL,
  ended TEXT,
  CHECK (ended IS NULL OR ended > started)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_ride_per_bike ON rides(bike_id) WHERE ended IS NULL;
--- hint
A ride that is still going on is one with `ended IS NULL`. The index should hold only those rows, so it needs a `WHERE`.
--- hint
The rule across two columns goes after `ended TEXT`: `CHECK (ended IS NULL OR ended > started)`. The index is its own statement after the table: `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX … ON rides(bike_id) WHERE …;`.
--- check query | A ride can start
INSERT INTO rides (id, bike_id, rider_id, started) VALUES (1, 1, 1, '2024-06-01 08:00') RETURNING id, ended
=> [[1, null]]
--- check query | A bike already out cannot start a second ride; another bike can
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO rides (id, bike_id, rider_id, started) VALUES (2, 1, 2, '2024-06-01 08:05'), (3, 2, 2, '2024-06-01 08:05') RETURNING id
=> [[3]]
--- check query | Once the ride ends, the bike can go out again
UPDATE rides SET ended = '2024-06-01 08:30' WHERE id = 1;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO rides (id, bike_id, rider_id, started) VALUES (4, 1, 2, '2024-06-01 09:00') RETURNING id
=> [[4]]
--- check query | A ride cannot end before it starts, or at the same moment
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO rides (id, bike_id, rider_id, started, ended) VALUES (5, 3, 1, '2024-06-01 10:00', '2024-06-01 09:00'), (6, 3, 1, '2024-06-01 10:00', '2024-06-01 10:00') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | The index is unique and partial
SELECT "unique", partial FROM pragma_index_list('rides') WHERE name = 'one_open_ride_per_bike'
=> [[1, 1]]

+++ practice | One primary phone per user
--- schema
CREATE TABLE users (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE phones (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id),
  number TEXT NOT NULL,
  is_primary INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (is_primary IN (0, 1))
);
INSERT INTO users (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo');
INSERT INTO phones (id, user_id, number, is_primary) VALUES
  (1, 1, '555-0101', 1),
  (2, 1, '555-0102', 0),
  (3, 2, '555-0201', 0);
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `users` (`id`, `name`) and `phones` (`id`, `user_id`, `number`, `is_primary`), where `is_primary` is 1 for the number to call first and 0 otherwise. A user may have many phones, but at most **one** primary one.

The tables already exist and hold data. Add the rule with a single statement: a unique partial index named `one_primary_phone`. The starter's index is too strict: it would allow only one phone per user, and it cannot even be created on this data.
--- starter
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_primary_phone ON phones(user_id);
--- solution
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_primary_phone ON phones(user_id) WHERE is_primary = 1;
--- hint
A partial index's `WHERE` can be any test on the row, not only `IS NULL`. Which rows should be forced to be unique per user?
--- hint
Only the primary phones: add `WHERE is_primary = 1` to the index.
--- check query | A user can add another phone that is not primary
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO phones (id, user_id, number, is_primary) VALUES (4, 1, '555-0103', 0) RETURNING id
=> [[4]]
--- check query | A second primary phone is refused; another user's is fine
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO phones (id, user_id, number, is_primary) VALUES (5, 1, '555-0104', 1), (6, 2, '555-0202', 1) RETURNING id
=> [[6]]
--- check query | The primary can move to another phone
UPDATE phones SET is_primary = 0 WHERE id = 1;
UPDATE phones SET is_primary = 1 WHERE id = 2;
SELECT id FROM phones WHERE user_id = 1 AND is_primary = 1
=> [[2]]
--- check query | The index is unique and partial
SELECT "unique", partial FROM pragma_index_list('phones') WHERE name = 'one_primary_phone'
=> [[1, 1]]

+++ practice | Books and copies from one flat list
--- schema
CREATE TABLE inventory_flat (
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL,
  barcode TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO inventory_flat (title, author, isbn, barcode) VALUES
  ('Dune', 'Frank Herbert',  '978-0441013593', 'D-1'),
  ('Dune', 'Frank Herbert',  '9780441013593',  'D-2'),
  ('Emma', 'Jane Austen',    '978-0141439587', 'E-1'),
  ('Dune', 'Frank Herbert',  ' 9780441013593', 'D-3'),
  ('Ubik', 'Philip K. Dick', '9780547572291',  'U-1');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `inventory_flat`: a spreadsheet with one row per physical copy (`title`, `author`, `isbn`, `barcode`). The ISBNs were typed in different ways, some with a hyphen and some with a space in front.

Split it into a book-and-copy schema:

1. Turn foreign keys on.
2. Create `books` (`id` integer primary key; `isbn` text, required, unique; `title` and `author` text, required) and `copies` (`id` integer primary key; `book_id` integer, required, referencing `books(id)`; `barcode` text, required, unique).
3. Fill `books` with one row per cleaned ISBN: trimmed, with every `'-'` removed by `replace(…, '-', '')`. Take `MIN(title)` and `MIN(author)`.
4. Fill `copies` with one row per barcode, linked to its book through the cleaned ISBN.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE copies (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  book_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES books(id),
  barcode TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);

INSERT INTO books (isbn, title, author)
SELECT replace(trim(isbn), '-', ''), MIN(title), MIN(author)
FROM inventory_flat
GROUP BY replace(trim(isbn), '-', '');

INSERT INTO copies (book_id, barcode)
SELECT b.id, f.barcode
FROM inventory_flat f
JOIN books b ON b.isbn = replace(trim(f.isbn), '-', '');
--- hint
This is "Store 2: loading the export" again, with the library's design: parents first. Books are grouped by the cleaned ISBN; copies then find their book through the same cleaned value.
--- hint
The cleaned ISBN is `replace(trim(isbn), '-', '')`, from "Building text: joining, replacing and LIKE". Use it in the books' `GROUP BY` and in the copies' join: `JOIN books b ON b.isbn = replace(trim(f.isbn), '-', '')`.
--- check query | Three books and five copies
SELECT (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM books), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM copies)
=> [[3, 5]]
--- check query | Every copy points at the right book
SELECT c.barcode, b.title, b.isbn FROM copies c JOIN books b ON b.id = c.book_id ORDER BY c.barcode
=> [["D-1", "Dune", "9780441013593"], ["D-2", "Dune", "9780441013593"], ["D-3", "Dune", "9780441013593"], ["E-1", "Emma", "9780141439587"], ["U-1", "Ubik", "9780547572291"]]
--- check query | A second book with the same ISBN, or a second copy with the same barcode, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO books (isbn, title, author) VALUES ('9780441013593', 'Dune again', 'F. H.') RETURNING id;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO copies (book_id, barcode) VALUES (1, 'D-1') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | copies references books
SELECT "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('copies')
=> [["books", "book_id"]]

+++ practice | Holds that end two ways
--- schema
CREATE TABLE members (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE copies (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  barcode TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
INSERT INTO members (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo'), (3, 'Cy');
INSERT INTO copies (id, barcode) VALUES (1, 'D-1'), (2, 'E-1');
--- task
This problem has its own tables, `members` (`id`, `name`) and `copies` (`id`, `barcode`). A member can put a **hold** on a copy to reserve it. A hold ends in one of two ways: it is fulfilled (the member collected the copy) or cancelled.

Create `holds` with these columns and rules:

1. `id` (integer primary key), `copy_id` (integer, required, referencing `copies(id)`), `member_id` (integer, required, referencing `members(id)`), `placed_on` (text, required), `fulfilled_on` (text, NULL until fulfilled), `cancelled_on` (text, NULL unless cancelled).
2. A hold cannot be both fulfilled and cancelled.
3. `fulfilled_on` and `cancelled_on`, when set, are on or after `placed_on`.
4. A copy can have at most one **active** hold: one that is neither fulfilled nor cancelled. Make it a unique partial index named `one_active_hold_per_copy`. Finished holds of either kind stay as history.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE holds (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  copy_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES copies(id),
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  placed_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  fulfilled_on TEXT,
  cancelled_on TEXT
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_active_hold_per_copy ON holds(copy_id) WHERE cancelled_on IS NULL;
--- solution
CREATE TABLE holds (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  copy_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES copies(id),
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  placed_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  fulfilled_on TEXT,
  cancelled_on TEXT,
  CHECK (fulfilled_on IS NULL OR cancelled_on IS NULL),
  CHECK (fulfilled_on IS NULL OR fulfilled_on >= placed_on),
  CHECK (cancelled_on IS NULL OR cancelled_on >= placed_on)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_active_hold_per_copy ON holds(copy_id)
WHERE fulfilled_on IS NULL AND cancelled_on IS NULL;
--- hint
"Active" needs both end dates to be NULL. The starter's index looks at only one of them, so a fulfilled hold still sits in the index and blocks the copy.
--- hint
Rule 2 says at least one of the two dates is NULL: `CHECK (fulfilled_on IS NULL OR cancelled_on IS NULL)`. Rule 3 is two more checks in the lesson's `IS NULL OR …` form.
--- hint
The index's condition joins two tests with `AND`: `WHERE fulfilled_on IS NULL AND cancelled_on IS NULL`.
--- check query | An active hold can be placed; a second one on the same copy is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO holds (id, copy_id, member_id, placed_on) VALUES (1, 1, 1, '2024-07-01'), (2, 1, 2, '2024-07-02') RETURNING id
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Old cancelled and fulfilled holds on that copy are kept as history
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO holds (id, copy_id, member_id, placed_on, fulfilled_on, cancelled_on) VALUES (3, 1, 2, '2024-06-01', NULL, '2024-06-02'), (4, 1, 3, '2024-05-01', '2024-05-03', NULL) RETURNING id
=> [[3], [4]]
--- check query | Fulfilled and cancelled at once, or ending before it was placed, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO holds (id, copy_id, member_id, placed_on, fulfilled_on, cancelled_on) VALUES (5, 2, 1, '2024-07-01', '2024-07-02', '2024-07-02'), (6, 2, 1, '2024-07-05', '2024-07-04', NULL), (7, 2, 1, '2024-07-05', NULL, '2024-07-01') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Once the active hold is cancelled, the copy can be held again
UPDATE holds SET cancelled_on = '2024-07-03' WHERE id = 1;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO holds (id, copy_id, member_id, placed_on) VALUES (8, 1, 2, '2024-07-03') RETURNING id
=> [[8]]

+++ practice | The tool that could only be lent once
--- schema
CREATE TABLE items (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE staff (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO items (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Torque wrench'), (2, 'Multimeter');
INSERT INTO staff (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo');
--- task
This problem has its own tables, `items` and `staff`. The starter creates `item_loans` for a workshop's tool crib. An item may be out on only one loan at a time, and once it comes back it must be lendable again.

With the starter, the first loan of each item works, but every later loan of the same item is refused, even after it was returned. Fix the schema: remove the rule that causes this, and enforce "one open loan per item" with a unique partial index named `one_open_loan_per_item`. Keep everything else.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE item_loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  item_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES items(id),
  staff_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES staff(id),
  out_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  back_on TEXT,
  CHECK (back_on IS NULL OR back_on >= out_on),
  UNIQUE (item_id)
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE item_loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  item_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES items(id),
  staff_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES staff(id),
  out_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  back_on TEXT,
  CHECK (back_on IS NULL OR back_on >= out_on)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_item ON item_loans(item_id) WHERE back_on IS NULL;
--- hint
`UNIQUE (item_id)` allows one loan per item **ever**. Which loans should really be unique per item?
--- hint
Delete the `UNIQUE (item_id)` line (and the comma before it), then add `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_item ON item_loans(item_id) WHERE back_on IS NULL;` after the table.
--- check query | An item can go out; a second open loan of it is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO item_loans (id, item_id, staff_id, out_on) VALUES (1, 1, 1, '2024-07-01'), (2, 1, 2, '2024-07-02') RETURNING id
=> [[1]]
--- check query | After it comes back, the item can go out again
UPDATE item_loans SET back_on = '2024-07-03' WHERE id = 1;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO item_loans (id, item_id, staff_id, out_on) VALUES (3, 1, 2, '2024-07-04') RETURNING id
=> [[3]]
--- check query | Both loans of the wrench are kept
SELECT id, back_on FROM item_loans WHERE item_id = 1 ORDER BY id
=> [[1, "2024-07-03"], [3, null]]
--- check query | The index is unique and partial
SELECT "unique", partial FROM pragma_index_list('item_loans') WHERE name = 'one_open_loan_per_item'
=> [[1, 1]]

+++ practice | Telescope time
--- schema
CREATE TABLE telescopes (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
INSERT INTO telescopes (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Hale'), (2, 'Keck'), (3, 'Zeiss');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `telescopes` (`id`, `name`). An observatory logs observing sessions. Design the rest:

1. Turn foreign keys on.
2. `sessions`: `id` (integer primary key), `telescope_id` (integer, required, referencing `telescopes(id)`), `observer` (text, required: the observer's email), `started` (text, required), `ended` (text, NULL while the session is running). `ended`, when set, is strictly after `started`.
3. A telescope can run only one session at a time.
4. An observer can run only one session at a time, whichever telescope it is on.
5. A view `idle_telescopes` with the columns `id` and `name`: the telescopes that have no running session right now.

Finished sessions stay in the table as history.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE sessions (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  telescope_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES telescopes(id),
  observer TEXT NOT NULL,
  started TEXT NOT NULL,
  ended TEXT
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE sessions (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  telescope_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES telescopes(id),
  observer TEXT NOT NULL,
  started TEXT NOT NULL,
  ended TEXT,
  CHECK (ended IS NULL OR ended > started)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_running_session_per_telescope ON sessions(telescope_id) WHERE ended IS NULL;
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_running_session_per_observer ON sessions(observer) WHERE ended IS NULL;

CREATE VIEW idle_telescopes AS
SELECT t.id, t.name
FROM telescopes t
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1 FROM sessions s WHERE s.telescope_id = t.id AND s.ended IS NULL
);
--- hint
Rules 3 and 4 are the same kind of rule on two different columns. Each gets its own unique partial index, both holding only the running sessions.
--- hint
The view is "finding what is missing": telescopes for which no running session exists. `NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sessions s WHERE s.telescope_id = t.id AND s.ended IS NULL)` is one way to say it.
--- check query | Foreign keys are on
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Two sessions can run on two telescopes
INSERT INTO sessions (id, telescope_id, observer, started) VALUES (1, 1, 'ana@obs.org', '2024-09-01 20:00'), (2, 2, 'bo@obs.org', '2024-09-01 20:30') RETURNING id
=> [[1], [2]]
--- check query | A busy telescope, or a busy observer, cannot start another session
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO sessions (id, telescope_id, observer, started) VALUES (3, 1, 'cy@obs.org', '2024-09-01 21:00'), (4, 3, 'ana@obs.org', '2024-09-01 21:00') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Only the Zeiss is idle
SELECT id, name FROM idle_telescopes ORDER BY id
=> [[3, "Zeiss"]]
--- check query | When a session ends, its observer and telescope are free again
UPDATE sessions SET ended = '2024-09-01 23:00' WHERE id = 1;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO sessions (id, telescope_id, observer, started) VALUES (5, 3, 'ana@obs.org', '2024-09-01 23:10') RETURNING id
=> [[5]]
--- check query | The Hale is idle now
SELECT id, name FROM idle_telescopes ORDER BY id
=> [[1, "Hale"]]
--- check query | A session cannot end before it starts
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO sessions (id, telescope_id, observer, started, ended) VALUES (6, 2, 'dee@obs.org', '2024-09-02 01:00', '2024-09-02 00:30') RETURNING id
=> []

=== sqlp-10 | Library 2: what can I borrow?
--- schema
CREATE TABLE members (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE copies (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  book_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES books(id),
  barcode TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  copy_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES copies(id),
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  loaned_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  due_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  returned_on TEXT,
  CHECK (due_on > loaned_on),
  CHECK (returned_on IS NULL OR returned_on >= loaned_on)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_copy ON loans(copy_id) WHERE returned_on IS NULL;
INSERT INTO members (id, name, email) VALUES (1, 'Ana', 'ana@mail.com'), (2, 'Bo', 'bo@mail.com'), (3, 'Cy', 'cy@mail.com');
INSERT INTO books (id, isbn, title, author) VALUES
  (1, '9780441013593', 'Dune',    'Frank Herbert'),
  (2, '9780141439587', 'Emma',    'Jane Austen'),
  (3, '9780547572291', 'Ubik',    'Philip K. Dick'),
  (4, '9780807083697', 'Kindred', 'Octavia E. Butler');
INSERT INTO copies (id, book_id, barcode) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'D-1'), (2, 1, 'D-2'), (3, 1, 'D-3'), (4, 2, 'E-1'), (5, 3, 'U-1'), (6, 3, 'U-2');
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on, returned_on) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-06-01', '2024-06-15', '2024-06-14'),
  (2, 2, 2, '2024-06-20', '2024-07-04', NULL),
  (3, 1, 3, '2024-06-25', '2024-07-09', NULL),
  (4, 4, 1, '2024-07-01', '2024-07-15', NULL),
  (5, 5, 2, '2024-05-01', '2024-05-15', '2024-05-30'),
  (6, 6, 1, '2024-04-01', '2024-04-15', NULL);
--- teach
Last lesson you designed the library's tables and wrote its rules into them. Now those tables hold real data, and you answer the question everyone at a library asks: can I borrow this book?

### The tables

These are the four tables from "Library 1: the schema": `members`, `books`, `copies` and `loans`. There are three members, four books, six copies and six loans. Here are the books with their copies:

| book id | title | copies (id and barcode) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Dune | 1 D-1, 2 D-2, 3 D-3 |
| 2 | Emma | 4 E-1 |
| 3 | Ubik | 5 U-1, 6 U-2 |
| 4 | Kindred | none yet |

*Kindred* has only now been [[cataloged|catalogue]] and has no copies yet. And here are the loans:

| id | copy_id | member_id | loaned_on | due_on | returned_on |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 2024-06-01 | 2024-06-15 | 2024-06-14 |
| 2 | 2 | 2 | 2024-06-20 | 2024-07-04 | NULL |
| 3 | 1 | 3 | 2024-06-25 | 2024-07-09 | NULL |
| 4 | 4 | 1 | 2024-07-01 | 2024-07-15 | NULL |
| 5 | 5 | 2 | 2024-05-01 | 2024-05-15 | 2024-05-30 |
| 6 | 6 | 1 | 2024-04-01 | 2024-04-15 | NULL |

### The question

The front desk and the website both ask the same thing: **for each book, how many copies are there, how many are out, and how many can be borrowed right now?**

Put the answer in a view named `book_availability`, as in "Views: saved queries". Then the desk, the website and the weekly report all use [[one definition of "available"|one-definition]].

### When is a copy out?

A copy is **out** when it has an **open loan**: a loan with `returned_on IS NULL`. Thanks to last lesson's partial index, a copy has at most one.

```sql
SELECT id, copy_id FROM loans WHERE returned_on IS NULL ORDER BY id;
```

| id | copy_id |
| --- | --- |
| 2 | 2 |
| 3 | 1 |
| 4 | 4 |
| 6 | 6 |

Copy 1 (D-1) has been lent twice. Loan 1 is closed, and loan 3 is the open one.

### Step 1: start from books

Build the query in the order of the tables. Start **from `books`**, then `LEFT JOIN` copies. As in "LEFT JOIN and NULL", every book stays, and a book with no copies gets NULLs on the copy side instead of disappearing:

```sql
SELECT b.title, c.barcode
FROM books b
LEFT JOIN copies c ON c.book_id = b.id;
```

| title | barcode |
| --- | --- |
| Dune | D-1 |
| Dune | D-2 |
| Dune | D-3 |
| Emma | E-1 |
| Ubik | U-1 |
| Ubik | U-2 |
| Kindred | NULL |

### Step 2: join only the open loans

Next, `LEFT JOIN` loans, with the open-loan condition **inside the `ON`**, joined with `AND`:

```sql
SELECT b.title, c.barcode, l.id AS loan_id
FROM books b
LEFT JOIN copies c ON c.book_id = b.id
LEFT JOIN loans l  ON l.copy_id = c.id AND l.returned_on IS NULL;
```

| title | barcode | loan_id |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Dune | D-1 | 3 |
| Dune | D-2 | 2 |
| Dune | D-3 | NULL |
| Emma | E-1 | 4 |
| Ubik | U-1 | NULL |
| Ubik | U-2 | 6 |
| Kindred | NULL | NULL |

Still one row per copy. Each copy shows its open loan, or NULL when it is on the shelf.

### Why the condition belongs in ON

This is the rule from "LEFT JOIN: where the filter goes": a condition about the right-hand table goes in its `ON`. Here are the counts you get the other two ways:

| where `l.returned_on IS NULL` goes | Dune: copies, on loan | Ubik: copies, on loan |
| --- | --- | --- |
| in the `ON` (right) | 3, 2 | 2, 1 |
| in the `WHERE` | 3, 2 | 1, 1 |
| left out | 4, 3 | 2, 2 |

Put it in `WHERE` instead, and U-1 disappears. Its only loan came back, so its joined row has a date in `returned_on`, and the `WHERE` throws the whole row away. Ubik then shows 1 copy instead of 2.

Leave the condition out, and D-1 matches both of its loans, the closed one and the open one. It becomes two rows. Dune then shows 4 copies and 3 on loan. That is [[fan-out|at-most-one]], from "Debugging: the join that double-counts".

### Step 3: count

Now group by book and count:

- `COUNT(c.id)` counts the copies. `COUNT(column)` skips NULLs, so Kindred gets 0.
- `COUNT(l.id)` counts the open loans.
- `available` is the difference: `COUNT(c.id) - COUNT(l.id)`.

The finished view gives:

| book_id | title | copies | on_loan | available |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Dune | 3 | 2 | 1 |
| 2 | Emma | 1 | 1 | 0 |
| 3 | Ubik | 2 | 1 | 1 |
| 4 | Kindred | 0 | 0 | 0 |

### The rule that makes it correct

Because each copy has at most one open loan, this join cannot fan out: one copy never turns into two rows. The partial index you designed last lesson is what makes this query correct. And because a view runs again every time you use it, the numbers are [[always up to date|always-fresh]]: the moment a copy comes back, it counts as available.

**Watch out:** do not count with `COUNT(*)`. Kindred has no copies, but it still has one joined row full of NULLs, and `COUNT(*)` counts that row, so Kindred would show 1 copy. Count `c.id` and `l.id`.

::: context catalogue What "cataloged" means
A library's **catalog** is its list of every title it holds, with the author, ISBN and subject, so people can search it. To **catalog** a book is to add its entry to that list. That often happens before the physical copies arrive, or while they are still being labeled, which is exactly why *Kindred* has a row in `books` and none in `copies`. A good report shows it with 0 copies rather than leaving it out. A missing row reads as "we have never heard of this book", which is a different answer.
:::

::: context one-definition Why one shared definition matters
"Available" sounds obvious until you ask around. Does a copy waiting to be repaired count? A copy someone has reserved? If the desk, the website and the weekly report each write their own query, sooner or later they disagree, and a member is told online that a book is on the shelf when the desk says it is not. With one view, the rule lives in one place. When the library decides that copies in repair are not available, you change the view, and every screen and report changes with it.
:::

::: context at-most-one Why the join stays one row per copy
A join pairs each copy with every loan that matches its `ON`. With the open-loan condition in the `ON`, only open loans match, and the partial index allows at most one per copy. So each copy meets one loan, or none and gets NULLs. Without the condition, D-1 would meet its closed loan too.

::: context always-fresh Counting instead of storing a number
Another design would give `books` an `available` column and add or subtract 1 at every loan and return. Every program that lends or returns a copy would then have to remember to update it, and one missed update leaves the number wrong for good. The view never drifts, because it counts the loans themselves each time it runs. The second check of this lesson does exactly that: it marks Emma's copy as returned, and `book_availability` shows Emma as available straight away.
:::
--- task
Create a view named `book_availability` with one row per book, including books that have no copies. Its columns, in this order:

1. `book_id`: the book's `id`.
2. `title`: the book's title.
3. `copies`: how many copies the book has.
4. `on_loan`: how many of those copies are out right now (they have a loan with `returned_on` NULL).
5. `available`: `copies` minus `on_loan`.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW book_availability AS
SELECT b.id AS book_id, b.title, COUNT(*) AS copies
FROM books b
JOIN copies c ON c.book_id = b.id
GROUP BY b.id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW book_availability AS
SELECT b.id AS book_id, b.title,
       COUNT(c.id) AS copies,
       COUNT(l.id) AS on_loan,
       COUNT(c.id) - COUNT(l.id) AS available
FROM books b
LEFT JOIN copies c ON c.book_id = b.id
LEFT JOIN loans l  ON l.copy_id = c.id AND l.returned_on IS NULL
GROUP BY b.id;
--- hint
Start `FROM books b`, then `LEFT JOIN copies c` and `LEFT JOIN loans l`, and `GROUP BY b.id`. The starter's plain `JOIN` drops Kindred.
--- hint
The loans join is `ON l.copy_id = c.id AND l.returned_on IS NULL`, so only open loans match.
--- hint
Count columns, not rows: `COUNT(c.id) AS copies`, `COUNT(l.id) AS on_loan` and `COUNT(c.id) - COUNT(l.id) AS available`.
--- check query | Every book, with copies, loans and availability
SELECT book_id, title, copies, on_loan, available FROM book_availability ORDER BY book_id
=> [[1, "Dune", 3, 2, 1], [2, "Emma", 1, 1, 0], [3, "Ubik", 2, 1, 1], [4, "Kindred", 0, 0, 0]]
--- check query | Returning a copy makes it available straight away
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-15' WHERE id = 4;
SELECT available FROM book_availability WHERE title = 'Emma'
=> [[1]]

+++ practice | Loans per member
--- task
Create a view named `member_loans` with one row for **every** member, including members who have never borrowed anything, and four columns:

1. `member_id`: the member's `id`.
2. `name`.
3. `open_loans`: how many of their loans are still out (`returned_on` is NULL).
4. `total_loans`: how many loans they have ever had, open or closed.

The checks add a new member and return a book, then read the view again.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW member_loans AS
SELECT m.id AS member_id, m.name, COUNT(*) AS open_loans, COUNT(*) AS total_loans
FROM members m
JOIN loans l ON l.member_id = m.id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL
GROUP BY m.id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW member_loans AS
SELECT m.id AS member_id, m.name,
       COUNT(CASE WHEN l.returned_on IS NULL THEN l.id END) AS open_loans,
       COUNT(l.id) AS total_loans
FROM members m
LEFT JOIN loans l ON l.member_id = m.id
GROUP BY m.id;
--- hint
This time both open and closed loans are needed, so the open-loan test cannot filter the join. Join every loan, and let each count decide what it counts.
--- hint
`COUNT(l.id)` counts all of a member's loans. For the open ones, count only the ids whose loan is still out: `COUNT(CASE WHEN l.returned_on IS NULL THEN l.id END)`. A member with no loans has NULL in `l.id`, so both counts give 0.
--- check query | Ana, Bo and Cy with their open and total loans
SELECT member_id, name, open_loans, total_loans FROM member_loans ORDER BY member_id
=> [[1, "Ana", 2, 3], [2, "Bo", 1, 2], [3, "Cy", 1, 1]]
--- check query | A new member with no loans shows 0 and 0
INSERT INTO members (id, name, email) VALUES (4, 'Dee', 'dee@mail.com');
SELECT open_loans, total_loans FROM member_loans WHERE member_id = 4
=> [[0, 0]]
--- check query | A return lowers the open count but not the total
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-10' WHERE id = 4;
SELECT open_loans, total_loans FROM member_loans WHERE member_id = 1
=> [[1, 3]]

+++ practice | What is on the shelf
--- task
Create a view named `on_shelf` listing every copy that can be borrowed right now, meaning it has no open loan (no loan with `returned_on` NULL). It has two columns: `barcode` and `title` (the copy's book).

A copy that has never been lent is on the shelf too. The checks return a copy and lend another, then read the view again.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW on_shelf AS
SELECT c.barcode, b.title
FROM copies c
JOIN books b ON b.id = c.book_id
JOIN loans l ON l.copy_id = c.id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NOT NULL;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW on_shelf AS
SELECT c.barcode, b.title
FROM copies c
JOIN books b ON b.id = c.book_id
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1 FROM loans l WHERE l.copy_id = c.id AND l.returned_on IS NULL
);
--- hint
"Has a closed loan" is not the same as "has no open loan": D-1 has both, and D-3 has neither. The question is about something that must **not** exist.
--- hint
`WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM loans l WHERE l.copy_id = c.id AND l.returned_on IS NULL)`, from "Correlated subqueries and EXISTS", keeps the copies with no open loan.
--- check query | D-3 has never been lent and U-1 is back
SELECT barcode, title FROM on_shelf ORDER BY barcode
=> [["D-3", "Dune"], ["U-1", "Ubik"]]
--- check query | A returned copy is on the shelf straight away
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-12' WHERE id = 2;
SELECT barcode FROM on_shelf ORDER BY barcode
=> [["D-2"], ["D-3"], ["U-1"]]
--- check query | A copy that goes out leaves the shelf
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on) VALUES (7, 5, 3, '2024-07-12', '2024-07-26');
SELECT barcode FROM on_shelf ORDER BY barcode
=> [["D-2"], ["D-3"]]

+++ practice | Available now, or back when?
--- task
Create a view named `book_status` with one row per book, books with no copies included, and five columns:

1. `book_id` and 2. `title`.
3. `available`: copies minus copies out on an open loan.
4. `next_due`: the earliest `due_on` among the book's open loans, or NULL when none of its copies is out.
5. `status`: `'no copies'` when the book has no copies, `'available'` when at least one copy can be borrowed, and `'all out'` otherwise.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW book_status AS
SELECT b.id AS book_id, b.title, COUNT(c.id) AS available
FROM books b
LEFT JOIN copies c ON c.book_id = b.id
GROUP BY b.id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW book_status AS
SELECT b.id AS book_id, b.title,
       COUNT(c.id) - COUNT(l.id) AS available,
       MIN(l.due_on) AS next_due,
       CASE WHEN COUNT(c.id) = 0 THEN 'no copies'
            WHEN COUNT(c.id) - COUNT(l.id) > 0 THEN 'available'
            ELSE 'all out' END AS status
FROM books b
LEFT JOIN copies c ON c.book_id = b.id
LEFT JOIN loans l  ON l.copy_id = c.id AND l.returned_on IS NULL
GROUP BY b.id;
--- hint
Start from the lesson's view: books, then copies, then only the open loans, all with `LEFT JOIN`. The earliest due date of the open loans is one more aggregate, `MIN(l.due_on)`, and it is NULL when nothing is out.
--- hint
The `CASE` checks the strictest case first: no copies at all (`COUNT(c.id) = 0`), then at least one available, and `'all out'` for the rest.
--- check query | Every book with its availability and next due date
SELECT book_id, title, available, next_due, status FROM book_status ORDER BY book_id
=> [[1, "Dune", 1, "2024-07-04", "available"], [2, "Emma", 0, "2024-07-15", "all out"], [3, "Ubik", 1, "2024-04-15", "available"], [4, "Kindred", 0, null, "no copies"]]
--- check query | Returning Emma makes it available, with nothing due
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-14' WHERE id = 4;
SELECT available, next_due, status FROM book_status WHERE book_id = 2
=> [[1, null, "available"]]
--- check query | Lending the last Dune on the shelf makes it all out
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on) VALUES (7, 3, 2, '2024-07-14', '2024-07-28');
SELECT available, next_due, status FROM book_status WHERE book_id = 1
=> [[0, "2024-07-04", "all out"]]

+++ practice | Copies, loans ever, and loans now
--- task
The library wants to know how popular each book is. Create a view named `title_counts` with one row per book, books with no copies included, and five columns:

1. `book_id` and 2. `title`.
3. `copies`: how many copies the book has.
4. `times_lent`: how many loans its copies have ever had, open or closed.
5. `on_loan`: how many of its copies are out right now.

Watch out: copy D-1 has been lent twice, so joining every loan repeats it.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW title_counts AS
SELECT b.id AS book_id, b.title,
       COUNT(c.id) AS copies,
       COUNT(l.id) AS times_lent,
       COUNT(l.id) AS on_loan
FROM books b
LEFT JOIN copies c ON c.book_id = b.id
LEFT JOIN loans l ON l.copy_id = c.id
GROUP BY b.id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW title_counts AS
SELECT b.id AS book_id, b.title,
       COUNT(DISTINCT c.id) AS copies,
       COUNT(l.id) AS times_lent,
       COUNT(CASE WHEN l.returned_on IS NULL THEN l.id END) AS on_loan
FROM books b
LEFT JOIN copies c ON c.book_id = b.id
LEFT JOIN loans l ON l.copy_id = c.id
GROUP BY b.id;
--- hint
To count every loan, the join cannot keep only the open ones, so D-1 becomes two rows. Each count must survive that: which one is inflated by the repeat?
--- hint
The copies count needs `COUNT(DISTINCT c.id)`. The loans count, `COUNT(l.id)`, is exactly right, since each loan appears once. For `on_loan`, count only open loans: `COUNT(CASE WHEN l.returned_on IS NULL THEN l.id END)`.
--- check query | Dune has 3 copies, lent 3 times, 2 out now
SELECT book_id, title, copies, times_lent, on_loan FROM title_counts ORDER BY book_id
=> [[1, "Dune", 3, 3, 2], [2, "Emma", 1, 1, 1], [3, "Ubik", 2, 2, 1], [4, "Kindred", 0, 0, 0]]
--- check query | Lending D-3 adds a loan, not a copy
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on) VALUES (7, 3, 2, '2024-07-14', '2024-07-28');
SELECT copies, times_lent, on_loan FROM title_counts WHERE book_id = 1
=> [[3, 4, 3]]
--- check query | A return keeps the loan in the history
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-15' WHERE id = 3;
SELECT copies, times_lent, on_loan FROM title_counts WHERE book_id = 1
=> [[3, 4, 2]]

+++ practice | Copies never lent
--- task
This query is meant to list the copies that have never been lent, with their `barcode` and `title`, sorted by `barcode`:

```sql
SELECT c.barcode, b.title
FROM copies c
JOIN books b ON b.id = c.book_id
LEFT JOIN loans l ON l.copy_id = c.id
WHERE l.id = NULL
ORDER BY c.barcode;
```

It runs without an error but returns no rows, although D-3 has never gone out. Fix it.
--- starter
SELECT c.barcode, b.title
FROM copies c
JOIN books b ON b.id = c.book_id
LEFT JOIN loans l ON l.copy_id = c.id
WHERE l.id = NULL
ORDER BY c.barcode;
--- solution
SELECT c.barcode, b.title
FROM copies c
JOIN books b ON b.id = c.book_id
LEFT JOIN loans l ON l.copy_id = c.id
WHERE l.id IS NULL
ORDER BY c.barcode;
--- hint
For D-3, the `LEFT JOIN` gives one row with NULL in `l.id`. What is `NULL = NULL` in SQL: true, false, or something else?
--- hint
Any comparison with `= NULL` is unknown, never true, so the `WHERE` keeps nothing. Test for NULL with `IS NULL`.
--- check result | Only D-3 has never been lent
ordered
[["D-3", "Dune"]]
--- check source | Tests for NULL with IS NULL
[Ii][Ss]\s+[Nn][Uu][Ll][Ll]
--- check source absent | No comparison with = NULL
=\s*[Nn][Uu][Ll][Ll]

+++ practice | Holds ready to fill
--- schema
CREATE TABLE members (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE copies (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  book_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES books(id),
  barcode TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  copy_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES copies(id),
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  loaned_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  due_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  returned_on TEXT,
  CHECK (due_on > loaned_on),
  CHECK (returned_on IS NULL OR returned_on >= loaned_on)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_copy ON loans(copy_id) WHERE returned_on IS NULL;
INSERT INTO members (id, name, email) VALUES (1, 'Ana', 'ana@mail.com'), (2, 'Bo', 'bo@mail.com'), (3, 'Cy', 'cy@mail.com');

INSERT INTO copies (id, book_id, barcode) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'D-1'), (2, 1, 'D-2'), (3, 1, 'D-3'), (4, 2, 'E-1'), (5, 3, 'U-1'), (6, 3, 'U-2');
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on, returned_on) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-06-01', '2024-06-15', '2024-06-14'),
  (2, 2, 2, '2024-06-20', '2024-07-04', NULL),

  (4, 4, 1, '2024-07-01', '2024-07-15', NULL),
  (5, 5, 2, '2024-05-01', '2024-05-15', '2024-05-30'),
  (6, 6, 1, '2024-04-01', '2024-04-15', NULL);
CREATE TABLE holds (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  book_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES books(id),
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  placed_on TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO holds (id, book_id, member_id, placed_on) VALUES
  (1, 2, 2, '2024-07-02'),
  (2, 2, 3, '2024-07-03'),
  (3, 1, 1, '2024-07-05'),
  (4, 1, 3, '2024-07-01'),
  (5, 3, 2, '2024-07-06'),
  (6, 4, 1, '2024-07-04');
--- task
This problem has the lesson's library data plus a new table, `holds` (`id`, `book_id`, `member_id`, `placed_on`): members waiting for a book. Holds are on books, not copies, and the member who placed a hold first is served first (on the same day, the smaller `id` first).

Create a view named `ready_holds` for the front desk, with three columns: `title`, `member` (the member's name) and `placed_on`. It lists, for each book that has **at least one copy available right now**, the one hold that should be filled next. Books with no available copy do not appear.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW ready_holds AS
SELECT b.title, m.name AS member, h.placed_on
FROM holds h
JOIN books b ON b.id = h.book_id
JOIN members m ON m.id = h.member_id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW ready_holds AS
WITH avail AS (
  SELECT b.id AS book_id, COUNT(c.id) - COUNT(l.id) AS available
  FROM books b
  LEFT JOIN copies c ON c.book_id = b.id
  LEFT JOIN loans l  ON l.copy_id = c.id AND l.returned_on IS NULL
  GROUP BY b.id
), first_hold AS (
  SELECT book_id, member_id, placed_on,
         ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY book_id ORDER BY placed_on, id) AS rn
  FROM holds
)
SELECT b.title, m.name AS member, h.placed_on
FROM first_hold h
JOIN avail a   ON a.book_id = h.book_id
JOIN books b   ON b.id = h.book_id
JOIN members m ON m.id = h.member_id
WHERE h.rn = 1 AND a.available > 0;
--- hint
Two questions, answered separately and then joined: how many copies of each book are available (the lesson's counting), and which hold is first for each book (a top-1-per-group question).
--- hint
Put each in its own CTE: `avail`, one row per book with `available`, and `first_hold`, numbering each book's holds with `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY book_id ORDER BY placed_on, id)`.
--- hint
The final `SELECT` joins `first_hold` to `avail`, `books` and `members`, and keeps `rn = 1 AND available > 0`.
--- check query | Cy is first for Dune and Bo for Ubik; Emma is all out and Kindred has no copies
SELECT title, member, placed_on FROM ready_holds ORDER BY title
=> [["Dune", "Cy", "2024-07-01"], ["Ubik", "Bo", "2024-07-06"]]
--- check query | When Emma comes back, Bo's hold is ready
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-10' WHERE id = 4;
SELECT title, member, placed_on FROM ready_holds ORDER BY title
=> [["Dune", "Cy", "2024-07-01"], ["Emma", "Bo", "2024-07-02"], ["Ubik", "Bo", "2024-07-06"]]
--- check query | When the last Dune on the shelf goes out, Dune drops off
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on) VALUES (7, 3, 2, '2024-07-10', '2024-07-24');
SELECT title FROM ready_holds ORDER BY title
=> [["Emma"], ["Ubik"]]

=== sqlp-11 | Library 3: overdue loans and fines
--- schema
CREATE TABLE members (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE copies (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  book_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES books(id),
  barcode TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  copy_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES copies(id),
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  loaned_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  due_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  returned_on TEXT,
  CHECK (due_on > loaned_on),
  CHECK (returned_on IS NULL OR returned_on >= loaned_on)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_copy ON loans(copy_id) WHERE returned_on IS NULL;
INSERT INTO members (id, name, email) VALUES (1, 'Ana', 'ana@mail.com'), (2, 'Bo', 'bo@mail.com'), (3, 'Cy', 'cy@mail.com');
INSERT INTO books (id, isbn, title, author) VALUES
  (1, '9780441013593', 'Dune',    'Frank Herbert'),
  (2, '9780141439587', 'Emma',    'Jane Austen'),
  (3, '9780547572291', 'Ubik',    'Philip K. Dick'),
  (4, '9780807083697', 'Kindred', 'Octavia E. Butler');
INSERT INTO copies (id, book_id, barcode) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'D-1'), (2, 1, 'D-2'), (3, 1, 'D-3'), (4, 2, 'E-1'), (5, 3, 'U-1'), (6, 3, 'U-2');
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on, returned_on) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-06-01', '2024-06-15', '2024-06-14'),
  (2, 2, 2, '2024-06-20', '2024-07-04', NULL),
  (3, 1, 3, '2024-06-25', '2024-07-09', NULL),
  (4, 4, 1, '2024-07-01', '2024-07-15', NULL),
  (5, 5, 2, '2024-05-01', '2024-05-15', '2024-05-30'),
  (6, 6, 1, '2024-04-01', '2024-04-15', NULL);
--- teach
Last lesson a view told the front desk which books can be borrowed. This lesson looks at the loans that should already have come back: the overdue report, with the fine each late loan has built up.

### The tables

The data is the same as last lesson. These are the four loans still out, with the member's name and the book's title filled in:

| loan | member | title | due_on |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 | Bo | Dune | 2024-07-04 |
| 3 | Cy | Dune | 2024-07-09 |
| 4 | Ana | Emma | 2024-07-15 |
| 6 | Ana | Ubik | 2024-04-15 |

### The rules

Every Monday the library emails members who have overdue books. Its rules:

- A loan is **overdue** when it is still out and its due date has passed.
- The fine is **25 cents per day** overdue, **capped at 10 dollars** (1000 cents) per loan. A **cap** is an upper limit: the fine grows each day until it reaches 1000, then [[stops growing|fine-cap]]. Nobody should owe more than a book is worth.

The fine is counted in whole cents, as in "Store 1: the schema", so every sum stays exact.

### Pick the report date

Think of a teacher marking homework late. "Late" only makes sense once you say *as of when*.

`date('now')`, from "Date arithmetic and date ranges", gives today's date. A report built on it is correct today and untestable forever: tomorrow it gives different numbers, and nobody can check yesterday's email. Reports that people check, compare and reproduce take the date as an [[input|report-date]]. Here the date is fixed: **as of 2024-07-15**.

### Which loans are overdue?

Two conditions, joined with `AND`: the loan is still out, and it was due **before** the report date. `<` means "less than", and for these dates that is "earlier than":

```sql
SELECT id, due_on FROM loans
WHERE returned_on IS NULL AND due_on < '2024-07-15'
ORDER BY id;
```

| id | due_on |
| --- | --- |
| 2 | 2024-07-04 |
| 3 | 2024-07-09 |
| 6 | 2024-04-15 |

Emma's loan 4 is not there. A book due on the 15th is not overdue on the 15th: the member has until the end of that day.

### Counting the days late

In "Date arithmetic and date ranges" you learned that `julianday(a) - julianday(b)` is the number of days between two dates:

```sql
SELECT julianday('2024-07-15') - julianday('2024-07-04') AS days,
       CAST(julianday('2024-07-15') - julianday('2024-07-04') AS INTEGER) AS whole_days;
```

| days | whole_days |
| --- | --- |
| 11.0 | 11 |

For whole dates like `'2024-07-15'` the difference is a whole number, but it is still a [[REAL|real-number]], a number with a decimal point. `CAST(… AS INTEGER)`, from "Problem solving: cohort retention", turns it into the integer 11 for display and arithmetic. Bo's loan is [[11 days overdue|overdue-timeline]].

### Capping the fine with MIN

Bo owes 11 × 25 = 275 cents. Ana's *Ubik* is 91 days late: 91 × 25 = 2275 cents, which is over the cap. The fine is the smaller of "days × 25" and 1000:

```sql
SELECT MIN(275, 1000) AS bo, MIN(2275, 1000) AS ana;
```

| bo | ana |
| --- | --- |
| 275 | 1000 |

In SQLite, `MIN` with two or more values inside the brackets is the ordinary "smaller of these values" function. It works on one row at a time. It is not the aggregate `MIN(x)` from the basics, which takes one column and finds the smallest value across many rows. The [[number of arguments|min-two-kinds]] decides which one you get. So the cap is `MIN(days * 25, 1000)`.

### The title is two joins away

A loan knows its `copy_id`, a copy knows its `book_id`, and the book knows its `title`. So the title is two joins away: loans → copies → books. The member's name is one more join, to `members`. This is the chain from "Joining three tables":

```sql
FROM loans l
JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
JOIN copies c  ON c.id = l.copy_id
JOIN books b   ON b.id = c.book_id
```

### Sorting

The report lists the biggest fines first: `ORDER BY fine_cents DESC`. When two fines are equal, it sorts by member name, so the order is always the same: `ORDER BY fine_cents DESC, member`. You can sort by a column's `AS` name.

**Watch out:** `due_on <= '2024-07-15'`, "on or before", looks close enough, but it pulls in Emma, due that very day. She would appear on an overdue email with 0 days and a fine of 0 cents. Use `<`.

::: context fine-cap Why fines have a limit
A fine is meant to nudge people to bring books back, not to punish them forever. Without a cap, a book forgotten for a year would cost 365 × 25 = 9125 cents, more than 90 dollars, far more than a new copy. Many libraries have gone further and dropped overdue fines altogether. Chicago Public Library did so in 2019, and found that many people who had stayed away because of old fines came back. The cap in this project is 1000 cents per loan.
:::

::: context report-date The date as an input
When the date is an input, running the same report twice gives the same answer, and anyone can check it. In an app, the program passes the date in as a parameter, the `?` you met in "Indexes and EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN": `due_on < ?`. On Monday it passes that Monday's date. When a member phones to say an email was wrong, you rerun the report with the same date and see exactly what it saw. Engineers replay spacecraft telemetry the same way: every calculation takes the time as an input, so a past moment can be recomputed exactly.
:::

::: context real-number Why julianday gives a REAL
A **REAL** is SQLite's type for numbers with a decimal point, stored as floating point. `julianday` returns one because a date can carry a time of day, and a time is a fraction of a day: 18:00 adds 0.75. That is why even whole dates give 11.0 rather than 11. `CAST(… AS INTEGER)` drops the part after the point: 11.75 becomes 11. For this project's whole dates that changes nothing. With times, `'2024-07-15 18:00:00'` against a due date of 4 July would count 11 whole days, not 12.
:::

::: context overdue-timeline Bo's loan on a timeline
Bo borrowed a copy of *Dune* on 20 June, due 14 days later on 4 July. The report looks from 15 July. The days between the due date and the report date are the days overdue: 11, so the fine is 11 × 25 = 275 cents.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 130" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="20" y1="60" x2="340" y2="60" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="40" y1="60" x2="180" y2="60" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="6"/>
  <line x1="180" y1="60" x2="290" y2="60" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="6"/>
  <circle cx="40" cy="60" r="5" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="180" cy="60" r="5" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="290" y1="44" x2="290" y2="76" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="40" y="86" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">lent 20 Jun</text>
  <text x="180" y="86" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">due 4 Jul</text>
  <text x="290" y="86" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">report 15 Jul</text>
  <text x="110" y="44" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">14 days on loan</text>
  <text x="235" y="44" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">11 days late</text>
  <text x="180" y="116" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">fine: 11 × 25 = 275 cents, under the 1000 cap</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context min-two-kinds Two MINs, and other databases
SQLite looks at how many values you pass. One, as in `MIN(due_on)`, is the aggregate: it squeezes many rows into one answer, and in this report it would collapse all the overdue loans into a single row. Two or more, as in `MIN(2275, 1000)`, is the ordinary function, worked out row by row. `MAX` has the same two kinds. Other databases keep them apart by name: PostgreSQL and MySQL call the row-by-row versions `LEAST` and `GREATEST`, so there you would write `LEAST(days * 25, 1000)`.
:::
--- task
Write the overdue report as of `2024-07-15`: one row for every loan that is still out and was due before that date. Its columns, in this order:

1. `member`: the member's name.
2. `title`: the book's title.
3. `due_on`: the loan's due date.
4. `days_overdue`: the whole days from `due_on` to `2024-07-15`, as an integer.
5. `fine_cents`: 25 cents per day overdue, at most 1000.

Sort by `fine_cents`, largest first, then by `member`.
--- starter
SELECT m.name AS member, l.due_on
FROM loans l
JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL;
--- solution
SELECT m.name AS member, b.title, l.due_on,
       CAST(julianday('2024-07-15') - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER) AS days_overdue,
       MIN(CAST(julianday('2024-07-15') - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000) AS fine_cents
FROM loans l
JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
JOIN copies c  ON c.id = l.copy_id
JOIN books b   ON b.id = c.book_id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL
  AND l.due_on < '2024-07-15'
ORDER BY fine_cents DESC, member;
--- hint
Overdue means `returned_on IS NULL AND due_on < '2024-07-15'`. The title is two joins away: add `JOIN copies c ON c.id = l.copy_id` and `JOIN books b ON b.id = c.book_id`.
--- hint
The days are `CAST(julianday('2024-07-15') - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER)`. Give that the name `days_overdue`.
--- hint
The fine repeats the days expression inside the two-value `MIN`: `MIN(CAST(julianday('2024-07-15') - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000) AS fine_cents`. End with `ORDER BY fine_cents DESC, member`.
--- check result | Ana's 91-day loan is capped; Emma, due today, is not overdue
ordered
[["Ana", "Ubik", "2024-04-15", 91, 1000], ["Bo", "Dune", "2024-07-04", 11, 275], ["Cy", "Dune", "2024-07-09", 6, 150]]

+++ practice | Due in the next two weeks
--- task
Before books go overdue, the library sends a friendly reminder. Write the reminder list as of `2024-07-05`: every loan that is still out and is due **on or after** that date and **no later than 14 days after it**. Work the last date out with `date('2024-07-05', '+14 days')` rather than typing it.

Return four columns, sorted by `due_on`:

1. `member`: the member's name.
2. `title`: the book's title.
3. `due_on`.
4. `days_left`: the whole days from `2024-07-05` to `due_on`, as an integer.
--- starter
SELECT m.name AS member, l.due_on
FROM loans l
JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
WHERE l.due_on >= '2024-07-05';
--- solution
SELECT m.name AS member, b.title, l.due_on,
       CAST(julianday(l.due_on) - julianday('2024-07-05') AS INTEGER) AS days_left
FROM loans l
JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
JOIN copies c  ON c.id = l.copy_id
JOIN books b   ON b.id = c.book_id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL
  AND l.due_on >= '2024-07-05'
  AND l.due_on <= date('2024-07-05', '+14 days')
ORDER BY l.due_on;
--- hint
Three conditions: the loan is still out, it is due on or after the report date, and it is due no later than 14 days after it. The title is two joins away, as in the lesson.
--- hint
The upper edge is `l.due_on <= date('2024-07-05', '+14 days')`. The days left count the other way from the lesson: `julianday(l.due_on) - julianday('2024-07-05')`, cast to an integer.
--- check result | Cy's Dune in 4 days and Ana's Emma in 10
ordered
[["Cy", "Dune", "2024-07-09", 4], ["Ana", "Emma", "2024-07-15", 10]]
--- check source | Counts days with julianday
julianday\s*\(
--- check source absent | Works out the end date instead of typing it
2024-07-19

+++ practice | What each member owes today
--- task
Write the fines summary as of `2024-07-08`: one row for **every** member, including members with nothing overdue, and three columns:

1. `member`: the name.
2. `overdue_loans`: how many of their loans are still out and were due before `2024-07-08`.
3. `fine`: the total of those loans' fines in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals, and 0 when they have none. Each loan's fine is the lesson's: 25 cents per whole day overdue, at most 1000 cents.

Sort by `member`.
--- starter
SELECT m.name AS member, COUNT(l.id) AS overdue_loans
FROM members m
JOIN loans l ON l.member_id = m.id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL AND l.due_on < '2024-07-08'
GROUP BY m.id
ORDER BY member;
--- solution
SELECT m.name AS member,
       COUNT(l.id) AS overdue_loans,
       ROUND(COALESCE(SUM(MIN(CAST(julianday('2024-07-08') - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000)), 0) / 100.0, 2) AS fine
FROM members m
LEFT JOIN loans l
  ON l.member_id = m.id
 AND l.returned_on IS NULL
 AND l.due_on < '2024-07-08'
GROUP BY m.id
ORDER BY member;
--- hint
Every member must appear, so start from `members` and `LEFT JOIN` only the overdue loans: both overdue conditions go in the `ON`.
--- hint
Each loan's fine is the lesson's two-value `MIN(days * 25, 1000)`. Add them up per member with the aggregate `SUM(…)` around it, then `COALESCE(…, 0)` for members with no rows, and divide by `100.0`.
--- check result | Ana's Ubik is capped, Bo's Dune is 4 days late, Cy owes nothing yet
ordered
[["Ana", 1, 10.0], ["Bo", 1, 1.0], ["Cy", 0, 0.0]]
--- check source | Caps each fine with a two-value MIN
[Mm][Ii][Nn]\s*\([^;]*,\s*1000\s*\)
--- check source | Keeps every member with a LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]

+++ practice | One date, typed once
--- task
A report date typed in three places will one day be changed in two of them. Write the overdue report as of `2024-07-20` with the date typed **exactly once**, in a CTE named `params` with one column, `as_of`, that the rest of the query joins to.

Return one row per overdue loan (still out, due before the report date) with four columns:

1. `member`: the name.
2. `title`.
3. `fine_cents`: 25 cents per whole day overdue, at most 1000.
4. `member_total`: the total of that member's `fine_cents` over all their overdue loans, shown on each of their rows.

Sort by `member`, then `title`.
--- starter
SELECT m.name AS member, b.title,
       MIN(CAST(julianday('2024-07-20') - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000) AS fine_cents
FROM loans l
JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
JOIN copies c  ON c.id = l.copy_id
JOIN books b   ON b.id = c.book_id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL AND l.due_on < '2024-07-20'
ORDER BY member, b.title;
--- solution
WITH params AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-20' AS as_of
), overdue AS (
  SELECT m.name AS member, b.title,
         MIN(CAST(julianday(p.as_of) - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000) AS fine_cents
  FROM loans l
  CROSS JOIN params p
  JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
  JOIN copies c  ON c.id = l.copy_id
  JOIN books b   ON b.id = c.book_id
  WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL AND l.due_on < p.as_of
)
SELECT member, title, fine_cents,
       SUM(fine_cents) OVER (PARTITION BY member) AS member_total
FROM overdue
ORDER BY member, title;
--- hint
A one-row CTE, `params`, holds the date. `CROSS JOIN params p` adds `p.as_of` to every loan without making more rows, the same trick as `last` in "Analytics 4: day-N retention".
--- hint
Compute each loan's fine in a second CTE, using `p.as_of` wherever the date was. The member's total then goes beside each row with a window: `SUM(fine_cents) OVER (PARTITION BY member)`.
--- check result | Ana owes for Emma and Ubik; Bo and Cy for Dune
ordered
[["Ana", "Emma", 125, 1125], ["Ana", "Ubik", 1000, 1125], ["Bo", "Dune", 400, 400], ["Cy", "Dune", 275, 275]]
--- check source | Puts the member's total on every row with a window
[Pp][Aa][Rr][Tt][Ii][Tt][Ii][Oo][Nn]\s+[Bb][Yy]
--- check source absent | The date is typed only once
2024-07-20[\s\S]*2024-07-20

+++ practice | On the cap, just under it, just past it
--- schema
CREATE TABLE loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  member TEXT NOT NULL,
  due_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  returned_on TEXT
);
INSERT INTO loans (id, member, due_on, returned_on) VALUES
  (1, 'Ana', '2024-07-24', NULL),
  (2, 'Bo',  '2024-07-23', NULL),
  (3, 'Cy',  '2024-07-22', NULL),
  (4, 'Dee', '2024-08-31', NULL),
  (5, 'Eli', '2024-09-01', NULL),
  (6, 'Fay', '2024-06-01', '2024-06-20'),
  (7, 'Gus', '2024-09-15', NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own table, `loans` (`id`, `member`, `due_on`, `returned_on`), built to test every edge of the fine rule. The rule is the lesson's: a loan is overdue when it is still out and due **before** the report date, and its fine is 25 cents per whole day overdue, capped at 1000 cents.

Write the report as of `2024-09-01` with three columns, `id`, `days_overdue` (an integer) and `fine_cents`, one row per overdue loan, sorted by `id`. Check your answer by hand: 39 days is 975 cents, 40 days reaches the cap exactly, and a loan due on the report date itself is not overdue.
--- starter
SELECT id,
       CAST(julianday('2024-09-01') - julianday(due_on) AS INTEGER) AS days_overdue,
       CAST(julianday('2024-09-01') - julianday(due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25 AS fine_cents
FROM loans
WHERE due_on <= '2024-09-01'
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id,
       CAST(julianday('2024-09-01') - julianday(due_on) AS INTEGER) AS days_overdue,
       MIN(CAST(julianday('2024-09-01') - julianday(due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000) AS fine_cents
FROM loans
WHERE returned_on IS NULL
  AND due_on < '2024-09-01'
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
The starter has three problems: it includes a returned loan, it includes the loan due on the report day, and nothing caps the fine.
--- hint
Filter with `returned_on IS NULL AND due_on < '2024-09-01'`, and cap with the two-value `MIN(days * 25, 1000)`.
--- check result | 975 just under the cap, 1000 on and past it, 25 for one day

+++ practice | The cap that became a floor
--- task
This overdue report as of `2024-07-12` is meant to cap each fine at 1000 cents. Instead, Ana's fine comes out as 2200 cents, and the two loans that are only a few days late show 1000 each:

```sql
SELECT m.name AS member, b.title,
       MAX(CAST(julianday('2024-07-12') - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000) AS fine_cents
FROM loans l
JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
JOIN copies c  ON c.id = l.copy_id
JOIN books b   ON b.id = c.book_id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL AND l.due_on < '2024-07-12'
ORDER BY fine_cents DESC, member;
```

Fix it. Keep the three columns and the sort.
--- starter
SELECT m.name AS member, b.title,
       MAX(CAST(julianday('2024-07-12') - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000) AS fine_cents
FROM loans l
JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
JOIN copies c  ON c.id = l.copy_id
JOIN books b   ON b.id = c.book_id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL AND l.due_on < '2024-07-12'
ORDER BY fine_cents DESC, member;
--- solution
SELECT m.name AS member, b.title,
       MIN(CAST(julianday('2024-07-12') - julianday(l.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000) AS fine_cents
FROM loans l
JOIN members m ON m.id = l.member_id
JOIN copies c  ON c.id = l.copy_id
JOIN books b   ON b.id = c.book_id
WHERE l.returned_on IS NULL AND l.due_on < '2024-07-12'
ORDER BY fine_cents DESC, member;
--- hint
Bo's loan is 8 days late, so 200 cents. Which is bigger, 200 or 1000, and which one does `MAX` pick?
--- hint
A cap keeps the **smaller** of the two values: the two-value `MIN`. `MAX(x, 1000)` makes 1000 the least anyone pays.
--- check result | Ana capped at 1000, Bo 8 days late, Cy 3 days late
ordered
[["Ana", "Ubik", 1000], ["Bo", "Dune", 200], ["Cy", "Dune", 75]]
--- check source absent | No MAX left in the fine
[Mm][Aa][Xx]\s*\(
