+++ practice | How old are the overdue loans?
--- task
Accountants sort unpaid bills into **aging buckets** by how late they are. Do the same for overdue loans as of `2024-07-10` (still out, due before that date). Return exactly three rows, one per bucket, even when a bucket is empty:

| bucket | days overdue |
| --- | --- |
| `'1-7 days'` | 1 to 7 |
| `'8-30 days'` | 8 to 30 |
| `'over 30 days'` | 31 or more |

Three columns: `bucket`, `loans` (how many overdue loans fall in it) and `fine_cents` (the total of their fines, each 25 cents per day, capped at 1000; 0 for an empty bucket). Sort from the youngest bucket to the oldest.
--- starter
SELECT CASE WHEN julianday('2024-07-10') - julianday(due_on) <= 7 THEN '1-7 days'
            WHEN julianday('2024-07-10') - julianday(due_on) <= 30 THEN '8-30 days'
            ELSE 'over 30 days' END AS bucket,
       COUNT(*) AS loans
FROM loans
WHERE returned_on IS NULL AND due_on < '2024-07-10'
GROUP BY bucket
ORDER BY bucket;
--- solution
WITH buckets(n, bucket) AS (
  SELECT 1, '1-7 days'
  UNION ALL SELECT 2, '8-30 days'
  UNION ALL SELECT 3, 'over 30 days'
), overdue AS (
  SELECT CAST(julianday('2024-07-10') - julianday(due_on) AS INTEGER) AS days
  FROM loans
  WHERE returned_on IS NULL AND due_on < '2024-07-10'
), tagged AS (
  SELECT CASE WHEN days <= 7 THEN 1 WHEN days <= 30 THEN 2 ELSE 3 END AS n,
         MIN(days * 25, 1000) AS fine
  FROM overdue
)
SELECT b.bucket, COUNT(t.n) AS loans, COALESCE(SUM(t.fine), 0) AS fine_cents
FROM buckets b
LEFT JOIN tagged t ON t.n = b.n
GROUP BY b.n, b.bucket
ORDER BY b.n;
--- hint
An empty bucket has no loans, so grouping the loans can never produce its row. The buckets need a list of their own, which the loans are then joined onto, like the calendar in "Analytics 2: daily active users".
--- hint
Write the three buckets as a small CTE with a number for sorting: `WITH buckets(n, bucket) AS (SELECT 1, '1-7 days' UNION ALL …)`. Tag each overdue loan with its bucket number and its capped fine in another CTE.
--- hint
Finish with `FROM buckets b LEFT JOIN tagged t ON t.n = b.n`, grouped by bucket, counting `t.n` (not `*`) and wrapping the sum in `COALESCE(…, 0)`.
--- check result | Two young loans, none in the middle, Ana's Ubik over 30 days
ordered
[["1-7 days", 2, 175], ["8-30 days", 0, 0], ["over 30 days", 1, 1000]]
--- check source | Caps each fine with a two-value MIN
[Mm][Ii][Nn]\s*\([^;]*,\s*1000\s*\)
--- check source | Keeps empty buckets with a LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]

=== sqlp-12 | Library 4: returns that charge fines
--- schema
CREATE TABLE members (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE copies (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  book_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES books(id),
  barcode TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  copy_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES copies(id),
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  loaned_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  due_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  returned_on TEXT,
  CHECK (due_on > loaned_on),
  CHECK (returned_on IS NULL OR returned_on >= loaned_on)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_copy ON loans(copy_id) WHERE returned_on IS NULL;
INSERT INTO members (id, name, email) VALUES (1, 'Ana', 'ana@mail.com'), (2, 'Bo', 'bo@mail.com'), (3, 'Cy', 'cy@mail.com');
INSERT INTO books (id, isbn, title, author) VALUES
  (1, '9780441013593', 'Dune',    'Frank Herbert'),
  (2, '9780141439587', 'Emma',    'Jane Austen'),
  (3, '9780547572291', 'Ubik',    'Philip K. Dick'),
  (4, '9780807083697', 'Kindred', 'Octavia E. Butler');
INSERT INTO copies (id, book_id, barcode) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'D-1'), (2, 1, 'D-2'), (3, 1, 'D-3'), (4, 2, 'E-1'), (5, 3, 'U-1'), (6, 3, 'U-2');
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on, returned_on) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-06-01', '2024-06-15', '2024-06-14'),
  (2, 2, 2, '2024-06-20', '2024-07-04', NULL),
  (3, 1, 3, '2024-06-25', '2024-07-09', NULL),
  (4, 4, 1, '2024-07-01', '2024-07-15', NULL),
  (5, 5, 2, '2024-05-01', '2024-05-15', '2024-05-30'),
  (6, 6, 1, '2024-04-01', '2024-04-15', NULL);
CREATE TABLE fines (
  loan_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES loans(id),
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (cents > 0)
);
--- teach
Last lesson's report showed fines building up while books are out. When a late book comes back, the fine becomes **real**: money the member now owes. This last library lesson records it by itself, at the moment of the return.

### The tables

`members`, `books`, `copies` and `loans` hold the same data as last lesson. There is one new table, `fines`, and it starts empty:

```sql
CREATE TABLE fines (
  loan_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES loans(id),
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (cents > 0)
);
```

- `loan_id` says which loan the fine is for. It is also the primary key, so each loan can have at most one fine.
- `cents` is the amount, and it must be more than 0.

### The picture

Think of a car park with a ticket barrier. Whichever exit you drive out of, the machine reads your ticket and works out what you owe. Nobody at the exit has to remember to charge you, and every exit charges by the same rule.

### Why a trigger

Returns are recorded in several places: the front desk, the self-service kiosk, and a librarian fixing a typo. Each is a different program. The fine must be recorded whichever one sets the return date.

"Whenever this column changes, do that" is a trigger, from "Triggers: reacting to changes". Here is its shape. The `…` is the amount, which comes a few steps below:

```sql
CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents) VALUES (NEW.id, …);
END;
```

Take it one line at a time.

### Only when the return date is set

`AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans` means: after an `UPDATE` on `loans` that [[sets the returned_on column|update-of]]. An update that edits only the due date does not fire it.

### Only on the actual return

`OLD.returned_on IS NULL` looks at the row *before* the change. It is true only when the loan was still out. So the trigger fires on the return itself, and not when someone later corrects the date of a loan that had already come back.

### Only when late

`AND NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on` looks at the row *after* the change. It is true only when the copy came back after its due date. A copy returned early, or on its due date, owes nothing. And the table's `CHECK (cents > 0)` would [[refuse a zero fine|zero-fine]] anyway.

### The amount

The fine uses the same rule as the report: 25 cents per day late, capped at 1000. The only change is the date. `NEW.returned_on`, the day the copy came back, replaces the fixed report date:

```sql
MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000)
```

For Bo's loan 2, due on 4 July and returned on 15 July, that is 11 days, so MIN(11 × 25, 1000) = MIN(275, 1000) = 275 cents.

### Watching it fire

Return Bo's *Dune* (loan 2) and Ana's *Ubik* (loan 6), both on 15 July, in one statement:

```sql
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-15' WHERE id IN (2, 6);
SELECT loan_id, cents FROM fines ORDER BY loan_id;
```

| loan_id | cents |
| --- | --- |
| 2 | 275 |
| 6 | 1000 |

The trigger fired once for each row. Ana's *Ubik* was 91 days late, so its fine is capped at 1000. Now return Ana's *Emma* (loan 4) on 15 July, its due date. It is on time, the `WHEN` is false, and `fines` still has two rows.

### A fine stays

A fine is money owed, so it is [[kept even if the loan record is edited later|ledger]]. Nothing in this design deletes a fine. And because `loan_id` is the primary key of `fines`, one loan can only ever be fined once.

**Watch out:** leave out `OLD.returned_on IS NULL` and every edit of a return date fires the trigger again. Say a librarian corrects loan 2's return from 15 July to 14 July. The trigger tries to insert a second fine for loan 2, the primary key refuses it with `UNIQUE constraint failed: fines.loan_id`, and the librarian's correction fails with it. Worse, correcting an old loan that came back late before the trigger existed, such as loan 5, would suddenly charge a fine for it.

::: context update-of What counts as setting a column
`UPDATE OF returned_on` fires when `returned_on` is one of the columns an `UPDATE` assigns in its `SET`, even if the new value equals the old one. `UPDATE loans SET returned_on = returned_on` fires it, and changes nothing. `UPDATE loans SET due_on = '2024-07-20'` does not fire it at all. So `UPDATE OF` narrows *which statements* wake the trigger, and the `WHEN` then decides, row by row, whether it does anything.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 200" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="fl" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <rect x="40" y="10" width="220" height="30" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="150" y="30" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">an UPDATE sets returned_on</text>
  <rect x="40" y="60" width="220" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="150" y="80" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">was it out? old value NULL</text>
  <rect x="40" y="110" width="220" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="150" y="130" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">late? new date after due_on</text>
  <rect x="40" y="160" width="220" height="30" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="150" y="180" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">INSERT the fine into fines</text>
  <line x1="150" y1="40" x2="150" y2="58" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#fl)"/>
  <line x1="150" y1="90" x2="150" y2="108" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#fl)"/>
  <line x1="150" y1="140" x2="150" y2="158" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#fl)"/>
  <text x="140" y="104" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="end">yes</text>
  <text x="140" y="154" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="end">yes</text>
  <text x="270" y="80" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">no: stop</text>
  <text x="270" y="130" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">no: stop</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context zero-fine Why the late test matters twice
Without `NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on`, returning Emma on her due date would make the trigger insert a fine of 0 cents. The `CHECK (cents > 0)` refuses it, and because a trigger runs inside the same statement as the change, the error cancels the whole `UPDATE`. The return itself would fail, and the desk could never check an on-time book back in. So the `WHEN` is not only tidier: it keeps on-time returns working. The `CHECK` stays as a second line of defense.
:::

::: context ledger Money is never quietly rewritten
Accountants keep a **ledger**: a list of every amount charged or paid, where old entries are never erased. A mistake is fixed by adding a new entry, such as a refund, so the history shows what happened and when. The `fines` table works the same way. The trigger only ever adds rows. And `fines.loan_id` references `loans(id)` with the default `ON DELETE` action, so with foreign keys switched on, a loan that has a fine cannot be deleted. The capstones that come next give you three more specifications to design this carefully, with no step-by-step lessons.
:::
--- task
Create a trigger named `loan_returned` on `loans` that records late-return fines:

1. It runs after an update of the `returned_on` column.
2. It acts only when the loan was still out before the change (the old `returned_on` was NULL) and the new `returned_on` is after `due_on`.
3. It inserts one row into `fines`: `loan_id` is the loan's id, and `cents` is 25 per whole day from `due_on` to the new `returned_on`, at most 1000.
--- starter
CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
BEGIN
  SELECT 1;
END;
--- solution
CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000));
END;
--- hint
Put a `WHEN` line between `AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans` and `BEGIN`, so the trigger fires only on a real, late return: `WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on`.
--- hint
Replace the starter's `SELECT 1;` with an `INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents) VALUES (NEW.id, …);`.
--- hint
The amount is last lesson's formula with `NEW.returned_on` in place of the report date: `MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000)`.
--- check query | A late return is fined per day; a very late one is capped
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-15' WHERE id IN (2, 6);
SELECT loan_id, cents FROM fines ORDER BY loan_id
=> [[2, 275], [6, 1000]]
--- check query | An on-time return is not fined
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-15' WHERE id = 4;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 4
=> [[0]]
--- check query | Editing a loan that was already returned charges nothing more
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-06-01' WHERE id = 5;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fines
=> [[2]]
--- check query | One day late costs 25 cents
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-10' WHERE id = 3;
SELECT cents FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 3
=> [[25]]

+++ practice | A refund recorded by itself
--- schema
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))
);
CREATE TABLE order_items (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id),
  product TEXT NOT NULL,
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  unit_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (unit_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product)
);
CREATE TABLE refunds (
  order_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES orders(id),
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (cents > 0)
);
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer, status) VALUES (1, 'ana', 'paid'), (2, 'bo', 'shipped'), (3, 'cy', 'paid'), (4, 'dee', 'refunded');
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product, qty, unit_cents) VALUES
  (1, 'MUG', 2, 900), (1, 'PEN', 1, 3250), (2, 'LAMP', 1, 4500), (3, 'NOTE', 3, 435), (4, 'PEN', 2, 3250);
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `orders` (`id`, `customer`, `status`), `order_items` (`order_id`, `product`, `qty`, `unit_cents`) and an empty `refunds` (`order_id`, `cents`), where `order_id` is the primary key. Order 4 was refunded long ago, before `refunds` existed.

Create a trigger named `order_refunded` that records a refund whenever an order **becomes** refunded:

1. It runs after an update of the `status` column of `orders`.
2. It acts only when the old status was not `'refunded'` and the new one is.
3. It inserts one row into `refunds`: the order's `id`, and `cents`, the total of `qty * unit_cents` over that order's lines.

Saving an order that is already refunded, or moving an order from `'paid'` to `'shipped'`, must record nothing.
--- starter
CREATE TRIGGER order_refunded
AFTER UPDATE OF status ON orders
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO refunds (order_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, (SELECT SUM(qty * unit_cents) FROM order_items WHERE order_id = NEW.id));
END;
--- solution
CREATE TRIGGER order_refunded
AFTER UPDATE OF status ON orders
WHEN OLD.status <> 'refunded' AND NEW.status = 'refunded'
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO refunds (order_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, (SELECT SUM(qty * unit_cents) FROM order_items WHERE order_id = NEW.id));
END;
--- hint
The starter records a refund on **every** status change. The lesson's trigger narrowed itself with a `WHEN` that looked at both the old and the new row.
--- hint
Put `WHEN OLD.status <> 'refunded' AND NEW.status = 'refunded'` between the `AFTER UPDATE OF …` line and `BEGIN`.
--- check query | Refunding an order records its total
UPDATE orders SET status = 'refunded' WHERE id = 1;
SELECT order_id, cents FROM refunds
=> [[1, 5050]]
--- check query | Shipping an order records nothing
UPDATE orders SET status = 'shipped' WHERE id = 3;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM refunds WHERE order_id = 3
=> [[0]]
--- check query | Saving an order that was already refunded records nothing more
UPDATE orders SET status = 'refunded' WHERE id IN (2, 4);
SELECT order_id, cents FROM refunds ORDER BY order_id
=> [[1, 5050], [2, 4500]]

+++ practice | A late loan typed in afterwards
--- task
Sometimes the system is down and a librarian types a loan in later, already returned. If that loan came back late, it must be fined too, and the lesson's trigger never sees it, because it watches updates, not inserts.

Create a second trigger named `loan_entered_late` on `loans`:

1. It runs after an insert into `loans`.
2. It acts only when the new loan already has a `returned_on` that is after its `due_on`.
3. It inserts one row into `fines`: the loan's id, and 25 cents per whole day from `due_on` to `returned_on`, at most 1000.

An open loan, or one typed in as returned on time, must be stored without a fine and without an error.
--- starter
CREATE TRIGGER loan_entered_late
AFTER INSERT ON loans
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000));
END;
--- solution
CREATE TRIGGER loan_entered_late
AFTER INSERT ON loans
WHEN NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000));
END;
--- hint
An insert has no old row, only `NEW`. So the test is only about the new row: was it returned, and returned late?
--- hint
`WHEN NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on` covers both. For an open loan, `returned_on` is NULL, the comparison is NULL rather than true, and the trigger does nothing.
--- check query | A loan typed in five days late is fined 125 cents
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on, returned_on) VALUES (7, 3, 2, '2024-05-01', '2024-05-15', '2024-05-20');
SELECT loan_id, cents FROM fines
=> [[7, 125]]
--- check query | A new open loan is stored without a fine
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on) VALUES (8, 5, 3, '2024-07-10', '2024-07-24');
SELECT (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM loans WHERE id = 8), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 8)
=> [[1, 0]]
--- check query | A loan typed in as returned on its due date is not fined
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on, returned_on) VALUES (9, 3, 1, '2024-06-01', '2024-06-15', '2024-06-15');
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 9
=> [[0]]
--- check query | A very late one is capped
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on, returned_on) VALUES (10, 3, 3, '2024-01-01', '2024-01-15', '2024-06-01');
SELECT cents FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 10
=> [[1000]]

+++ practice | A running total per member
--- task
The desk wants to see each member's total fines without adding them up every time. The starter creates `member_totals` (`member_id` primary key, `fined_cents`), empty. Keep it, and create a trigger named `loan_returned_total` on `loans` that keeps it up to date:

1. It runs after an update of `returned_on`, and acts only on a real, late return: the old `returned_on` was NULL and the new one is after `due_on`.
2. It adds that loan's fine (25 cents per whole day late, at most 1000) to the member's row in `member_totals`. If the member has no row yet, it creates one.

Do both in one statement inside the trigger: an upsert, `INSERT … ON CONFLICT (member_id) DO UPDATE`.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE member_totals (
  member_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES members(id),
  fined_cents INTEGER NOT NULL
);
--- solution
CREATE TABLE member_totals (
  member_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES members(id),
  fined_cents INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned_total
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO member_totals (member_id, fined_cents)
  VALUES (NEW.member_id, MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000))
  ON CONFLICT (member_id) DO UPDATE SET fined_cents = fined_cents + excluded.fined_cents;
END;
--- hint
The trigger's shape is the lesson's: the same `AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on`, the same `WHEN`, the same fine. Only the statement inside changes.
--- hint
Inside, insert the member and the fine. When the member already has a row, the primary key clashes, and `ON CONFLICT (member_id) DO UPDATE SET fined_cents = fined_cents + excluded.fined_cents` adds the new fine instead. `excluded.fined_cents` is the fine that tried to go in.
--- check query | Two late returns start two totals
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-15' WHERE id IN (2, 6);
SELECT member_id, fined_cents FROM member_totals ORDER BY member_id
=> [[1, 1000], [2, 275]]
--- check query | A second late return adds to the member's total
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-20' WHERE id = 4;
SELECT fined_cents FROM member_totals WHERE member_id = 1
=> [[1125]]
--- check query | An on-time return, or an edit of an old return, adds nothing
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-09' WHERE id = 3;
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-06-01' WHERE id = 5;
SELECT COUNT(*), SUM(fined_cents) FROM member_totals
=> [[2, 1400]]

+++ practice | Returned, undone, returned again
--- schema
CREATE TABLE loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  due_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  returned_on TEXT
);
CREATE TABLE fines (
  loan_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES loans(id),
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (cents > 0)
);
INSERT INTO loans (id, due_on, returned_on) VALUES
  (1, '2024-07-01', NULL), (2, '2024-07-01', NULL), (3, '2024-07-01', NULL),
  (4, '2024-07-01', NULL), (5, '2024-07-01', NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `loans` (`id`, `due_on`, `returned_on`) and an empty `fines` (`loan_id` primary key, `cents` above 0). Every loan was due on 1 July.

Create a trigger named `return_fine` that fines a late return: after an update of `returned_on`, when the old value was NULL and the new one is after `due_on`, insert the loan's fine (25 cents per whole day late, at most 1000).

One more rule, from the desk: sometimes a return is scanned by mistake, undone (set back to NULL), and scanned again later. A loan is fined **at most once**, and the first fine stands. Scanning it again must not fail and must not change the fine.
--- starter
CREATE TRIGGER return_fine
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000));
END;
--- solution
CREATE TRIGGER return_fine
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT OR IGNORE INTO fines (loan_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000));
END;
--- hint
Trace the undo: the return sets a date (fine inserted), the undo sets NULL, the second scan sets a date again. Is the old value NULL on that second scan? What happens when the trigger inserts a second fine for the same loan?
--- hint
The primary key on `fines.loan_id` refuses the second fine, and that error would cancel the scan. `INSERT OR IGNORE`, from "Constraints: rules the database enforces", skips it quietly, so the first fine stands.
--- check query | On time is free; 1 day, 39 days and 40 days are fined
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = CASE id WHEN 1 THEN '2024-07-01' WHEN 2 THEN '2024-07-02' WHEN 3 THEN '2024-08-09' WHEN 4 THEN '2024-08-10' END WHERE id IN (1, 2, 3, 4);
SELECT loan_id, cents FROM fines ORDER BY loan_id
=> [[2, 25], [3, 975], [4, 1000]]
--- check query | Undo and scan again: the scan works and the first fine stands
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = NULL WHERE id = 2;
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-05' WHERE id = 2;
SELECT l.returned_on, f.cents FROM loans l JOIN fines f ON f.loan_id = l.id WHERE l.id = 2
=> [["2024-07-05", 25]]
--- check query | Changing the due date of an open loan charges nothing
UPDATE loans SET due_on = '2024-06-01' WHERE id = 5;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 5
=> [[0]]

+++ practice | The return the desk could not save
--- task
This version of the lesson's trigger was meant to fine late returns only:

```sql
CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on >= NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000));
END;
```

Late returns are fined correctly, but when Ana brings *Emma* back on its due date, the desk cannot record the return at all: the update fails with `CHECK constraint failed: cents > 0`. Fix the trigger.
--- starter
CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on >= NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000));
END;
--- solution
CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000));
END;
--- hint
Work out the fine the trigger tries to insert for a return on the due date itself. What does `fines` say about an amount like that?
--- hint
A return on the due date is 0 days late, so the fine is 0, and `CHECK (cents > 0)` refuses it, cancelling the whole update. The `WHEN` should let only strictly late returns through: `>`, not `>=`.
--- check query | Emma comes back on its due date: saved, and no fine
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-15' WHERE id = 4;
SELECT (SELECT returned_on FROM loans WHERE id = 4), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 4)
=> [["2024-07-15", 0]]
--- check query | Cy's Dune three days late costs 75 cents
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-12' WHERE id = 3;
SELECT cents FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 3
=> [[75]]
--- check query | Bo's Dune one day late costs 25 cents
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-05' WHERE id = 2;
SELECT cents FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 2
=> [[25]]

+++ practice | Late, damaged, or both
--- schema
CREATE TABLE members (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE books (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  isbn TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  author TEXT NOT NULL,
  replace_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (replace_cents > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE copies (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  book_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES books(id),
  barcode TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE loans (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  copy_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES copies(id),
  member_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES members(id),
  loaned_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  due_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  returned_on TEXT,
  damaged INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (damaged IN (0, 1)),
  CHECK (due_on > loaned_on),
  CHECK (returned_on IS NULL OR returned_on >= loaned_on)
);
CREATE TABLE fines (
  loan_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES loans(id),
  reason TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (reason IN ('late', 'damage')),
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (cents > 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (loan_id, reason)
);
INSERT INTO members (id, name, email) VALUES (1, 'Ana', 'ana@mail.com'), (2, 'Bo', 'bo@mail.com'), (3, 'Cy', 'cy@mail.com');
INSERT INTO books (id, isbn, title, author, replace_cents) VALUES
  (1, '9780441013593', 'Dune',    'Frank Herbert',     1800),
  (2, '9780141439587', 'Emma',    'Jane Austen',       1200),
  (3, '9780547572291', 'Ubik',    'Philip K. Dick',    1500),
  (4, '9780807083697', 'Kindred', 'Octavia E. Butler', 2000);
INSERT INTO copies (id, book_id, barcode) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'D-1'), (2, 1, 'D-2'), (3, 1, 'D-3'), (4, 2, 'E-1'), (5, 3, 'U-1'), (6, 3, 'U-2');
INSERT INTO loans (id, copy_id, member_id, loaned_on, due_on, returned_on) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-06-01', '2024-06-15', '2024-06-14'),
  (2, 2, 2, '2024-06-20', '2024-07-04', NULL),
  (3, 1, 3, '2024-06-25', '2024-07-09', NULL),
  (4, 4, 1, '2024-07-01', '2024-07-15', NULL),
  (5, 5, 2, '2024-05-01', '2024-05-15', '2024-05-30'),
  (6, 6, 1, '2024-04-01', '2024-04-15', NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own copy of the library with two additions: each book has a `replace_cents` (what a new copy costs), and each loan has a `damaged` flag (0 or 1). The desk sets `returned_on` and `damaged` in the **same** `UPDATE` when a copy comes back. `fines` now has a `reason` (`'late'` or `'damage'`), and its primary key is `(loan_id, reason)`: at most one fine of each kind per loan.

Create a trigger named `loan_returned` that, on a real return (the old `returned_on` was NULL):

1. inserts a `'late'` fine if the copy came back after `due_on`: 25 cents per whole day, at most 1000;
2. inserts a `'damage'` fine if `damaged` is 1: the `replace_cents` of the copy's book (two joins away: copy, then book).

A return can get one fine, both, or none. Editing a loan that was already returned charges nothing.
--- starter
CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL AND NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, reason, cents)
  VALUES (NEW.id, 'late', MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000));
END;
--- solution
CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN OLD.returned_on IS NULL
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, reason, cents)
  SELECT NEW.id, 'late', MIN(CAST(julianday(NEW.returned_on) - julianday(NEW.due_on) AS INTEGER) * 25, 1000)
  WHERE NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on;

  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, reason, cents)
  SELECT NEW.id, 'damage', b.replace_cents
  FROM copies c
  JOIN books b ON b.id = c.book_id
  WHERE c.id = NEW.copy_id AND NEW.damaged = 1;
END;
--- hint
The trigger's `WHEN` can only hold what both fines share: a real return. Each fine then needs its own condition, inside the body.
--- hint
An `INSERT … SELECT` with a `WHERE` inserts nothing when the `WHERE` is false. So each fine becomes `INSERT INTO fines (…) SELECT … WHERE …;`, one after the other between `BEGIN` and `END`.
--- hint
The damage fine reads the price through the copy: `SELECT NEW.id, 'damage', b.replace_cents FROM copies c JOIN books b ON b.id = c.book_id WHERE c.id = NEW.copy_id AND NEW.damaged = 1`.
--- check query | Late and damaged: two fines
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-15', damaged = 1 WHERE id = 2;
SELECT loan_id, reason, cents FROM fines ORDER BY loan_id, reason
=> [[2, "damage", 1800], [2, "late", 275]]
--- check query | On time but damaged: only the damage fine
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-15', damaged = 1 WHERE id = 4;
SELECT reason, cents FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 4
=> [["damage", 1200]]
--- check query | Late but undamaged: only the late fine
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-11' WHERE id = 3;
SELECT reason, cents FROM fines WHERE loan_id = 3
=> [["late", 50]]
--- check query | Editing a loan already returned charges nothing
UPDATE loans SET returned_on = '2024-07-16', damaged = 1 WHERE id = 3;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fines
=> [[4]]

=== sqlp-13 | Capstone: event ticketing
--- schema
CREATE TABLE venues (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  capacity INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (capacity > 0)
);
INSERT INTO venues (id, name, capacity) VALUES (1, 'Hall', 3), (2, 'Club', 100);
--- teach
Last lesson a trigger charged a fine the moment a late book came back, and the library was finished. That was the third project built step by step. Now come the three capstones promised at the start of the course. In each one you design a whole database yourself, from a written description.

### How a capstone works

Until now, each lesson showed you the pieces and then asked for them. A capstone does not. You get a **specification**, or **spec**: a [[numbered list of what the database must do|spec]], with no code in it. There is no worked example, and the starter is almost empty.

The checks then use your database the way an app would. They insert good rows, try bad ones, and read your view. They rely only on what the spec states: the **table names, the column names and how things must behave**. Everything else is yours to decide: the names of your constraints, which checks you write, how you build the view.

You may add extra columns of your own, as long as each one has a `DEFAULT` or allows NULL. The checks insert rows that name only the spec's columns, so any other column must be able to fill itself in.

### Work like you would on the job

1. **Read the whole spec first.** List the tables, their columns and every rule.
2. **Decide where each rule lives.** A rule the database can enforce (a unique value, a required value, a range, a link to another table) becomes a constraint. A number other people read becomes a view.
3. **Write the tables, then try to break them.** Insert good rows, then bad ones, and see each bad one refused.
4. **Build the view last**, and check it by hand against a few rows.

### The table you start with

This spec is a small ticketing system: a venue hosts events, and people buy tickets for seats. The list of venues already exists, as `venues`:

| id | name | capacity |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Hall | 3 |
| 2 | Club | 100 |

`capacity` is how many seats the venue has. The table has `CHECK (capacity > 0)`, so every venue has at least one seat.

### The spec

1. Foreign keys are enforced.
2. `events`: `id` (integer primary key), `venue_id` (required, and it must be a real venue), `title` (required), `starts_at` (required; a timestamp stored as text, like `'2024-09-01 19:00'`).
3. `tickets`: `id` (integer primary key), `event_id` (required, a real event), `seat` (required text, like `'A1'`), `buyer_email` (required), `price_cents` (required integer, zero or more).
4. **A seat can be [[sold only once per event|one-seat]].** The same seat at a different event is fine.
5. Deleting an event [[deletes its tickets|cancel-history]].
6. A view `event_sales` with one row per event, **including events with no tickets sold**, and these columns:
   - `event_id` and `title`
   - `sold`: how many tickets the event has sold
   - `capacity`: its venue's capacity
   - `remaining`: capacity minus sold
   - `revenue_cents`: the total of its ticket prices, and 0 when none are sold

### Where each tool comes from

You have met every tool this spec needs. Here is where:

| spec | tool | lesson |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1, 2, 3: a real venue, a real event | `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON`, `REFERENCES` | "Foreign keys" |
| 2, 3: required, zero or more | `NOT NULL`, `CHECK` | "Constraints: rules the database enforces" |
| 4: once per event | a rule across two columns, `UNIQUE (…, …)` | "Constraints: rules the database enforces" |
| 5: delete with the event | `ON DELETE CASCADE` | "Foreign keys" |
| 6: the view | `CREATE VIEW` | "Views: saved queries" |
| 6: unsold events too | `LEFT JOIN`, counting a column | "LEFT JOIN: where the filter goes" |
| 6: 0, not NULL | `COALESCE` | "Testing for NULL" |

"Store 1: the schema" used most of these together. It also stored money as whole cents, the same way `price_cents` does here.

### Try to break it

The quickest way to test a rule is to [[break it on purpose|break-it]]. From "Constraints: rules the database enforces": `INSERT OR IGNORE` skips a row that breaks a rule, and `RETURNING id` hands back only the rows that got in. Try it on `venues`, which refuses a capacity of 0:

```sql
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO venues (id, name, capacity) VALUES (3, 'Shed', 0) RETURNING id;
```

No rows come back: the row was refused. Change the capacity to 40 and run it again, and one row comes back: `3`.

The checks test your tables in exactly this way. Write a bad insert for every rule in the spec, and watch each one come back empty. Then take your test inserts out before you run the checks: the checks add rows with their own ids, and a test row with the same id would get in their way.

### Check the view by hand

After a few test inserts, work out one row of the view yourself and compare. Say an event at the Hall has no tickets yet. Its row must still be there, with `sold` 0, `capacity` 3, `remaining` 3 and `revenue_cents` 0. If the event is missing, or shows 1 sold, or has NULL revenue, the view has a bug.

**Watch out:** the unsold event is where views like this go wrong. An inner `JOIN` to `tickets` drops it completely. A `LEFT JOIN` keeps it, as one row with NULL in every ticket column. Then `COUNT(*)` counts that row and says 1 sold, while counting a ticket column, such as `COUNT(t.id)`, says 0. And `SUM` of nothing is NULL, not 0.

::: context spec What a spec is for
In a real team, the person who knows what is needed (a customer, a product manager, a mission planner) is rarely the person who writes the tables. They write down what the system must do, and engineers turn that into a design. That document is the specification. A good spec says *what* must be true, not *how* to build it, and it numbers each requirement so that each one can be tested. Spacecraft are built the same way: every requirement gets a number, and before launch each one is checked off by a test, an analysis or an inspection.
:::

::: context one-seat Why the database must be the referee
On a busy sale, two people can click the same seat in the same second. The app asks "is A1 free?" for both, both answers are yes, and both try to buy. If the rule lived only in the app, both tickets would be written. A rule across the pair `(event_id, seat)` lets the database refuse the second sale, however the two requests are timed. The same seat at another event is a different pair, so it is allowed.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 180" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="nx" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#b4232c"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <text x="20" y="69" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">event 10</text>
  <rect x="90" y="50" width="44" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="112" y="69" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">A1</text>
  <rect x="140" y="50" width="44" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="162" y="69" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">A2</text>
  <rect x="190" y="50" width="44" height="28" fill="#fff" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="212" y="69" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">A3</text>
  <text x="20" y="119" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">event 11</text>

  <rect x="140" y="100" width="44" height="28" fill="#fff" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="162" y="119" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">A2</text>
  <rect x="190" y="100" width="44" height="28" fill="#fff" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="212" y="119" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">A3</text>

  <text x="304" y="69" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">new: 10, A1</text>

  <path d="M 304 50 C 304 18, 112 18, 112 48" fill="none" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="4 3" marker-end="url(#nx)"/>
  <text x="180" y="152" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">blue = sold. Each (event, seat) pair can be sold once:</text>
  <text x="180" y="168" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">(10, A1) is taken, and (11, A1) is a different pair</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context cancel-history Deleting is not the only way
In this spec, canceling an event deletes its tickets, and `ON DELETE CASCADE` does that in one statement. Real ticket systems are often more careful. A sold ticket is a record of money paid, and a canceled show usually means refunds. So many systems keep the rows and set a column such as `status` to `'cancelled'` instead. The history stays, and a report can still say how much must be paid back. Keeping a row but marking it as gone is called a **soft delete**. The appointment capstone, two lessons from now, uses exactly this idea.
:::

::: context break-it Test the refusals, not only the successes
A test that only inserts good rows proves half the job: a table with no rules at all would pass it. Testers call the other half **negative tests**, inputs that must be refused. For each rule, try one row that breaks it and one that barely keeps it: a price of -1 and a price of 0, the same seat twice at one event and once at another. The edges are where mistakes hide, such as `>` written where `>=` was meant.
:::
--- task
Build everything in the spec, rules 1 to 6: turn foreign keys on; create the `events` table (`id`, `venue_id`, `title`, `starts_at`) and the `tickets` table (`id`, `event_id`, `seat`, `buyer_email`, `price_cents`) with every rule the spec states for them; and create the view `event_sales` with the columns `event_id`, `title`, `sold`, `capacity`, `remaining` and `revenue_cents`.
--- starter
-- Ticketing: your schema goes here.
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  venue_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id),
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  seat TEXT NOT NULL,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  UNIQUE (event_id, seat)
);

CREATE VIEW event_sales AS
SELECT e.id AS event_id, e.title,
       COUNT(t.id) AS sold,
       v.capacity,
       v.capacity - COUNT(t.id) AS remaining,
       COALESCE(SUM(t.price_cents), 0) AS revenue_cents
FROM events e
JOIN venues v ON v.id = e.venue_id
LEFT JOIN tickets t ON t.event_id = e.id
GROUP BY e.id, e.title, v.capacity;
--- hint
Go through the spec one rule at a time and match each to the table in "Where each tool comes from". "Once per event" is a rule across two columns: at the end of `tickets`, after the last column, write `UNIQUE (event_id, seat)`.
--- hint
`event_id` in `tickets` needs `NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE`, and `price_cents` needs `CHECK (price_cents >= 0)`. Put `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;` first.
--- hint
The view starts from `events`, joins its venue, and LEFT JOINs `tickets`, so an event with no sales still has a row. Group by event; `sold` is `COUNT(t.id)`, `remaining` is `v.capacity - COUNT(t.id)`, and the revenue is `COALESCE(SUM(t.price_cents), 0)`.
--- check query | Foreign keys are enforced
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Events and tickets can be created
INSERT INTO events (id, venue_id, title, starts_at) VALUES (10, 1, 'Quartet', '2024-09-01 19:00'), (11, 2, 'DJ night', '2024-09-02 22:00'), (12, 1, 'Poetry', '2024-09-03 18:00');
INSERT INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (1, 10, 'A1', 'ana@x.io', 2500), (2, 10, 'A2', 'bo@x.io', 2500), (3, 11, 'A1', 'cy@x.io', 1500) RETURNING id
=> [[1], [2], [3]]
--- check query | A seat already sold for that event is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (4, 10, 'A1', 'dee@x.io', 2500) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | A negative price or a missing buyer is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (5, 10, 'B1', 'eli@x.io', -100), (6, 10, 'B2', NULL, 100) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | event_sales covers every event, unsold ones included
SELECT event_id, title, sold, capacity, remaining, revenue_cents FROM event_sales ORDER BY event_id
=> [[10, "Quartet", 2, 3, 1, 5000], [11, "DJ night", 1, 100, 99, 1500], [12, "Poetry", 0, 3, 3, 0]]
--- check query | Cancelling an event removes its tickets
DELETE FROM events WHERE id = 10;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM tickets
=> [[1]]
--- check query | A missing venue, title, start time or seat is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO events (id, venue_id, title, starts_at) VALUES (20, NULL, 'Nowhere', '2024-09-04 19:00'), (21, 1, NULL, '2024-09-04 19:00'), (22, 1, 'No time', NULL);
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (7, 11, NULL, 'fay@x.io', 100);
SELECT (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM events WHERE id IN (20, 21, 22)), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM tickets WHERE id = 7)
=> [[0, 0]]
--- check query | An event must name a real venue, and a ticket a real event
SELECT 'events', "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('events')
UNION ALL
SELECT 'tickets', "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('tickets')
=> [["events", "venues", "venue_id"], ["tickets", "events", "event_id"]]

+++ practice | A venue's seat map
--- task
Build from this short spec, on top of the lesson's `venues` table:

1. Foreign keys are enforced.
2. `seats`: `id` (integer primary key), `venue_id` (required, a real venue), `row_label` (required text, like `'A'`), `number` (required integer, 1 or more).
3. A venue cannot have the same seat twice: the same `row_label` and `number` at one venue is refused. The same seat name at another venue is fine.
--- starter
-- Seat map: your schema goes here.
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE seats (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  venue_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id),
  row_label TEXT NOT NULL,
  number INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (number >= 1),
  UNIQUE (venue_id, row_label, number)
);
--- hint
Rule 3 is a rule across three columns at once. It goes after the last column, like the two-column rules you have written.
--- hint
`UNIQUE (venue_id, row_label, number)`. `venue_id` needs `NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id)`, and the number a `CHECK (number >= 1)`.
--- check query | Foreign keys are enforced
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Seats can be added, the same name at two venues included
INSERT INTO seats (id, venue_id, row_label, number) VALUES (1, 1, 'A', 1), (2, 1, 'A', 2), (3, 2, 'A', 1) RETURNING id
=> [[1], [2], [3]]
--- check query | The same seat twice at one venue is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO seats (id, venue_id, row_label, number) VALUES (4, 1, 'A', 1) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Seat number 0, or a missing row or venue, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO seats (id, venue_id, row_label, number) VALUES (5, 1, 'B', 0), (6, 1, NULL, 3), (7, NULL, 'C', 1) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | seats references venues
SELECT "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('seats')
=> [["venues", "venue_id"]]

+++ practice | A night at each venue
--- schema
CREATE TABLE venues (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  capacity INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (capacity > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  venue_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id),
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  seat TEXT NOT NULL,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  UNIQUE (event_id, seat)
);
INSERT INTO venues (id, name, capacity) VALUES (1, 'Hall', 3), (2, 'Club', 100), (3, 'Barn', 50);
INSERT INTO events (id, venue_id, title, starts_at) VALUES
  (10, 1, 'Quartet',  '2024-09-01 19:00'),
  (11, 1, 'Poetry',   '2024-09-03 18:00'),
  (12, 2, 'DJ night', '2024-09-02 22:00');
INSERT INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES
  (1, 10, 'A1', 'ana@x.io', 2500),
  (2, 10, 'A2', 'bo@x.io',  2500),
  (3, 10, 'A3', 'cy@x.io',  2000),
  (4, 12, 'A1', 'dee@x.io', 1500),
  (5, 12, 'A2', 'eli@x.io', 1500);
--- task
This problem has its own copy of the ticketing schema, already built and filled: `venues`, `events` and `tickets`, as in the lesson's spec. The venue managers want one more view.

Create a view named `venue_nights` with one row per venue, **including venues with no events**, and these columns:

- `venue_id` and `name`;
- `events`: how many events the venue hosts;
- `tickets_sold`: how many tickets were sold across those events;
- `revenue_cents`: the total price of those tickets, and 0 when there are none.

An event with several tickets must still count as one event.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW venue_nights AS
SELECT v.id AS venue_id, v.name,
       COUNT(e.id) AS events,
       COUNT(t.id) AS tickets_sold,
       SUM(t.price_cents) AS revenue_cents
FROM venues v
JOIN events e ON e.venue_id = v.id
LEFT JOIN tickets t ON t.event_id = e.id
GROUP BY v.id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW venue_nights AS
SELECT v.id AS venue_id, v.name,
       COUNT(DISTINCT e.id) AS events,
       COUNT(t.id) AS tickets_sold,
       COALESCE(SUM(t.price_cents), 0) AS revenue_cents
FROM venues v
LEFT JOIN events e ON e.venue_id = v.id
LEFT JOIN tickets t ON t.event_id = e.id
GROUP BY v.id;
--- hint
Joining the tickets repeats each event once per ticket, so the Quartet appears three times. Of the three counts, only one is hurt by that.
--- hint
Count events with `COUNT(DISTINCT e.id)`. Tickets appear once each, so `COUNT(t.id)` is right. Keep the Barn with `LEFT JOIN events`, and wrap the sum in `COALESCE(…, 0)`.
--- check query | Every venue, the empty Barn included
SELECT venue_id, name, events, tickets_sold, revenue_cents FROM venue_nights ORDER BY venue_id
=> [[1, "Hall", 2, 3, 7000], [2, "Club", 1, 2, 3000], [3, "Barn", 0, 0, 0]]
--- check query | An event with no tickets still counts as an event
INSERT INTO events (id, venue_id, title, starts_at) VALUES (13, 3, 'Folk', '2024-09-05 20:00');
SELECT events, tickets_sold, revenue_cents FROM venue_nights WHERE venue_id = 3
=> [[1, 0, 0]]
--- check query | A ticket for the Poetry night adds a ticket, not an event
INSERT INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (6, 11, 'A1', 'fay@x.io', 800);
SELECT events, tickets_sold, revenue_cents FROM venue_nights WHERE venue_id = 1
=> [[2, 4, 7800]]

+++ practice | Refunded seats go back on sale
--- schema
CREATE TABLE venues (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  capacity INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (capacity > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  venue_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id),
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO venues (id, name, capacity) VALUES (1, 'Hall', 3), (2, 'Club', 100);
INSERT INTO events (id, venue_id, title, starts_at) VALUES
  (10, 1, 'Quartet',  '2024-09-01 19:00'),
  (11, 2, 'DJ night', '2024-09-02 22:00'),
  (12, 1, 'Poetry',   '2024-09-03 18:00');
--- task
This problem has its own `venues` and `events`, already built and filled. A new spec for the tickets, where refunds keep their history:

1. Foreign keys are enforced.
2. `tickets`: `id` (integer primary key), `event_id` (required, a real event; deleting the event deletes its tickets), `seat` (required text), `buyer_email` (required), `price_cents` (required integer, zero or more), `status` (required, `'sold'` or `'refunded'`, and `'sold'` by default).
3. A seat can be held by only one **sold** ticket per event. A refunded ticket stays in the table, and its seat can be sold again.
4. A view `event_sales` with one row per event, including events with nothing sold, and the columns `event_id`, `title`, `sold` (how many sold tickets) and `revenue_cents` (their total price, 0 when none). Refunded tickets count for nothing.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  seat TEXT NOT NULL,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'sold' CHECK (status IN ('sold', 'refunded')),
  UNIQUE (event_id, seat)
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  seat TEXT NOT NULL,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'sold' CHECK (status IN ('sold', 'refunded'))
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_sold_ticket_per_seat ON tickets(event_id, seat) WHERE status = 'sold';

CREATE VIEW event_sales AS
SELECT e.id AS event_id, e.title,
       COUNT(t.id) AS sold,
       COALESCE(SUM(t.price_cents), 0) AS revenue_cents
FROM events e
LEFT JOIN tickets t ON t.event_id = e.id AND t.status = 'sold'
GROUP BY e.id;
--- hint
Rule 3 is "unique among some of the rows", the job of a partial index from "Library 1: the schema". The starter's `UNIQUE (event_id, seat)` counts refunded tickets too, so a refunded seat can never be sold again.
--- hint
Replace the table rule with `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX … ON tickets(event_id, seat) WHERE status = 'sold';`. In the view, put `t.status = 'sold'` in the `LEFT JOIN`'s `ON`, so events with only refunds keep their row.
--- check query | Foreign keys are enforced
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Tickets are sold by default
INSERT INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (1, 10, 'A1', 'ana@x.io', 2500), (2, 10, 'A2', 'bo@x.io', 2500), (3, 11, 'A1', 'cy@x.io', 1500) RETURNING id, status
=> [[1, "sold"], [2, "sold"], [3, "sold"]]
--- check query | A seat already sold for that event is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (4, 10, 'A1', 'dee@x.io', 2500) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | After a refund, the seat can be sold again
UPDATE tickets SET status = 'refunded' WHERE id = 1;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (5, 10, 'A1', 'dee@x.io', 2000) RETURNING id
=> [[5]]
--- check query | An unknown status is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents, status) VALUES (6, 10, 'B1', 'eli@x.io', 100, 'void') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Sales count sold tickets only, every event included
SELECT event_id, title, sold, revenue_cents FROM event_sales ORDER BY event_id
=> [[10, "Quartet", 2, 4500], [11, "DJ night", 1, 1500], [12, "Poetry", 0, 0]]
--- check query | Deleting an event deletes its tickets, refunded ones too
DELETE FROM events WHERE id = 10;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM tickets
=> [[1]]

+++ practice | Free tickets and full houses
--- schema
CREATE TABLE venues (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  capacity INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (capacity > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  venue_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id),
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  seat TEXT NOT NULL,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  UNIQUE (event_id, seat)
);
INSERT INTO venues (id, name, capacity) VALUES (1, 'Hall', 3), (2, 'Club', 100), (3, 'Booth', 1);
INSERT INTO events (id, venue_id, title, starts_at) VALUES
  (10, 1, 'Quartet',  '2024-09-01 19:00'),
  (11, 2, 'DJ night', '2024-09-02 22:00'),
  (12, 3, 'Solo',     '2024-09-04 20:00'),
  (13, 1, 'Poetry',   '2024-09-03 18:00');
INSERT INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES
  (1, 10, 'A1', 'ana@x.io', 2500),
  (2, 10, 'A2', 'guest@x.io', 0),
  (3, 10, 'A3', 'cy@x.io',  2500),
  (4, 11, 'A1', 'dee@x.io', 1500),
  (5, 11, 'A2', 'eli@x.io', 1500),
  (6, 13, 'A1', 'fay@x.io', 0);
--- task
This problem has its own copy of the ticketing schema, built and filled. Some tickets are free (0 cents), and one venue has a single seat. Create a view named `event_status` with one row per event and these columns:

1. `event_id` and 2. `title`.
3. `sold`: how many tickets were sold, free ones included.
4. `capacity`: the venue's capacity.
5. `pct_sold`: `sold` as a percentage of `capacity`, rounded to 1 decimal.
6. `revenue_cents`: the total of the ticket prices, 0 when none.
7. `status`: `'sold out'` when `sold` has reached the capacity, otherwise `'on sale'`.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW event_status AS
SELECT e.id AS event_id, e.title,
       COUNT(t.price_cents > 0) AS sold,
       v.capacity,
       ROUND(100 * COUNT(t.id) / v.capacity, 1) AS pct_sold,
       SUM(t.price_cents) AS revenue_cents,
       CASE WHEN COUNT(t.id) > v.capacity THEN 'sold out' ELSE 'on sale' END AS status
FROM events e
JOIN venues v ON v.id = e.venue_id
JOIN tickets t ON t.event_id = e.id
GROUP BY e.id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW event_status AS
SELECT e.id AS event_id, e.title,
       COUNT(t.id) AS sold,
       v.capacity,
       ROUND(100.0 * COUNT(t.id) / v.capacity, 1) AS pct_sold,
       COALESCE(SUM(t.price_cents), 0) AS revenue_cents,
       CASE WHEN COUNT(t.id) >= v.capacity THEN 'sold out' ELSE 'on sale' END AS status
FROM events e
JOIN venues v ON v.id = e.venue_id
LEFT JOIN tickets t ON t.event_id = e.id
GROUP BY e.id, e.title, v.capacity;
--- hint
Check the starter against each edge: the Solo night has no tickets, the Poetry night has one free ticket, the Quartet is exactly full, and one ticket in a hall of three is not a whole percent.
--- hint
Count tickets with `COUNT(t.id)`, whatever they cost, over a `LEFT JOIN`. Divide with `100.0` so a third comes out as 33.3, and "reached the capacity" is `>=`, not `>`.
--- check query | Every event, with free tickets counted and a full house marked
SELECT event_id, title, sold, capacity, pct_sold, revenue_cents, status FROM event_status ORDER BY event_id
=> [[10, "Quartet", 3, 3, 100.0, 5000, "sold out"], [11, "DJ night", 2, 100, 2.0, 3000, "on sale"], [12, "Solo", 0, 1, 0.0, 0, "on sale"], [13, "Poetry", 1, 3, 33.3, 0, "on sale"]]
--- check query | Selling the Booth's only seat sells the Solo night out
INSERT INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (7, 12, 'A1', 'gus@x.io', 4000);
SELECT sold, pct_sold, revenue_cents, status FROM event_status WHERE event_id = 12
=> [[1, 100.0, 4000, "sold out"]]
--- check query | Another free ticket adds to sold, not to revenue
INSERT INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (8, 11, 'A3', 'hal@x.io', 0);
SELECT sold, pct_sold, revenue_cents, status FROM event_status WHERE event_id = 11
=> [[3, 3.0, 3000, "on sale"]]

+++ practice | The seat that could be sold only once, ever
--- task
Here is a ticketing schema with one wrong rule. A seat is meant to be sold once **per event**. With this schema, once seat A1 is sold for any event, it can never be sold for another event.

```sql
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  venue_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id),
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  seat TEXT NOT NULL,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  UNIQUE (seat)
);
```

Fix the rule, keeping everything else.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  venue_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id),
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  seat TEXT NOT NULL,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  UNIQUE (seat)
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  venue_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id),
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  seat TEXT NOT NULL,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  UNIQUE (event_id, seat)
);
--- hint
The rule should be about a pair: which event, and which seat. Which column is missing from it?
--- hint
`UNIQUE (event_id, seat)`: the same seat at a different event is a different pair.
--- check query | Seat A1 can be sold for two different events

INSERT OR IGNORE INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (1, 10, 'A1', 'ana@x.io', 2500), (2, 11, 'A1', 'bo@x.io', 1500) RETURNING id
=> [[1], [2]]
