=> [[2]]

+++ practice | Festival passes
--- schema
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;
CREATE TABLE venues (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  capacity INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (capacity > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  venue_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES venues(id),
  title TEXT NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  seat TEXT NOT NULL,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  UNIQUE (event_id, seat)
);
INSERT INTO venues (id, name, capacity) VALUES (1, 'Hall', 3), (2, 'Club', 100);
INSERT INTO events (id, venue_id, title, starts_at) VALUES
  (10, 1, 'Quartet',  '2024-09-01 19:00'),
  (11, 2, 'DJ night', '2024-09-02 22:00'),
  (12, 1, 'Poetry',   '2024-09-03 18:00');
INSERT INTO tickets (id, event_id, seat, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES
  (1, 10, 'A1', 'cy@x.io', 2500),
  (2, 11, 'A1', 'dee@x.io', 1500),
  (3, 11, 'A2', 'eli@x.io', 1500);
--- task
This problem has its own ticketing schema, built and filled. The festival now also sells **passes**: one pass gets its holder into several events, and one event can be reached with many passes. Build it from this spec:

1. `passes`: `id` (integer primary key), `buyer_email` (required), `price_cents` (required integer, zero or more).
2. `pass_events`: which events each pass covers. `pass_id` (required, a real pass; deleting the pass deletes these rows), `event_id` (required, a real event). A pass covers an event at most once.
3. A view `event_attendance` with one row per event, including events nobody is coming to, and the columns `event_id`, `title`, `tickets` (tickets sold for it), `passes` (passes that cover it) and `total` (`tickets` plus `passes`).

Foreign keys are already on.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE passes (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
);
--- solution
CREATE TABLE passes (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  buyer_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
);
CREATE TABLE pass_events (
  pass_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES passes(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  event_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES events(id),
  PRIMARY KEY (pass_id, event_id)
);

CREATE VIEW event_attendance AS
WITH t AS (
  SELECT event_id, COUNT(*) AS n FROM tickets GROUP BY event_id
), p AS (
  SELECT event_id, COUNT(*) AS n FROM pass_events GROUP BY event_id
)
SELECT e.id AS event_id, e.title,
       COALESCE(t.n, 0) AS tickets,
       COALESCE(p.n, 0) AS passes,
       COALESCE(t.n, 0) + COALESCE(p.n, 0) AS total
FROM events e
LEFT JOIN t ON t.event_id = e.id
LEFT JOIN p ON p.event_id = e.id;
--- hint
`pass_events` is a many-to-many link table, as in "Design: many-to-many for an app": its primary key is the pair `(pass_id, event_id)`.
--- hint
The view hangs two separate one-to-many lists off each event, tickets and passes. Joined together they pair every ticket with every pass. Count each list on its own first, one row per event, then join both counts onto `events`.
--- check query | Passes and their events can be stored
INSERT INTO passes (id, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (1, 'ana@x.io', 5000), (2, 'bo@x.io', 4000);
INSERT INTO pass_events (pass_id, event_id) VALUES (1, 10), (1, 11), (1, 12), (2, 11) RETURNING pass_id
=> [[1], [1], [1], [2]]
--- check query | A pass cannot cover the same event twice; a negative price is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO pass_events (pass_id, event_id) VALUES (1, 10) RETURNING pass_id;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO passes (id, buyer_email, price_cents) VALUES (3, 'cy@x.io', -1) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Attendance counts tickets and passes without multiplying them
SELECT event_id, title, tickets, passes, total FROM event_attendance ORDER BY event_id
=> [[10, "Quartet", 1, 1, 2], [11, "DJ night", 2, 2, 4], [12, "Poetry", 0, 1, 1]]
--- check query | Deleting a pass deletes the events it covered
DELETE FROM passes WHERE id = 1;
SELECT event_id, tickets, passes, total FROM event_attendance ORDER BY event_id
=> [[10, 1, 0, 1], [11, 2, 1, 3], [12, 0, 0, 0]]
--- check query | pass_events references passes (cascading) and events
SELECT "table", "from", on_delete FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('pass_events') ORDER BY "from"
=> [["events", "event_id", "NO ACTION"], ["passes", "pass_id", "CASCADE"]]

=== sqlp-14 | Capstone: splitting expenses
--- schema
CREATE TABLE people (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
INSERT INTO people (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo'), (3, 'Cy'), (4, 'Dee');
--- teach
Last lesson you built a ticketing system from a spec. This capstone is an app for splitting shared costs among a group of friends. Someone pays for dinner. The app records what share of it each person owes. At any moment it can say who is up and who is down. Work the same way as last time: read the whole spec, decide where each rule lives, build the tables, try to break them, and build the views last.

### The table you start with

The group already exists, as `people`:

| id | name |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Ana |
| 2 | Bo |
| 3 | Cy |
| 4 | Dee |

Each `name` is `UNIQUE`, so no two people share one.

### The picture

Picture the notebook a group keeps on a trip: a [[page of who paid for what|ledger]], and under each payment, who owes a piece of it. Ana pays 90.00 for dinner for three, and each of Ana, Bo and Cy owes 30.00 of it. In cents:

| person | paid | owed | paid minus owed |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ana | 9000 | 3000 | 6000 |
| Bo | 0 | 3000 | -3000 |
| Cy | 0 | 3000 | -3000 |

The last column is each person's **net balance**: what they paid minus what they owe. Ana is 6000 up, so the group owes her 60.00. Bo and Cy are each 3000 down. Add the three together: 6000 − 3000 − 3000 = 0. That is [[no accident|net-zero]]. When the friends [[settle up|settle-up]], Bo and Cy each pay Ana 30.00, and everyone is back to 0.

### The spec

1. Foreign keys are enforced.
2. `expenses`: `id` (integer primary key), `paid_by` (required, a real person), `description` (required), `amount_cents` (required integer, **more than zero**).
3. `shares`, one row for one person's part of one expense: `expense_id` (required, a real expense), `person_id` (required, a real person), `share_cents` (required integer, zero or more).
4. Deleting an expense deletes its shares.
5. A person has **at most one share per expense**.
6. A view `balances` with one row for **every** person, including people with no expenses, and these columns:
   - `person_id` and `name`
   - `paid_cents`: the total of the expenses they paid
   - `owed_cents`: the total of their shares
   - `net_cents`: paid minus owed, so a positive number means the group owes them
7. A view `unbalanced_expenses` listing every expense whose shares do not add up to its amount, **including expenses with no shares at all**, with the columns `expense_id`, `amount_cents` and `shared_cents` (the total of its shares, 0 if there are none).

### Where each tool comes from

| spec | tool | lesson |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1, 2, 3, 4 | `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON`, `REFERENCES`, `ON DELETE CASCADE` | "Foreign keys" |
| 2, 3 | `NOT NULL`, `CHECK` | "Constraints: rules the database enforces" |
| 5 | a primary key made of two columns | "Design: normalizing a flat table" |
| 6 | one total per step, joined at the end | "CTEs: a query in named steps", "Debugging: the join that double-counts" |
| 6, 7 | `LEFT JOIN`, `COALESCE` | "LEFT JOIN: where the filter goes", "Testing for NULL" |
| 7 | `HAVING`, `<>` | "Grouping", "Filtering with WHERE" |

"Store 1: the schema" used the same two-column primary key for `order_items`: one line per product per order. Here it is one share per person per expense.

### Two lists hanging off one person

A warning from experience. `balances` links each person to **two** different one-to-many tables: the expenses they paid, and the shares they owe. You met this trap in "Debugging: the join that double-counts".

Suppose Ana paid two expenses, of 9000 and 5000 cents, and owes two shares, of 3000 and 2500. Join both tables onto Ana in one query, and the join pairs [[every expense with every share|fan-out]]:

| name | amount_cents | share_cents |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Ana | 9000 | 3000 |
| Ana | 9000 | 2500 |
| Ana | 5000 | 3000 |
| Ana | 5000 | 2500 |

`SUM(amount_cents)` now gives 28000, twice the real 14000. `SUM(share_cents)` gives 11000, twice the real 5500. Nothing errors. The numbers are only wrong.

The fix is to **build each total separately** before combining them. One step adds up what each person paid, one row per person. Another adds up what each person owes, one row per person. Only then join both onto `people`. Each total has at most one row per person, so nothing can multiply.

### The expenses that do not add up

An expense is balanced when its shares add up to its amount. Here are three:

| expense | amount_cents | its shares | shared_cents | listed? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Dinner | 9000 | 3000, 3000, 3000 | 9000 | no |
| Tickets | 5000 | 2500, 2000 | 4500 | yes |
| Snacks | 1200 | none | 0 | yes |

Tickets has 500 cents that nobody owes. Snacks has no shares at all, so its whole amount is unaccounted for. Both [[belong in the view|remainder-cent]]. In a query: group each expense's shares, then keep the groups whose total is `<>` ("not equal to") the amount. A filter on a group's total is a `HAVING`, from the lesson "Grouping".

**Watch out:** Snacks is the row that goes missing. After a `LEFT JOIN`, an expense with no shares has a share total of NULL, not 0. And `NULL <> 1200` is not true: it is NULL, so `HAVING` quietly drops the group. Turn the NULL into 0 with `COALESCE` before you compare, and Snacks stays.

::: context ledger A book of money in and out
A **ledger** is a record of every amount of money that moved: who paid, how much, and what for. Shops, banks and space agencies all keep them. The rule that makes a ledger trustworthy is that entries are added, not rubbed out. That is why this spec stores each expense and each share as its own row, and works out the balances with a view. A balance stored in a column could drift away from the rows it came from. A view is worked out fresh from the rows every time.
:::

::: context net-zero Why the balances add up to zero
Every cent in a fully shared expense appears twice: once as money paid, by the person who paid, and once as money owed, spread over the shares. Paid minus owed, added over the whole group, cancels to 0. So if you add up everyone's `net_cents` and do not get 0, some expense's shares do not match its amount. That is exactly what `unbalanced_expenses` finds. Accountants call this kind of cross-check **reconciliation**: two ways of counting the same money must agree.
:::

::: context settle-up How the app decides who pays whom
Once the balances are known, the people below zero pay the people above zero until everyone is at 0. A simple way: take the person who owes the most, have them pay the person who is owed the most as much as they can, and repeat. Each payment brings at least one person to exactly 0, so a group of 4 never needs more than 3 payments. Real expense-splitting apps work out a short list of payments like this from the same balances you are about to build.
:::

::: context fan-out Why the join doubles every total
The join finds every row that matches Ana on each side and pairs each one on the left with each one on the right. Two expenses times two shares gives four rows. Each expense now appears twice, once per share, and each share appears twice, once per expense. With three shares, each expense would be counted three times.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="70" y="22" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">Ana paid</text>
  <text x="290" y="22" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">Ana owes</text>
  <rect x="20" y="40" width="100" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="70" y="59" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">9000</text>
  <rect x="20" y="100" width="100" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="70" y="119" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">5000</text>
  <rect x="240" y="40" width="100" height="28" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="290" y="59" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">3000</text>
  <rect x="240" y="100" width="100" height="28" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="290" y="119" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2500</text>
  <line x1="120" y1="54" x2="240" y2="54" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="120" y1="54" x2="240" y2="114" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="120" y1="114" x2="240" y2="54" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="120" y1="114" x2="240" y2="114" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="158" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">2 × 2 = 4 joined rows: every number is counted twice</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context remainder-cent How an expense ends up unbalanced
Often it is plain rounding. Split 1000 cents three ways and each share is 333, with 1 cent left over, because 3 × 333 = 999. An app has to give that last cent to someone, and a bug that forgets to leaves the expense 1 cent short. Other times a share was deleted, or someone was left out when the expense was entered. Listing every unbalanced expense, including the ones with no shares yet, lets the app ask a person to fix it before anyone settles up.
:::
--- task
Build everything in the spec, rules 1 to 7: turn foreign keys on; create the `expenses` table (`id`, `paid_by`, `description`, `amount_cents`) and the `shares` table (`expense_id`, `person_id`, `share_cents`) with every rule the spec states for them; and create the views `balances` (`person_id`, `name`, `paid_cents`, `owed_cents`, `net_cents`) and `unbalanced_expenses` (`expense_id`, `amount_cents`, `shared_cents`).
--- starter
-- Expense splitting: your schema goes here.
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE expenses (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  paid_by INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  description TEXT NOT NULL,
  amount_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (amount_cents > 0)
);

CREATE TABLE shares (
  expense_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES expenses(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  person_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  share_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (share_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (expense_id, person_id)
);

CREATE VIEW balances AS
WITH paid AS (
  SELECT paid_by AS person_id, SUM(amount_cents) AS cents FROM expenses GROUP BY paid_by
), owed AS (
  SELECT person_id, SUM(share_cents) AS cents FROM shares GROUP BY person_id
)
SELECT p.id AS person_id, p.name,
       COALESCE(pd.cents, 0) AS paid_cents,
       COALESCE(ow.cents, 0) AS owed_cents,
       COALESCE(pd.cents, 0) - COALESCE(ow.cents, 0) AS net_cents
FROM people p
LEFT JOIN paid pd ON pd.person_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN owed ow ON ow.person_id = p.id;

CREATE VIEW unbalanced_expenses AS
SELECT e.id AS expense_id, e.amount_cents,
       COALESCE(SUM(s.share_cents), 0) AS shared_cents
FROM expenses e
LEFT JOIN shares s ON s.expense_id = e.id
GROUP BY e.id
HAVING COALESCE(SUM(s.share_cents), 0) <> e.amount_cents;
--- hint
`shares` needs a primary key made of two columns, `PRIMARY KEY (expense_id, person_id)`, written after its last column: that is "one share per person per expense". `amount_cents` needs `CHECK (amount_cents > 0)`, and `expense_id` needs `ON DELETE CASCADE`.
--- hint
For `balances`, total the paid amounts per person and the owed shares per person in two separate CTEs (`WITH paid AS (…), owed AS (…)`), each grouped by person. Then start from `people` and LEFT JOIN both onto it, with `COALESCE(…, 0)` around each total.
--- hint
`unbalanced_expenses` LEFT JOINs `shares` onto `expenses`, groups by expense, and keeps the groups with `HAVING COALESCE(SUM(s.share_cents), 0) <> e.amount_cents`.
--- check query | Foreign keys are enforced
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Expenses can be recorded; a zero amount is refused
INSERT INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES (1, 1, 'Dinner', 9000), (2, 2, 'Taxi', 3000), (3, 1, 'Tickets', 5000), (5, 3, 'Snacks', 1200);
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES (4, 1, 'Free walk', 0);
SELECT id FROM expenses ORDER BY id
=> [[1], [2], [3], [5]]
--- check query | Shares can be recorded
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES (1, 1, 3000), (1, 2, 3000), (1, 3, 3000), (2, 2, 1500), (2, 3, 1500), (3, 1, 2500), (3, 2, 2000) RETURNING expense_id
=> [[1], [1], [1], [2], [2], [3], [3]]
--- check query | A second share for the same person and expense, or a negative share, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES (1, 1, 100), (2, 4, -5) RETURNING expense_id
=> []
--- check query | Balances cover everyone and are not inflated by the joins
SELECT person_id, name, paid_cents, owed_cents, net_cents FROM balances ORDER BY person_id
=> [[1, "Ana", 14000, 5500, 8500], [2, "Bo", 3000, 6500, -3500], [3, "Cy", 1200, 4500, -3300], [4, "Dee", 0, 0, 0]]
--- check query | Expenses whose shares do not add up are flagged, including ones with no shares
SELECT expense_id, amount_cents, shared_cents FROM unbalanced_expenses ORDER BY expense_id
=> [[3, 5000, 4500], [5, 1200, 0]]
--- check query | Deleting an expense deletes its shares
DELETE FROM expenses WHERE id = 1;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM shares
=> [[4]]
--- check query | An expense needs a payer and a description
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES (6, 2, NULL, 500), (7, NULL, 'Coffee', 500);
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM expenses WHERE id IN (6, 7)
=> [[0]]
--- check query | paid_by and person_id must name real people
SELECT 'expenses', "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('expenses')
UNION ALL
SELECT 'shares', "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('shares')
ORDER BY 1, 3
=> [["expenses", "people", "paid_by"], ["shares", "expenses", "expense_id"], ["shares", "people", "person_id"]]

+++ practice | Saving up together
--- task
Build from this short spec, on top of the lesson's `people` table. Friends save towards shared goals, called jars:

1. Foreign keys are enforced.
2. `jars`: `id` (integer primary key), `name` (required), `target_cents` (required integer, more than zero).
3. `contributions`: `jar_id` (required, a real jar; deleting the jar deletes its contributions), `person_id` (required, a real person), `cents` (required integer, more than zero).
4. Each person has at most one contribution row per jar.
--- starter
-- Shared jars: your schema goes here.
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE jars (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  target_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (target_cents > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE contributions (
  jar_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES jars(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  person_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (cents > 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (jar_id, person_id)
);
--- hint
Go through the spec one rule at a time, as in the capstone. Rule 4 is a primary key made of two columns.
--- hint
`jar_id` needs `NOT NULL REFERENCES jars(id) ON DELETE CASCADE`; after the last column of `contributions`, write `PRIMARY KEY (jar_id, person_id)`. "More than zero" is `> 0`.
--- check query | Foreign keys are enforced
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Jars and contributions can be recorded
INSERT INTO jars (id, name, target_cents) VALUES (1, 'Trip', 60000), (2, 'Gift', 5000);
INSERT INTO contributions (jar_id, person_id, cents) VALUES (1, 1, 20000), (1, 2, 15000), (2, 1, 2500) RETURNING jar_id, person_id
=> [[1, 1], [1, 2], [2, 1]]
--- check query | A second row for the same person and jar, or a zero contribution, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO contributions (jar_id, person_id, cents) VALUES (1, 1, 500), (2, 3, 0) RETURNING jar_id
=> []
--- check query | A jar with a zero target is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO jars (id, name, target_cents) VALUES (3, 'Nothing', 0) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Deleting a jar deletes its contributions
DELETE FROM jars WHERE id = 1;
SELECT jar_id, person_id FROM contributions
=> [[2, 1]]
--- check query | contributions references jars (cascading) and people
SELECT "table", "from", on_delete FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('contributions') ORDER BY "from"
=> [["jars", "jar_id", "CASCADE"], ["people", "person_id", "NO ACTION"]]

+++ practice | Who owes whom
--- schema
CREATE TABLE people (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE expenses (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  paid_by INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  description TEXT NOT NULL,
  amount_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (amount_cents > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE shares (
  expense_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES expenses(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  person_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  share_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (share_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (expense_id, person_id)
);
INSERT INTO people (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo'), (3, 'Cy'), (4, 'Dee');
INSERT INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'Dinner',  9000),
  (2, 2, 'Taxi',    3000),
  (3, 1, 'Tickets', 4000),
  (4, 3, 'Snacks',  1200);
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES
  (1, 1, 3000), (1, 2, 3000), (1, 3, 3000),
  (2, 2, 1500), (2, 1, 1500),
  (3, 1, 2000), (3, 3, 2000),
  (4, 3, 400), (4, 1, 400), (4, 2, 400);
--- task
This problem has its own copy of the expense schema, built and filled. Balances say how far up or down each person is; this view says **who pays whom**.

Each share of an expense is a debt from the person who owes it to the person who paid, unless they are the same person. Two people's debts in opposite directions cancel out: if Bo owes Ana 3000 and Ana owes Bo 1500, then Bo owes Ana 1500 and Ana owes Bo nothing.

Create a view named `debts` with the columns `debtor` and `creditor` (names) and `cents`: one row per pair of people where the debtor still owes something after cancelling, with the amount owed.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW debts AS
SELECT d.name AS debtor, c.name AS creditor, SUM(s.share_cents) AS cents
FROM shares s
JOIN expenses e ON e.id = s.expense_id
JOIN people d ON d.id = s.person_id
JOIN people c ON c.id = e.paid_by
GROUP BY d.name, c.name;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW debts AS
WITH owed AS (
  SELECT s.person_id AS debtor, e.paid_by AS creditor, SUM(s.share_cents) AS cents
  FROM shares s
  JOIN expenses e ON e.id = s.expense_id
  WHERE s.person_id <> e.paid_by
  GROUP BY s.person_id, e.paid_by
), net AS (
  SELECT o.debtor, o.creditor, o.cents - COALESCE(r.cents, 0) AS cents
  FROM owed o
  LEFT JOIN owed r ON r.debtor = o.creditor AND r.creditor = o.debtor
)
SELECT d.name AS debtor, c.name AS creditor, n.cents
FROM net n
JOIN people d ON d.id = n.debtor
JOIN people c ON c.id = n.creditor
WHERE n.cents > 0;
--- hint
First add up the raw debts per ordered pair (debtor, creditor), leaving out the shares people owe themselves. Then compare each pair with the same pair the other way round.
--- hint
Join the raw debts to themselves with the roles swapped: `LEFT JOIN owed r ON r.debtor = o.creditor AND r.creditor = o.debtor`. What is left is `o.cents - COALESCE(r.cents, 0)`; keep only the rows where that is above 0.
--- check query | Bo owes Ana and Cy; Cy owes Ana; Ana's debts are cancelled out
SELECT debtor, creditor, cents FROM debts ORDER BY debtor, creditor
=> [["Bo", "Ana", 1500], ["Bo", "Cy", 400], ["Cy", "Ana", 4600]]
--- check query | A new lunch paid by Bo turns the debt around
INSERT INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES (5, 2, 'Lunch', 5000);
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES (5, 1, 2500), (5, 2, 2500);
SELECT debtor, creditor, cents FROM debts ORDER BY debtor, creditor
=> [["Ana", "Bo", 1000], ["Bo", "Cy", 400], ["Cy", "Ana", 4600]]
--- check query | Debts that cancel exactly disappear
INSERT INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES (6, 3, 'Coffee', 800);
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES (6, 2, 400), (6, 3, 400);
INSERT INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES (7, 2, 'Parking', 1600);
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES (7, 3, 800), (7, 2, 800);
SELECT debtor, creditor, cents FROM debts ORDER BY debtor, creditor
=> [["Ana", "Bo", 1000], ["Cy", "Ana", 4600]]

+++ practice | Splitting evenly, down to the last cent
--- schema
CREATE TABLE expenses (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  amount_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (amount_cents > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE splits (
  expense_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES expenses(id),
  person_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (expense_id, person_id)
);
INSERT INTO expenses (id, amount_cents) VALUES (1, 1000), (2, 900), (3, 1001), (4, 5);
INSERT INTO splits (expense_id, person_id) VALUES
  (1, 1), (1, 2), (1, 3),
  (2, 2), (2, 4),
  (3, 1), (3, 2), (3, 3), (3, 4),
  (4, 3), (4, 1), (4, 2);
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `expenses` (`id`, `amount_cents`) and `splits` (`expense_id`, `person_id`), which lists who shares each expense. The app splits each expense **evenly**, in whole cents, and the shares must add up to the amount exactly.

Split 1000 cents three ways and each person gets 333, with 1 cent left over. The leftover cents go one each to the people with the smallest `person_id`s: 334, 333, 333.

Write one query that returns `expense_id`, `person_id` and `share_cents`, one row per row of `splits`, sorted by `expense_id`, then `person_id`.
--- starter
SELECT s.expense_id, s.person_id, e.amount_cents / 3 AS share_cents
FROM splits s
JOIN expenses e ON e.id = s.expense_id
ORDER BY s.expense_id, s.person_id;
--- solution
WITH counted AS (
  SELECT s.expense_id, s.person_id, e.amount_cents,
         COUNT(*) OVER (PARTITION BY s.expense_id) AS n,
         ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY s.expense_id ORDER BY s.person_id) AS k
  FROM splits s
  JOIN expenses e ON e.id = s.expense_id
)
SELECT expense_id, person_id,
       amount_cents / n + CASE WHEN k <= amount_cents - (amount_cents / n) * n THEN 1 ELSE 0 END AS share_cents
FROM counted
ORDER BY expense_id, person_id;
--- hint
Each row needs two numbers about its expense without losing its own row: how many people share it, and where this person comes in order of `person_id`. Both are window functions.
--- hint
`COUNT(*) OVER (PARTITION BY s.expense_id)` is the number of people, `n`. `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY s.expense_id ORDER BY s.person_id)` is the person's place, `k`. Dividing two whole numbers throws the remainder away, so `amount_cents / n` is the base share.
--- hint
The leftover is `amount_cents - (amount_cents / n) * n`. The first that many people get one cent more: add `CASE WHEN k <= leftover THEN 1 ELSE 0 END` to the base share.
--- check result | Every expense adds up exactly, extra cents to the smallest ids
ordered
[[1, 1, 334], [1, 2, 333], [1, 3, 333], [2, 2, 450], [2, 4, 450], [3, 1, 251], [3, 2, 250], [3, 3, 250], [3, 4, 250], [4, 1, 2], [4, 2, 2], [4, 3, 1]]
--- check source | Numbers the people with ROW_NUMBER
[Rr][Oo][Ww]_[Nn][Uu][Mm][Bb][Ee][Rr]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not divide by a typed head count
/\s*[34]\b

+++ practice | The whole group, even when it is empty
--- schema
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;
CREATE TABLE people (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE expenses (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  paid_by INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  description TEXT NOT NULL,
  amount_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (amount_cents > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE shares (
  expense_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES expenses(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  person_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  share_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (share_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (expense_id, person_id)
);
INSERT INTO people (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo'), (3, 'Cy'), (4, 'Dee');
INSERT INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'Dinner',  9000),
  (2, 2, 'Taxi',    3000),
  (3, 1, 'Tickets', 5000),
  (4, 3, 'Snacks',  1200);
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES
  (1, 1, 3000), (1, 2, 3000), (1, 3, 3000),
  (2, 2, 1500), (2, 3, 1500),
  (3, 1, 2500), (3, 2, 2000);
--- task
This problem has its own copy of the expense schema, built and filled. Before anyone settles up, the app runs one health check on the whole group.

Create a view named `group_check` that always returns **exactly one row**, with four columns:

1. `total_paid`: the total of all expense amounts.
2. `total_owed`: the total of all shares.
3. `difference`: `total_paid` minus `total_owed`.
4. `unbalanced`: how many expenses have shares that do not add up to their amount, counting expenses with no shares at all.

When the group has no expenses, the row reads 0, 0, 0 and 0, never NULL.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW group_check AS
SELECT SUM(e.amount_cents) AS total_paid,
       SUM(s.share_cents) AS total_owed,
       SUM(e.amount_cents) - SUM(s.share_cents) AS difference,
       0 AS unbalanced
FROM expenses e
JOIN shares s ON s.expense_id = e.id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW group_check AS
WITH per_expense AS (
  SELECT e.id, e.amount_cents, COALESCE(SUM(s.share_cents), 0) AS shared
  FROM expenses e
  LEFT JOIN shares s ON s.expense_id = e.id
  GROUP BY e.id, e.amount_cents
)
SELECT COALESCE(SUM(amount_cents), 0) AS total_paid,
       COALESCE(SUM(shared), 0) AS total_owed,
       COALESCE(SUM(amount_cents - shared), 0) AS difference,
       COUNT(CASE WHEN shared <> amount_cents THEN 1 END) AS unbalanced
FROM per_expense;
--- hint
The starter repeats each expense once per share, so the Dinner is counted three times, and it loses the Snacks, which have no shares. Shrink to one row per expense first, with its shares' total beside it.
--- hint
In a CTE, `LEFT JOIN` the shares onto the expenses and group by expense, with `COALESCE(SUM(s.share_cents), 0) AS shared`. Then add up that CTE with no `GROUP BY`: an aggregate over the whole table always gives exactly one row.
--- hint
Over an empty table, `SUM` gives NULL and `COUNT` gives 0. Wrap each `SUM` in `COALESCE(…, 0)`, and count the unbalanced expenses with `COUNT(CASE WHEN shared <> amount_cents THEN 1 END)`.
--- check query | 1700 cents are unaccounted for, across two expenses
SELECT total_paid, total_owed, difference, unbalanced FROM group_check
=> [[18200, 16500, 1700, 2]]
--- check query | Fixing both expenses balances the group
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES (3, 3, 500), (4, 4, 1200);
SELECT total_paid, total_owed, difference, unbalanced FROM group_check
=> [[18200, 18200, 0, 0]]
--- check query | With no expenses at all, one row of zeros
DELETE FROM expenses;
SELECT total_paid, total_owed, difference, unbalanced FROM group_check
=> [[0, 0, 0, 0]]

+++ practice | The friend who never paid
--- schema

  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE expenses (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  paid_by INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  description TEXT NOT NULL,
  amount_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (amount_cents > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE shares (
  expense_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES expenses(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  person_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  share_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (share_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (expense_id, person_id)
);
INSERT INTO people (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo'), (3, 'Cy'), (4, 'Dee');
INSERT INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'Dinner',  9000),
  (2, 1, 'Tickets', 5000),
  (3, 3, 'Snacks',  1200);
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES
  (1, 1, 3000), (1, 2, 3000), (1, 3, 3000),
  (2, 1, 2500), (2, 2, 2500),
  (3, 2, 600), (3, 3, 600);
--- task
This problem has its own copy of the expense schema, built and filled. This `balances` view builds each total separately, as the capstone taught, but Bo, who owes the most, is missing from it, and so is Dee:

```sql
CREATE VIEW balances AS
WITH paid AS (
  SELECT paid_by AS person_id, SUM(amount_cents) AS cents FROM expenses GROUP BY paid_by
), owed AS (
  SELECT person_id, SUM(share_cents) AS cents FROM shares GROUP BY person_id
)
SELECT p.id AS person_id, p.name,
       COALESCE(pd.cents, 0) AS paid_cents,
       COALESCE(ow.cents, 0) AS owed_cents,
       COALESCE(pd.cents, 0) - COALESCE(ow.cents, 0) AS net_cents
FROM people p
JOIN paid pd ON pd.person_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN owed ow ON ow.person_id = p.id;
```

Fix it so every person appears.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW balances AS
WITH paid AS (
  SELECT paid_by AS person_id, SUM(amount_cents) AS cents FROM expenses GROUP BY paid_by
), owed AS (
  SELECT person_id, SUM(share_cents) AS cents FROM shares GROUP BY person_id
)
SELECT p.id AS person_id, p.name,
       COALESCE(pd.cents, 0) AS paid_cents,
       COALESCE(ow.cents, 0) AS owed_cents,
       COALESCE(pd.cents, 0) - COALESCE(ow.cents, 0) AS net_cents
FROM people p
JOIN paid pd ON pd.person_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN owed ow ON ow.person_id = p.id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW balances AS
WITH paid AS (
  SELECT paid_by AS person_id, SUM(amount_cents) AS cents FROM expenses GROUP BY paid_by
), owed AS (
  SELECT person_id, SUM(share_cents) AS cents FROM shares GROUP BY person_id
)
SELECT p.id AS person_id, p.name,
       COALESCE(pd.cents, 0) AS paid_cents,
       COALESCE(ow.cents, 0) AS owed_cents,
       COALESCE(pd.cents, 0) - COALESCE(ow.cents, 0) AS net_cents
FROM people p
LEFT JOIN paid pd ON pd.person_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN owed ow ON ow.person_id = p.id;
--- hint
Bo and Dee have one thing in common: neither has paid for anything, so neither has a row in `paid`. What does a plain `JOIN` do with a person who has no match?
--- hint
The `COALESCE`s are ready for a missing total, but the join throws the row away first. Make the join to `paid` a `LEFT JOIN`, like the one to `owed`.
--- check query | Everyone appears, Bo and Dee included
SELECT person_id, name, paid_cents, owed_cents, net_cents FROM balances ORDER BY person_id
=> [[1, "Ana", 14000, 5500, 8500], [2, "Bo", 0, 6100, -6100], [3, "Cy", 1200, 3600, -2400], [4, "Dee", 0, 0, 0]]
--- check query | The balances add up to zero
SELECT SUM(net_cents) FROM balances
=> [[0]]
--- check query | When Bo pays for something, his row changes
INSERT INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES (4, 2, 'Fuel', 2000);
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES (4, 2, 1000), (4, 4, 1000);
SELECT paid_cents, owed_cents, net_cents FROM balances WHERE person_id = 2
=> [[2000, 7100, -5100]]

+++ practice | Settling up
--- schema
CREATE TABLE people (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE expenses (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  paid_by INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  description TEXT NOT NULL,
  amount_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (amount_cents > 0)
);
CREATE TABLE shares (
  expense_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES expenses(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  person_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  share_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (share_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (expense_id, person_id)
);
INSERT INTO people (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo'), (3, 'Cy'), (4, 'Dee');
INSERT INTO expenses (id, paid_by, description, amount_cents) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'Dinner',  9000),
  (2, 1, 'Tickets', 5000),
  (3, 3, 'Snacks',  1200);
INSERT INTO shares (expense_id, person_id, share_cents) VALUES
  (1, 1, 3000), (1, 2, 3000), (1, 3, 3000),
  (2, 1, 2500), (2, 2, 2500),
  (3, 2, 600), (3, 3, 600);
--- task
This problem has its own copy of the expense schema, built and filled. Now friends pay each other back, and the app must record it. Extend the design:

1. Foreign keys are enforced.
2. `settlements`: `id` (integer primary key), `from_id` (required, a real person: who paid), `to_id` (required, a real person: who received), `cents` (required integer, more than zero). Nobody can pay themselves.
3. A view `balances_after` with one row for **every** person and the columns `person_id`, `name` and `net_cents`, where `net_cents` is what they paid for expenses, minus what they owe in shares, plus what they have sent in settlements, minus what they have received.

When everyone has settled up, every `net_cents` is 0.
--- starter
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE settlements (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  from_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  to_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL
);
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE settlements (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  from_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  to_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES people(id),
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (cents > 0),
  CHECK (from_id <> to_id)
);

CREATE VIEW balances_after AS
WITH paid AS (
  SELECT paid_by AS person_id, SUM(amount_cents) AS cents FROM expenses GROUP BY paid_by
), owed AS (
  SELECT person_id, SUM(share_cents) AS cents FROM shares GROUP BY person_id
), sent AS (
  SELECT from_id AS person_id, SUM(cents) AS cents FROM settlements GROUP BY from_id
), received AS (
  SELECT to_id AS person_id, SUM(cents) AS cents FROM settlements GROUP BY to_id
)
SELECT p.id AS person_id, p.name,
       COALESCE(pd.cents, 0) - COALESCE(ow.cents, 0)
         + COALESCE(se.cents, 0) - COALESCE(re.cents, 0) AS net_cents
FROM people p
LEFT JOIN paid pd     ON pd.person_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN owed ow     ON ow.person_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN sent se     ON se.person_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN received re ON re.person_id = p.id;
--- hint
Four separate lists now hang off each person: expenses paid, shares owed, settlements sent and settlements received. The capstone's rule holds: total each one on its own, one row per person, before joining.
--- hint
Two of the totals come from the same table, `settlements`, once grouped by `from_id` and once by `to_id`. The rule "nobody pays themselves" is a check across two columns: `CHECK (from_id <> to_id)`.
--- hint
Four CTEs, `paid`, `owed`, `sent` and `received`, each `LEFT JOIN`ed onto `people`, and `net_cents` built from four `COALESCE(…, 0)` terms: plus paid, minus owed, plus sent, minus received.
--- check query | Before anyone pays back, the balances match the expenses
SELECT person_id, name, net_cents FROM balances_after ORDER BY person_id
=> [[1, "Ana", 8500], [2, "Bo", -6100], [3, "Cy", -2400], [4, "Dee", 0]]
--- check query | Bo pays Ana back in full
INSERT INTO settlements (id, from_id, to_id, cents) VALUES (1, 2, 1, 6100);
SELECT person_id, net_cents FROM balances_after ORDER BY person_id
=> [[1, 2400], [2, 0], [3, -2400], [4, 0]]
--- check query | Paying yourself, or paying nothing, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO settlements (id, from_id, to_id, cents) VALUES (2, 3, 3, 500), (3, 3, 1, 0) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Once Cy pays too, everyone is at 0
INSERT INTO settlements (id, from_id, to_id, cents) VALUES (4, 3, 1, 2400);
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM balances_after WHERE net_cents <> 0
=> [[0]]
--- check query | settlements references people twice
SELECT "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('settlements') ORDER BY "from"
=> [["people", "from_id"], ["people", "to_id"]]

=== sqlp-15 | Capstone: appointment booking
--- schema
CREATE TABLE staff (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO staff (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Dr Ng'), (2, 'Dr Oz');
--- teach
Last lesson you split expenses, and kept two one-to-many totals from multiplying. This is the last capstone, and the [[last lesson of the SQL courses|what-next]]: bookings for a small clinic. Clients book appointments with staff. The clinic needs to spot [[double bookings|double-booking]] and see each day's workload.

Most of the spec uses tools you have used before. Two are new: telling whether two time slots overlap, and listing each pair of rows only once. Each gets its own short step below, before you start.

### The table you start with

The staff list already exists, as `staff`:

| id | name |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Dr Ng |
| 2 | Dr Oz |

### The spec

1. `clients`: `id` (integer primary key), `email` (required, and unique **ignoring case**: `ANA@x.io` and `ana@x.io` are the same client), `name`.
2. `appointments`: `id` (integer primary key), `staff_id` (required, a real staff member), `client_id` (required, a real client), `starts_at` and `ends_at` (required timestamps stored as text, `'YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM'`), `status` (required; `'booked'` or `'cancelled'`; **defaults to `'booked'`**).
3. An appointment must **end after it starts**.
4. A view `double_bookings` with the columns `staff_id`, `first_id` and `second_id`: every pair of **booked** appointments for the same staff member whose times overlap, with `first_id` less than `second_id`. Two time slots overlap when each starts before the other ends. Back-to-back appointments (one ends at 09:30, the next starts at 09:30) do **not** overlap. Canceled appointments never count.
5. A view `daily_schedule` with the columns `staff_id`, `day`, `appointments` and `booked_minutes`: for each staff member and calendar day (`'YYYY-MM-DD'`) that has booked appointments, how many there are and their total length in whole minutes.
6. An index that lets the app list one staff member's appointments [[in time order without a separate sort|index-order]]: its first column `staff_id`, its second `starts_at`.

"A real staff member" and "a real client" are foreign keys, so turn foreign keys on first, as in the other capstones.

### Where each tool comes from

| spec | tool | lesson |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | a unique index on `lower(email)` | "Deduplicating: keep the latest row" |
| 2 | `REFERENCES`, `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON` | "Foreign keys" |
| 2 | `CHECK (… IN (…))`, `DEFAULT` | "Constraints: rules the database enforces" |
| 3 | a `CHECK` comparing two columns | "Library 1: the schema" |
| 4 | a self-join, plus the two new steps below | "Self-joins" |
| 5 | `date()` | "Date arithmetic and date ranges" |
| 5 | `unixepoch()` | "Sessionising events" |
| 6 | an index on two columns | "Indexes and EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN" |

### Same email, any capitals

`lower(...)` turns text into lowercase:

```sql
SELECT lower('ANA@x.io');
```

```
ana@x.io
```

In "Deduplicating: keep the latest row" you made a `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX` on an expression, so that no two rows could share its value. Build one on `lower(email)`. Then `'ANA@x.io'` and `'ana@x.io'` both become `'ana@x.io'` inside the index, and the second one is refused. A plain `UNIQUE` on `email` would not do it: to it, those are two different pieces of text. There is also [[another way to ignore case|nocase]].

### New: do two time slots overlap?

Picture two meetings in one diary. They clash when [[each one starts before the other ends|overlap-picture]]. For appointments A and B:

- A starts before B ends, **and**
- B starts before A ends.

Timestamps written as `'YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM'` sort in time order, so `<` on them means "earlier than". Here is one appointment from 09:30 to 10:00, and another from 09:45 to 10:15 (the dates are left off to keep it short):

```sql
SELECT '09:30' < '10:15' AND '09:45' < '10:00' AS overlap;
```

| overlap |
| --- |
| 1 |

Both tests are true, so they overlap. Now two back-to-back appointments, 09:00 to 09:30 and 09:30 to 10:00:

```sql
SELECT '09:00' < '10:00' AND '09:30' < '09:30' AS overlap;
```

| overlap |
| --- |
| 0 |

The second test is false: 09:30 is not *before* 09:30. That is exactly what the spec wants. The strict `<` ("less than") lets back-to-back appointments through, where `<=` ("less than or equal to") would flag every one of them.

### New: each pair only once

To compare appointments with each other, join `appointments` to itself, as in "Self-joins". But a self-join pairs every row with every row: each row with itself, and each pair twice, once in each order. See it on `staff`:

```sql
SELECT a.name, b.name FROM staff a JOIN staff b ON a.id <> b.id;
```

| name | name |
| --- | --- |
| Dr Ng | Dr Oz |
| Dr Oz | Dr Ng |

`<>` ("not equal") removes each row paired with itself, but the pair still comes twice. Use `<` instead:

```sql
SELECT a.name, b.name FROM staff a JOIN staff b ON a.id < b.id;
```

| name | name |
| --- | --- |
| Dr Ng | Dr Oz |

Now each pair comes once, with the smaller id first: the same rule as `first_id` less than `second_id`. It also keeps each appointment from being paired with itself, which matters here, because every appointment overlaps itself.

### Minutes between two times

In "Sessionising events", `unixepoch(...)` turned a moment into a count of seconds. Subtract two of them to get the seconds in between, and divide by 60 for minutes:

```sql
SELECT (unixepoch('2024-09-02 10:30') - unixepoch('2024-09-02 09:59')) / 60;
```

```
31
```

Both numbers are whole, so the division gives a whole number, as in the note on dividing whole numbers in "Choosing columns". For the day, `date('2024-09-02 09:59')` gives `'2024-09-02'`, from "Date arithmetic and date ranges".

**Watch out:** canceled appointments must drop out of both views. In the self-join, test the status of **both** copies: `a.status = 'booked' AND b.status = 'booked'`. Test only `a`, and a canceled `b` can still be reported as a clash.

::: context what-next Where SQL goes from here
Every lesson here has run on SQLite, a small database that lives inside the program using it. It is built into every Android phone and iPhone and into most web browsers. Bigger systems, with many users writing at once, often use a database server such as PostgreSQL or MySQL. The SQL you learned carries over almost unchanged: `SELECT`, joins, `GROUP BY`, window functions, constraints, views. What changes is small, like some function names for dates and times.
:::

::: context double-booking Refusing a clash, not only reporting it
This spec only reports double bookings, with a view. Some databases can refuse them. PostgreSQL has an **exclusion constraint**, which refuses a new row whose time range overlaps a row already in the table. SQLite has nothing like that, but you could get close with what you learned in "Triggers: reacting to changes": a `BEFORE INSERT` trigger that looks for an overlapping booked appointment and calls `RAISE(ABORT, ...)` if it finds one. A clinic might still prefer a report, because sometimes a double booking is on purpose.
:::

::: context index-order Why the index saves a sort
In "Indexes and EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN", listing one customer's orders by date needed an extra line in the plan, `USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY`: the database found the rows, then sorted them. An index on two columns, sorted by the first and then by the second, holds each staff member's appointments side by side, already in time order. So a query like `SELECT * FROM appointments WHERE staff_id = 1 ORDER BY starts_at` loses that sort step. An app that shows a doctor's day runs that query all the time.
:::

::: context nocase Ignoring case in the column itself
Instead of an index on `lower(email)`, you can tell SQLite that the column itself ignores case. Write `COLLATE NOCASE` after the type: `email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE COLLATE NOCASE`. A **collation** is a rule for comparing text, and `NOCASE` treats A to Z the same as a to z. Now `UNIQUE` sees `'ANA@x.io'` and `'ana@x.io'` as the same, and so does `=` in a `WHERE` on that column. One limit: `NOCASE` only knows the 26 English letters, so `'É'` and `'é'` still differ. Either way passes this lesson.
:::

::: context overlap-picture Overlapping, and only touching
On this time line, appointment 2 runs from 09:30 to 10:00 and appointment 3 from 09:45 to 10:15. Each starts before the other ends, so they share the red stretch. Appointment 1 ends at 09:30, the very minute appointment 2 starts: the two touch, but share no time.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="180" y="30" width="50" height="94" fill="#b4232c" opacity="0.18"/>
  <rect x="30" y="36" width="100" height="22" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="80" y="51" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1</text>
  <rect x="130" y="66" width="100" height="22" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="155" y="81" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2</text>
  <rect x="180" y="96" width="100" height="22" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="255" y="111" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">3</text>
  <line x1="30" y1="134" x2="330" y2="134" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="30" y1="130" x2="30" y2="138" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <line x1="130" y1="130" x2="130" y2="138" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <line x1="230" y1="130" x2="230" y2="138" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <line x1="330" y1="130" x2="330" y2="138" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="30" y="152" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">09:00</text>
  <text x="130" y="152" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">09:30</text>
  <text x="230" y="152" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">10:00</text>
  <text x="330" y="152" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">10:30</text>
  <text x="205" y="22" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">overlap</text>
  <text x="180" y="166" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">1 and 2 only touch; 2 and 3 overlap</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Build everything in the spec, rules 1 to 6: turn foreign keys on; create the `clients` table (`id`, `email`, `name`) and the `appointments` table (`id`, `staff_id`, `client_id`, `starts_at`, `ends_at`, `status`) with every rule the spec states for them; create the views `double_bookings` (`staff_id`, `first_id`, `second_id`) and `daily_schedule` (`staff_id`, `day`, `appointments`, `booked_minutes`); and create an index on `appointments` whose first column is `staff_id` and second is `starts_at`.
--- starter
-- Clinic bookings: your schema goes here.
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE clients (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE COLLATE NOCASE,
  name TEXT
);

CREATE TABLE appointments (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  staff_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES staff(id),
  client_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES clients(id),
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  ends_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'booked' CHECK (status IN ('booked', 'cancelled')),
  CHECK (ends_at > starts_at)
);

CREATE INDEX idx_appointments_staff_start ON appointments(staff_id, starts_at);

CREATE VIEW double_bookings AS
SELECT a.staff_id, a.id AS first_id, b.id AS second_id
FROM appointments a
JOIN appointments b
  ON b.staff_id = a.staff_id
 AND a.id < b.id
 AND a.starts_at < b.ends_at
 AND b.starts_at < a.ends_at
WHERE a.status = 'booked' AND b.status = 'booked';

CREATE VIEW daily_schedule AS
SELECT staff_id,
       date(starts_at) AS day,
       COUNT(*) AS appointments,
       SUM((unixepoch(ends_at) - unixepoch(starts_at)) / 60) AS booked_minutes
FROM appointments
WHERE status = 'booked'
GROUP BY staff_id, date(starts_at);
--- hint
Case-insensitive uniqueness: a unique index on the expression `lower(email)`, as in the expert course's deduplication lesson: `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX clients_email_key ON clients(lower(email));`. (Declaring the column `UNIQUE COLLATE NOCASE`, which makes it compare ignoring case, works too.) "Ends after it starts" is `CHECK (ends_at > starts_at)` at the end of `appointments`, and the status is `status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'booked' CHECK (status IN ('booked', 'cancelled'))`.
--- hint
Overlap is a self-join, `FROM appointments a JOIN appointments b ON …`: same staff, `a.id < b.id`, `a.starts_at < b.ends_at AND b.starts_at < a.ends_at`, and both booked. Select `a.staff_id`, `a.id AS first_id`, `b.id AS second_id`.
--- hint
Minutes: `(unixepoch(ends_at) - unixepoch(starts_at)) / 60`, summed per staff member and `date(starts_at)`, keeping only booked rows. The index is `CREATE INDEX idx_appointments_staff_start ON appointments(staff_id, starts_at);`.
--- check query | Clients can be added; the same email in other capitals is refused
INSERT INTO clients (id, email, name) VALUES (1, 'ana@x.io', 'Ana'), (2, 'bo@x.io', 'Bo');
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO clients (id, email, name) VALUES (3, 'ANA@x.io', 'Ana again');
SELECT id FROM clients ORDER BY id
=> [[1], [2]]
--- check query | Appointments are booked by default
INSERT INTO appointments (id, staff_id, client_id, starts_at, ends_at) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 09:30'),
  (2, 1, 2, '2024-09-02 09:30', '2024-09-02 10:00'),
  (3, 1, 2, '2024-09-02 09:45', '2024-09-02 10:15'),
  (4, 2, 1, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 10:00'),
  (5, 1, 1, '2024-09-03 14:00', '2024-09-03 15:00')
RETURNING id, status
=> [[1, "booked"], [2, "booked"], [3, "booked"], [4, "booked"], [5, "booked"]]
--- check query | An appointment ending before it starts, or with an unknown status, is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO appointments (id, staff_id, client_id, starts_at, ends_at, status) VALUES
  (8, 1, 1, '2024-09-04 10:00', '2024-09-04 09:00', 'booked'),
  (9, 1, 1, '2024-09-04 11:00', '2024-09-04 11:30', 'maybe')
RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Overlaps are found; back-to-back is fine
SELECT staff_id, first_id, second_id FROM double_bookings ORDER BY staff_id, first_id, second_id
=> [[1, 2, 3]]
--- check query | Cancelled appointments stop counting; a one-minute overlap is caught
UPDATE appointments SET status = 'cancelled' WHERE id = 3;
INSERT INTO appointments (id, staff_id, client_id, starts_at, ends_at) VALUES (6, 2, 2, '2024-09-02 09:59', '2024-09-02 10:30');
SELECT staff_id, first_id, second_id FROM double_bookings ORDER BY staff_id, first_id, second_id
=> [[2, 4, 6]]
--- check query | The daily schedule counts booked appointments and minutes
SELECT staff_id, day, appointments, booked_minutes FROM daily_schedule ORDER BY staff_id, day
=> [[1, "2024-09-02", 2, 60], [1, "2024-09-03", 1, 60], [2, "2024-09-02", 2, 91]]
--- check query | An index leads with staff_id, then starts_at
SELECT COUNT(*) > 0
FROM pragma_index_list('appointments') il
JOIN pragma_index_info(il.name) a ON a.seqno = 0 AND a.name = 'staff_id'
JOIN pragma_index_info(il.name) b ON b.seqno = 1 AND b.name = 'starts_at'
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Foreign keys are enforced
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Each appointment names a real staff member and client
SELECT "table", "from" FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('appointments') ORDER BY "from"
=> [["clients", "client_id"], ["staff", "staff_id"]]

+++ practice | Rooms booked twice
--- schema
CREATE TABLE bookings (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  room TEXT NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  ends_at TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO bookings (id, room, starts_at, ends_at) VALUES
  (1, 'Apollo', '2024-10-01 09:00', '2024-10-01 10:00'),
  (2, 'Apollo', '2024-10-01 09:30', '2024-10-01 11:00'),
  (3, 'Apollo', '2024-10-01 11:00', '2024-10-01 12:00'),
  (4, 'Gemini', '2024-10-01 09:00', '2024-10-01 10:00'),
  (5, 'Apollo', '2024-10-01 09:45', '2024-10-01 09:50'),
  (6, 'Gemini', '2024-10-02 09:00', '2024-10-02 10:00');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `bookings` (`id`, `room`, `starts_at`, `ends_at`), for meeting rooms. Times are text in the form `'YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM'`.

Write one query that lists every pair of bookings of the same room whose times overlap, as three columns: `room`, `first_id` and `second_id`, with `first_id` less than `second_id`, so each pair appears once. Two bookings overlap when each starts before the other ends; one that starts exactly when another ends does not overlap it. Sort by `room`, `first_id`, `second_id`.
--- starter
SELECT a.room, a.id AS first_id, b.id AS second_id
FROM bookings a
JOIN bookings b ON b.room = a.room AND a.id <> b.id
ORDER BY a.room, first_id, second_id;
--- solution
SELECT a.room, a.id AS first_id, b.id AS second_id
FROM bookings a
JOIN bookings b
  ON b.room = a.room
 AND a.id < b.id
 AND a.starts_at < b.ends_at
 AND b.starts_at < a.ends_at
ORDER BY a.room, first_id, second_id;
--- hint
The starter pairs every Apollo booking with every other one, in both orders. Two things are missing: the overlap test, and a rule that keeps only one order of each pair.
--- hint
Replace `a.id <> b.id` with `a.id < b.id`, and add both halves of the overlap test with strict `<`: `a.starts_at < b.ends_at AND b.starts_at < a.ends_at`.
--- check result | Three clashes in Apollo; the 11:00 booking only touches, and Gemini is fine
ordered
[["Apollo", 1, 2], ["Apollo", 1, 5], ["Apollo", 2, 5]]
--- check source | Keeps each pair once with a.id < b.id
\w+\.id\s*<\s*\w+\.id
--- check source absent | Does not pick bookings by id
\bid\s*(=|[Ii][Nn])\s*\(?\s*\d

+++ practice | Gaps between appointments
--- schema
CREATE TABLE staff (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE appointments (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  staff_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES staff(id),
  client_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  ends_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'booked' CHECK (status IN ('booked', 'cancelled')),
  CHECK (ends_at > starts_at)
);
INSERT INTO staff (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Dr Ng'), (2, 'Dr Oz');
INSERT INTO appointments (id, staff_id, client_id, starts_at, ends_at, status) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 09:30', 'booked'),
  (2, 1, 2, '2024-09-02 10:00', '2024-09-02 10:45', 'booked'),
  (3, 1, 1, '2024-09-02 11:00', '2024-09-02 11:30', 'cancelled'),
  (4, 1, 2, '2024-09-02 13:00', '2024-09-02 14:00', 'booked'),
  (5, 2, 1, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 10:00', 'booked'),
  (6, 2, 2, '2024-09-02 10:00', '2024-09-02 10:30', 'booked'),
  (7, 1, 1, '2024-09-03 09:00', '2024-09-03 09:30', 'booked');
--- task
This problem has its own `staff` and `appointments` tables, built as in the capstone and filled. The clinic wants to see how much free time lies between appointments.

For every **booked** appointment, return `staff_id`, `id`, and `gap_minutes`: the whole minutes from its end to the start of the same staff member's **next booked** appointment on the **same day**. The last booked appointment of a day gets NULL. Canceled appointments are ignored completely. Sort by `staff_id`, then `starts_at`.
--- starter
SELECT staff_id, id,
       (unixepoch(LEAD(starts_at) OVER (PARTITION BY staff_id ORDER BY starts_at)) - unixepoch(ends_at)) / 60 AS gap_minutes
FROM appointments
ORDER BY staff_id, starts_at;
--- solution
WITH booked AS (
  SELECT staff_id, id, starts_at, ends_at,
         LEAD(starts_at) OVER (PARTITION BY staff_id, date(starts_at) ORDER BY starts_at) AS next_start
  FROM appointments
  WHERE status = 'booked'
)
SELECT staff_id, id, (unixepoch(next_start) - unixepoch(ends_at)) / 60 AS gap_minutes
FROM booked
ORDER BY staff_id, starts_at;
--- hint
`LEAD(starts_at)`, from "LAG and LEAD: comparing with neighbors", reads the next appointment's start. Two things decide what "next" means: which rows are in the window, and how it is partitioned.
--- hint
Filter to booked appointments **before** the window runs, in a CTE, so a canceled one is never anyone's neighbor. Partition by staff member **and** day: `PARTITION BY staff_id, date(starts_at)`.
--- check result | 30 and 135 minutes for Dr Ng, back-to-back for Dr Oz
ordered
[[1, 1, 30], [1, 2, 135], [1, 4, null], [1, 7, null], [2, 5, 0], [2, 6, null]]
--- check source | Reads the next appointment with LEAD
[Ll][Ee][Aa][Dd]\s*\(
--- check source | Minutes come from unixepoch
unixepoch\s*\(

+++ practice | Loading clients whatever the capitals
--- schema
CREATE TABLE client_batch (
  row_no INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  name TEXT
);
INSERT INTO client_batch (row_no, email, name) VALUES
  (1, 'ana@x.io',  'Ana'),
  (2, 'BO@x.io',   'Bo'),
  (3, 'Ana@X.io',  'Ana Ruiz'),
  (4, 'cy@x.io',   'Cy'),
  (5, 'bo@x.io ',  'Bo Chen');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `client_batch` (`row_no`, `email`, `name`): sign-ups from a web form, in the order they arrived. The same person sometimes signed up twice, with the email typed in other capitals or with a space at the end.

1. Create `clients`: `id` (integer primary key), `email` (text, required), `name` (text).
2. Create a unique index named `clients_email_key` on `lower(trim(email))`, so the same email in any capitals, with or without stray spaces, is one client.
3. Load the batch with one `INSERT … SELECT` that is also an upsert. Rows go in in `row_no` order. A new person gets a row; a person already there keeps their `id` and the email as it was first stored, but takes the newer `name`.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE clients (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  name TEXT
);

INSERT INTO clients (email, name)
SELECT email, name FROM client_batch ORDER BY row_no;
--- solution
CREATE TABLE clients (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  name TEXT
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX clients_email_key ON clients(lower(trim(email)));

INSERT INTO clients (email, name)
SELECT email, name FROM client_batch
ORDER BY row_no
ON CONFLICT (lower(trim(email))) DO UPDATE SET name = excluded.name;
--- hint
The index is the capstone's idea, on `lower(trim(email))` this time. Once it exists, a second Ana is a conflict, and an upsert decides what to do with it.
--- hint
The conflict target must be the same expression as the index: `ON CONFLICT (lower(trim(email))) DO UPDATE SET name = excluded.name`. Only the name changes, so the first email and the `id` stay.
--- check query | Three clients, each with their first email and latest name
SELECT id, email, name FROM clients ORDER BY id
=> [[1, "ana@x.io", "Ana Ruiz"], [2, "BO@x.io", "Bo Chen"], [3, "cy@x.io", "Cy"]]
--- check query | The same email in capitals is refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO clients (email, name) VALUES ('CY@X.IO', 'Cy again') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | clients_email_key is a unique index
SELECT name, "unique" FROM pragma_index_list('clients') WHERE name = 'clients_email_key'
=> [["clients_email_key", 1]]
--- check source | Uses an upsert
[Oo][Nn]\s+[Cc][Oo][Nn][Ff][Ll][Ii][Cc][Tt]

+++ practice | How long each clash lasts
--- schema
CREATE TABLE staff (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE appointments (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  staff_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES staff(id),
  client_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  ends_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'booked' CHECK (status IN ('booked', 'cancelled')),
  CHECK (ends_at > starts_at)
);
INSERT INTO staff (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Dr Ng'), (2, 'Dr Oz');
INSERT INTO appointments (id, staff_id, client_id, starts_at, ends_at, status) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 10:00', 'booked'),
  (2, 1, 2, '2024-09-02 09:15', '2024-09-02 09:45', 'booked'),
  (3, 1, 3, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 10:00', 'booked'),
  (4, 1, 4, '2024-09-02 10:00', '2024-09-02 10:30', 'booked'),
  (5, 1, 5, '2024-09-02 08:30', '2024-09-02 09:00', 'booked'),
  (6, 1, 6, '2024-09-02 09:30', '2024-09-02 09:40', 'cancelled'),
  (7, 2, 1, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 10:00', 'booked'),
  (8, 1, 7, '2024-09-02 23:30', '2024-09-03 00:30', 'booked'),
  (9, 1, 8, '2024-09-03 00:00', '2024-09-03 00:15', 'booked');
--- task
This problem has its own `staff` and `appointments`, built as in the capstone and filled with every awkward case: an appointment inside another, two identical ones, some that only touch, a canceled one, and one that runs past midnight.

Create a view named `clashes` with the columns `staff_id`, `first_id`, `second_id` and `overlap_minutes`: every pair of booked appointments of the same staff member that overlap (each starts before the other ends), with `first_id` less than `second_id`, and the whole minutes the two share. The shared stretch runs from the **later** of the two starts to the **earlier** of the two ends.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW clashes AS
SELECT a.staff_id, a.id AS first_id, b.id AS second_id,
       (unixepoch(a.ends_at) - unixepoch(b.starts_at)) / 60 AS overlap_minutes
FROM appointments a
JOIN appointments b
  ON b.staff_id = a.staff_id
 AND a.id < b.id
 AND a.starts_at <= b.ends_at
 AND b.starts_at <= a.ends_at
WHERE a.status = 'booked';
--- solution
CREATE VIEW clashes AS
SELECT a.staff_id, a.id AS first_id, b.id AS second_id,
       (unixepoch(MIN(a.ends_at, b.ends_at)) - unixepoch(MAX(a.starts_at, b.starts_at))) / 60 AS overlap_minutes
FROM appointments a
JOIN appointments b
  ON b.staff_id = a.staff_id
 AND a.id < b.id
 AND a.starts_at < b.ends_at
 AND b.starts_at < a.ends_at
WHERE a.status = 'booked' AND b.status = 'booked';
--- hint
Three bugs hide in the starter: touching appointments count as clashes, a canceled appointment can still clash, and the overlap is measured from the wrong ends.
--- hint
Use strict `<` in both halves of the overlap test, and check the status of **both** copies. The times sort as text, so the two-value `MAX` and `MIN` from "Library 3: overdue loans and fines" pick the later start and the earlier end.
--- hint
`overlap_minutes` is `(unixepoch(MIN(a.ends_at, b.ends_at)) - unixepoch(MAX(a.starts_at, b.starts_at))) / 60`.
--- check query | Four clashes, the one past midnight included
SELECT staff_id, first_id, second_id, overlap_minutes FROM clashes ORDER BY staff_id, first_id, second_id
=> [[1, 1, 2, 30], [1, 1, 3, 60], [1, 2, 3, 30], [1, 8, 9, 15]]
--- check query | Cancelling one of the identical pair removes its clashes
UPDATE appointments SET status = 'cancelled' WHERE id = 3;
SELECT first_id, second_id FROM clashes ORDER BY first_id, second_id
=> [[1, 2], [8, 9]]
--- check query | Moving a start one minute earlier makes a one-minute clash
UPDATE appointments SET starts_at = '2024-09-02 09:59' WHERE id = 4;
SELECT first_id, second_id, overlap_minutes FROM clashes ORDER BY first_id, second_id
=> [[1, 2, 30], [1, 4, 1], [8, 9, 15]]

+++ practice | Minutes that come up short
--- schema
CREATE TABLE staff (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE appointments (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  staff_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES staff(id),
  client_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  ends_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'booked' CHECK (status IN ('booked', 'cancelled')),
  CHECK (ends_at > starts_at)
);
INSERT INTO staff (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Dr Ng'), (2, 'Dr Oz');
INSERT INTO appointments (id, staff_id, client_id, starts_at, ends_at, status) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 09:30', 'booked'),
  (2, 1, 2, '2024-09-02 11:00', '2024-09-02 11:20', 'booked'),
  (3, 1, 1, '2024-09-03 14:00', '2024-09-03 14:50', 'booked'),
  (4, 2, 2, '2024-09-02 10:00', '2024-09-02 10:45', 'booked'),
  (5, 2, 1, '2024-09-04 15:00', '2024-09-04 15:40', 'cancelled');
--- task
This problem has its own `staff` and `appointments`, built as in the capstone and filled. A view `staff_load` is meant to show, for each staff member, the number of different days they have booked appointments and the total booked minutes:

```sql
CREATE VIEW staff_load AS
SELECT staff_id,
       COUNT(DISTINCT date(starts_at)) AS days,
       SUM(CAST((julianday(ends_at) - julianday(starts_at)) * 24 * 60 AS INTEGER)) AS booked_minutes
FROM appointments
WHERE status = 'booked'
GROUP BY staff_id;
```

Dr Ng's appointments last 30, 20 and 50 minutes, but the view says 98 instead of 100. Fix the minutes so they are exact.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW staff_load AS
SELECT staff_id,
       COUNT(DISTINCT date(starts_at)) AS days,
       SUM(CAST((julianday(ends_at) - julianday(starts_at)) * 24 * 60 AS INTEGER)) AS booked_minutes
FROM appointments
WHERE status = 'booked'
GROUP BY staff_id;
--- solution
CREATE VIEW staff_load AS
SELECT staff_id,
       COUNT(DISTINCT date(starts_at)) AS days,
       SUM((unixepoch(ends_at) - unixepoch(starts_at)) / 60) AS booked_minutes
FROM appointments
WHERE status = 'booked'
GROUP BY staff_id;
--- hint
Try `SELECT (julianday('2024-09-02 11:20') - julianday('2024-09-02 11:00')) * 24 * 60;` on its own. A fraction of a day turned back into minutes lands a hair below 20, and the cast chops it to 19.
--- hint
Work in whole seconds instead, as "Sessionising events" did: `(unixepoch(ends_at) - unixepoch(starts_at)) / 60`.
--- check query | Exact minutes for both doctors
SELECT staff_id, days, booked_minutes FROM staff_load ORDER BY staff_id
=> [[1, 2, 100], [2, 1, 45]]
--- check query | A new 50-minute appointment adds exactly 50
INSERT INTO appointments (id, staff_id, client_id, starts_at, ends_at) VALUES (6, 2, 1, '2024-09-04 14:00', '2024-09-04 14:50');
SELECT days, booked_minutes FROM staff_load WHERE staff_id = 2
=> [[2, 95]]
--- check source absent | No julianday left in the minutes
julianday

+++ practice | The first free half hour
--- schema
CREATE TABLE staff (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE appointments (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  staff_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES staff(id),
  client_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  ends_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'booked' CHECK (status IN ('booked', 'cancelled')),
  CHECK (ends_at > starts_at)
);
INSERT INTO staff (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Dr Ng'), (2, 'Dr Oz'), (3, 'Dr Wu');
INSERT INTO appointments (id, staff_id, client_id, starts_at, ends_at, status) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 09:30', 'booked'),
  (2, 1, 2, '2024-09-02 09:30', '2024-09-02 10:15', 'booked'),
  (3, 1, 3, '2024-09-02 10:30', '2024-09-02 11:00', 'booked'),
  (4, 1, 4, '2024-09-02 11:00', '2024-09-02 12:00', 'booked'),
  (5, 1, 5, '2024-09-02 12:00', '2024-09-02 13:00', 'cancelled'),
  (6, 2, 1, '2024-09-02 09:10', '2024-09-02 09:50', 'booked'),
  (7, 2, 2, '2024-09-02 10:15', '2024-09-02 17:00', 'booked'),
  (8, 3, 3, '2024-09-02 09:30', '2024-09-02 10:00', 'booked'),
  (9, 3, 4, '2024-09-03 09:00', '2024-09-03 10:00', 'booked');
--- task
This problem has its own `staff` (three doctors) and `appointments`, built as in the capstone and filled. A client phones asking for the earliest 30-minute slot on `2024-09-02`. The clinic is open from `09:00` to `17:00`.

For each staff member, find the earliest start time `t` on that day such that:

- `t` is 09:00, or the end of one of that staff member's booked appointments that day (a free slot always starts at opening time or right after an appointment);
- the slot from `t` to 30 minutes later ends no later than 17:00;
- no booked appointment of that staff member overlaps the slot (touching is fine).

`strftime('%Y-%m-%d %H:%M', t, '+30 minutes')` gives the time 30 minutes after `t`, written the same way as the table's times. Return `staff_id`, `name` and `first_free` (the time `t`, or NULL if the staff member has no free slot that day), one row per staff member, sorted by `staff_id`.
--- starter
SELECT s.id AS staff_id, s.name, MIN(a.ends_at) AS first_free
FROM staff s
LEFT JOIN appointments a ON a.staff_id = s.id
GROUP BY s.id, s.name
ORDER BY s.id;
--- solution
WITH day_appts AS (
  SELECT staff_id, starts_at, ends_at
  FROM appointments
  WHERE status = 'booked' AND date(starts_at) = '2024-09-02'
), candidates AS (
  SELECT id AS staff_id, '2024-09-02 09:00' AS t FROM staff
  UNION
  SELECT staff_id, ends_at FROM day_appts
), free AS (
  SELECT k.staff_id, k.t
  FROM candidates k
  WHERE strftime('%Y-%m-%d %H:%M', k.t, '+30 minutes') <= '2024-09-02 17:00'
    AND NOT EXISTS (
      SELECT 1 FROM day_appts a
      WHERE a.staff_id = k.staff_id
        AND a.starts_at < strftime('%Y-%m-%d %H:%M', k.t, '+30 minutes')
        AND k.t < a.ends_at
    )
)
SELECT s.id AS staff_id, s.name, MIN(f.t) AS first_free
FROM staff s
LEFT JOIN free f ON f.staff_id = s.id
GROUP BY s.id, s.name
ORDER BY s.id;
--- hint
Break it into steps: the booked appointments of that day; the candidate start times for each staff member (opening time, plus every end); the candidates whose half hour is free; and finally the earliest free one per staff member.
--- hint
The candidates are a `UNION` of two lists: `SELECT id AS staff_id, '2024-09-02 09:00' AS t FROM staff` and `SELECT staff_id, ends_at FROM` the day's booked appointments. A candidate is free when `NOT EXISTS` an appointment of that staff member overlapping the slot, using the capstone's overlap test with the slot's start and end.
--- hint
Finish from `staff` with a `LEFT JOIN` to the free candidates and `MIN(f.t)`, so a staff member with no free slot still gets a row, with NULL.
--- check result | Dr Ng at noon, nothing for Dr Oz, Dr Wu at opening time
ordered
[[1, "Dr Ng", "2024-09-02 12:00"], [2, "Dr Oz", null], [3, "Dr Wu", "2024-09-02 09:00"]]
--- check source | Works out the end of the slot with strftime
strftime\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type the answer
12:00

=== sqlp-gate | SQL projects: mastery gate
--- teach
This gate covers the whole course: designing schemas that refuse bad data, cleaning and loading an export, reports that survive refunds, NULLs, fan-out and empty days, JSON ingest, active users, funnels, retention, triggers, and designs built from a written spec. Every problem is new, and most of them combine two or three projects. You have 110 minutes for ten problems, of which you need seven, and eleven questions, of which you need nine. To get ready, redo the practice problems of the lessons that felt hard, especially the edge-case and debugging ones, and work each answer out by hand before you trust the query.
--- gate
pass 7
questions 9
minutes 110

+++ problem | A roaster's export, loaded and reconciled
--- schema
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;
CREATE TABLE customers (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'refunded'))
);
CREATE TABLE order_items (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL,
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  unit_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (unit_cents >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, sku)
);
CREATE TABLE export_rows (
  row_no INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  order_ref INTEGER NOT NULL,
  placed TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT NOT NULL,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL,
  price TEXT NOT NULL,
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO export_rows (row_no, order_ref, placed, email, sku, price, qty, status) VALUES
  (1, 501, '2024-05-01 10:00:00', 'Kim@Mail.com',  'BEANS',   '12.35', 2, 'PAID'),
  (2, 501, '2024-05-01 10:00:00', 'kim@mail.com ', 'FILTER',  '4.35',  1, 'paid'),
  (3, 502, '2024-05-02 11:00:00', 'raj@mail.com',  'BEANS',   '12.35', 1, 'Refunded '),
  (4, 503, '2024-05-03 12:00:00', 'uma@mail.com',  'MUG',     '9.95',  3, 'paid'),
  (5, 503, '2024-05-03 12:00:00', 'uma@mail.com',  'MUG',     '9.95',  3, 'paid'),
  (6, 504, '2024-05-04 13:00:00', ' KIM@mail.com', 'GRINDER', '79.99', 1, 'paid');
--- task
A coffee roaster's shop has its own tables, empty: `customers` (`id`, `email` unique), `orders` (`id`, `customer_id`, `placed`, `status` one of `'paid'` or `'refunded'`) and `order_items` (`order_id`, `sku`, `qty`, `unit_cents`, with primary key `(order_id, sku)`). Its spreadsheet export is `export_rows` (`row_no`, `order_ref`, `placed`, `email`, `sku`, `price` as text in dollars, `qty`, `status`), one row per order line. The emails and statuses are typed in any capitals with stray spaces, and one line appears twice.

Load all three tables, parents first:

1. `customers`: one per email trimmed and in lower case, numbered in order of first appearance (by `row_no`).
2. `orders`: one per `order_ref`, used as the order's `id`, with its customer, `placed`, and the status trimmed and in lower case.
3. `order_items`: one per order and SKU, with `unit_cents` the price in whole cents, rounded before it is cast to an integer.

The loaded lines must add up to exactly 15124 cents.
--- starter
INSERT INTO customers (email)
SELECT DISTINCT email FROM export_rows;
--- solution
INSERT INTO customers (email)
SELECT lower(trim(email))
FROM export_rows
GROUP BY lower(trim(email))
ORDER BY MIN(row_no);

INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed, status)
SELECT DISTINCT r.order_ref, c.id, r.placed, lower(trim(r.status))
FROM export_rows r
JOIN customers c ON c.email = lower(trim(r.email));

INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, sku, qty, unit_cents)
SELECT DISTINCT order_ref, sku, qty, CAST(ROUND(CAST(price AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER)
FROM export_rows;
--- check query | 3 customers, 4 orders, 5 lines
SELECT (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM customers), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders), (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM order_items)
=> [[3, 4, 5]]
--- check query | Customers are clean and numbered by first appearance
SELECT id, email FROM customers ORDER BY id
=> [[1, "kim@mail.com"], [2, "raj@mail.com"], [3, "uma@mail.com"]]
--- check query | Each order belongs to the right customer, with a clean status
SELECT o.id, c.email, o.status FROM orders o JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id ORDER BY o.id
=> [[501, "kim@mail.com", "paid"], [502, "raj@mail.com", "refunded"], [503, "uma@mail.com", "paid"], [504, "kim@mail.com", "paid"]]
--- check query | Prices are exact cents and the total reconciles
SELECT (SELECT unit_cents FROM order_items WHERE order_id = 501 AND sku = 'BEANS'), (SELECT unit_cents FROM order_items WHERE sku = 'FILTER'), SUM(qty * unit_cents) FROM order_items
=> [[1235, 435, 15124]]

+++ problem | Renting out camping gear
--- task
Build a small rental database from this spec:

1. Foreign keys are enforced.
2. `gear`: `id` (integer primary key), `tag` (required text, unique), `kind` (required, one of `'tent'`, `'stove'`, `'pack'`), `day_rate_cents` (required integer, more than zero).
3. `rentals`: `id` (integer primary key), `gear_id` (required, a real piece of gear; deleting gear that has rentals is refused), `renter_email` (required), `out_on` and `due_on` (required dates as text), `back_on` (a date, NULL while the gear is out). `due_on` is after `out_on`, and `back_on`, when set, is on or after `out_on`.
4. A piece of gear can be out on only one rental at a time. Returned rentals stay as history.
5. A view `gear_status` with one row for every piece of gear and the columns `gear_id`, `tag`, `kind` and `status`: `'out'` when it has a rental that is not back yet, otherwise `'in'`.
6. A view `rental_charges` with one row per **returned** rental and the columns `rental_id`, `days` and `charge_cents`: `days` is the whole days from `out_on` to `back_on`, but at least 1 (a same-day return counts as one day), and `charge_cents` is `days` times the gear's `day_rate_cents`.
--- starter
-- Gear rental: your schema goes here.
--- solution
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE gear (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  tag TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  kind TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (kind IN ('tent', 'stove', 'pack')),
  day_rate_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (day_rate_cents > 0)
);

CREATE TABLE rentals (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  gear_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES gear(id),
  renter_email TEXT NOT NULL,
  out_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  due_on TEXT NOT NULL,
  back_on TEXT,
  CHECK (due_on > out_on),
  CHECK (back_on IS NULL OR back_on >= out_on)
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_rental_per_gear ON rentals(gear_id) WHERE back_on IS NULL;

CREATE VIEW gear_status AS
SELECT g.id AS gear_id, g.tag, g.kind,
       CASE WHEN COUNT(r.id) > 0 THEN 'out' ELSE 'in' END AS status
FROM gear g
LEFT JOIN rentals r ON r.gear_id = g.id AND r.back_on IS NULL
GROUP BY g.id, g.tag, g.kind;

CREATE VIEW rental_charges AS
WITH d AS (
  SELECT r.id AS rental_id, g.day_rate_cents,
         MAX(CAST(julianday(r.back_on) - julianday(r.out_on) AS INTEGER), 1) AS days
  FROM rentals r
