  JOIN gear g ON g.id = r.gear_id
  WHERE r.back_on IS NOT NULL
)
SELECT rental_id, days, days * day_rate_cents AS charge_cents
FROM d;
--- check query | Foreign keys are enforced
PRAGMA foreign_keys
=> [[1]]
--- check query | Gear can be added; a repeated tag, an unknown kind or a zero rate is refused
INSERT INTO gear (id, tag, kind, day_rate_cents) VALUES (1, 'T-1', 'tent', 1500), (2, 'S-1', 'stove', 500), (3, 'P-1', 'pack', 800);
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO gear (id, tag, kind, day_rate_cents) VALUES (4, 'T-1', 'tent', 1500), (5, 'K-1', 'kayak', 3000), (6, 'P-2', 'pack', 0) RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | Rentals can be stored
INSERT INTO rentals (id, gear_id, renter_email, out_on, due_on, back_on) VALUES (1, 1, 'kim@x.io', '2024-06-01', '2024-06-05', NULL), (2, 2, 'raj@x.io', '2024-06-01', '2024-06-03', '2024-06-01') RETURNING id
=> [[1], [2]]
--- check query | Gear already out cannot go out again; bad dates are refused
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO rentals (id, gear_id, renter_email, out_on, due_on, back_on) VALUES (3, 1, 'uma@x.io', '2024-06-02', '2024-06-04', NULL), (5, 3, 'lee@x.io', '2024-06-05', '2024-06-04', NULL), (6, 3, 'lee@x.io', '2024-06-05', '2024-06-07', '2024-06-04') RETURNING id
=> []
--- check query | The tent is out; the rest is in
SELECT gear_id, tag, kind, status FROM gear_status ORDER BY gear_id
=> [[1, "T-1", "tent", "out"], [2, "S-1", "stove", "in"], [3, "P-1", "pack", "in"]]
--- check query | Once the tent is back it can go out again
UPDATE rentals SET back_on = '2024-06-04' WHERE id = 1;
INSERT OR IGNORE INTO rentals (id, gear_id, renter_email, out_on, due_on) VALUES (4, 1, 'uma@x.io', '2024-06-05', '2024-06-08') RETURNING id
=> [[4]]
--- check query | Charges for returned rentals only; a same-day return is one day
SELECT rental_id, days, charge_cents FROM rental_charges ORDER BY rental_id
=> [[1, 3, 4500], [2, 1, 500]]
--- check query | rentals references gear with the default rule
SELECT "table", "from", on_delete FROM pragma_foreign_key_list('rentals')
=> [["gear", "gear_id", "NO ACTION"]]

+++ problem | Monthly invoicing with quiet months
--- schema
CREATE TABLE invoices (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  issued TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'void'))
);
CREATE TABLE invoice_lines (
  invoice_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES invoices(id),
  plan TEXT NOT NULL,
  cents INTEGER NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO invoices (id, customer_id, issued, status) VALUES
  (1, 1, '2024-01-05 10:00:00', 'paid'),
  (2, 2, '2024-01-20 12:00:00', 'paid'),
  (3, 1, '2024-03-02 09:00:00', 'paid'),
  (4, 3, '2024-03-15 16:00:00', 'void'),
  (5, 2, '2024-04-01 08:00:00', 'void');
INSERT INTO invoice_lines (invoice_id, plan, cents) VALUES
  (1, 'pro', 5000), (1, 'seats', 2000),
  (2, 'pro', 5000),
  (3, 'team', 9900),
  (4, 'pro', 5000),
  (5, 'pro', 3000);
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `invoices` (`id`, `customer_id`, `issued`, `status` `'paid'` or `'void'`) and `invoice_lines` (`invoice_id`, `plan`, `cents`). A void invoice was cancelled and counts for nothing.

Write one query with one row for every month from `2024-01` to `2024-04`, including months with no paid invoices, and four columns:

1. `month`: `'YYYY-MM'`.
2. `invoices`: how many paid invoices were issued that month.
3. `revenue`: the total `cents` of those invoices' lines, in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals (0.0 for a quiet month).
4. `change`: `revenue` minus the previous month's `revenue`, in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals, and NULL for the first month.

Sort by `month`.
--- starter
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', i.issued) AS month, COUNT(*) AS invoices, SUM(l.cents) / 100.0 AS revenue
FROM invoices i
JOIN invoice_lines l ON l.invoice_id = i.id
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE months(m) AS (
  SELECT '2024-01-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(m, '+1 month') FROM months WHERE m < '2024-04-01'
), totals AS (
  SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', i.issued) AS month,
         COUNT(DISTINCT i.id) AS invoices,
         SUM(l.cents) AS cents
  FROM invoices i
  JOIN invoice_lines l ON l.invoice_id = i.id
  WHERE i.status = 'paid'
  GROUP BY month
), filled AS (
  SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', mo.m) AS month,
         COALESCE(t.invoices, 0) AS invoices,
         COALESCE(t.cents, 0) AS cents
  FROM months mo
  LEFT JOIN totals t ON t.month = strftime('%Y-%m', mo.m)
)
SELECT month, invoices,
       ROUND(cents / 100.0, 2) AS revenue,
       ROUND((cents - LAG(cents) OVER (ORDER BY month)) / 100.0, 2) AS change
FROM filled
ORDER BY month;
--- check result | January's two invoices, a quiet February, and a void-only April
ordered
[["2024-01", 2, 120.0, null], ["2024-02", 0, 0.0, -120.0], ["2024-03", 1, 99.0, 99.0], ["2024-04", 0, 0.0, -99.0]]
--- check source absent | Never divides by a whole 100
/\s*100(?![.\d])
--- check source | Compares with the month before using LAG
[Ll][Aa][Gg]\s*\(

+++ problem | Customers who need a call
--- schema
CREATE TABLE customers (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'))
);
CREATE TABLE order_items (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id),
  sku TEXT NOT NULL,
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL,
  unit_cents INTEGER NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, sku)
);
CREATE TABLE support_tickets (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  state TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (state IN ('open', 'closed'))
);
INSERT INTO customers (id, email) VALUES (1, 'ana@mail.com'), (2, 'bo@mail.com'), (3, 'cy@mail.com'), (4, 'dee@mail.com');
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, status) VALUES (10, 1, 'paid'), (11, 1, 'shipped'), (12, 2, 'refunded'), (13, 3, 'paid');
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, sku, qty, unit_cents) VALUES
  (10, 'MUG', 1, 1000), (10, 'PEN', 1, 2500),
  (11, 'LAMP', 1, 4000),
  (12, 'HEAD', 1, 3000),
  (13, 'NOTE', 1, 500);
INSERT INTO support_tickets (id, customer_id, state) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'open'), (2, 1, 'closed'), (3, 2, 'open'), (4, 2, 'open'), (5, 4, 'open');
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `customers` (`id`, `email`), `orders` (`id`, `customer_id`, `status`), `order_items` (`order_id`, `sku`, `qty`, `unit_cents`) and `support_tickets` (`id`, `customer_id`, `state` `'open'` or `'closed'`).

Write one query with one row for **every** customer and five columns:

1. `email`.
2. `orders`: how many non-refunded orders they have.
3. `spent`: the total of `qty * unit_cents` over those orders, in dollars, rounded to 2 decimals, and 0 when none.
4. `open_tickets`: how many of their support tickets are open.
5. `flag`: `'call'` when they have more open tickets than non-refunded orders, otherwise `'ok'`.

Sort by `email`.
--- starter
SELECT c.email,
       COUNT(o.id) AS orders,
       SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) / 100.0 AS spent,
       COUNT(t.id) AS open_tickets
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN support_tickets t ON t.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status <> 'refunded' AND t.state = 'open'
GROUP BY c.id
ORDER BY c.email;
--- solution
WITH order_totals AS (
  SELECT o.customer_id, COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_cents) AS cents
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
  GROUP BY o.customer_id
), open_tickets AS (
  SELECT customer_id, COUNT(*) AS n
  FROM support_tickets
  WHERE state = 'open'
  GROUP BY customer_id
)
SELECT c.email,
       COALESCE(ot.orders, 0) AS orders,
       ROUND(COALESCE(ot.cents, 0) / 100.0, 2) AS spent,
       COALESCE(tk.n, 0) AS open_tickets,
       CASE WHEN COALESCE(tk.n, 0) > COALESCE(ot.orders, 0) THEN 'call' ELSE 'ok' END AS flag
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN order_totals ot ON ot.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN open_tickets tk ON tk.customer_id = c.id
ORDER BY c.email;
--- check result | Ana is fine; Bo and Dee need a call; Cy has no tickets
ordered
[["ana@mail.com", 2, 75.0, 1, "ok"], ["bo@mail.com", 0, 0.0, 2, "call"], ["cy@mail.com", 1, 5.0, 0, "ok"], ["dee@mail.com", 0, 0.0, 1, "call"]]
--- check source absent | Does not type any email
@mail\.com
--- check source absent | Never divides by a whole 100
/\s*100(?![.\d])

+++ problem | Webhook orders, one line at a time
--- schema
CREATE TABLE raw_webhooks (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  body TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO raw_webhooks (id, body) VALUES
  (1, '{"order": "A1", "email": "kim@x.io", "items": [{"sku": "MUG", "qty": 2, "price": "9.95"}, {"sku": "PEN", "qty": 1, "price": "4.35"}]}'),
  (2, '{"order": "A2", "email": "raj@x.io", "items": [{"sku": "MUG", "qty": 0, "price": "9.95"}, {"qty": 1, "price": "2.00"}, {"sku": "CUP", "qty": 3, "price": "0.29"}]}'),
  (3, '{"order": "A3", "email": "uma@x.io", "items": [{"sku": "LAMP"'),
  (4, '{"order": "A1", "email": "kim@x.io", "items": [{"sku": "MUG", "qty": 5, "price": "9.95"}]}'),
  (5, '{"order": "A4", "email": "uma@x.io", "items": []}'),
  (6, '{"email": "lee@x.io", "items": [{"sku": "MUG", "qty": 1, "price": "9.95"}]}');
--- task
This problem has its own table, `raw_webhooks` (`id`, `body`), holding order messages from a web shop in the order they arrived. Each good body is a JSON object with an `order` reference, an `email` and an `items` array; each item has a `sku`, a `qty` and a `price` (text, in dollars). The shop re-sends an order when it is not sure it got through, and the first copy received is the true one.

1. Create `order_lines` with `order_ref` (text, required), `sku` (text, required), `qty` (integer, required, more than zero), `unit_cents` (integer, required), and the primary key `(order_ref, sku)`.
2. Load it with one `INSERT OR IGNORE … SELECT`, reading the messages in the order they arrived and each message's items in their order. Skip bodies that are not valid JSON and messages with no `order`. An item with no `sku`, or with a `qty` of 0 or less, is not stored. `unit_cents` is the price in whole cents, rounded before the cast.
--- starter
CREATE TABLE order_lines (
  order_ref TEXT NOT NULL,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL,
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  unit_cents INTEGER NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (order_ref, sku)
);
--- solution
CREATE TABLE order_lines (
  order_ref TEXT NOT NULL,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL,
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  unit_cents INTEGER NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (order_ref, sku)
);

INSERT OR IGNORE INTO order_lines (order_ref, sku, qty, unit_cents)
SELECT w.body ->> '$.order',
       i.value ->> '$.sku',
       i.value ->> '$.qty',
       CAST(ROUND(CAST(i.value ->> '$.price' AS REAL) * 100) AS INTEGER)
FROM (SELECT id, body FROM raw_webhooks WHERE json_valid(body)) w,
     json_each(w.body, '$.items') AS i
WHERE w.body ->> '$.order' IS NOT NULL
  AND i.value ->> '$.sku' IS NOT NULL
  AND i.value ->> '$.qty' > 0
ORDER BY w.id, i.key;
--- check query | Three good lines; the retry of A1 did not change it
SELECT order_ref, sku, qty, unit_cents FROM order_lines ORDER BY order_ref, sku
=> [["A1", "MUG", 2, 995], ["A1", "PEN", 1, 435], ["A2", "CUP", 3, 29]]
--- check query | The message with no order reference was skipped
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM order_lines WHERE order_ref IS NULL OR order_ref = ''
=> [[0]]
--- check query | Every quantity is above zero
SELECT MIN(qty) FROM order_lines
=> [[1]]

+++ problem | How much of the audience shows up
--- task
Using the course's `events` table, return one row for every day from `2024-07-01` to `2024-07-10` with four columns:

1. `day`: `'YYYY-MM-DD'`.
2. `dau`: how many different users had any event that day (0 for a day with none).
3. `total_users`: how many different users had been seen **on or before** that day, over all of `events`.
4. `pct_active`: `dau` as a percentage of `total_users`, rounded to 1 decimal.

Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
FROM events
GROUP BY date(ts)
ORDER BY day;
--- solution
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT '2024-07-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT date(day, '+1 day') FROM days WHERE day < '2024-07-10'
), active AS (
  SELECT date(ts) AS day, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS dau
  FROM events
  GROUP BY date(ts)
), firsts AS (
  SELECT first_day AS day, COUNT(*) AS new_users
  FROM (SELECT user_id, MIN(date(ts)) AS first_day FROM events GROUP BY user_id)
  GROUP BY first_day
), daily AS (
  SELECT d.day, COALESCE(a.dau, 0) AS dau, COALESCE(f.new_users, 0) AS new_users
  FROM days d
  LEFT JOIN active a ON a.day = d.day
  LEFT JOIN firsts f ON f.day = d.day
), running AS (
  SELECT day, dau, SUM(new_users) OVER (ORDER BY day) AS total_users
  FROM daily
)
SELECT day, dau, total_users, ROUND(100.0 * dau / total_users, 1) AS pct_active
FROM running
ORDER BY day;
--- check result | Ten days; the share falls as the audience grows
ordered
[["2024-07-01", 2, 2, 100.0], ["2024-07-02", 3, 3, 100.0], ["2024-07-03", 3, 4, 75.0], ["2024-07-04", 0, 4, 0.0], ["2024-07-05", 3, 5, 60.0], ["2024-07-06", 2, 5, 40.0], ["2024-07-07", 2, 6, 33.3], ["2024-07-08", 1, 6, 16.7], ["2024-07-09", 2, 6, 33.3], ["2024-07-10", 1, 6, 16.7]]
--- check source | Counts users once each
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]
--- check source absent | Does not type the middle days
2024-07-0[2-9]

+++ problem | A funnel on one clock
--- task
Using the course's `events` table, build the funnel visit → signup → activate, where both later steps race **one** clock that starts at the user's first visit:

- step 1: the user's first `visit`;
- step 2: their first `signup` after that visit, counted only if it is within 72 hours of the **first visit**;
- step 3: their first `activate` after that signup, counted only if it too is within 72 hours of the **first visit**.

Both limits include the boundary. Return one row per step with five columns: `step`, `name`, `users`, `pct_first` (percentage of step 1, rounded to 1 decimal) and `pct_prev` (percentage of the step before, rounded to 1 decimal, NULL for step 1). Sort by `step`.
--- starter
SELECT name, COUNT(DISTINCT user_id) AS users
FROM events
GROUP BY name;
--- solution
WITH s1 AS (
  SELECT user_id, MIN(ts) AS t FROM events WHERE name = 'visit' GROUP BY user_id
), s2 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, s1.t AS t0, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s1 ON s1.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'signup' AND e.ts > s1.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id, s1.t
  HAVING unixepoch(MIN(e.ts)) - unixepoch(s1.t) <= 72 * 3600
), s3 AS (
  SELECT e.user_id, MIN(e.ts) AS t
  FROM events e JOIN s2 ON s2.user_id = e.user_id
  WHERE e.name = 'activate' AND e.ts > s2.t
  GROUP BY e.user_id, s2.t0
  HAVING unixepoch(MIN(e.ts)) - unixepoch(s2.t0) <= 72 * 3600
), steps AS (
  SELECT 1 AS step, 'visit' AS name, COUNT(*) AS users FROM s1
  UNION ALL SELECT 2, 'signup', COUNT(*) FROM s2
  UNION ALL SELECT 3, 'activate', COUNT(*) FROM s3
)
SELECT step, name, users,
       ROUND(100.0 * users / FIRST_VALUE(users) OVER (ORDER BY step), 1) AS pct_first,
       ROUND(100.0 * users / LAG(users) OVER (ORDER BY step), 1) AS pct_prev
FROM steps
ORDER BY step;
--- check result | 6 visitors, 5 signed up in time, only 2 activated before the clock ran out
ordered
[[1, "visit", 6, 100.0, null], [2, "signup", 5, 83.3, 83.3], [3, "activate", 2, 33.3, 40.0]]
--- check source | Measures the clock in seconds
unixepoch\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type the counts
\b[256]\s+AS\s+users

+++ problem | Retention by signup day
--- task
Using the course's `events` table, report day-1, day-3 and day-7 retention for each signup day. A user's day 0 is the date of their first `signup` event, and day N is the Nth calendar day after it. A user is eligible for day N only if their day N is on or before the last day in `events`, which you find with `MAX(date(ts))`.

Return one row per signup day with five columns:

1. `signup_day`: `'YYYY-MM-DD'`.
2. `users`: how many users signed up that day.
3. `d1`, 4. `d3`, 5. `d7`: the percentage of that day's **eligible** users who had any event on exactly day 1, 3 or 7, rounded to 1 decimal. When nobody in the group is eligible yet, the value is NULL.

Sort by `signup_day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(MIN(ts)) AS signup_day, COUNT(*) AS users
FROM events
WHERE name = 'signup'
GROUP BY user_id;
--- solution
WITH signups AS (
  SELECT user_id, date(MIN(ts)) AS d0 FROM events WHERE name = 'signup' GROUP BY user_id
), active AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT user_id, date(ts) AS d FROM events
), last AS (
  SELECT MAX(date(ts)) AS d FROM events
), n(day) AS (
  SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 3 UNION ALL SELECT 7
), pairs AS (
  SELECT s.d0, n.day,
         EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM active a
                 WHERE a.user_id = s.user_id AND a.d = date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days')) AS hit
  FROM signups s
  CROSS JOIN n
  CROSS JOIN last
  WHERE date(s.d0, '+' || n.day || ' days') <= last.d
), sizes AS (
  SELECT d0, COUNT(*) AS users FROM signups GROUP BY d0
)
SELECT z.d0 AS signup_day, z.users,
       ROUND(100.0 * SUM(CASE WHEN p.day = 1 THEN p.hit END) / NULLIF(COUNT(CASE WHEN p.day = 1 THEN 1 END), 0), 1) AS d1,
       ROUND(100.0 * SUM(CASE WHEN p.day = 3 THEN p.hit END) / NULLIF(COUNT(CASE WHEN p.day = 3 THEN 1 END), 0), 1) AS d3,
       ROUND(100.0 * SUM(CASE WHEN p.day = 7 THEN p.hit END) / NULLIF(COUNT(CASE WHEN p.day = 7 THEN 1 END), 0), 1) AS d7
FROM sizes z
LEFT JOIN pairs p ON p.d0 = z.d0
GROUP BY z.d0, z.users
ORDER BY z.d0;
--- check result | The last two signup days have not reached day 7
ordered
[["2024-07-01", 1, 100.0, 0.0, 100.0], ["2024-07-02", 2, 50.0, 50.0, 50.0], ["2024-07-03", 1, 0.0, 100.0, 100.0], ["2024-07-05", 1, 0.0, 0.0, null], ["2024-07-07", 1, 0.0, 0.0, null]]
--- check source | Finds the last day with MAX
[Mm][Aa][Xx]\s*\(\s*date\s*\(
--- check source absent | Finds the last day from the data, not by typing it
'\d{4}-\d\d-\d\d'

+++ problem | Stock that keeps itself right
--- schema
CREATE TABLE products (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  sku TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  stock INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (stock >= 0)
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('paid', 'shipped', 'returned', 'cancelled'))
);
CREATE TABLE order_items (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id),
  product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES products(id),
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)
);
INSERT INTO products (id, sku, stock) VALUES (1, 'MUG', 10), (2, 'PEN', 5), (3, 'LAMP', 2);
INSERT INTO orders (id, status) VALUES (1, 'paid'), (2, 'paid'), (3, 'paid'), (4, 'shipped');
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty) VALUES (1, 1, 2), (1, 2, 1), (2, 3, 2), (3, 1, 1), (4, 2, 3);
--- task
This problem has its own tables: `products` (`id`, `sku`, `stock`, never below 0), `orders` (`id`, `status`: `'paid'`, `'shipped'`, `'returned'` or `'cancelled'`) and `order_items` (`order_id`, `product_id`, `qty`). Order 4 was shipped before this change, and its stock was taken off at the time.

Make the warehouse stock update itself with two triggers on `orders`:

1. `order_shipped`: when an order's status **becomes** `'shipped'` (it was something else before), take each of its lines' `qty` off that product's `stock`.
2. `order_returned`: when an order's status goes from `'shipped'` to `'returned'`, put each line's `qty` back.

No other status change touches the stock, and saving an order that is already shipped changes nothing.
--- starter
CREATE TRIGGER order_shipped
AFTER UPDATE OF status ON orders
BEGIN
  UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1;
END;
--- solution
CREATE TRIGGER order_shipped
AFTER UPDATE OF status ON orders
WHEN OLD.status <> 'shipped' AND NEW.status = 'shipped'
BEGIN
  UPDATE products
  SET stock = stock - (SELECT oi.qty FROM order_items oi WHERE oi.order_id = NEW.id AND oi.product_id = products.id)
  WHERE id IN (SELECT product_id FROM order_items WHERE order_id = NEW.id);
END;

CREATE TRIGGER order_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF status ON orders
WHEN OLD.status = 'shipped' AND NEW.status = 'returned'
BEGIN
  UPDATE products
  SET stock = stock + (SELECT oi.qty FROM order_items oi WHERE oi.order_id = NEW.id AND oi.product_id = products.id)
  WHERE id IN (SELECT product_id FROM order_items WHERE order_id = NEW.id);
END;
--- check query | Shipping order 1 takes 2 mugs and 1 pen
UPDATE orders SET status = 'shipped' WHERE id = 1;
SELECT sku, stock FROM products ORDER BY id
=> [["MUG", 8], ["PEN", 4], ["LAMP", 2]]
--- check query | Saving it as shipped again changes nothing
UPDATE orders SET status = 'shipped' WHERE id = 1;
SELECT sku, stock FROM products ORDER BY id
=> [["MUG", 8], ["PEN", 4], ["LAMP", 2]]
--- check query | Returning the old order 4 puts 3 pens back
UPDATE orders SET status = 'returned' WHERE id = 4;
SELECT stock FROM products WHERE sku = 'PEN'
=> [[7]]
--- check query | Cancelling a paid order changes nothing; shipping the last lamps empties the shelf
UPDATE orders SET status = 'cancelled' WHERE id = 3;
UPDATE orders SET status = 'shipped' WHERE id = 2;
SELECT sku, stock FROM products ORDER BY id
=> [["MUG", 8], ["PEN", 7], ["LAMP", 0]]

+++ problem | A clinic's busy days
--- schema
CREATE TABLE staff (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE appointments (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  staff_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES staff(id),
  client_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  starts_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  ends_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'booked' CHECK (status IN ('booked', 'cancelled')),
  CHECK (ends_at > starts_at)
);
INSERT INTO staff (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Dr Ng'), (2, 'Dr Oz');
INSERT INTO appointments (id, staff_id, client_id, starts_at, ends_at, status) VALUES
  (1, 1, 1, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 10:00', 'booked'),
  (2, 1, 2, '2024-09-02 09:30', '2024-09-02 10:30', 'booked'),
  (3, 1, 3, '2024-09-02 13:00', '2024-09-02 13:45', 'booked'),
  (4, 1, 4, '2024-09-02 10:00', '2024-09-02 11:00', 'cancelled'),
  (5, 1, 1, '2024-09-03 09:00', '2024-09-03 09:45', 'booked'),
  (6, 2, 2, '2024-09-02 09:00', '2024-09-02 12:00', 'booked'),
  (7, 2, 3, '2024-09-02 10:00', '2024-09-02 10:30', 'booked'),
  (8, 2, 4, '2024-09-02 11:00', '2024-09-02 11:30', 'booked');
--- task
This problem has its own `staff` and `appointments`, built as in the appointment capstone and filled. A working day is 480 minutes.

Create a view named `utilization` with one row per staff member and calendar day that has booked appointments, and five columns:

1. `staff_id`.
2. `day`: `'YYYY-MM-DD'`, the date of `starts_at`.
3. `booked_minutes`: the total whole minutes of that day's booked appointments.
4. `pct_of_day`: `booked_minutes` as a percentage of 480, rounded to 1 decimal.
5. `clashes`: how many pairs of that day's booked appointments of that staff member overlap (each starts before the other ends; touching is not a clash), each pair counted once.

Canceled appointments count for nothing.
--- starter
CREATE VIEW utilization AS
SELECT a.staff_id, date(a.starts_at) AS day,
       SUM((unixepoch(a.ends_at) - unixepoch(a.starts_at)) / 60) AS booked_minutes,
       COUNT(b.id) AS clashes
FROM appointments a
LEFT JOIN appointments b
  ON b.staff_id = a.staff_id AND a.id < b.id
 AND a.starts_at < b.ends_at AND b.starts_at < a.ends_at
GROUP BY a.staff_id, date(a.starts_at);
--- solution
CREATE VIEW utilization AS
WITH booked AS (
  SELECT id, staff_id, starts_at, ends_at
  FROM appointments
  WHERE status = 'booked'
), minutes AS (
  SELECT staff_id, date(starts_at) AS day,
         SUM((unixepoch(ends_at) - unixepoch(starts_at)) / 60) AS booked_minutes
  FROM booked
  GROUP BY staff_id, date(starts_at)
), pairs AS (
  SELECT a.staff_id, date(a.starts_at) AS day, COUNT(*) AS clashes
  FROM booked a
  JOIN booked b
    ON b.staff_id = a.staff_id
   AND a.id < b.id
   AND date(b.starts_at) = date(a.starts_at)
   AND a.starts_at < b.ends_at
   AND b.starts_at < a.ends_at
  GROUP BY a.staff_id, date(a.starts_at)
)
SELECT m.staff_id, m.day, m.booked_minutes,
       ROUND(100.0 * m.booked_minutes / 480, 1) AS pct_of_day,
       COALESCE(p.clashes, 0) AS clashes
FROM minutes m
LEFT JOIN pairs p ON p.staff_id = m.staff_id AND p.day = m.day;
--- check query | Three busy days, with their minutes and clashes
SELECT staff_id, day, booked_minutes, pct_of_day, clashes FROM utilization ORDER BY staff_id, day
=> [[1, "2024-09-02", 165, 34.4, 1], [1, "2024-09-03", 45, 9.4, 0], [2, "2024-09-02", 240, 50.0, 2]]
--- check query | Cancelling one side of Dr Ng's clash removes it and its minutes
UPDATE appointments SET status = 'cancelled' WHERE id = 2;
SELECT booked_minutes, pct_of_day, clashes FROM utilization WHERE staff_id = 1 AND day = '2024-09-02'
=> [[105, 21.9, 0]]
--- check query | A day whose only appointment is cancelled disappears
UPDATE appointments SET status = 'cancelled' WHERE id = 5;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM utilization WHERE day = '2024-09-03'
=> [[0]]

+++ question | Cents that go missing
--- ask
`price` is the text `'4.35'`. What does this return?

```sql
SELECT CAST(CAST('4.35' AS REAL) * 100 AS INTEGER);
```
--- answer
434
--- why
4.35 has no exact binary form, so times 100 it is 434.99999999999994. `CAST(… AS INTEGER)` chops off everything after the point instead of rounding, which gives 434. That is why the course always writes `CAST(ROUND(…) AS INTEGER)`.

+++ question | Why whole cents
--- ask
Why does the store keep money in an `INTEGER` column of cents instead of a `REAL` column of dollars?
--- choice
An `INTEGER` column takes less space, and prices are small numbers.
--- choice correct
Whole numbers add up exactly, while most decimals cannot be stored exactly as floating point, so sums of many prices would drift by fractions of a cent.
--- choice
`REAL` columns cannot have a `CHECK` constraint.
--- choice
SQLite rounds every `REAL` to two decimals when it stores it, which loses the cents.
--- why
Floating point stores most decimals only approximately: `0.1 + 0.2` is `0.30000000000000004`. Integers have no such error, so a total of a million payments in cents is right to the cent. Divide by `100.0` only when showing the number.

+++ question | The refund filter in the WHERE
--- ask
Every customer should appear in this report, but some do not:

```sql
SELECT c.email, COUNT(o.id) AS orders
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status <> 'refunded'
GROUP BY c.id;
```

Which customers go missing?
--- choice
Only customers whose orders were all refunded.
--- choice
Only customers who have never ordered.
--- choice correct
Customers who have never ordered, and customers whose only orders were refunded.
--- choice
None: a `LEFT JOIN` always keeps every customer.
--- why
A customer with no orders gets one joined row with `o.status` NULL, and `NULL <> 'refunded'` is not true, so the `WHERE` drops it. A customer whose only orders were refunded has only rows that fail the test. The `LEFT JOIN` kept both; the `WHERE` undid it. The filter belongs in the `ON`.

+++ question | The index that refuses nothing
--- ask
This index is meant to stop a copy from being on two open loans at once:

```sql
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_loan_per_copy ON loans(copy_id) WHERE returned_on = NULL;
```

What does it actually do?
--- choice
It refuses every loan whose `returned_on` is NULL.
--- choice correct
It holds no rows at all, so it never refuses anything.
--- choice
It stops each copy from ever being lent twice, open or closed.
--- choice
SQLite refuses to create it, with a syntax error.
--- why
`returned_on = NULL` is never true, not even when `returned_on` is NULL: any comparison with NULL is unknown. So no row matches the index's `WHERE`, the index is empty, and two open loans for the same copy go straight in. The condition must be `WHERE returned_on IS NULL`.

+++ question | A sum over no rows
--- ask
No order line has a `qty` above 100. What does this return? Type the value.

```sql
SELECT SUM(qty * unit_cents) FROM order_items WHERE qty > 100;
```
--- answer
NULL
--- why
`SUM` over no rows is NULL, not 0: there is nothing to add. `COUNT` over no rows is 0. That difference is why reports wrap sums in `COALESCE(SUM(…), 0)` when the truth is "nothing was spent".

+++ question | Why ORDER BY id in the ingest
--- ask
The ingest in "Analytics 1: ingesting raw events" ends with `ORDER BY id`. What goes wrong without it?
--- choice
The events would be stored in random order, and later queries could not sort them.
--- choice
`INSERT OR IGNORE` refuses to run unless its `SELECT` is sorted.
--- choice correct
Nothing guarantees which copy of a repeated event is inserted first, so "keep the first copy received" could keep a retry instead.
--- choice
The primary key would let both copies in.
--- why
`INSERT OR IGNORE` keeps whichever copy arrives first and skips the rest. Without `ORDER BY id`, the database may read the rows in any order, so the copy that wins is not reliably the one received first. Sorting by arrival makes "first" mean what the rule says.

+++ question | Users whose day 7 has not come
--- ask
In a day-7 retention report, a user signed up 3 days before the data ends. Why is that user left out of the day-7 numbers instead of being counted as "not retained"?
--- choice
Because users who signed up recently are usually test accounts.
--- choice correct
Their day 7 has not happened yet, so whether they come back is unknown; counting them as "no" would push the number down for no real reason.
--- choice
Because `date(d0, '+7 days')` returns NULL for a date after the last event.
--- choice
Because `EXISTS` cannot look at dates after the last event.
--- why
The data is censored: it stops before the answer arrives. A user can only be counted for day N once day N is on or before the last day of data. That is why eligible users, not all users, are the denominator.

+++ question | What the two-column index saves
--- ask
`appointments` has an index on `(staff_id, starts_at)`. For one staff member with k appointments in a table of n rows, how much work does this query take?

```sql
SELECT * FROM appointments WHERE staff_id = 1 ORDER BY starts_at;
```
--- choice
O(n log n): every row is read and then sorted.
--- choice
O(n): every row is read, but no sort is needed.
--- choice correct
About O(log n + k): the index finds staff member 1's first entry, then reads their k entries already in time order, with no separate sort.
--- choice
O(k log k): the k rows are found at once but still have to be sorted.
--- why
An index is sorted by its first column, then its second. Finding `staff_id = 1` takes about log n steps, and that staff member's entries sit side by side, already ordered by `starts_at`, so the plan has no `USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY` step.

+++ question | A trigger with no memory of the past
--- ask
The fines trigger is written without `OLD.returned_on IS NULL`:

```sql
CREATE TRIGGER loan_returned
AFTER UPDATE OF returned_on ON loans
WHEN NEW.returned_on > NEW.due_on
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO fines (loan_id, cents) VALUES (NEW.id, 250);
END;
```

Loan 2 came back late, and was fined. A librarian now corrects its return date to another late date. What happens?
--- choice
The fine is updated to match the new date.
--- choice
Nothing: a trigger fires only once per row.
--- choice correct
The trigger tries to insert a second fine for loan 2, the primary key on `fines.loan_id` refuses it, and the librarian's correction fails with the same error.
--- choice
A second fine for loan 2 is added, and the member is charged twice.
--- why
Without the test on `OLD`, every update that sets a late `returned_on` fires the trigger, including a correction. The second `INSERT` breaks the primary key, and because a trigger runs inside the same statement, the error cancels the whole `UPDATE`.

+++ question | Back to back
--- ask
Appointment a runs from `'09:00'` to `'09:30'` and appointment b from `'09:30'` to `'10:00'`, on the same day. What does this return, 0 or 1?

```sql
SELECT '09:00' < '10:00' AND '09:30' < '09:30' AS overlap;
```
--- answer
0
--- why
Two slots overlap when each starts before the other ends. a starts before b ends, but b does not start before a ends: `'09:30' < '09:30'` is false. Back-to-back appointments only touch, which is what the strict `<` allows.

+++ question | Two lists on one person
--- ask
Ana paid for two expenses (9000 and 5000 cents) and owes two shares (3000 and 2500 cents). What is wrong with this balance query?

```sql
SELECT p.name, SUM(e.amount_cents) AS paid, SUM(s.share_cents) AS owed
FROM people p
LEFT JOIN expenses e ON e.paid_by = p.id
LEFT JOIN shares s ON s.person_id = p.id
GROUP BY p.id;
```
--- choice
Nothing; it gives 14000 paid and 5500 owed.
--- choice correct
The two joins pair every expense with every share, so Ana gets four rows and both totals come out doubled: 28000 and 11000.
--- choice
It drops people who have no expenses.
--- choice
`SUM` cannot be used twice in one query.
--- why
Expenses and shares are two separate one-to-many lists hanging off each person. Joining both at once multiplies them: 2 × 2 = 4 rows, and every amount is counted twice. Total each list on its own, one row per person, and only then join the totals.
